Level Z1

1. A Hip-Hop History

1Cheryl James and Sandra Denton are known as Salt-N-Pepa. They pose with their DJ, Deidra Roper, called DJ Spinderella. In the 1970s, New York City's economy was falling apart. High unemployment led to poverty, homelessness, more crime, and crumbling buildings. Officials cut funding for schools, community services, and job programs. Racism created barriers for people of color, especially Black people. Most could not move away, as many white people did. Shops, theaters, and clubs closed, so young people found new ways to express themselves.

2One afternoon in 1973, Cindy Campbell asked her brother Clive to DJ a party in the South Bronx. The neighborhood was mostly Black and Puerto Rican. The Campbells had come from Jamaica five years earlier. Clive performed as DJ Kool Herc with two turntables and a guitar amplifier. He extended the drum breaks so people could dance longer. The party made him famous, and he is called the Father of Hip-Hop. His style drew on Jamaican toasting, which grew from West African griots. Griots are storytellers, and many fans see rappers as modern griots.

3Hip-hop is a whole culture, not just music. At first, artists had no clubs, stages, or galleries, so they created in the streets. The four pillars are the DJ, breaking, graffiti art, and the MC. A DJ fades in a new song so the music never stops. Scratching comes from moving a record back and forth on the turntable. Crowds loved the break beat, the part where only the drums play. DJs such as Grandmaster Flash repeated that beat to make new music from old songs. They preferred breaking, and called themselves b-boys, b-girls, or breakers.

4Breakers danced to hip-hop, funk, and breakbeat music. They showed their skills during the break beat with six kinds of moves. Top-rock is quick footwork done standing, and down-rock is footwork on the floor. Power moves are acrobatic and need speed and strength. Freezes are sudden stops in a hard pose. Popping moves the arms and legs in a smooth way, and locking snaps limbs at sharp angles. Dance battles were common, and the crowd judged creativity and skill. In the early 1970s, artists painted trains and bridges, and Lady Pink honored women in her art.

5Damaging public property is illegal, but many artists called themselves outlaws. Some outsiders saw pollution, yet hip-hop treated graffiti as art. The art later appeared in galleries, museums, and books. Sandra Fabara is Lady Pink, and Fred Brathwaite is Fab 5 Freddy. Jean-Michel Basquiat and Donald Joseph White, called Dondi, are also famous. The MC, or master of ceremonies, hyped the crowd and then began to rhyme. Rappers are judged by their ideas, their flow with the beat, and their delivery. KRS-One says knowledge is a fifth pillar that can power social change.

6Women have helped shape hip-hop from the start. MC Sha Rock, or Sharon Green, is called the first female rapper. In 1979, she became the first female rapper to get a record deal with Funky 4 + 1. In 1981, they were the first hip-hop group on national TV. Sylvia Robinson, called the Mother of Hip-Hop, started Sugar Hill Records in 1979. Rapper's Delight by the Sugarhill Gang became the first nationwide hip-hop hit. MC Lyte, Queen Latifah, Salt-N-Pepa, and Lauryn Hill used rap to empower women. A judge made Roxanne Shante lose a battle so a girl would not win.

7Hip-hop began as party music, but early songs also faced hard issues. Grandmaster Flash's song The Message told of poverty and misery in New York City. Public Enemy, a top group in the 1990s, spoke of Black pride and racism. MC Lyte, or Lana Michelle Moorer, rapped about racism, sexism, and drug use. In the 1990s, gangsta rap grew popular, and some people found the words offensive. Many Black artists were given deals only if they made that style. Rap is also seen as poetry, and Maya Angelou saw that link too. The Last Poets and the Watts Prophets spoke poems on pride, racism, and justice.

8From the mid-1980s to the early 1990s, hip-hop spread across the country. Record companies spent a lot of money as radio and clubs demanded more. Hardcore rap and boombap grew in New York, and G-Funk grew in Los Angeles. Run DMC, De La Soul, and Salt-N-Pepa mixed hip-hop with other styles. Sampling took a piece of a song, or a movie clip, and used it in a new song. In the early 1980s, hip-hop reached the Maori, indigenous people of New Zealand. They were left out of mainstream culture and strongly identified with hip-hop. Breaking spread through Wild Style and Breakin', and graffiti appeared from Brazil to China.

9Graffiti decorates a building in Brazil. A b-girl from Japan dances at a competition in Hungary. A member of a Muslim hip-hop duo performs in England. This movement began at a young girl's street party in the South Bronx in 1973. It has become a global sensation. A lack of resources led to creative new music and art in the twentieth century. Today hip-hop still influences popular culture worldwide. It has left a lasting mark.

2. A King Found Beneath the Parking Lot

1A rough floor plan shows Greyfriars Abbey. It shows how the abbey may have looked. The abbey was destroyed about fifty years after Richard III died. The plan marks trenches. The plan marks walls that were found. The plan marks walls that were only guessed. The plan marks a bench that was only guessed. This book has these parts. Under the Parking Lot comes first. Behind the Search comes next. Then comes Ancient History. Then comes Modern Science. Then comes Honors for a King. Then comes Villain or Victim? The glossary comes last. A team member uses ground penetration radar. The tool is called GPR. The place is the Greyfriars parking lot. The team hopes to find the grave of Richard III. Under the Parking Lot. An ancient skeleton starts this tale.

2Finding the king's long-hidden body under a city parking lot was not an accident. Historical records pointed the way. Researchers knew what lay under that plain parking lot. The buried ruins of the Greyfriars Priory were there. It was a monastery. Catholic monks lived there from 1255 to the late 1530s. A medieval historian recorded Richard's burial. Battle death came first. Burial in the priory followed. The battle was at nearby Bosworth Field. The month was August. The year was 1485. Richard's side had lost the battle. His side had lost the throne. Claiming his body was probably a brave act for the monks. Signs show that he was buried quickly. His skull was jammed upright. That was so he would fit. The grave was slightly too small for his body. There was no coffin. The grave sat in a position of honor. It was near the church's high altar. Even so, it was unmarked. It was under the monastery floor. Just over fifty years later, the abbey at Greyfriars was destroyed. The year was 1538. Who won and who lost in 1485 led straight to that destruction. In time the town grew and covered the site. By the time modern researchers hunted for Richard, the church had been underground a very long time. Its time underground was almost as long as his. They consulted old maps. In the centuries after the abbey was destroyed, street names had changed. Finding the exact grave took careful detective work. Even after they found the site, almost nobody expected a skeleton inside. The archaeologists themselves did not expect one. Some ancient accounts said Richard's body had not been buried at all. They said it had been thrown in the River Soar. That was after the body was paraded through the streets of Leicester. The king's first burial, by monks, was under the monastery altar. As soon as the archaeologists found the skeleton, they were pretty certain whose it was. The bones expert saw a gash in the skull. Then she saw the twisted spine. She reported that the hair on her neck rose. This was King Richard III.

3King Richard hoped a brave charge would change the battle. He charged toward Henry. He hoped the charge would inspire his troops. He hoped they would renew the fight. He hoped they would defeat Henry's forces. Richard died in 1485. The place was the Battle of Bosworth Field. The field is a few miles from Leicester. He led a direct charge toward his enemy. The enemy was Henry Tudor. Slippery ground cost Richard his horse. Henry's soldiers overran him. He lost his life. His crown fell beside his body. Men he trusted betrayed him. Bosworth Field is where Richard III died. Writers of the time described the blows that killed him. Similar injury marks were on the skeleton beneath the parking lot. There were ten blows. Eight of them were to the skull. A large fracture in the skull was likely the killing wound. The fracture was behind his left ear. No later English king died in battle.

4Henry Tudor led the triumphant procession back into town. Richard's body was slung over the back of a horse. Two days in the town square was how long Richard's body was left. The display was proof that he was dead. It was proof that Henry was now the king. The disrespectful display also showed his crooked back. Everyone could see it. In the play Richard III, Shakespeare depicts Richard as a hunchback. The character has a raised right shoulder. A crooked spine would cause that. In the play, this deformity brings scorn more than sympathy. Real life is another matter. The skeleton of Richard III shows a clear curve in his spine. His spine began to bend when he was young. The bend probably started between the ages of 10 and 12. Doctors at the time may have known the cause. They certainly would not have known how to help him. Because of the crooked spine, he stood inches shorter than his true height. His true height was five feet eight inches. That was tall for a medieval man. As an adult he probably lived with pain that almost never stopped. Centuries later we know the name of his condition. He suffered from scoliosis. Archaeologists lifted the arms from the grave first. They lifted the legs. They lifted the skull. When they finally reached the torso, they saw the crooked spine. Then they knew they had found Richard.

5Ancient history pointed one way. The bones belonged to Richard III. Next came modern science. Scientists studied the bones. The man was about thirty-two years old. Richard was that age when he died. His food was rich in meat. His food was rich in ocean fish. Only wealthy people ate that well then. A king would eat that well. They used carbon dating on two ribs. The death fell between 1455 and 1540. Richard died in 1485. Michael Ibsen comes from Richard's sister, Anne. He is a seventeenth-generation descendant. Scientists studied DNA. DNA is information in our cells. It shapes many traits. That DNA type shows up in under 2 percent of people in England. Scientists took DNA from two living descendants. The samples matched. A picture comes next, with the story of honors. The picture shows the tomb of Queen Elizabeth I. She ruled from 1558 to 1603. Many people call her a great ruler. Tradition places British monarchs at Westminster Abbey. The abbey is in London. People then asked where to bury Richard. Within twenty-four hours, two towns claimed his body. Leicester wanted him nearby. The spot would be barely 100 yards from the bones. The place would be Leicester Cathedral. A center for visitors was part of the plan. Richard came from York. York is 100 miles north of Leicester. Some people wanted burial in York. Other people wanted London. They wanted Westminster Abbey. Officials chose Leicester Cathedral. The public first saw the tomb in March 2015.

6People do not agree about what sort of man Richard was. We know he met a violent end. The question is whether he deserved what he got. For centuries, almost no English king was hated more. Shakespeare's play Richard III depicts him in a harsh way. He is limping. He is deformed. He is haunted. Shakespeare never knew the king. More than a hundred years after Richard's death, the play was written. He wrote during the reign of the Tudors. The Tudors were Richard's enemies. Some people argue that the Tudors wronged Richard III. They argue that Shakespeare wronged him too. They say he was a victim of their propaganda. What they said and wrote made him seem worse than he was. A picture shows the playwright William Shakespeare. The picture he gave of Richard III may have been false. Certainty may stay out of reach. Here is what we do know. In 1483 Richard's older brother died. The brother was King Edward IV. He probably died of pneumonia. Loyalty to his brother had been Richard's habit. Before Edward died, he named Richard to a role. Richard would be the royal protector of Edward's eldest son. In time that son would have become Edward V of England. Instead, Richard ascended the throne. He began to consolidate power for himself. He imprisoned his brother's two sons in the Tower of London. The sons were Edward and Richard. After that, nobody saw the young princes. No one knows what happened to them. Two years into Richard's reign, a battle was fought for control of England. It was between Richard's forces and the followers of Henry Tudor. The place was Bosworth Field. Men he thought were his supporters betrayed him. He lost his horse. He fought desperately on foot. He was killed. Someone lifted the crown from the spot where it fell. On that battlefield, Henry Tudor received the crown of England. He became King Henry VII. Years later, in 1534, Henry's son broke with the Catholic Church. The son was Henry VIII. He dissolved the monasteries. That act led to the destruction of Greyfriars Priory. It led to the loss of the priory. Centuries later, Richard's skeleton was uncovered there.

7Clarity fades for the rest of the tale. After Henry VII took the throne, rumors spread. The rumors were about Richard. The talk concerned the possible fate of the two young princes held in the Tower. Their murder may have been Richard III's order. Maybe they lived through the two years he reigned as king. Maybe the new king, Henry VII, killed them later. Henry VII had killed Richard. A secret trip out of the country may have carried the princes to safety. Today, fans of Richard III hold a hope. They believe the discovery of his skeleton may change people's opinions. In their view he cared deeply about ordinary people's rights. They believe he established many principles of modern law. One idea says a defendant stays innocent unless guilt is proved. Another idea is that defendants have a right to bail. Another idea is that all laws must be known. They must be published. His story may be told differently because of what happens now. A saying puts it this way. Victors are the ones who write the history. He lost his life more than 525 years ago. He lost his throne then too. A significant victory may still be his. The victory comes from a shallow grave. The grave was hidden under a parking lot. These words have special meanings in this book. An archaeologist studies what ancient cultures left behind. Ascended means moved upward. It can mean rose in position or rank. Betrayed means hurt another person by being unfaithful, false, or disloyal. Carbon dating is a test used to determine age. The test measures certain kinds of carbon inside a very old object. A deformity is a body part or an object part that looks abnormal. Descendants are the later family of a person or group from earlier times. Despised means disliked very much. It means hated. DNA holds the genetic information of a living thing. The long name is deoxyribonucleic acid. Medieval means of or relating to the Middle Ages. That period in European history runs from the year 500 to the year 1500. Monarchs are rulers of a kingdom or an empire. Kings are monarchs. Queens are monarchs. Monks or nuns live and worship in the buildings of a monastery. Propaganda is news, arts, or other media. It often uses false or exaggerated information to influence people.

3. A New Beat That Would Not Fade

1Bill Haley and the Comets made Rock Around the Clock, the earliest rock 'n' roll song to reach number 1. This book follows that song back to the blues. It follows the change from rhythm and blues into rock 'n' roll. It looks at teenagers, at racism, and at barriers that fell. It ends by showing why the music stayed, and it explains hard words. May 1955 brought the movie Blackboard Jungle into theaters. Its story was set inside a high school in New York City. As the movie opened, the room heard Rock Around the Clock, recorded by Bill Haley and the Comets. No rock 'n' roll song had been heard in a movie before. The tune was so catchy that teenagers danced in the theaters. Many adults were not pleased with this new music called rock 'n' roll. At best they called it a fad. At worst they called it a danger. The words often touched taboo subjects. Popular media usually stayed away from those subjects. So the music looked like rebellion. Adults labeled rock 'n' roll junk. They labeled it poison. They labeled it a disease. Many adults demanded a radio ban. They burned records. They smashed records. Blackboard Jungle helped launch the music. The anger did not matter. Rock 'n' roll had already arrived to stay.

2No single year marks the birth of rock 'n' roll. No single place marks it either. The music grew across time. Players all over the United States mixed styles. The mix drew on jazz and on swing, on gospel, on country-western, and on rhythm and blues. Rhythm and blues, called R&B, shaped early rock 'n' roll more than any other style. R&B itself grew from older music called blues. Blues began in the South near the end of the 1800s. Work songs and spirituals were its start. Enslaved people sang them on plantations. The music spoke the suffering of Black farmers in rural places. It also spoke their hope. From the 1920s on, millions of Black people left the South. They wanted to escape racism that was brutal. They wanted better jobs in cities of the North. Blues traveled north with the musicians. Bessie Smith was one artist who carried blues to a much wider public. City players later changed how they sang. They changed how they performed. New styles appeared, including Delta blues and urban blues. Electric guitars replaced acoustic ones. Electric sound could cut through a noisy room. Songs got faster. A heavier beat made dancing easy. That is how R&B was born. Sister Rosetta Tharpe came long before the famous rock 'n' roll stars of the 1950s. People now call her the Godmother of Rock 'n' Roll. She joined pieces of blues, jazz, and gospel. What she made was an early form of rock 'n' roll. She was a Black woman in a business run by white men. Barriers and hard trials stood in her way. She became a star anyway. Before she turned twenty-five, she played some of the country's most famous music rooms. Her voice and her guitar shaped famous rock players of the 1950s and the 1960s. The Hall of Fame for rock music took those players in many decades ahead of her. Her own induction waited until 2018. Forty-five years had passed since her death.

3A picture places LaVern Baker at the far right. She ranks among the most successful early artists of R&B. Fans stand with her. The year was 1951. Two small companies began to work as partners. Chess Records was in Chicago, Illinois. Sun Records was in Memphis, Tennessee. They searched for new R&B artists and recorded them. Some of the best-known R&B players reached a wider public through them. Stations across the country put the songs on the air. Players in the Mid-South heard that radio. Country-western music was already popular there, and they blended it with R&B. From country-western they took drums. They took a vocal twang. They took the steel guitar as the lead instrument. In R&B, the lead was usually a saxophone or a piano. The blend made lively music called rockabilly. Rockabilly was one of the first styles of rock 'n' roll. Early rock 'n' roll was fast. It was cheerful. A heavy rhythm made it good for dancing. Melodies stuck in the ear. Words were simple enough to sing along. Many of the first artists were young. Songs often talked about fun or about falling in love. Teenagers of the 1950s responded to all of that. In that same decade, Mexican American players in California and the Southwest mixed rock 'n' roll with Latin music. Chicano is a name sometimes used in the United States for people whose descent is Mexican. The music they made was called Chicano rock. Ritchie Valens carried Chicano rock to the whole country. He was seventeen when two songs became top hits in 1958. One song, La Bamba, came from an old Mexican folk song. Every word of it was Spanish. A few months later, while on tour, Valens died in a plane crash. Other Chicano rock players followed the path he opened. The Rock & Roll Hall of Fame inducted him in 2001.

4The American economy boomed in the 1950s. Pay was high. Few people were out of work. Earlier generations of teenagers often had to help support the family. Many teenagers of this time did not. Money they earned could stay with them. Parents were earning more, so allowances were common too. Teenagers had more spending money than ever. Two new inventions made fresh music easier to reach. The first was the 45. A 45 is a vinyl record with one song on each side. Older records were larger. A 45 was small enough to carry to school or to a friend's house. The price was low. Almost any teenager with an allowance could buy one. Jukeboxes were machines that played vinyl records. A transistor radio was the other new device. Before it, a radio was a big piece of furniture for the whole family. A transistor radio let teenagers hear favorite music while they were out. Parents' music and parents' programs no longer had to fill their ears. Stations saw teenagers as a new audience and built shows for them. The Moondog House was one of the most popular. Alan Freed, a host in Cleveland, Ohio, ran it. The show grew so popular that stations nationwide aired it. The name became Rock 'n' Roll Party. Freed did not invent the words rock 'n' roll. He was first to use those words for the style he played. Rock 'n' Roll Party was among the first radio shows made for both Black and white listeners. Deep segregation marked both society in America and its popular culture then. American Bandstand was a television show seen across the nation. Teenagers danced to rock 'n' roll on it. Every weekday, millions of teenagers could watch after school. They heard the newest hits. They learned new dances. Black teenagers had often danced those steps first. They danced them on another program, The Mitch Thomas Show. Black musicians did perform on American Bandstand. Black teenagers were often barred from dancing there. They were often barred from sitting in the studio audience. The show was never officially segregated. Even so, Black teenagers did not appear there regularly until 1964. The show had already been on the air for twelve years.

5When Freed's radio show began, R&B was called race music. Black artists made it, mostly for Black listeners. Mainstream radio mostly ignored it. White listeners mostly ignored it. Then shop owners who sold records noticed a change. Radio hosts noticed it too. White teenagers were growing interested in R&B. Jerry Lee Lewis sang songs first made by Black artists. Companies wanted the money in that interest. Companies thought a white teenager would usually reject the record after learning the singer was Black. So the companies hired white players to cover the songs. Black artists had written those songs and sung them first. The white singers usually softened the raw feeling in the voice. They rewrote some of the words. A broader audience was supposed to find the songs more acceptable. The watered-down copies often outsold the originals. Mainstream radio promoted them and played them. The Black writers were paid little. Sometimes they were paid nothing. Racism held back even the most successful Black artists. At a show in Alabama, the Flamingos were ordered not to meet the eyes of white people in the crowd. At a Tennessee concert, a white band took Chuck Berry's place. The organizer had learned that Berry was Black. In 1957 the first primetime television show of rock 'n' roll was taken off the air. Frankie Lymon, a Black singer of only fourteen, had danced with a white girl. Pat Boone and Elvis Presley were two leading white rock 'n' roll artists of the 1950s. Both grew famous by covering songs from Black artists. Boone softened the songs so white listeners would like them more. Presley kept the gritty, emotional sound. He kept the content too. Boone looked and sounded friendly and clean-cut. White parents approved. Those same parents saw Presley as wild and rebellious. That reputation is probably why teenagers wanted him. From 1956 to 1960, Presley had fifteen songs at number 1. Boone had five.

6Racism fed a fear. People feared rock 'n' roll would draw Black and white teenagers together. In some ways, that is what happened. The music crossed the racial split in popular culture. Shows by Black musicians and by white musicians usually drew a mixed crowd. Some shows began with the crowd split apart. A rope held the groups separate. People still crossed that line. By the night's end they were dancing together. The Staple Singers belong in this story. Soon rock players used songs to back equality in a direct way. Ray Charles refused segregated stages in the 1960s, and so did James Brown and the Beatles. Other artists wrote songs that called for racial justice. Chuck Berry did. The Impressions did. The Staple Singers did. Marchers and protesters for civil rights even sang those songs. By the 1960s, rock 'n' roll was popular around the world. Players began to treat it as art. It was no longer only popular entertainment. It became a force that could change society. Young players used it to protest war. They used it to protest injustice. The civil rights movement gained national notice. The Black Power movement did too. Frustration went into songs by Marvin Gaye, by Aretha Franklin, by Nina Simone, and by Sam Cooke. They put hope into music too. A picture shows folk singer Joan Baez at the March on Washington. That famous civil rights protest was in 1963.

7Four artists in one picture helped keep rock popular through the 1970s and the 1980s. Jimi Hendrix is at the bottom left. Joan Jett is at the top left. Tina Turner is in the center. Elton John is on the right. The 1970s and the 1980s brought many new forms of rock 'n' roll. Punk rock was one. Glam rock was another. Heavy metal was another. Today, dozens of smaller styles sit inside rock. New rock artists rise every year. Players who were famous decades ago still tour the country. Their shows still sell out. Rock 'n' roll was not the short fad many people in the 1950s expected. It was not the fad they wanted. Acoustic describes an instrument whose sound does not come from an electronic device. Allowances are sums of money given on a regular schedule. Appealed means caught someone's interest. Banned means not allowed. Catchy means pleasant, or easy to remember. A fad is wide excitement for something popular only a short time. Mainstream means the beliefs a society accepts most widely. Racism is unequal treatment based on a belief that some races are better. A rebellion is a fight against authority or power. Records are vinyl disks that hold recorded music. Segregated means kept apart by group differences such as race. Taboo means forbidden, or avoided, because it clashes with common social values.

4. A New Skyline

1In 1930, a worker sat on a beam and bolted the frame of the Empire State Building. A Port Authority policeman watched traffic from an electric patrol car in the Holland Tunnel. What would Paris be without the Eiffel Tower, or Dubai without the Burj Khalifa? Cities are known by the tall shapes that make their skylines. Skylines can change, unlike fingerprints. In Manhattan, skyscrapers have risen, fallen, and risen again. New York began as an indigenous homeland, was colonized by the Dutch, and is now a major world hub. Its skyline has changed with the times.

2An 1884 picture of Lower Manhattan shows how little of the skyline was built then. Indigenous tribes lived there before Dutch settlers came in the seventeenth century. In 1609, Henry Hudson sailed the Half Moon into the river later named for him and called the port New Amsterdam. The British took New Amsterdam, renamed it New York, and the port kept growing. By the American Revolution, four thousand buildings stood packed on southern Manhattan. After the Civil War in 1865, formerly enslaved people came north for work, and immigrants from Ireland, Poland, and Italy followed. By the late 1800s New York was the most crowded city in the United States, and by 1925 it was the most crowded in the world. Manhattan is only about 23 square miles, so when ground space ran out, the city had to grow upward, but thick-walled buildings could not be very tall.

3A steel frame, not thick walls, held up the new tall buildings. Huge steel beams made a skeleton, and walls hung from the beams like curtains. By 1900, New York had skyscrapers, and builders competed to make the world's tallest. Walter Chrysler funded the Art Deco Chrysler Building. The Park Row Building in 1899 was 391 feet and 29 stories, and the Singer Building in 1908 was 670 feet and 37 stories. The Met Life Tower in 1909 was 700 feet and 50 stories, and the Woolworth Building in 1913 was 792 feet and 55 stories. The Chrysler Building in 1930 was 1,048 feet and 77 stories, and the Empire State Building in 1931 was 1,454 feet and 103 stories. One World Trade Center in 2014 is 1,776 feet, or 1,792 with its spire, and 104 stories.

4In 1928 the Chrysler Building dueled a nearby tower for the world's tallest title. A smuggled spire made it the tallest, and the only building over 1,000 feet. Its lead was short, and the Empire State Building took the crown. Work began in January 1930, and more than 3,000 workers finished it in 410 days. They added four and a half stories a week to the steel and granite tower. Sixty-four elevators reached the 80th floor in under a minute, and decks on the 86th and 102nd floors gave wide views. It was a symbol of New York and a modern wonder, and the world's tallest for nearly forty years. The Port Authority, formed in 1921, built the George Washington Bridge and still runs much of the 16-acre World Trade Center.

5After the Empire State Building, a weak economy slowed new skyscrapers until 1970. In the 1960s, officials planned a world trade center in Lower Manhattan, and the Port Authority led the work. Minoru Yamasaki, a Detroit-born Japanese American, designed twin 110-story towers in a complex of offices and shops. Elisha Otis had made tall buildings possible in 1854 with a safety elevator that dropped only a few inches if its cable broke. At the busiest time, over 3,500 workers built the towers. They used tube frames, like hollow square pipes, with close columns around the outside. Fewer inside supports left more floor space. Express elevators went to sky lobbies, then local cars stopped at the next ten floors, like subway lines, so fewer shafts were needed.

6Work on the towers began in 1966. The North Tower was done in December 1970, stood 1,727 feet at its spire, and had the Windows on the World restaurant on top. The South Tower, 1,362 feet tall, was finished seven months later, with a garage, a railroad, and subway stations below both towers. Set apart from other tall towers, they were Manhattan's tallest buildings for thirty years. On September 11, 2001, hijackers flew two passenger jets into the towers. The South Tower fell within an hour, the North Tower fell thirty minutes later, and nearly three thousand people died. People mourned and vowed to rebuild, and the Port Authority later unveiled a new design. The new design set aside a memorial park, with reflecting pools and waterfalls where the towers stood, and a museum of artifacts from the old towers.

7One World Trade Center, nicknamed the Freedom Tower, is a new 104-story tower nearby. It rises 1,776 feet to mark the year the United States won freedom from British rule, and it is taller than the old towers. Glass panels form a twisted eight-sided shape that mirrors changes in the light. A spire holds a light beam that can be seen for miles. Other high towers are being built at the site too. By 2021, four of the six planned towers were complete, plus the memorial and museum. The two reflecting pools have the largest human-made waterfalls in the United States. New York's changing skyline shows a city that keeps adapting and growing.

5. Are GMOs Safe?

1Demonstrators in Tucson, Arizona, protest GMO foods and the Monsanto company. In 1994, the FDA approved a new tomato for sale in the United States. Researchers changed its seeds in a laboratory. The tomato stayed firm long after it ripened, so it could stay on the vine longer. It was labeled as genetically modified and sold well at first. Within two years, safety worries turned people against it, and it was taken off sale. Dr. Virginia Ursin worked on the first genetically modified tomatoes. For almost ten thousand years, farmers have bred crops, and today's corn came from a wild grass.

2Genetically modified cornfields look just like fields that have not been modified with a soil bacterium. Scientists can add a new gene to a seed in a laboratory. The gene may come from another plant or a different living thing. Most corn in the United States now has genes from a soil bacterium that kills insects. The new corn kills insects that eat it, and many Americans eat GMO foods every day without knowing it. About 40 percent of U.S. crops are GMOs, and about 90 percent of corn, cotton, and soybeans are GMOs. Common GMO crops include alfalfa, cotton, canola, corn, sugar beets, soy, and zucchini. From 60 to 80 percent of processed foods contain GMOs, but labels usually do not say so.

3Some people want the U.S. government to require labels on foods with GMO ingredients. They say shoppers have a right to know if their food was grown with GMO technology. GMO foods became part of the U.S. diet in the mid-1990s. Some experts say pest-fighting genes let farmers spray fewer pesticides. Many GMO crops survive herbicides, so farmers can spray a whole field and still save the crop. Supporters say this can mean fewer chemicals, higher yields, and less work. Other researchers say farmers have used more chemicals since planting GMO crops. They fear insects and weeds will become superbugs and superweeds that need stronger chemicals.

4Scientists record temperature and light on laptops to see how those things affect plant growth. Supporters say bigger GMO yields can help feed a growing world population. Researcher David Weisser says limiting GMOs only hurts the world's starving people. If everyone grew the same GMO corn and a disease hit it, a key food could be lost at once. Experts at Princeton University warn that fewer crop varieties can let diseases spread faster. Wind can carry GMO pollen to wildflowers, and their young may gain new crop traits. Some people fear crops that kill pests may also kill ladybugs, bees, and butterflies. Monarch numbers fell by up to 90 percent in twenty years, and herbicides on GMO fields cut their milkweed.

5Supporters say GMOs can grow more food and help fight disease in poor areas. Scientists are testing rice changed to make vitamin A, which may prevent blindness. Others say people should not eat GMO food until long-term health effects are known. They worry that GMO crops or farm chemicals may cause cancer or new allergies. Soy, eggs, milk, and peanuts are common food allergens. Some GMO foods contain proteins that people have never eaten before.

6More than sixty countries ban GMOs until they are proved safe, or they require labels. In the United States, GMO foods may be sold unless they are proved harmful. The U.S. government does not require special GMO labels, but most shoppers want them. In the United Kingdom, foods with GMOs must be labeled, and some U.S. non-GMO foods carry labels. In 2014, Vermont became the first state to require GMO labels, and some grocery stores promised labels too. In 2012, France's prime minister voted in Paris to keep a temporary ban on growing GMO foods. GMO companies say required labels would hurt business, scare people, and not teach the facts. They also say labels would be costly and would slow new products that might help people.

7People have eaten food from genetically engineered seeds for only two decades. Hundreds of reports have looked at the benefits and risks of GMO foods. Many people still are not sure whether GMO foods are safe. A century ago, farmers could buy seeds for more than three hundred kinds of corn. Today, most commercial corn comes from hybrid seeds of just a few varieties. Many historic foods have gone extinct, including 90 percent of old U.S. fruit and vegetable varieties. People have started seed banks around the world to save seeds from traditional crops. They hope to keep crop variety and growers' knowledge so future people have more food choices.

6. Battling for Independence

1The United States began as thirteen colonies under King George III of Great Britain. Britain taxed the colonists and enforced unpopular new laws. The colonists wanted no taxation without representation in Parliament. The Sugar Act, the Quartering Act, the Stamp Act, and the Townshend Acts were passed. The Boston Massacre, the Boston Tea Party, and the Intolerable Acts followed, and the First Continental Congress met. On April 19, 1775, war began at Lexington and Concord with the shot heard round the world. No one knows who fired first. Native peoples had lived there for thousands of years, and millions had died.

2When King George refused to repeal the taxes, the colonies had to fight. Patrick Henry cried, Give me liberty or give me death! Liberty or Death became a Patriot battle cry. On May 10, 1775, the Second Continental Congress met at Independence Hall in Philadelphia. Fifty-six delegates chose George Washington to lead volunteer troops called the Continental Army. His farmers, carpenters, and blacksmiths had few weapons and little training. He expected his reputation to be ruined, but he served without pay. The king refused their petition and called their acts open rebellion.

3King George had tried to keep Native peoples and colonists apart. Britain reserved some lands for Native peoples, and the colonists did not like that. Some Native peoples joined the Patriots, and others joined the British. When Britain lost, it gave the United States land Native peoples had lived on for centuries. They lost cultures and countless lives too. Earlier, Ottawa leader Pontiac tried to drive out the British in Pontiac's War from 1763 to 1766. On May 10, 1775, Ethan Allen and Benedict Arnold captured Fort Ticonderoga, and Crown Point fell the next day. Colonists then chose to be Loyalists or Patriots.

4After Lexington, British soldiers led by Major John Pitcairn retreated to Boston. About 16,000 Patriot troops surrounded the city and cut the British supply line. General Thomas Gage feared forts on Bunker Hill and Breed's Hill in Charlestown. On June 16, 1775, Patriot soldiers dug trenches on Breed's Hill by mistake. British ships crossed the Charles River with cannons. Officers said, Wait until you see the whites of their eyes. The British took both hills only after the third attack, when the Patriots ran out of gunpowder.

5More than 1,000 British soldiers were wounded or killed, and the Patriots lost 441 men. Most of the fighting was on Breed's Hill, but the battle is called Bunker Hill. The Patriots gained morale because they could fight professional soldiers. General Washington arrived in July 1775 and besieged the British in Boston for nearly a year. Patriots captured Montreal in November, but Benedict Arnold's December attack on Quebec failed. In winter, Patriots used captured British cannons against Boston. On March 17, 1776, British soldiers and thousands of Loyalists left, and Washington's troops entered the city.

6John Hancock signed the Declaration of Independence in large letters in 1776. His bold name encouraged the other 53 signers. Thomas Paine wrote Common Sense and said independence was the only correct choice. The booklet sold 150,000 copies and swayed many Loyalists. In June 1776, Congress chose Thomas Jefferson to write a statement on self-government. Congress approved the Declaration of Independence on July 4, 1776. A month later, General William Howe brought 30,000 soldiers into New York Harbor. Nathan Hale, a 21-year-old teacher, was caught spying and hanged.

7In October 1776, Benedict Arnold’s gunboats lost at Lake Champlain in Canada. The Patriots fought so fiercely that the British grew concerned. Conquering the rebels might not be easy. On December 24, 1776, George Washington ferried 2,400 men across the icy Delaware to Trenton, New Jersey. They crossed in sleet and snow in one night. The Patriots won in less than an hour. It was the first major victory of the war. Arnold was a brave general, but he felt Congress and the army had mistreated him. In 1779, Washington put him in charge of West Point, a fort on the lower Hudson River. Arnold planned to sell the fort to the British. When the plan was revealed, he joined the British as a traitor.

8Saratoga was a turning point because France saw that the Patriots might win. In 1778, France sent supplies, and Spain soon declared war on Britain. Even so, soldiers often lacked food, clothing, and shelter. Washington's troops suffered a cold, hungry winter at Valley Forge. In June 1778, neither side won at Monmouth, the last major battle in the northern colonies. Women could not serve as soldiers, but they made homespun, bullets, blankets, and uniforms. They cooked, nursed, and foraged in the camps. Mary Hays, called Molly Pitcher, carried water at Monmouth.

9The British captured Savannah, Georgia, in December 1778. They captured Charleston, South Carolina, in May 1780, and won at Camden in August 1780. These three battles had the highest casualty rates of the war. Many southern colonists were Loyalists. Britain was winning in the South, but the Patriots would not give up. In October, Patriots beat Loyalists at King’s Mountain in sixty-five minutes. No British soldiers fought in that battle. The Patriots also won at Cowpens. Then they pushed General Charles Cornwallis north into Virginia.

10The most important battle of the war was about to begin. French ships drove off the British rescue ships at Yorktown, Virginia. The French fleet also brought extra soldiers. French and Patriot troops besieged the British for three weeks. Cornwallis surrendered on October 19, 1781, and the Revolutionary War ended. The Patriots were now independent from Britain. The Peace of Paris in 1783 made that independence official. Colonies used to another country’s support now had to build a new nation.

11Charles Cornwallis led British troops in the South under Henry Clinton. He surrendered at Yorktown on October 19, 1781. Marquis de Lafayette was a young French commander under Washington. He helped win the French military aid that won the war. Important sites included Quebec, Ticonderoga, Concord, and Boston. They also included Long Island, Valley Forge, and Yorktown. King's Mountain, Camden, Charleston, and Savannah were important in the South. These places marked the long fight for independence.

7. Believe It or Not?

1In 1952, an amateur photographer took a picture. Many people thought it showed an alien spacecraft. Do you think the picture is real or a fake? Would you believe stories of hairy, smelly giants in the wild? Would you believe that aliens make pictures in wheat fields? A friend might say an old curse causes people to die. Something in the Atlantic Ocean may make ships and planes disappear. This book shares evidence so you can judge four unsolved mysteries.

2In the remote Pacific Northwest, people tell of a creature called Bigfoot. For hundreds of years, Native Americans described huge, shy, apelike beings. The Salish peoples of Canada called them Sasquatch, meaning wild man of the woods. Other tribes use different names, but English speakers usually say Bigfoot. A map marks sightings in Washington and Oregon in the United States. Roger Patterson took a puzzling photograph in 1967. Railway workers, forest workers, hikers, and travelers have reported it too. Most had never heard the old stories, so those tales did not shape their reports.

3A Bigfoot may be 8 feet, or 2.4 meters, tall, with hair all over. Deep footprints suggest 350 to 800 pounds, or 160 to 360 kilograms. People report high-pitched screams and a terrible smell. Experts who study unknown animals checked thousands of reports and found no proof. Bigfoot is thought to be shorter than a grizzly but taller than an average person. In 1998, Dr. John Bindernagel examined a nest he thought belonged to Bigfoot. In 1924, gold seeker Albert Ostman was carried off in his sleeping bag. He met a father, a mother, and two children, then escaped after nearly a week.

4In wild forests and glaciers, dead bodies rot or are eaten very fast. Scientists say a body or fossils should exist, but nothing has ever been found. A cast of a Bigfoot print is much bigger than a man's foot. In 1977, a northern Washington sighting was a hoax made with gorilla suits and radios. Crop circles in tall crops were reported as early as 1590. Farmers blamed the wind, but in the 1980s the designs grew complex. One circle near Stonehenge had 149 circles and formed in 20 minutes of daylight. Most form at night, and some people report spaceships, lights, humming, or radiation.

5Plants inside a circle are bent at the lowest node, not crushed. Those bent plants often grow better than plants outside the circle. Tests suggest that strong microwave beams might bend the stalks this way. Some people think the circles are messages from beings on other planets. Field watchers say complex patterns can appear in just a few minutes. People make circles to prove they can, to play tricks, or from claimed inspiration. In 1991, Doug Bower and David Chorley claimed every circle since 1978. Seven years later, Doug took back his claim and blamed unknown forces.

6Some people suggest tornadoes, but those storms wreck a wide area. Crop circle designs are controlled and intricate. A plasma vortex is an electrically charged whirlwind. It can create strange lights and humming sounds. It can hover in one place or move as if under remote control. That could make the vortex look like a UFO. Some think secret test shots of a U.S. and British microwave weapon make the circles. Do you think crop circles are real or a hoax?

7In 1922, Howard Carter and local Egyptian workers found King Tut's tomb. The tomb of the pharaoh was filled with valuable treasures. Newspapers told of a wall curse that promised swift death to those who entered. Early Egyptians believed that cobras protect the pharaohs. On the day the tomb was opened, a cobra swallowed Carter's canary. Lord Carnarvon funded the search and died in Egypt in 1923. Papers said a curse killed him and his dog in England at the same minute. They reported twenty-six deaths in ten years, and the tale inspired movies, books, and games.

8In the early 1900s, reporters sometimes invented facts to excite readers. Lord Carnarvon had been sick for twenty years, so his death may be chance. Research shows that only six people died in the first ten years. Carter lived seventeen more years, and the reported curse was not in the tomb. Bacteria from bodies may have made unprotected early robbers sick. Pharaohs were buried in pyramids in the Valley of the Kings. They were buried with riches, and danger stories may have kept people away. Do you think the curse of the mummy is real or a hoax?

9Strange things have happened for years to ships and planes in the Atlantic. The area lies generally between Florida, Bermuda, and Puerto Rico. In 1965, Vincent Gaddis first named it the Bermuda Triangle. In his book Invisible Horizons, he also called it the Triangle of Death. Planes, ships, and crews vanish, fogs appear, and compasses fail. In 1945, Flight 19's five Avenger bombers vanished on a clear training day. The rescue airship that went to look for them vanished as well. In 1881, the Ellen Austin found an empty ship, and a second crew vanished in fog.

10The USS Cyclops vanished in 1918 with 306 people, an early linked case. In 1966, Captain Donald Henry towed a barge from Puerto Rico toward Florida. Dense fog partly cut the Good News's power, but it pulled the barge free in five minutes. Some people blame a strange power or UFOs for the many losses. Others blame hurricanes, waterspouts, and tidal waves that wash ships away. Some rescued crews say they were forced to leave storm-damaged ships. Some say the losses were hundreds of miles away, or the ships turned up later. Do you think the Bermuda Triangle is real or a hoax?

11Rapa Nui is also known as Easter Island. How were the moai statues carved and moved? Why and how were the structures at Stonehenge built? Mysterious events that we cannot understand will always exist. Some people will think they are nonsense, and others will claim they are real. Some sources are more reliable than others. We must look at evidence before we accept any claim, extraordinary or otherwise. What do you think about these mysteries, and what others can you find?

8. Big Ben and Westminster Palace

1The Great Bell hangs in the Elizabeth Tower at Westminster Palace. It is 2.7 meters wide and a little over 2.2 meters tall. An average person could fit inside it many times. People often call the tower or the clock Big Ben. The name first meant the huge bell behind the clock faces. The bronze bell weighs over 13 metric tons, about two African elephants. In the 1830s, the government wanted a clock accurate to one second each day, and a chiming bell. The bell was cast in East London on April 10, 1858.

2Two common stories tell how Big Ben got its name. In 1859, Parliament met after the bell rang for the first time. Sir Benjamin Hall, a large man called Big Ben, gave a long speech. Another member said the bell should take his name, and his name is on the bell. Some people think it was named for boxer Benjamin Caunt, also called Big Ben. The minute hands are almost 4.2 meters long. In a year, one minute hand travels about 189.9 kilometers. London is the capital of the United Kingdom, and Westminster Palace is the home of Parliament.

3Westminster Palace was not always home to Parliament. Kings and queens lived there from the middle of the eleventh century to the sixteenth century. Westminster Hall was built between 1097 and 1099 under King William II. The palace was outside London then, but the city grew around it. King Richard II added the huge wooden ceiling in 1393. Under King Henry III, from 1216 to 1272, it became the center of government. King Henry VIII had six wives, divorced two, and beheaded two others. He was the last monarch to live there, and after a fire in 1512 he moved to Whitehall Palace.

4Westminster Hall was constructed between 1097 and 1099. In 1393, a free-standing roof replaced the original roof. In 1512, King Henry VIII moved out, and he was the last monarch to live at Westminster. In 1642, King Charles I was the last monarch to set foot inside the House of Commons. Fire destroyed the Houses of Parliament in 1834, but Westminster Hall was saved. In 1940, nine months of bombing destroyed many historic London buildings, yet Big Ben still chimed every fifteen minutes. The clock tower was officially renamed the Elizabeth Tower in 2012.

5Under Elizabeth and James, many Catholics were treated unfairly for their beliefs. Plotters planned to blow up Parliament on November 5, 1605, when King James would be there. They started a tunnel, then rented a cellar under Parliament and smuggled in gunpowder. Guy Fawkes was caught with thirty-six barrels, then arrested, imprisoned, and executed. The United Kingdom still marks Guy Fawkes Day, and guards search the cellars before Parliament opens. Over two hundred years later, a huge fire in 1834 almost destroyed the Houses of Parliament. Charles Barry won the design contest, but the work took thirty years and cost much more than planned. Pugin died in 1852 and Barry in 1860, and people still argue which architect deserves more credit.

6Today the United Kingdom has two houses: the Commons and the Lords. The Commons has 650 members elected by the people to debate and vote on laws. Lords are not elected; some inherit titles, some receive titles, and some are bishops or archbishops. Bills become Acts of Parliament when both houses approve them. Visitors may tour on Saturdays, or on weekdays when Parliament is in recess. Barry and Pugin's buildings stand by the Thames, but Westminster Hall inside is over nine hundred years old. The hall has held feasts, coronations, rallies, and market stalls, and it has Northern Europe's largest medieval wooden roof. In April 1685, 174 dishes were served at the coronation of James II and Queen Mary.

7Opening day comes after an election and is the only day both houses meet the king or queen. No monarch may enter the House of Commons; Charles I was last, in 1642. No one may wear a suit of armor inside Parliament. A seven-hundred-year-old belief says no one may die in the palace, so a sick person is led out. The grand entrance is used for state affairs and by the monarch at the opening after elections. Victoria Tower, named for Queen Victoria, who reigned from 1837 to 1901, is the tallest at 98.5 meters. Finished in 1860, it was long the largest square stone tower, still holds the papers, and few may enter. The Central Tower is 91.4 meters tall, has eight sides and a spire, and was made as a chimney.

8The Elizabeth Tower stands at the north end and holds Big Ben. It is 96.31 meters tall and was called the Clock Tower until 2012. It was renamed to mark Queen Elizabeth II's sixtieth year as queen of England. Digging in a nearby street made the tower lean, but it is not about to fall. Not counting gardens, it covers 32,374 square meters and has 100 staircases and 1,100 rooms. The tower is often photographed, has appeared in many movies, and millions see Big Ben each year. Citizens may climb 334 steps with no elevator and see London and Big Ben from 62 meters up. People from around the world may tour Parliament, watch debates, and have afternoon tea.

9Around the world, Big Ben and the Palace of Westminster are famous symbols of Britain. The building has housed monarchs and politicians and has survived fires and intense bombing. It still stands proudly as a national monument. Residents and visitors know the chiming of Big Ben's bell. Some TV channels broadcast the bell live at midnight every New Year's Eve. On November 11, Big Ben rings at 11:00 a.m. for a two-minute silence to remember people lost in wars. Every quarter of an hour, Big Ben rings and reminds people it is there. Since 2000, millions gather along the Thames each New Year's Eve for fireworks.

9. Black Cavalrymen Known as Buffalo Soldiers

1A memorial stands in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania. It honors Black soldiers. Every war of the United States is part of what it honors. This book tells how a new army formed. It tells why men joined. It tells about duty out west. It tells how a reputation grew. It tells about later service. It tells how discrimination went on. It tells what the buffalo soldiers left behind. Word meanings come last. The Revolutionary War effort of the United States included help from more than 5,000 Black soldiers. They helped the country gain independence from Great Britain. Nearly a hundred years later, another large group served. The Northern Union Army had help from more than 170,000 Black soldiers. They helped win the Civil War in 1865. Only after that war could Black men join the United States Army in peacetime. The Army Reorganization Act became law in 1866. Black soldiers received their first peacetime regiments from that act. Many Black men who joined had served in the Union Army. Others had formerly been enslaved. The first all-Black units were infantry regiments. They were the 38th through the 41st. The 9th Cavalry Regiment was also among the first. So was the 10th Cavalry Regiment. Infantry soldiers fought on foot. Cavalry soldiers rode horses. Native peoples of the Great Plains gave a name to the men of the 10th Cavalry. They called them buffalo soldiers. Soon the name was used for all the men who served in these regiments. A picture shows the 10th Cavalry in 1898.

2Sharecropping was the work of many Black people once the Civil War ended. A sharecropper is a farmer who rents land from the owner. The farmer also rents supplies. In exchange, the owner receives part of the crop. New laws came from white lawmakers in the South once slavery ended in the United States. The laws greatly restricted the freedom of Black people. People called these racist laws the Black codes. Employment was required of Black people. They also limited the kinds of jobs Black people could hold. The jobs were usually farming. They were usually service positions. Black people had to sign work contracts every year. The pay was very low. If they did not sign, they would be arrested. For a time they would have to labor without pay. The labor might be in prison. It might be for a white employer. Union Army fighters from the Civil War appear in a picture. Equal salary for every soldier began in the army in 1864. Race did not change the pay. White workers earned much more than Black workers at most jobs. Fair pay was rare for a Black man outside the army. Army pay beat what most of them could earn in other work. They also received food. They received clothes. They received medical care. They received shelter. Many of these men had been enslaved. They had been kept from a formal education. The army gave them a chance to learn to read. It gave them a chance to learn to write. Black soldiers still faced severe racial discrimination. Service by Black soldiers was opposed by most white soldiers. Most white officers thought the same. At first, Black soldiers could not rise to the ranks of commanding officers. White officers always led their regiments. Command of Black regiments was refused by many white officers.

3White civilians showed racism too. They did not want armed Black soldiers near their communities. That is why most Black soldiers were posted far off. They went to the Great Plains. They went to deserts on the western frontier. Women were not allowed to enlist in the United States Army until 1943. Even so, every American war has included important work by Black women. During the Civil War, spies collected information for the Union Army. Mary Jane Richards was one. Harriet Tubman was another. They passed that information along. Guide was another role Tubman took on. She acted as a scout. She went with Union soldiers on raids. The raids were on plantations in the South. She led hundreds of enslaved people to freedom. World War II espionage against the Nazis included actress Josephine Baker. She passed information in secret messages. The messages were written in invisible ink. War efforts have received the work of thousands of Black women. They have served as spies. They have served as scouts. They have served as guides. They have served as nurses. They have served as doctors. They have served as soldiers. They did this while facing discrimination based on gender. They also faced racial discrimination. A picture shows Maj. Charity A. Earley inspecting her troops. During World War II she was the highest ranking Black female officer. In the 1800s the United States government wanted to expand the country's borders. The expansion would go west. The government signed many treaties with Native leaders. A treaty is an agreement. The treaties set borders between United States territory and Native territories. The government broke these treaties again and again. The breaks let white pioneers settle on land that belonged to Native peoples. Decades of war followed. The war was between the United States Army and Native peoples. Those peoples were defending lands they had lived on for thousands of years. The Native peoples included the Apache. They included the Comanche. They included the Cheyenne. They included the Kiowa. Others were involved too. As years passed, the army pushed growing numbers of Native peoples from the lands of their ancestors. The people were pushed onto reservations. The land set aside as reservations was small. They were usually the poorest farming lands available. They were usually the poorest hunting lands too. A picture shows members of the Lakota Nation. They camp near the Pine Ridge Reservation. The place is South Dakota. The year is 1891.

4A picture from the 19th century shows a battle. Buffalo soldiers fight Native peoples of the Plains. The year of that battle is 1868. Government officials encouraged a mass killing of buffalo. Settlers took part. Soldiers took part. Officials knew Native peoples depended on the buffalo. Buffalo were crucial to the diet on the Plains. They were crucial to the way of life there. Hunger to the point of starvation was usual on the reservations. Disease spread quickly. The communities were crowded. Many Native peoples resisted the move onto reservations. They fought back against the army. Black soldiers were sent west to fight them. Protection of white settlers was another job given to Black soldiers. The West was vast. It was hard to enforce laws as more white settlers arrived. Outlaws rode freely. They stole cattle. They stole horses. They robbed banks. They robbed stagecoaches. Stagecoaches brought important supplies. They brought mail. Black soldiers were sent to protect settlers from these thieves. They were sent to protect stagecoaches. They were sent to protect railroad crews. Black soldiers were given the hardest duties. They were given the most dangerous duties. Some of the country's roughest terrain lay on their route. They searched for water. They built roads. They mapped lands that white settlers had not explored. Summer brought extreme heat that they had to bear. They endured freezing temperatures in the winter. They received old horses. They received supplies that were not adequate. They received damaged equipment. White settlers they were there to protect also showed them hatred. They met violence. Sometimes that violence was deadly. Despite all of this, the troops did their duties well. In time they earned a reputation. People saw them as brave. People saw them as hardworking. People saw them as talented soldiers. Native peoples they fought on the Plains gave them the name buffalo soldiers. Those peoples may have been the Cheyenne. A link to buffalo, many people think, is why Native peoples chose the name. Buffalo fur coats on the soldiers are one suggested source of the name. Others believe the soldiers got the name because they were brave in battle. They were fierce, like the buffalo. Evidence for this belief has not been found. Many Native leaders today challenge the idea.

5Cathay Williams is known as the one woman who served as a buffalo soldier. The 38th Infantry Regiment took her in during 1866. Women were not allowed to serve as soldiers then. A man's disguise hid who she was. She used the name William Cathay. She served for nearly two years. A doctor discovered she was a woman during a hospital visit. She was discharged in 1868. An artist made a picture of her. Buffalo soldiers served in the West through the decades of that war. The war was between the United States Army and Native peoples. Eighteen buffalo soldiers received the Medal of Honor between 1870 and 1890. No United States soldier's award ranks higher than the Medal of Honor. As the wars in the West ended, many Black soldiers were sent to a new fight. They fought in the Spanish-American War. The conflict in 1898 lasted ten weeks. Five more Medals of Honor went to buffalo soldiers. After that war ended, hundreds of buffalo soldiers took a different job. They worked as park rangers in California. They worked at Yosemite National Park. They worked at Sequoia National Park. Between 1899 and 1904 they oversaw the building of roads. They oversaw the building of trails. They fought forest fires. They stopped people from hunting animals. They stopped people from stealing timber. A picture shows buffalo soldiers at Yosemite National Park. In the 1890s the army asked a question about bicycles. Could bikes replace horses? Bikes were cheaper. They were easier to care for. The buffalo soldiers rode 1,900 miles on that test. The distance is 3,057 kilometers. They rode from Missoula, Montana. They rode to St. Louis, Missouri. They crossed mountains. They crossed rivers. They crossed shifting sandhills. They still averaged 50 miles a day. That average is 80.4 kilometers. Before long, motorcycles replaced the bikes. A picture shows buffalo soldiers with their bikes. The place is Yellowstone National Park. The year is 1896.

6The buffalo soldiers had a strong reputation. They had many achievements. Black soldiers still experienced discrimination. White citizens of Brownsville, Texas, brought an accusation in August 1906. They accused buffalo soldiers stationed nearby. They said the soldiers had killed a white man. They said the soldiers had hurt another person. Proof that the soldiers did this was missing. Bed was where the officers said the men were when the shots were fired. President Theodore Roosevelt still gave an order. The order removed 167 Black soldiers from the army. Leaving the army caused them to lose their pensions. A pension is a payment a person receives after army service ends. A new investigation came in 1972. Only then were the soldiers found innocent of the crime. World War I lasted from 1914 to 1918. President Woodrow Wilson followed racist policies. Those policies kept Black soldiers from a role in Europe. They could not join white United States soldiers in battle there. Command over them went to the French army instead. A picture shows Black soldiers arriving home after service in World War I. World War II included the United States starting in 1941. Those regiments, called buffalo soldiers, had already been broken up. They had been reorganized. Black soldiers and white soldiers stayed separated until the 1950s. When Black troops finally joined white regiments, racism did not stop. They endured it from fellow officers. They endured it from fellow soldiers. Three men stand out among the buffalo soldiers. No Black student had graduated from the United States Military Academy before Lt. Henry O. Flipper. The academy is at West Point in New York. Command of Black soldiers by a Black officer began with him. In 1887, command of the 9th Cavalry went to Lt. John Hanks Alexander. No other Black officer then held a commission to lead troops in combat. He was commissioned to lead troops in combat. Col. Charles Young was a commanding officer of the 9th Cavalry. The first Black national park superintendent was his role starting in 1903.

7Since the Revolutionary War, every major American conflict has included Black soldiers. The buffalo soldiers of the original 9th Cavalry are perhaps the best known. The original 10th Cavalry is just as well known. People have written books about them. People have made movies. People have made television shows. People have even written songs. People have built museums in their honor. People have built monuments. The monuments stand in Kansas. They stand in Alabama. They stand in Texas. Buffalo soldiers faced severe racial discrimination. Sometimes it was fatal. The discrimination came from inside the army. It came from outside the army too. Even so, leaving their units was rarer for them than for other soldiers. They showed incredible courage. They faced fierce combat. They faced deadly violence. They faced harsh living conditions. United States history includes their service as an important part. A monument honors their memory. It is the Buffalo Soldier Monument. It stands in Fort Leavenworth, Kansas. These words have special meanings in this book. Ancestral points to family or tribal generations that lived earlier. Contracts are formal or legal written agreements. They are between two or more people. A disease is an illness. Evidence is something that supports a theory or a claim. Fierce means very powerful, forceful, or violent. Regiments are military units. Each one holds at least two large groups of soldiers. Reputation is the general way that other people think of a person or a thing. Restricted means limited or controlled. A salary is set pay for work, usually figured by the year. It is usually calculated per year. Severe means extremely harsh or unpleasant. Starvation is extreme hunger. It can mean death because of a lack of food. Terrain is the natural form of a piece of land.

10. Brazil across a Wide Continent

1Brazil sits in the eastern part of South America. Nearly half the continent is taken up by this one country. The whole United States is only a little larger. Brazil meets the Atlantic Ocean on its eastern side. The Atlantic shore is very long. Measured along the Atlantic, the shore passes 7,400 kilometers. That length is 4,600 miles. Crossing the entire United States is a shorter trip. Brazil's shore is almost twice that distance. A map around Brazil marks the countries of Guyana, Venezuela, Suriname, and French Guiana. It marks Colombia, Peru, Bolivia, Paraguay, Argentina, and Uruguay. The cities of São Luis, Brasília, Rio de Janeiro, and São Paulo are marked too. The Atlantic Ocean and the Pacific Ocean are marked. North America and South America are marked as well.

2Many smaller countries share Brazil’s western border. Venezuela is one of them. Colombia is one. Peru is one. Bolivia is one. Paraguay is one. Argentina is one. Uruguay is one. Every other country in South America touches Brazil except two. Ecuador is one exception. Chile is the other. For many years the capital was Rio de Janeiro, on the eastern coast. Say Rio as REE-oh DAY, then zhuh-NAIR-oh. A new capital was chosen in the 1950s. Builders placed Brasília near the country’s center. Say Brasília as bruh-ZIL-yuh. Clearing a huge wild area was part of the giant job. Roads had to connect Brasília to other parts of Brazil. An artificial lake was in the plan, and it had to be dug. The official capital changed to Brasília in 1960. Planners expected only a few hundred thousand residents. Residents passed two million early in the present century. Problems nobody had fully planned for followed that growth. The artificial lake grew dirty. A plant was required to clean the water. The web of highways now puts wild places at risk. The Amazon rainforest is one of those places. Brazil, like many countries, mixes many ethnic groups. European descent accounts for about half the people. Portuguese ancestors are the largest European share. Say Portuguese as POR-chuh-geez. Spanish ancestors are part of the mix. Italian ancestors are part of it. German ancestors are part of it. Polish ancestors are part of it. Portugal held Brazil as a colony for three hundred years. Portuguese is the country’s main language. Other Brazilians descend from people of Angola and of the Congo. They were enslaved and shipped in as laborers. Big farms used that labor. Mines used it too. Still others descend from Native peoples who lived in South America first. In every group of ten Brazilians, about four have mixed ancestry. Industry is concentrated in the big city of São Paulo. Say it SOW, then POW-loh. Brazil’s residents number more than two hundred million. Eight out of ten of them live in cities. No city in South America is larger than São Paulo. City growth over the past sixty years has been rapid. Problems have come with that speed. People with little money have had to settle on vacant ground beside the cities. Homes there are built from whatever materials can be found.

3Service work employs some people, including banking. Construction is another service field. Education is another. Health care is another. Coastal hotels and restaurants employ people as well. Away from cities, mining provides jobs. Oil work provides jobs. Farmers raise sugarcane. They also raise a large share of the world’s coffee. Agriculture matters greatly to the country. Oranges are produced. Soybeans are produced. Pork, cattle, and chicken are produced. Lumber is produced. Ranching in the far south has a long history. Ranch cowboys known as gauchos were at work by the 1500s. Say gauchos as GOW-chohz. Cattle were in their care. Sheep were in their care. Horses were in their care. Clothing, songs, and dances of their own grew up over the centuries. Ranches in southern Brazil still employ modern gauchos. Landform maps show the Guiana Highlands and the Amazon River. They show the Amazon Basin and the Brazilian Highlands. They show Brasília and the Pantanal Wetlands. They show the coastal lowlands, the Pacific Ocean, and the Atlantic Ocean. The Amazon River and the Amazon rainforest, both inside the Amazon basin, are what Brazil is famous for. This rainforest is the world’s largest tropical rainforest. Its nickname is lungs of the planet. Oxygen used around the world gets about 20 percent of its supply from those plants. Highlands appear in two regions. Mountain ranges fill the far north. Steep cliffs are there. Waterfalls are there. Deep valleys are there. Southeast Brazil holds the larger highlands, with both hills and low mountains.

4The capybara, native to the Pantanal, is the world’s largest rodent. The Pantanal, in central Brazil, is one of the largest freshwater ecosystems on Earth. Its reach can pass 210,000 square kilometers. That area is 81,000 square miles. Rivers flood the land in the wet season, from October through March. Waters then rise several meters. Later the waters recede. An amazing variety of life is supported. Downstream places are protected from floodwater during the wet season. The dry season brings a steady release of water downstream. Pollutants are removed, so the region works like a giant water treatment plant. Transportation uses its waterways. Earth’s water cycle depends on the region in an important way. Sandy beaches along the eastern coastal lowlands draw many tourists. The Guarani were among the first known peoples. The Yanomami were among them too. Rainforests are still home to some of these groups. The Amazon’s shores are still home to some of them. Portugal conquered the area in the early sixteenth century. At first the land was used to collect a wood called pau brasil, or brazilwood. Say it POW, then brah-SEEL. Native peoples were enslaved by the Portuguese. Huge fields of sugarcane were the forced workplace. Later, millions of African people were enslaved and brought over. Coffee plantations used their labor. Diamond mines used their labor. Gold mines used their labor. Added together, the Caribbean and North America received fewer enslaved Africans than Brazil did. Independence came in 1822, under Emperor Pedro I. Slavery ended in 1888. In 1889 the military forced Pedro II to step down. Dictators led the country for the next hundred years. Turmoil and uprisings filled those years. Democracy arrived in 1985. Serious challenges remain. Poverty presses on the people. Unemployment presses on them. Deforestation threatens the natural environment.

5Carnaval fills Rio’s Sambadrome with singers and with dancers. They ride elaborate floats. The dance they perform is the samba. Religion shapes many holidays. The Catholic faith is the religion of most people in Brazil. The Portuguese brought that religion. Carnaval lasts four days and leads up to Lent. It is the largest holiday. It is also the most popular. Streets fill with crowds for parades and for dancing. Elaborate costumes are worn, with masks and with headdresses. Preparation lasts all year. Rio de Janeiro has samba schools. Dancers there practice all year. Enslaved African people brought the samba many years ago. The planet’s widest mix of living things is here. Thousands of insect kinds live in the Amazon rainforest by itself. Thousands of kinds of fish live there. Thousands of kinds of birds live there. Reptiles live there in huge variety. Amphibians do too. Mammals do too. Some are beautiful. Some are dangerous. A toucan, said TOO-kan, is easy to know by its bill. The bill is brightly colored and almost as long as the body. Black shows in the feathers. White shows in them. Green shows in them. Yellow shows in them. Red shows in them. Toucans are heard constantly. Sightings are rare. The birds keep to the treetops. The rainforest’s bright frogs are beautiful. Few creatures anywhere carry a stronger poison. Touch alone can kill, for some species. Many of these frogs get their skin poison from the harmful foods they eat. Red-bellied piranhas, said pi-RAH-nuhz, hunt in the Amazon River. A pack may hold one hundred of these fish. Some packs are even larger. Teeth on these meat-eaters are very sharp. Their jaws are strong. Animal flesh is their food. Humans are rarely attacked. Dead animals are the usual meal.

6Hyacinth macaws and the giant anteater live in these lands. A snakelike fish in the river can shock. It is the electric eel. Length can reach almost 2 meters, or 6 feet. Weight can reach almost 22 kilograms, or 48 pounds. Prey is stunned by a strong electric shock. Enemies are stunned by it too. The Pantanal holds fascinating animals as well. Storks build nests in its swamps. Herons build nests there. Parrots of many colors pass in the sky. On the ground, giant anteaters wander. Jaguars roam it. Deer roam it. Habitat loss could erase some animals of the Amazon rainforest and of the Pantanal from Earth forever. A destroyed share of the Amazon rainforest already stands above one fifth. The loss cannot be undone. Saving what remains is shared work for many groups and for Brazil’s government. Futebol, also called soccer, is a sport many Brazilians love to watch and to play. Say futebol as FOOT-bohl. The World Cup is the world’s largest soccer tournament. Brazil has won it more often than any other country. The most famous player from this country is Pelé, and the world knows his name. He led Brazil to World Cup victory three times. The years were 1958, 1962, and 1970. Land, animals, and people of many kinds give Brazil its color. Life of many kinds fills the Amazon River. It fills the Amazon rainforest. It fills the Pantanal. People here are diverse. The culture is vibrant. Celebrations are amazing. No other place feels quite like it.

7Population is 211,049,527. Land area is 8,458,417 square kilometers. The same area is 3,265,813 square miles. Brasília is the capital. Portuguese is the primary language. Catholic is the primary religion. The currency is the real, said ray-AHL. Figures come from the Central Intelligence Agency in 2019 and from the World Bank. A basin is a low place, a depression, in Earth’s surface. Highlands are lands full of mountains, set high above the sea. The word Catholic points to the Roman Catholic Church. That church is one branch of Christianity. A plantation is a large farm with many workers. Samba is a rhythmic Brazilian dance that began in Africa, and it is also the music for that dance. Deforestation means clearing trees and other plants out of an area. Sugarcane is a tropical grass, and sugar comes from it. A dictator holds complete power. Force is the usual way that power is kept.
