Toxic means poisonous and dangerous to life. An ecosystem is living things together with the places they inhabit. The water cycle is water’s path, and the changes it passes through, as it moves in the environment. Gauchos are cowboys of the grassy plains of South America.

11. China from the Himalayas to the Sea

1Skyscrapers, tall apartments, and crowds fill Beijing, the capital of China. The country’s official name is the People’s Republic of China. It is one of the largest countries on Earth. A significant share of Asia lies inside its borders. Square kilometers of land pass 9.5 million. That area is 3.4 million square miles. The whole continent of Europe is only a little larger. Many countries line the borders. Russia is one. India is one. North Korea is one. Vietnam is one. Coastline totals about 14,500 kilometers, or 9,000 miles. The East China Sea borders the eastern edge of the mainland. The South China Sea borders the southeastern edge. A regional map also shows Mongolia, Kazakhstan, and Myanmar, also called Burma. It shows the Yellow Sea, Shanghai, and Hong Kong.

2Hong Kong, Beijing, and Shanghai are among China’s large cities. Beijing has stood for more than three thousand years. That age makes it one of the oldest cities anywhere. Ancient and modern sit side by side. Skyscrapers give the city a modern face. Subways do too. Fast-food restaurants do too. Temples, monuments, and courtyards are the ancient neighbors. The Forbidden City is a massive palace from the fifteenth century. Emperors made it their home across hundreds of years. A strict rule gave the palace its name. Every room could be entered only by the emperor. Visitors now walk the palace as they would a museum. A bridge near Beijing carries the name of Marco Polo. He was an Italian explorer. His book about the journey to China dates from the thirteenth century. The East China Sea is the water beside Shanghai. It is China’s largest city. It is the country’s largest industrial center. It is one of the largest ports on Earth. Great numbers of workers move there each year. The population is more than twenty million. In its busiest section, each square kilometer may hold 50,000 people. That density is 125,000 people in each square mile. New York City’s busiest district is less packed. Set against New York’s busiest district, the ratio is greater than one and a half. China’s population is more than 1.3 billion, the largest of any country. The United States, by comparison, holds about 316 million. That is about one fourth of China’s population. Towns and villages are home to more than half of China’s people. Rural people often grow crops. They often raise livestock. Town and village people may manufacture goods. They may do other jobs as well. Large cities hold many people too. Those cities are centers of trade and of business. Mandarin is what most people speak. The name means common language. Regions may use dialects of Mandarin. Many other languages are spoken, including Cantonese. English is spoken as well. Cell phone users in China outnumber those of every other country. A cell phone is in the hands of about 75 percent of residents.

3Mandarin characters can stand for English ideas such as fire. A character can stand for mountain. A character can stand for forest. A character can stand for woman. Some characters look like the thing they mean. English uses an alphabet for separate sounds inside words. Chinese writing uses characters for whole words, or for parts of words. Experts place the start of this writing around 2000 bc. That age makes it one of the oldest writing systems. Draw the peaks of a mountain, and you have nearly drawn that character. An educated person knows about four thousand characters on average. The full language holds tens of thousands. School is required for nine years. After that, students may go on to secondary school. Job training may follow. University may follow. Admission to a university is very difficult. Each year, admission goes to just a few students. Landform maps show the Zunghaer Basin and Beijing. They show the Gobi Desert and the Turfan Depression. They show the Tarim Basin and Shanghai. They show the East China Sea and the Tibetan Plateau. They show the Himalayan Mountains and the Sichuan Basin. They show Hong Kong and Mount Everest. Many kinds of landforms fit inside a country this vast. Southern mountain ranges are among the most outstanding features. About one third of the land is mountainous. The Himalayas hold many of the world’s highest peaks. The tallest is Mount Everest. Its height is 8,850 meters, or 29,035 feet. The peak stands on the border China shares with Nepal. Western areas also have mountains. Plateaus rise there too. The east is lowland, with forests and with wetlands. China’s large cities sit in that eastern lowland.

4A huge desert crosses about 1,600 kilometers of the north, a distance of 1,000 miles. The Gobi Desert meets Mongolia. Waterless place is what the name Gobi means. Like every desert, the Gobi gets little rain. Yearly rain in the west can be as low as 5 centimeters, or 2 inches. In the northeast it can reach 20 centimeters, or 8 inches. People still manage to live in this harsh place. They are nomadic herders. Underground water lets them raise cattle. They raise sheep. They raise goats. They raise camels. Wild camels live in the Gobi too. Gazelles live there. China’s rare wild horses live there. Thousands of rivers cross China. The longest is the Yangtze, said yank-SEE. It runs 6,300 kilometers, or 3,915 miles. People and goods both travel on it. People call that basin the granary of the nation. About half of the country’s farming sits in that basin. Rice farming there is the country’s largest amount. Barley is grown in the basin. Wheat is grown there. Corn is grown there. Birth weight for a giant panda is typically only about four ounces. The mother is far larger. Fame follows the giant panda more than almost any other animal in China. It belongs to the bear family. It is very rare. Wild pandas, scientists think, number only about 1,600. The drop came after habitat disappeared. Those lost forests were in central China’s mountains. Forests are the panda’s home. Bamboo is its only food. Many bamboo forests were cleared. Agriculture took the land. Timber took it. Fuel took it. Special nature reserves were created to save the giant panda. Breeding pandas away from the wild has been attempted by scientists. Some of those scientists work in China. Some work in other countries. Births are uncommon once pandas leave the habitat they come from. Only in recent years have babies been born in captivity.

5Only two alligator species exist in the world. China’s alligator is included in that pair. It is smaller than its American cousin. Native habitat follows the Yangtze River. Habitat loss has cut its numbers sharply, as with the giant panda. Wild numbers are down to a few hundred. Breeding in captivity has gone very well. Scientists are working with farmers beside the Yangtze. Safety in the home habitat is the aim of that work. Seeing a golden snub-nosed monkey is a surprise. Golden fur is not the oddest part. The blue face is. Forests of central China are its home. Habitat loss hurts this primate too. Reserves have been set aside so some habitat remains. The monkeys are also bred in captivity. Groups are large. Four hundred or more may live in one group. China is among the world’s oldest civilizations. Evidence from science places some of the earliest humans here. The dates run from about 770,000 years ago to about 230,000 years ago. Emperors ruled for thousands of years. A single family might hold power over parts of the land for hundreds of years. Those stretches of rule are called dynasties. Written history begins with a dynasty called the Shang. The start sits around 1600 bc. Further dynasties came later, including the Zhou, said JHOW. They include the Qin, said CHIN. They include the Han, said HAHN. They include the Jin. They include the Tang, said TAHNG. They include the Song, said SUNG. They include the Ming. The Qin Dynasty added several important pieces to China’s history. Shi Huangdi, said SHI HWAHNG-dee, was the Qin emperor. His years of rule were 259 to 210 bc. A wall against invaders was built on his order. Building continued under later emperors. Ming rulers did much of that later work. The work went on for nearly 2,000 years. People now call it the Great Wall. It is really a series of walls. The walls run through northern China and through Southern Mongolia. One section runs about 8,850 kilometers, or 5,500 miles. Across, the section measures 5 to 8 meters. That width is 15 to 25 feet.

6Great advances and many inventions came from ancient China. The abacus was an early tool for complex mathematical calculations. The magnetic compass was invented there and helped early sailors find their way. Gunpowder was invented to blast rocks apart. It was also used to make fireworks. Fabric made from silk was first made by the ancient Chinese. No export was more popular than silk. Buyers came from distant lands. Their route was called the Silk Road. Silkworms spin cocoons, and silk fibers are taken from those cocoons. Pulling the fibers free was a skill the ancient Chinese learned. Spinning turned the fibers into thread that was strong. Weaving turned that thread into beautiful cloth. Massive change came in 1912. War with Japan, plus strife inside the country, ended the Qing Dynasty. China became a republic. Politics stayed stormy, and conflict continued, for years after that. Government power passed in 1949 to the Chinese Communist Party, known as the CCP. Food baskets hang from a farmer’s shoulders in Yangshuo. Crowds visit a shopping district in Shanghai. Ancient and modern meet in China. Rural life and urban life meet there too. The world still feels the effects of this long past. Diverse peoples show that history in cultures that are unique and interesting. The land stretches across much of Asia. Breathtaking mountains are part of the landscape. Huge deserts are part of it. Ancient rivers are part of it. Amazing structures draw visitors from all over the world. A visitor has many choices for a first stop.

7Population is 1,355,692,576. Land area is 9,569,901 square kilometers. The same area is 3,694,959 square miles. The capital is Beijing. The primary language is Mandarin. Daoism is a primary religion. Buddhism is a primary religion. The currency is the yuan, said yoo-WAHN. Figures come from the Central Intelligence Agency in July 2014 and from the World Bank. Agriculture means the science or the practice of farming and of raising livestock. Long leaves and woody stems that stretch out mark the bamboo plant. A dialect is a form of a language used in a certain region or by a certain group. A dynasty is a line of rulers from the same family. An export is a product sent or sold out of a country or a region. Grain is kept in a building called a granary. Industrial describes the making of goods in factories, the goods themselves, or the people who make them. Nomadic means moving from place to place with no lasting home. A plateau is a large, raised stretch of flat land. Rain on a river basin runs off into that river. It may also run into streams that feed the river. Silk is strong thread made by a spider or by a silkworm. A temple is a building used as a place of worship.

12. Cities of the Two Rivers

1A clay tablet kept a record of food shares and goods. A sharp tool cut the pictures into the clay. A map of this old land shows the Black Sea and the Caspian Sea. The Fertile Crescent is marked on that map. Akkad is marked there too. The Tigris River runs through the mapped land. The Euphrates River runs through it as well. The map names the cities Mari, Babylon, Umma, Ur, Lagash, and Uruk. The Mediterranean Sea is shown to the west. Europe and Africa lie past that sea. This book begins with the first people who farmed the river land. It then tells how Uruk became the first known city. Later pages tell how city-states went to war. They also tell of a richer time of new tools and ideas. The story closes when the empire falls. A word list comes at the end. About 6,000 years ago, the earliest civilizations started to form. Cities are home to at least some people in a civilization. Those people do specialized jobs. A civilization has a government. Writing often belongs to a civilization. Arts often belong to one too. Science often belongs to one too. Trade often belongs to one too. Monuments often belong to one too. Ancient Mesopotamia is one of the earliest civilizations. The land takes in what is now Turkey. It also takes in present-day Syria, Iraq, and Kuwait. Many historians see this land as the place where civilization was born. The name Mesopotamia means land set between a pair of rivers. The Tigris is one of those rivers. The Euphrates is the other.

2The heart of this old civilization lay in the Fertile Crescent. That heart sat between the Tigris River and the Euphrates River. The first people to live in Mesopotamia settled the far southern part of the crescent. That southern part is now Iraq and Kuwait. Modern archaeologists worked at a site called Tell al 'Ubaid. Say that name TEL EL oh-BAYD. The site lies near the Euphrates River. They uncovered ancient stone tools. They uncovered bricks. They uncovered pottery. Those artifacts were the only clues to how these people lived. The items show farming settlements as early as 5000 bce. Those settlers are called the Ubaid. Their name was taken from that find-spot. A small mosaic box may have held a musical instrument. The box shows scenes of life in early Mesopotamia. Sumer was first farmed and developed by the Ubaid. Earlier, Ubaid groups and other Sumerian tribes hunted and gathered while moving camp. They moved from place to place. Weather decided where they went. The supply of food and water decided it too. In time, the Ubaid found ways to stay in one place. They drained marshes. They grew crops in the rich soil. They built irrigation canals. The canals carried water to drier ground. They made pottery from river clay. They wove baskets from reeds. Those reeds grew in shallow water. Copper lion heads were common guardian figures. People set them on Mesopotamian buildings for protection. The river supplied what families needed. It also supplied goods to trade with travelers. Major trade routes ran from Asia and from Europe. Those routes crossed the rich plains of Sumeria. If the Ubaid could neither grow an item nor make it, traders supplied it. Cedar from Lebanon was one item they wanted badly. Metals from other regions were wanted badly too.

3Over the years, more Sumerian settlements rose in the Fertile Crescent. A number of those settlements grew into true cities. Some of the cities became city-states. The most influential city was Uruk. Uruk is modern-day Warka, in Iraq. Between 4100 and 3000 bce, authority in the area centered on Uruk. It was also the center of trade. Uruk's population once reached as many as eighty thousand. Uruk spread across over 2.6 square kilometers, or 1 square mile. Huge buildings of mud bricks stood in the city. A multitiered ziggurat, or temple, stood there too. People used the ziggurat for worship. They also sacrificed animals and crops there. Painted clay mosaics decorated the ziggurat and other important buildings. Relief carvings decorated them as well. A drawing shows a rebuilt ziggurat from ancient Mesopotamia. The stone base of Uruk's ziggurat still stands at the original site. Mesopotamian pictographs showed up first in Uruk. This early writing grew because Uruk was a center of commerce. Workers marked plain pictures onto tablets of clay. The marks tallied workers' food shares. They also recorded goods. During the following thousand years, those pictures grew into the writing called cuneiform. This was a first in human history. Symbols now stood for sounds. They did not stand only for objects. Any language could be written with those symbols. An account of monthly grain shares was written in cuneiform. That account sits on a clay tablet from around 2350 bce.

4This phonologic system had advantages over a pictographic system. Writers could shuffle one symbol set and spell many different words. Because of that, fewer symbols were needed. The system also made abstract ideas easier to express. Those ideas would be hard to draw as pictures. Clay tablets carry wedge-shaped cuneiform symbols. The tablets tell about Sumerian life in that period. People in Uruk and in other Sumerian city-states lived in structured societies. At the top was a priest-king. He led other ranked officials. One official was a leader of the plow. Another was a leader of the law. There were still more officials of this kind. Mesopotamian society had three main classes. One class was free. Another class was semi-free. A third class was enslaved. Daily life for Sumer's lower classes is mostly unknown. Wives and mothers made up most of the women in Sumer. Home and family care filled their days. School was closed to most girls. Girls from a wealthy family were the exception. Women in Sumer held more rights than women did in later cultures of Mesopotamia. They could own property. They could run businesses. They could act as judges. They could act as witnesses in court. They could become priestesses, scribes, and physicians. One of the most famous rulers was King Gilgamesh. In Sumerian his name is Bilgames. His rule over Uruk is placed near 2700 bce. The Early Dynastic Period ran from 2900 to 2350 bce. Song and legend turned him into an epic hero. His reign of 126 years was the subject of poems. Stories called him a demigod. His father was said to be King Lugalbanda. His mother was said to be the Sumerian mother-goddess Ninsumun. Credit for Uruk's huge protective wall goes to Gilgamesh. That wall still stands today. Ancient poems from Mesopotamia make up the Epic of Gilgamesh. The poems tell an ancient king's battles, struggles, and achievements. He works to gain immortality. Many people also believe this epic is history's oldest literary work that still survives. Stone tablets with single poems date back to about 2100 bce. A total of twelve tablets tell the tales. Gilgamesh builds the great wall of Uruk. He battles the wild man Enkidu and later befriends him. He fights the monstrous demon Humbaba. In the tale, lion skins cover Gilgamesh, and he is the one who slew those lions.

5Around 600 bce, a carving shows Gilgamesh fighting. His opponent in the carving is the wild man Enkidu. Modern archaeologists have long worked to sort fact from fiction about Gilgamesh. A reign longer than a century is not likely. A German team of archaeologists has found support for details in the ancient poem. The poem is The Epic of Gilgamesh. In 2003, the team said they had found the tomb of Gilgamesh. The tomb lay beneath an ancient bed of the Euphrates. Certain buildings named in the epic poem were among the finds. The discoveries show that Gilgamesh was a real person. Growing Sumerian city-states started to rival one another. Every city-state sought power in the region. Battles grew steadily more common. One battle is the first Sumerian war described in detail. Lagash fought Umma in the year 2525 bce. Pictures and writing were cut into a tall stone pillar. That pillar is a stele (STEE-lee). The pictures show infantrymen in copper helmets. The soldiers bring swords. They also bring spears and shields. Wheeled carts carried the military leaders. Pairs of onagers pulled the carts. An onager is a native species of donkey. Historians rank this stele fragment among the oldest documents of past events. War scenes on it involve Lagash and Umma.

6Trade routes were vital. They brought the materials needed to make the tools of war. Blacksmiths had learned something important about two soft metals. Smelting could join copper with tin. Together they made a fairly hard substance. This alloy is called bronze. Bronze was well suited for swords and spear tips. Tin, however, was rare. Traders from central Asia were one source. Traders from Europe were the other source. Feuding city-states often tried to break trade routes. They knew a shortage of smelting materials would hurt the other army. In one scene, a priest of Sumer points at a stele. Umma and Lagash used that stele to mark their border. The two city-states argued constantly over who owned the land. The Sumerians' constant fighting ended under a new king. About 2340 bce, Akkad's King Sargon seized the region. Akkad lay in southern Mesopotamia. That victory fused the city-states. The new realm was the Akkadian Empire. Sargon kept order by putting trusted allies in powerful posts across the kingdom. Sargon's descendants kept up this practice. A map of Sargon's Akkadian Empire shows the Caspian Sea. It also shows the Mediterranean Sea, Syria, Akkad, and Egypt. By 2150 bce, the Akkadian Empire was finished. Westward, the realm met the Mediterranean Sea. It reached Arabia in the south. It reached Iran in the east. Northward, it met the Anatolian Peninsula. Sumerian was not the Akkadian spoken language. They did adapt the Sumerian cuneiform script. They used it to write their own language. Only scholars and literary scribes still used the Sumerian language. They used it in official and religious ceremonies. Enheduanna is the first poet in world history with a known name. She was Sargon's daughter. Ur was the city where she served as high priestess. The goddess Inanna is praised in many of those poems. No deity in Mesopotamia was more popular than Inanna. People in Mesopotamia placed a high value on literacy. Mari's large library is proof of that value. Cuneiform tablets in that library numbered over twenty thousand.

7The Akkadian years of peace changed Mesopotamia. The first postal system was a Mesopotamian creation under Akkadian rulers. Cuneiform messages were cut into clay tablets. Each tablet was then wrapped in a clay envelope. A mark on the envelope was the sender's seal. It was also marked with the name of the person who should receive it, and with the place it should go. Only the recipient could read the message. Someone had to break the clay envelope before the inside could be read. A centralized government was set up. Laws and regulations were enforced. Trade networks grew. Commerce boomed. Personal property carried relief figures. These ornate images did much more than decorate. Cylinder seals made the figures. A cylinder seal was a carved tube. People pressed it into clay to show who owned an item. Cuneiform script covers a clay tablet that is a letter 2,300 years old. Every Sumerian used a cylinder seal from ancient Mesopotamia as a signature. The seals showed property rights. Daily business in the empire also depended on those seals. People approved documents and transactions by pressing a seal into the clay. The Mesopotamians also developed numerical place value. Their counting system was base sixty. Another name for it is a sexagesimal system. The way we measure time uses this same system. The way we calculate angles uses it too. The system began thousands of years ago. Certain objects are counted with numerical base systems. Eggs counted by the dozen use base twelve. Twelve eggs equal one dozen. Time is counted in base sixty. The ancient Mesopotamians devised that system. One minute holds sixty seconds. There are sixty minutes in one hour. Historians are not quite sure why the Mesopotamians used base sixty. One theory says sixty can be split evenly in many ways. That made math easier in a world without calculators.

8The earliest pottery wheels have been found in Sumer. Sumer has also yielded the oldest wheels used to move loads. Modern life still depends on wheels. Near 3500 bce, Sumerian workers started to use a flat round machine. They used it to make clay bowls. An axis held the potter's wheel in a flat, level position. Spinning let the potter form a bowl whose edges and surfaces stayed even. After another three hundred years, wheels took on a new Sumerian job. Wheels made it easier to move objects. They also made it easier to transport people. Many trials led the Sumerians to a fresh layout. A single axle got one wheel on the left and one wheel on the right. Loads became easy to haul with this device. Little effort sent the cart rolling over the ground. The design may have been copied by other civilizations. Soon wheels were used around the world. About 2150 bce marks the end of the Akkadian Empire. A severe drought was one cause. Gutian invaders (GOO-tee-uhnz) were the second cause. These Gutians were nomads. Their home was the mountain country to the north. Bands of Gutian fighters often struck the kingdom's outer lands. As a result, trade slowed. Fields lay untended. Shar-Kali-Sharri was Akkad's ruler then. He struggled to keep control as the Gutians pushed farther into Mesopotamia. Akkad eventually fell to the Gutian nomads. The Akkadian Empire collapsed. An ancient statue shows Gudea. He was a Gutian prince. Lagash was under his rule from 2144 to 2124 bce.

9Sumerians pray to their gods. Their prayer asks the gods to let them win against the Gutians. Running a society this complex was new to the Gutians. They were poor administrators. They let the canal network fall into disrepair. A terrible famine followed. A grim time in Sumer followed that rule, and the rule lasted 125 years. People later called that time Mesopotamia's Dark Age. Mesopotamia did not regain its place as a thriving empire until later. The change came in the seventeenth century bce. King Hammurabi was the ruler then. The empire was called Babylonia. Hammurabi, king of Babylonia, set down a law code. Many later civilizations used that law code as their model. An alloy is a metal made by combining two or more metals. Nomadic describes moving from place to place with no lasting home. City-states are cities that act as independent countries. Phonologic means related to speech sounds inside a language or among languages. Civilizations are organized societies with steady food, government, social structure, culture, writing, and religion. Pictographs are pictures or symbols that stand for words or ideas. Smelted means melted or fused with extreme heat. Commerce means buying goods and selling them. People also call this business or trade. A stele is a tall rock slab, or a rock column set on end. It is usually carved with designs or words that honor a person or an event. Another spelling is stela. Cuneiform is a writing system of wedge-shaped marks. People in some areas of the old Middle East used this script. A ziggurat is a rectangular building of ancient Mesopotamia. Steps climb its sides, and a temple sits at the top. In myth, a demigod has one human parent and one divine parent.

13. Cities That Live with Water

1Twelve water places appear in this book. They are Venice in Italy, Amsterdam in the Netherlands, and Reine in Norway. Singapore is on the list. So is Palm Jumeirah in Dubai. Ganvié in Benin and Fadiouth in Senegal are on the list too. The Uros Floating Village in Peru is included. Mexcaltitán in Mexico is included. Auckland in New Zealand, Sausalito in California, and Manarola in Italy close the list. The chapters move from living near water to Europe, Asia, and Africa. South America, North America, and Oceania follow. A chapter on people and water comes next. A word list ends the book. Boats cross the Grand Canal, Venice's main waterway. They carry people and goods among the many islands the city stands on. Visitors from every part of the globe come to Venice, Italy. They come to see its famous canals and bridges. Builders set Venice on over one hundred islands. Venice is the water city people know best. It is not the only water city. A water city is a place built on water or beside it. Some are tiny communities. Others are huge urban areas. Oceans hold some of them. Seas, bays, lakes, and rivers hold others. Some sit in those waters. They appear on every continent except Antarctica. People built them for many reasons. Each city has a story worth hearing.

2Europe holds more than one famous canal city. Amsterdam, in the Netherlands, is another. Amsterdam is the capital of the Netherlands. It is also that country's largest city. Roughly one-third of Dutch land lies below sea level. Polders make up about 17 percent of the Netherlands. A polder is a wide stretch of land people took back from water. The water came from lakes and from the North Sea. People pumped that water out. Amsterdam itself sits about 2 meters below sea level. In feet, that depth is 6.6. The city began in the late twelfth century. It started as a fishing village. In the seventeenth century it became an important seaport. Today it is one of Europe's largest seaports. More than 1,200 bridges cross its web of canals. A picture of the city shows that web. A moat ringed Amsterdam from 1480 to 1585. In the 1600s the population surged as the city grew. Workers drained swampland. They built three more canals in half circles. Those canals share one center. They wrap the downtown area. The four waterways served transportation. They also served defense. They served water management too. About 160 more canals run through the city, beyond the main ones. Together they stretch over 100 kilometers (62 mi.). Those canals split Amsterdam into nearly 100 little islands. Over 1,200 bridges join those islands. For centuries the canals were the main routes for getting around. Today smaller barges and boats are the main users. The canals still shape the city's special character. Reine is a tiny fishing village in Norway. Reine lies more than 100 kilometers above the Arctic Circle. In miles, that distance is 62. A nickname calls it the world's most beautiful place. Its shore belongs to one island in the Lofoten group. Visitors can fish, kayak, ski, watch whales, and hike. Gorgeous beaches are there as well. The village is also a fine spot for striking views of the northern lights.

3Singapore is one island, yet it is a city and a country at the same time. It is the world's only island city-state. It is not only one of Asia's busiest port cities. Few port cities anywhere are busier. The country is made of sixty-three islands. It has few natural resources. It must bring in almost everything from outside. Projects that started in the 1960s grew its land area. The gain was almost 25 percent. Amsterdam made new land by taking seawater away. Singapore did something different. Rocks went into the water, and sand went in too, so the shore moved outward. A project begun in 2016 will pump water out to make polders. That method matches Amsterdam's. As the city grew more urban, the government planted greenery. The goal was a city set in a garden. Parks and nature reserves now cover close to one-tenth of the land. Inside the United Arab Emirates, seven political territories exist. One of those seven has Dubai as its capital. Oil found in the 1960s made the country rich. The economy now centers on tourism. Real estate matters too. So do other industries. Dubai is full of building projects. One project is the Palm Islands. These are three human-made islands shaped like palm trees. Workers build them along the coast. They pile up huge amounts of sand and rock. The islands add hundreds of miles of shoreline to the city. They also add homes for many thousands of people. Building started in 2001. The first island was finished in late 2016. A crescent-shaped breakwater wraps the island. It guards the island from strong waves and storms. Palm Jumeirah belongs to the Palm Islands. It lies on the Persian Gulf. Among artificial islands, few in the world are larger. From above, it was planned to look like a palm tree.

4People sometimes call Ganvié the Venice of Africa. Benin, in Africa, is its country. Nearly every building in Ganvié stands on stilts over the water. Ganvié looks nothing like the modern cities of Singapore and Dubai. No stilt village in Africa is larger. Lake Nokoué holds the village at its center. Say the lake name NOHK-we. Benin is the country. About twenty thousand people live in Ganvié. The village has three thousand buildings. Only the school stands on land. A cemetery that is only partly built stands on land too. Ganvié began during the West African slave trade of the seventeenth century. A powerful tribe called the Fon captured people from other tribes. The Fon sold those people into slavery. Fon religion did not allow them to chase people who lived on water. The Tofinu were another tribe. They escaped by going to Lake Nokoué. They wanted to avoid capture. They founded the stilt village. The way of life they built still thrives. Their success shows in the village name. The name means we survived. Fishing is the main way villagers earn a living. Underwater fences hold fish and let the people breed them. Grass patches grow in the water. Chickens live on them. Goats live on those patches too. Their boats are long and narrow. Those canoes are called pirogues. Fadiouth, also called Shell Island, is in Senegal. Say the name fad-YOOT. The people there live on an island made only of clamshells. For more than three centuries they have eaten clams. They reuse the shells. Shells form buildings. Shells form streets. Shells form the land itself. Tree roots and reed roots hold the shells in place.

5The Uru people live in the Uros Floating Village on Lake Titicaca, in Peru. Reeds grow along the lake shore. The Uru turn those reeds into canoes. Those canoes are how they travel. Like the Tofinu, the people of Uros moved onto water to stay safe. The Uru fled to Lake Titicaca more than five hundred years ago. The Inca and the Colla had taken their land. The lake is now home to about two thousand Uru. They use about seventy floating islands. Totora reeds form the islands. Thickness runs from 1.2 meters up to 2.4 meters. In feet, that span is 4 to 8. The islands break apart from the bottom. People keep adding fresh reeds on top. The Uru also use the reeds for canoes. They use them for homes. They use them for furniture. Fishing and the sale of handicrafts provide their living. Old traditions remain. Some modern amenities are part of life too. A radio station serves the community. Some residents have solar panels. Some have motorboats. Mexcaltitán sits on a lonely sand bar off Mexico's Pacific coast. The bar stretches 400 meters. In feet, that length is 1,300. The Aztec people ruled wide parts of Mesoamerica. Their rule ran from the fourteenth century to the sixteenth century. Legend says their first home was Aztlán. Say that name ahst-LAHN. Stories describe Aztlán as an island of myth set in a lagoon. Northern Mexico may be where that real place stood. Then their gods told them to find a new homeland. They founded Tenochtitlán. The trip there lasted two hundred years. That city was their capital. Spanish conquerors took it in 1521. After that, Tenochtitlán was known as Mexico City.

6Some historians think Aztec civilization began at Mexcaltitán. Say that name mes-kahl-tee-TAHN. It is a small island people made near Mexico's Pacific coast. Old manuscripts show the early Aztecs traveling by canoe. The canoes look like the ones people in Mexcaltitán still use. A map from 1579 puts Aztlán and Mexcaltitán in the very same spot. Mexcaltitán measures about 350 meters by 40 meters. In feet, that is 1,150 by 130. About eight hundred people live there now. Their houses have tile roofs. Adobe forms the houses. Brick forms them too. Cement forms them too. Many people earn a living by catching shrimp. Local restaurants buy the shrimp. People sometimes call this water town the Mexican Venice. In summer, the rainy season floods the streets. Boats are then the only way to move through town. Local children play a game they call water soccer. They play it on the island's only soccer pitch. That pitch lies under water. During flood season, people boat through the streets and swim in them. Auckland is not the capital of New Zealand. It is the country's largest city. This modern city has 1.5 million people. New Zealand's North Island is where the city stands. That island is in the South Pacific Ocean. Two large harbors flank the city. The city sits on an isthmus. At its narrowest, the isthmus is less than 2 kilometers across. In miles, that width is 1.2. One harbor opens to the Pacific Ocean on the east. The other opens to the Tasman Sea on the west. People call Auckland the City of Sails. About one-sixth of the people who live there own a boat. The city has hosted the America's Cup yacht race two times. Residents and visitors enjoy thousands of beaches. Water fun includes kayaking and parasailing. It also includes swimming, surfing, boating, and fishing. Diving and horseback riding on the beach are part of it too.

7The water cities already described are not the only kind. A picture set shows California, Italy, and an aircraft carrier. Manarola, in Italy, is a tiny fishing town. Brightly colored homes are piled together there. They cling to steep, rocky cliffs. Sausalito, in Northern California, is a floating community. Its houseboats are fancy, simple, and vintage. Aircraft carriers are huge ships built to launch aircraft. They house thousands of military people for months at a time. Water means different things to different people. For people in water cities, it may mean play. It may mean a link to nature. It may mean a way of life. It may mean safety. It may mean survival. It may mean a needed link to the rest of the world. Water cities around the world differ in remarkable ways. That variety shows how people adapt and invent. Adaptability is the skill of changing to fit a new place or situation. Innovation is a new idea, product, or method, or the act of bringing new things in. Amenities are features that add ease, comfort, or pleasure. An isthmus is a narrow land strip that joins two larger land areas. A breakwater is a wall built in the sea. It shelters a harbor or a coast from waves. Reclaimed means taken back or improved so people can use it. A seaport is a town or harbor used by ships that sail the sea. Concentric describes things that share one center. Disintegrate means something breaks down into tiny pieces. A stilt is a post that holds a structure above ground or water. The same word can mean one of two long poles for walking high up. Diversity is a wide mix of many different things. Urban is a word for city life and town life, and for the people there.

14. Comic Cons

1Robin, the Joker, and Batman meet at the 2014 New York Comic Con. Thousands of fans wait in an auditorium for the stars. It is warm inside a Chewbacca costume. The fan two rows down wears a helmet. The audience stands and cheers. Robert Downey Jr. walks onstage as Iron Man. The Avengers panel has begun. Comic cons are colorful, crowded, and built for pop culture fans.

2Cons happen all over the country. Some are huge and last several days. Some are very small and last one afternoon. They welcome children, adults, gamers, and readers. Shel Dorf organized gatherings in Detroit in the 1960s. He founded Comic-Con International in San Diego in 1970. About 100 fans came the first year, and over 130,000 attend now. Stan Lee helped create many Marvel superheroes and died in 2018.

3Writers and artists continue to explore new topics and styles. A comic might tell a superhero story, a war story, or a family story. Graphic novels are popular young adult fiction. Art Spiegelman’s Maus won the Pulitzer Prize. Maus is based on interviews with his father, a Holocaust survivor. Movies such as Iron Man and Black Panther brought new fans. Actors and directors draw huge crowds on panels. Cons now include video games, novels, and TV shows.

4Many fans attend cons for anime and manga from Japan. Other cons focus on sci-fi, fantasy, steampunk, horror, or humor. Smaller cons outside San Diego and New York still show huge enthusiasm. Local fans mingle with cartoonists and movie stars. You might meet a favorite star or a local artist and make new friends. Cosplay means dressing as characters from comics, anime, and fantasy. Costumes can be bought, custom-built, or handmade. Many cons hold contests where cosplayers pose and act in skits.

5Michael B. Jordan and Danai Gurira appear on a panel at the 2017 San Diego Comic Con. Fans hear stars speak about making books, films, and shows. Audience members can ask questions, take photos, and get autographs. These meetings become treasured memories. Celebrities often say the fans are warm, friendly, and devoted. The exhibit hall is the center where people mingle and shop. Fans buy comics, action figures, toys, and rare editions. Cosplayers wander by while movie trailers play.

6Contests, panels, screenings, and shopping make cons entertainment on their own. Some fans feel the bigger cons have grown too large. They say the focus has moved too far from comic books. About forty percent of recent San Diego attendees are female. More than half of recent con attendees are under twenty-nine. Some comics on race, gender, or war face banned-book campaigns. Spider-Man and Batman comics have been challenged for adult themes. Panels ask what role comics should have in libraries and society.

7It is an exciting time for comic cons even when growth is hard. Fans of every age, gender, and nationality can find themselves in a comic. A hero might look like Superman or a young girl from Botswana. A hero might be a reporter on the ground in Bosnia. There is no limit to what can happen or who can be a hero. Cons let ordinary people become heroes. Fans dress up, trade books, and shake hands with artists. A young girl shows a pink Star Wars stormtrooper costume at San Diego.

15. Copying the Tricks of Living Things

1A male peacock spreads his feathers so mates will notice him. A peacock's feathers hold little color of their own. The special shape of the feathers bends light. That bend makes the feathers look colored. Locusts never crash into one another. A special part of a locust brain notices something in front of it. That part reacts much faster than a human brain can. Living things of this kind fill nature in vast numbers. Each one has abilities that help it survive. For many years, humans have studied nature. We have studied how nature works. A science called biomimicry is now growing. It looks at lessons from nature. It asks how we can use those lessons in our own lives. This book asks what biomimicry is. It shows inventions that began with a surprise from nature. Velcro is one example. Road reflectors are another. Screens that cut glare are another. Paint and roof tiles that clean themselves are another. A fast train in Japan is another. Swimsuits are another. Ways to cool a building are another. Materials for building are another. The book asks why this science matters so much. It looks ahead. Artificial leaves are part of that look ahead. Spider silk is part of it. Fish antifreeze is part of it. Natural plastics are part of it. Medicine is part of it. A closing thought comes near the end. Word meanings come last.

2In Florida, a biologist is shown studying a manatee. Two older words were joined to make biomimicry. Bio means life. Mimic means to copy. People also call this field biomimetics. It is the science of copying life. That can sound simple. The idea behind the science is pretty simple. Organisms in nature have faced many different challenges. Living things have adapted across many thousands of years. Adapted means they have changed. The changes help them survive better in their environments. Biologists study those changes. So do other scientists. So do designers. So do engineers. So do other people. They look at how organisms solved the challenges they face. Then they look at the challenges humans face. They ask how nature's adaptations can help us find answers. Biomimicry does not use living things. It learns from them. The idea is not new. For hundreds of years, people have turned to nature to solve problems. Leonardo da Vinci was a scientist. He was an inventor. He was an artist. He lived from 1452 to 1519. He designed many flying machines. He based them on what he saw birds do. Every one of those machines failed. You can see the same kind of copying in the shape of boats. It also shows up in places that may surprise you. A famous drawing by da Vinci shows one of his flying machines. Janine Benyus stands among the earliest modern experts on this subject. She and her peers say the field asks two main questions. One question is how nature solves a problem. The other question is which problems are worth solving. Watching animals deal with problems is what these scientists recommend. Humans can then use what they see. They can use it on similar human problems. Teaching biomimicry to more people is one goal Benyus holds. Several awards have recognized her work.

3A close view shows hook-and-loop fabric. A small picture shows a burr. Another picture shows a boy in a suit of that fabric, hanging on a wall covered with it. In 1941 a Swiss engineer took an autumn hike. His name was Georges de Mestral. His dog went with him through a meadow. Prickly seeds called burrs stuck in the dog's fur. He looked at the burrs under a microscope. Little hooks covered parts of every burr. The hooks could catch on fibers. Hair is one kind of fiber they catch. From that look, he created Velcro. One fabric has hooks. The other fabric has loops. Together they hold things for a while, then let go. The same adaptation lets those little seeds travel far. People have used the idea in hundreds of ways. Those uses have made daily life easier. The idea even helped people go to the Moon. A cat's eyes shine when light hits them. A small picture shows a road marker in Yorkshire, in the United Kingdom. At night, light bounces off plastic pieces set along roads. The pieces sit in the middle of the road. They also sit along the sides. Markers like these are common all around the world. The first version was Percy Shaw's invention. One night in 1933 he was driving in fog. Suddenly a pair of cat eyes shone back in his headlights. A highly reflective layer of cells sits at the back of a cat's eye. That layer is what makes the eyes glow when light hits them. That glow was all Shaw needed. He created a marker he called the Cat's Eye. It uses two highly reflective spheres. The spheres are set into the center of a road. The marker has changed over the years. Even so, the idea from those two shining eyes has helped countless drivers. It has helped them get home safely at night. It has helped them in fog too.

4A tiny view shows the surface of a moth's eye. That surface cuts down reflection. Scientists have studied insects for many years. In the 1960s they found something about a moth's eye. The surface reduces reflection. The eyes of certain kinds of flies do the same thing. Almost forty years passed. Then human tools were ready. Lasers could make screens that copied a moth's eye surface. These new screens are often found on computers. They greatly reduce glare. Working becomes much easier on our eyes. Playing becomes easier on our eyes too. Tiny views also show self-cleaning paint. The paint copies the surface of a lotus leaf. Water beads up on it. The water rolls off. A lotus leaf can do something pretty amazing. Lotus plants grow in ponds. They grow in rivers. Dirt can cover them easily. Mud can cover them easily. A lotus leaf may get very dirty. A light sprinkle of rain still cleans the surface completely. When water lands on the leaf, the special surface makes the water bead up. The beads roll off. As the water rolls, it picks up the dirt. The leaf ends up clean. A group of scientists noticed this feature. Engineers noticed it too. They made paints with a surface like the lotus leaf. They made roof tiles with that kind of surface too. Rain then cleans the outsides of houses that use these paints. It cleans houses that use these tiles.

5A kingfisher is a small bird. In 2010 a Shinkansen train pulled into a station in Tokyo. Japan's Shinkansen is also called the bullet train. It used to have a rounded nose. That nose worked well until the train entered a tunnel. Its speed reached as high as 320 kilometers per hour. That speed is 199 miles per hour. When the train left the tunnel, the air around it was under great pressure. The pressure made a booming noise. Designers looked to the kingfisher to fix the problem. The bird's beak has a unique shape. Its head has a unique shape too. Those shapes let it dive for fish without a big splash. The designers based a new train nose on a kingfisher's beak. The new nose solved the noise problem. It also made the train faster. It made the train more efficient. A swimmer models a swimsuit made to work like a shark's skin. A close picture shows shark scales. A great white shark is shown as well. Ocean sharks were already swimming before dinosaurs lived. They are well adapted to moving in water. Their skin is strong evidence of that. The skin looks smooth. A hand run across it would feel sandpaper-rough. A shark's skin is made of tiny grooved scales. The special shape of the scales helps the shark. The shark moves through the water more easily. It also moves faster. At the 2000 Olympic Games in Sydney, a number of companies showed new swimsuits. The suits were designed to work like a shark's skin. Of the swimmers who wore those suits, 80 percent won medals.

6The Eastgate Centre lets hot air out through vents. The vents run along the sides of the building. They also run along the top. The cooling plan works like a termite mound. Some kinds of termites build huge mounds to live in. Biologists found a surprise about those mounds. Outside, the air might be very hot. Inside, the temperature stayed steady. It stayed cooler. The termites cool the mound with a complex set of tunnels. Vents are part of that set. Tunnels link the deep ground with the mound's top. Warmer air rises. Hot air leaves the mound through the top. Air from lower down comes up cooler. The mound stays at a steady temperature. The Eastgate Centre stands in Harare, Zimbabwe. Builders used these same ideas there. The building uses materials that resist heat. Special vents at the top help keep the large building cool. It stays cool without air conditioning. An ordinary building of the same size would need ten times as much energy as this one. The Moss Landing power plant is in California. It was one of the first plants to turn carbon dioxide into cement. The process is similar to one that corals use. Greenhouse gases include carbon dioxide. Those gases cause climate change. We burn fossil fuels to make electricity. We burn them to drive cars. That burning puts more and more carbon dioxide into the air. Some living things use carbon dioxide to build structures. Coral is one of those living things. Scientists studied how these living things use carbon dioxide. Then they found a way to copy the process. Some of the carbon dioxide from burning fossil fuels can now be used. It can be made into materials similar to concrete. We can build new things with those materials.

7Making what people want is something humans do well. We are great at making the things we need. Our species has been here for many thousands of years. In that time we once lived in caves. We had to hunt for food. We had to gather food. Now we sleep in homes. We shop at stores. Our recent growth has forced us to face some tough challenges. One challenge is how we produce all the things we use. We make food. We make homes. We make plastic bottles. We make cars. We make video games. Those are only a few examples. We use great quantities of resources to make them. Waste materials come from us in large amounts too. We produce pollution. Often the energy that makes these things comes from fossil fuels. The energy that runs them often comes from fossil fuels too. Greenhouse gases collect in the air because of fossil fuels. We also create many artificial chemicals. Those chemicals may harm the environment. When we finish using things, they often end up in landfills. The buildup of greenhouse gases is starting to cause problems. Garbage is part of that problem. Other waste materials are part of it too. Pollution is causing the climate to change. We are running out of fossil fuels. We are running out of other resources. Our garbage is contaminating Earth in a variety of ways. Other waste materials are contaminating Earth too. Garbage pours from a truck into a landfill. A small village in the United Kingdom sits near the landfill.

8One thing we can do is learn from nature. Nature is great at making what it needs in order to survive. It does this in a very simple way. It does this with little waste. A clam makes its shell from seawater and food. It needs nothing else for that shell. Water travels a long way inside the world's tallest tree. The water goes from the roots to the top branches. The energy comes from the Sun. The tree uses no water pump. Another organism can use any waste as a resource. A dead tree can become food for hundreds of living things. It can become shelter. It can become energy. An ecosystem's parts all work as one. They work in a sustainable way. They do not use up resources. They do not damage resources. These sustainable environments took many thousands of years to form. Each living thing plays a certain role. Its adaptations play a role too. Mushrooms grow on a fallen tree. Moss grows there too. Biomimicry gives us a chance to see how these environments keep going. We can see how the organisms in them work together. Through them we can learn. We can change how we live. We can change how we make the things we use. That can help solve our challenges. It can help us live in more sustainable ways. A design called Solar Ivy acts like artificial leaves. It turns sunlight into energy. A tree does that too. Sunlight is what trees use when they make energy. The process is called photosynthesis. Scientists are working on artificial trees. The leaves would use sunlight to produce electricity. They would use wind as well. One day these trees might make enough energy for a change. We could burn less fossil fuel.

9A tiny view shows a spider spinning silk. A funnel spider builds its web in the larger picture. Have you ever walked into a spider's web by accident? If you have, you know the silk is sticky. You know it is strong. Scientists have proved a comparison. Compared with steel, spider silk is about three times stronger. Now they are trying to learn how a spider makes silk. The process is not simple. Then they want to create the silk themselves. Artificial spider silk would be a fabric. It would be incredibly strong. It would be light. It would be natural. It could have many different uses. Antarctic ice fish live in water that is below freezing. A natural antifreeze in their blood lets them survive. Oxygen can enter through their skin as well. Many kinds of fish can live in temperatures below freezing. Some live in the Antarctic. Some live at the bottom of the ocean. That extreme cold would kill most creatures. These fish survive because of antifreezes in their bodies. The antifreezes are of different kinds. Scientists are trying to learn how these antifreezes are made. They are trying to learn how they work. If they succeed, they may find a variety of uses. Organ transplants are one possible use. Ocean exploration is another.

10Tiny views show two kinds of microorganisms. Those microorganisms make natural plastics. Each year, people go through tons of plastic. Great amounts of energy go into the usual way of making plastic. It creates huge amounts of waste. Scientists have found something in some microorganisms. Those tiny living things make the building blocks of plastics. They make them inside their cells. Scientists want to learn how that making begins. If they can, they can copy the process. Plastics could then be made with less energy. They could be made with much less waste. A chimpanzee eats medicinal plants. Her baby eats them too. Other animals have survived in the wild for thousands of years. Sickness happens to them sometimes, as it does to us. Even so, treating their illnesses seems to be something they know. They use native plants. They use minerals. Many people around the world have used natural remedies too. They have treated illnesses this way for thousands of years. They have treated injuries this way too.

11Two children look closely at a dragonfly. Nature still has a lot to teach us. We can learn how nature helps us make new things. We can learn how it helps us make better things. We can learn how it helps sustain life. That includes our life. It includes every other life-form on Earth. Humans as a species face many challenges. Some of those challenges are easy. Others are very difficult. As we try to solve these problems, a look outside might help. You might be playing in a park. You might be walking in a forest. You might be doing anything in nature. Stop and look around. Look at how many living things share your world. A special role belongs to each one. That role helps every part of the environment work. You have a role too. The most interesting solutions to our challenges may be very close. The most efficient solutions may be close too. They may be waiting to be found through the science of biomimicry. These words have special meanings in this book. Adapted means changed to fit a new situation or a specific environment. Fossil fuels are energy sources taken from the ground. Coal is one. Oil is one. Natural gas is one. An antifreeze is added so a substance freezes only at a colder point. Glare is a bright light. It is usually light reflected off something. Greenhouse gases are gases in Earth's atmosphere. They trap heat. They contribute to global warming. Biologists are scientists who study living things. Contaminating means making something unusable or unsafe. A harmful or unwanted substance is added. Microorganisms are microscopic organisms. Viruses are examples. Single bacterial cells are examples. An ecosystem is living things plus the habitat they share. Resources are supplies of very useful or valuable things. Efficient means making good use of time or resources. Sustainable means something can be used without using a resource up completely. It also means use that does not cause permanent damage to a resource.

16. Cricket

1Fans wave the national flag of India during a brief rain delay at a match in Mumbai. In cricket, a batter hits a ball with a large flat bat. The ball is about the same size as a baseball. Cricket is one of the top five most popular sports in the world. One source estimates two to three billion cricket fans worldwide. How did a children's game from the sixteenth century become a favorite sport? The story begins in England, the birthplace of cricket.

2The first written note of "creckett" is from 1598, in a paper about a land dispute. A witness said he and his classmates played on that land. Children played the game, and adults later played matches between rival towns. Rich patrons formed teams, and in 1788 the Marylebone Cricket Club wrote laws still used today. People played in London in 1743, and the nineteenth century was the Golden Age. In 1864 overarm bowling was allowed, giving the ball speed and spin, and batters found new strokes. Cricket took hold in Canada, Australia, India, South Africa, New Zealand, and the Caribbean. Test matches began in 1877, women's clubs grew, and West Indies players made bowling more aggressive.

3Cricket is most popular in the United Kingdom and lands once ruled by Britain. A team needs Test status to play at the top, and twelve teams had it in 2024. They were England, Australia, South Africa, the West Indies, New Zealand, India, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, Zimbabwe, Bangladesh, Ireland, and Afghanistan. West Indies players come from sixteen countries and colonies. There are community games, pro games, women's Tests, and matches with special rules. Cricket was in the Olympics in 1900, and supporters still lobby to bring it back. Baseball came from early cricket, but the games differ, and cricket uses an oval and a pitch. "Not cricket" means unfair, and a sticky wicket means a wet field or a tough spot.

4An Australian wicketkeeper stands behind an Indian batter and prepares to catch the ball. Balls can travel about 95 to 100 miles per hour, and a regulation field is at least 16,000 square yards. A wicket is three short posts, called stumps, with two bails on top. The bowler throws, the wicketkeeper stands behind the wicket, and nine fielders go where the captain says. The striker and the nonstriker stand at opposite ends of the pitch. A fair overhand bowl must be thrown without bending the elbow. If the bowler knocks the bails off, the striker is out, so the striker hits the ball away. The pitch is 22 yards long, and a run scores when the batters cross the popping crease and swap ends.

5The batters run back and forth. They try to score as many runs as they can. A fielder can run the striker out. The striker must be safe behind the crease. If a fielder knocks the bails off, the striker is out. A striker is also out if a fielder catches the ball before it bounces. If the striker is not out, the batter at the old end bats next. Players wear pads, a face guard, and gloves. They use a bat and a ball. Why are there two wickets if there is one striker? After six balls, a new bowler throws.

6W. G. Grace lived from 1848 to 1915, worked as a doctor, and played as an amateur. He invented a batting style that worked from many stances and used many different strokes. Kumar Shri Ranjitsinhji lived from 1872 to 1933, ruled Nawanagar under Britain, and was called Ranji. The Ranji Trophy is named for him, an icon in India, and he represented India in the League of Nations, predecessor of the United Nations. Sir Donald Bradman lived from 1908 to 2001 and is known as the best Test batter, with an average of 99.94. The next best is 60.97, and the Invincibles played thirty-four undefeated matches, 112 days of a 144-day tour. Since 1889 Wisden has named five Cricketers of the Year each year, and Claire Taylor was the first woman in 2009. She was born in 1975, batted with men in college, and became one of the finest batters.

7How did cricket become a sport loved in many countries? The history of the British Empire played an important part. New and exciting styles of play played an important part too. Perhaps the answer is in two special features of the game. When a player is at bat, it does not matter who that player is or where the player came from. All players compete as equals, and tradition says each player deserves the same respect. Cricket may be popular because equality and respect are values everybody can share. Young players work on their skills with a bat, a ball, and a wicket, as these boys do in northern India.

17. Digital Reality Tech

1An augmented reality heads-up display can show weather and traffic. Reality is everyday life, from a bus ride to setting the table. People call life outside computers IRL, or in real life. Digital realities are made with computer hardware and software. There are three main types: virtual reality, augmented reality, and merged reality. Virtual reality builds a fully artificial world that a headset surrounds you with. Augmented reality does not block the real world. It adds images or facts, such as a live map on a phone or glasses.

2Virtual reality puts you in a fully digital world with no sense of real life. Augmented reality lays digital facts over the real world. A phone app can show how a new chair would look in a room. Merged reality lets virtual and real objects interact. It can map a room and track your movements. Your hands can push virtual buttons or move a real book into the virtual world. Two people can play virtual chess on a real table. Game designers, doctors, architects, and teachers are finding new uses as the tools improve.

3Most games help players jump into other worlds. Virtual reality covers the eyes and ears and makes the game feel intense. Sensors track a player's moves and show them in the game. Most players hold a controller in each hand. Some feel sick because the game fools the brain and body. Suits and gloves use electric currents so muscles feel a push or a grab. In Dubai, United Arab Emirates, a young gamer plays in a mock city at a virtual reality park. A researcher shows a glove made to give feedback in that world.

4Mobile adventures use augmented reality and GPS, the Global Positioning System, to find players. At set places, characters appear and must be dealt with. In one game, players throw balls to collect virtual creatures. Merged reality scans a room into a 3D map and lays a hologram over it. Players can use voice, hands, and movement, and even throw virtual balls with real hands. Film and television makers want virtual reality to surround viewers with action. Some parks may add a headset and superhero stories to a roller coaster.

5Three kinds of tracking help users feel inside another world. Head tracking notes if you look up, turn, or tilt, and the view changes. Motion tracking uses sensors and lasers around a room to follow you. Eye tracking can tell where you look and adjust focus, but it is still being developed. Merged reality smart glasses show virtual things while you still see the real world. In class you may walk the Acropolis, ask about Artemis or Athena, explore a cell, or paint a 3D jungle. Studies say the brain treats virtual action as real and people retain more by doing. Virtual trips can reach the Great Pyramid of Giza, Machu Picchu, the Great Barrier Reef, Sequoia National Park, or Mars.

6Digital tools are entering science and math class. Biology apps help students study human anatomy. Programs for chemistry and physics aim for lab lessons with no cut or fire risk. Math tools help students work with geometric shapes. Art students paint in three dimensions, and virtual paint cannot spill. A therapist tests waterproof goggles that let disabled swimmers meet wild dolphins. Some students with autism can practice telling if a person is happy or upset. The National Autistic Society film Too Much Information shows others what that life feels like.

7Experts say this technology holds great potential for schools. Students who learn best by moving and doing may improve with it. Students and teachers can discover good uses and create new experiences. A second grader uses goggles for a virtual field trip to the Grand Canyon. Businesses adapt the tools to design products and reach customers. Ford's lab in Dearborn, Michigan, is called FIVE, for Ford Immersive Vehicle Environment. Designers and engineers wear headsets while cameras record their movements. They use 3D sketching, walk around virtual vehicles, and climb inside for a closer look.

8Virtual reality can save makers time and money by fixing problems before prototypes. Architects can see a new office or house on its site before they break ground. They can tour clients through a building that is still only a plan. For the Atlanta Braves stadium, builders could view the field from any seat. They changed the computer model instead of tearing up concrete later. Surgeons practice procedures in virtual reality before they work on real patients. A doctor or dentist may soon wear glasses that show your health records as you talk. A merged reality headset can color-code vertebrae and show other facts during spine surgery.

9These tools blur the line between real life and digital life. They offer more natural ways to use computers for work and daily life. Big computing companies are investing billions of dollars. Headset prices are falling, so more people, schools, and workplaces can afford them. Better software is being made for the growing technology. Cheap apps can be built with smartphones and cardboard goggles. A sailor can aim a tablet at a technical manual page to see a 3D model of ship equipment. Digital reality is no longer just for games, and it may soon change how we live, learn, and work.

18. Early Birds: Fossils and Feathers

1A fossil shows the flightless bird Diatryma. Most species that ever lived on Earth have died out. A species may die out if the climate cools or the food supply falls. This happens if it cannot adapt to the new conditions. Paleontologists learn about extinct life from fossils kept in rock or other material. A fossil may be a footprint, a trail, a body print, bones, or a shell. Fossils show how new kinds of species developed over very long ages. Scientists think birds came from reptiles, maybe dinosaurs, more than 150 million years ago.

2Bacteria and fungi usually break a dead body down until it is gone. A fossil can form if the body is buried at once in mud or sand near water. It can show what the animal looked like and how it lived. The animal dies near water, the water rises, and the soft tissues decay. Sediment covers the bones, and minerals such as calcium replace them. A rocky copy of the skeleton remains, or the bone decays and leaves a hollow space. That space may keep prints of feathers or scales. John Day Fossil Beds in Oregon is a typical place where fossils are found.

3Fossil bones help scientists picture the animal and compare it with living species. Wing-like bones may mean it could fly for a long time and that birds are related to it. Because sediment builds up, deeper fossils are older than fossils near the surface. Scientists also test chemicals in the rock, mainly by radioisotope dating. Radioactive isotopes break down at a known rate, so the amounts left show the age. Paleontologists find fossils, study them, and sometimes display them in museums. The earliest known bird is Archaeopteryx, a primitive reptile-like bird about 150 million years old. These important fossils show that birds descended from reptiles.

4Archaeopteryx was about the size of a crow. It had feathers, wings, a wishbone, teeth, a long bony tail, and three clawed fingers on each wing. Its flat breastbone shows it probably flew poorly, without the strong flight muscles of modern birds. It was one of the first flyers besides insects. Hesperornis and Ichthyornis were water birds that lived about 90 million years ago. Hesperornis looked like a large loon, with webbed feet, tiny teeth, and only small wing bones, so it could not fly. Ichthyornis looked like a gull, had long pointed wings, flew well, and dived for fish. By about 35 million years ago, birds like chickens, doves, ducks, parrots, penguins, owls, and songbirds existed.

5Reptile-like bird fossils suggest that birds developed from reptiles. Feathered dinosaur fossils suggest that those reptiles were dinosaurs. Coelurosaurs were meat-eating dinosaurs with many birdlike traits, and a label names Coelophysis. The most birdlike coelurosaur fossils are about 155 million to 135 million years old. Most were 2 to 10 feet, or 0.6 to 3 meters, long and ran on two legs with four clawed toes. Hollow light bones, big eyes, and feathers suggest these dinosaurs developed into birds. Two well-preserved Compsognathus fossils, about 145 million years old, were found in Europe. Some were chicken-sized, with a long thin neck and tail and only two claws on each short front leg.

6One fossil holds a long-legged lizard, so Compsognathus had to be quick, like a fast insect-catching bird. A 121-million-year-old fossil shows a feathered baby bird curled in its egg, with strong bones and a large skull. It may have fed itself right after hatching, while most birds today hatch weak and bare and need parents. Fossils prove that long ago some animals had both bird traits and reptile traits. These fossils lead scientists to theorize that birds developed from dinosaurs. After more than 150 million years, dinosaurs could not adapt about 65 million years ago, perhaps after a meteorite hit. Birds survived, and more than 9,700 species now thrive from the Arctic and Antarctic to the hot tropics. Some scientists call birds living dinosaurs, so might a songbird really be a dinosaur?

7Scientists divide Earth's history into eras and periods, and the ages are in millions of years. Precambrian time ran from 4.5 billion to 543 million years ago and had bacteria, worms, and jellyfish. In the Paleozoic era, Cambrian seas had trilobites, mollusks, and jawless fish, and Ordovician seas had squid-like nautiloids. Silurian life included mosses and coral reefs, and Devonian life included sharks and amphibians. Carboniferous times had reptiles and giant insects, and the Permian had seed plants. In the Mesozoic era, dinosaurs came in the Triassic, from 248 to 213. Birds and mammals came in the Jurassic, from 213 to 145. Flowering plants came in the Cretaceous, from 145 to 65. In the Cenozoic era, horses and apes came in the Tertiary, and humans came in the Quaternary. Birds first appeared in the Jurassic period of the Mesozoic era.

19. Eight Billion and Counting

1Giant statues on Rapa Nui were too heavy to carry. Dutch explorers arrived there in 1722. They found hundreds of huge stone statues along the coast. The statues faced inland, not out to sea. One statue was more than 32 feet long and weighed 164,000 pounds. The people had only old tools and no large trees for rollers. How did they carve and move these moai?

2Long ago, Rapa Nui was covered with forests. People settled there almost eight hundred years ago and cut trees for homes and fields. The population grew until about 1680, then it dropped. Experts think the people used up food, water, and wood. Many people died after the forests were gone. In the same way, eight billion humans today may be using up Earth's resources.

3Earth may not look crowded, with empty fields and forests. Even New York City has parks, with 29,729 people per square mile. All people could fit in Los Angeles in tall buildings, but it would not be comfortable. Overpopulation means people use more resources than a place can give. Earth's carrying capacity may be two to forty billion people, depending on resource use. American-style living could support only about two billion people. China and India each have over one billion people and are using more resources. Use rose from 43 billion metric tons in 1990 to 92 billion, and may reach 190 billion by 2060.

4Scientist Jared Diamond wrote that ten thousand or more people once lived on Rapa Nui. By the 1700s, the island could barely support two thousand people. The forests were gone and the best soil had washed away. Farming began about ten thousand years ago in the Fertile Crescent. Irrigation helped crops, but salt and lost trees could ruin the soil. Floods from the Tigris and Euphrates became less regular, and old civilizations faded. The Rapa Nui had no wood for boats and could not reach neighbors thousands of miles away. Rich soil takes hundreds of years to form, but wind or water can take it in a few years.

5In 1800, one billion people lived on Earth. It had taken 120,000 years to reach that number. By 1927 the number had doubled, and by 1960 it was three billion. In 2022, the eight billionth person was born, and 200,000 more arrive each day. The Industrial Revolution brought new farm tools, fertilizers, and steam power. Railroads moved food, and more children survived disease. In 1796, Edward Jenner found the smallpox vaccine. Penicillin, found in 1928, was the first antibiotic and saves many lives.

6In 1798, Thomas Malthus warned that people were growing faster than food. He feared global starvation if families did not have fewer children. The world then had about eight hundred million people. In the 1940s, the Green Revolution brought better seeds, fertilizers, and pest control. Mexico, India, and Middle Eastern countries grew much more food. Food production rose about three times, but chemicals polluted water and land. Rachel Carson's 1962 book Silent Spring warned about this harm. Earth gets enough sun energy in 1.5 hours for a year, but less than 1 percent of water is fresh and ready to use.

7In Tucson, Arizona, the underground water level fell 170 feet as the city grew. One quarter of people, mostly in Africa and parts of Asia, lack safe drinking water. Seventy percent of fresh water is used to water crops. The Colorado River often fails to reach the sea because cities and farms take so much. Coal, copper, and other metals take millions of years to form and cannot be replaced. China uses the most coal, and the United States uses 20 percent of the world's oil with under 5 percent of the people. Nearly two-thirds of tropical rainforests are gone, and thousands of species die out each year. China's river dolphin, the baiji, lived for more than twenty million years and is now extinct.

8In July 2021, heavy rains and deadly floods hit Germany. The largest palm on Rapa Nui is extinct, and the people lost fuel, wood, and healthy soil. Burning oil and coal traps heat, so ice caps shrink and weather grows wilder. By 2010, world population growth had slowed from 2.2 percent to 1.1 percent a year. In many rich countries, the birthrate is below 2.1 children per woman. Wind and solar power are growing, and many countries passed laws to protect air and water. Since 1985, each person in the United States uses less water. New forests have grown, but old-forest ecosystems take a long time to return.

9The United Nations predicts nearly ten billion people by 2050. Most estimates say that is almost twice a sustainable number. China's population is almost 1.5 billion, the largest in the world. A 1979 one-child policy may have lowered China's population by 400 million. China later allowed two children, and in 2021 it allowed three. Educating women helps most, and birthrates have fallen in many Arab countries. When children get clean water, food, and health care, families often have fewer children. Support for old and sick people can also help parents choose smaller families.

10The United States uses more land's worth of resources than the land it has. For everyone to live like most Americans, Earth would need many more planets. A third of the world's food is wasted, enough to feed two billion people. A third of all grain is fed to livestock instead of people. Eating less meat saves grain and water and protects grasslands. China passed the United States in total energy use in 2009. Americans could shrink their footprint by eating smarter, buying less, and saving energy. In 2020, carbon dioxide emissions fell about 6 percent, then rose again by December.

11The people of Rapa Nui carved records, but no one can read that language yet. Some experts think tribes fought over resources and toppled the moai. Thousands died, and European diseases killed more. By 1877, only 111 people remained. Today the population is growing again, and some moai have been restored. Most native plants and animals are extinct, and the old culture is lost. This small island can teach us to protect Earth's limited resources. Earth is our own little island in space.

20. Electric Cars: History and Future

1Teens get on a school bus in Miami, Florida. You grab your lunch and your backpack. Then you head out the door. You hear the engine rumble before you see the bus. It is the school bus. Other kids on your block already wait at the stop. You run and get in line as the bus pulls up.

2Heat from the exhaust warms your legs, and fumes enter your nose and mouth. You climb aboard, sit, and soon the bus stops. Cars, trucks, and motorcycles fill the street, but none are moving. The noise is so loud you cannot hear the kid across from you. You wonder when the racket will end. School buses move through traffic like this in Toronto, Ontario.
