That smelly, noisy jam might have ended long ago if electric cars had been more popular early. So what is an electric car? Electric cars began in the 1830s, when Robert Anderson of Scotland invented the first electric carriage. In 1842 Robert Davidson and Thomas Davenport used electric cells that could not be recharged. Later Gaston Plante and Camille Faure made a rechargeable lead-acid battery.

3A lead-acid battery uses sulfuric acid and lead. When the acid eats the lead, a chemical reaction makes an electric charge. That charge runs the motor until the battery must be recharged. This breakthrough made electric cars more popular, and France and Great Britain led for years. The United States joined in 1891, when A. L. Ryker built an electric tricycle and William Morrison built a six-passenger wagon. America was hooked, and many early prototypes were made. In 1897 New York bought electric taxis from the Electric Carriage and Wagon Company of Philadelphia. They looked like horseless carriages and cost nearly $2,000, or at least $50,000 today. In 1899 the famous race driver Jenatzy went above 100 miles per hour in an electric car.

4By the early twentieth century, more people moved to cities and wanted cars. Inventors powered cars with steam, gasoline, and electricity. Electric sales soared in 1899 and 1900 because those cars did not shake, smell, or roar. Shifting gears was a nuisance on gasoline cars, but electric and steam cars needed no gear shift. Steam cars could take forty-five minutes to start on cold mornings, and gasoline cars needed a hand crank. Steam cars soon needed more water, while electric cars went farther on one charge. City roads made short, quiet trips common, and a standard electric car cost a bit under $1,000. Fancy cars for rich buyers neared $3,000 by 1910, and that price haunted electrics. In 1911 Rauch and Lang of Ohio offered one of the highest-priced models.

5The Lohner Porsche Mixte appeared in 1900. A hybrid runs on two energy sources, such as electricity and gas. Ferdinand Porsche invented the first hybrid in Germany in 1898. A gasoline engine spun generators, and the electricity ran the motors. After an early surge, electric car sales fell fast, and not only because of price. By the 1920s, better roads linked cities, so drivers needed cars that could go farther. Charles Kettering’s electric starter removed the need for a hand crank. Oil found in Texas made gasoline cheaper, and cows began to share fields with oil rigs.

6Ford's assembly lines let gas cars sell for $500 to $1,000. In 1912 a fancy electric cost about $1,750, while a gas car cost about $600. By 1935 electric vehicles were almost extinct. They returned in the 1960s as people saw harmful gases, harm to the environment, and reliance on foreign oil. In the late 1960s Battronic's truck carried 2,500 pounds at 25 miles per hour. In 1973 a gas shortage left many cars idle, and drivers waited in long lines. In the mid-1970s GE asked Battronic for 175 electric vans and 20 electric buses. About 2,000 CitiCars went 55 miles at 44 miles per hour, and the Elcar went 60 miles at 45. In 1975 the Postal Service tried 350 jeeps from American Motor Company that topped 50 miles per hour but only went 40 miles. In 2002 London police used two electric cars with a 53-mile range and a 56 mile per hour top speed.

7In 1990 and 1992 the United States passed the Clean Air Act Amendment and the Energy Policy Act. Some states also limited toxic emissions and gasoline use. Carmakers and the Department of Energy converted cars such as the Chevrolet S-10 and Geo Metro to electric power. General Motors built the EV1, a two-seat sports car that reached 50 miles per hour in under 7 seconds. It topped 80 miles per hour and could travel 80 miles on one charge. Other electrics included Toyota’s RAV4, Honda’s EV Plus, and Chrysler’s EPIC minivan. Like a century earlier, they cost $30,000 to $40,000, though makers hoped more production would cut prices. In 1999 a Nissan Altra EV wagon plugged in to recharge.

8Electric cars do not make emissions like gasoline cars. Their motors make about the same horsepower at any speed. They speed up faster and more quietly, and batteries can recharge from sun or wind power. Batteries may hold toxic chemicals that pollute if thrown away badly. Some cars need special sockets, charging can take many hours, and batteries wear out and cost a lot. A center in France recycles old car batteries. Late in the twentieth century, lithium-ion batteries mostly replaced lead-acid ones. They use the metal lithium, cost more, store more power, last longer, and leave more energy ready to use. The tzero was the first car to use them, hit 60 miles per hour in under 4 seconds, and cost about $220,000.

9Tesla Motors began in 2003 and promoted the tzero and lithium-ion batteries. In 2008 the Tesla Roadster was the first mass-produced lithium-ion car sold for legal public roads. It won fans, went over 240 miles on one charge, and hit 60 miles per hour in under 4 seconds, but cost $109,000. The affordable 2011 Nissan Leaf was a five-door hatchback for city and highway trips. More than 300,000 had sold by early 2018. The Tesla Model 3 and Chevrolet Bolt sold better, yet the Leaf helped more budgets reach electric cars. The 2021 Rivian R1T hits 60 miles per hour in 3.3 seconds and goes over 300 miles, but costs about $80,000. A cheaper Slate Truck is set for 2027. Hybrids, first made in 1898, use a gas engine and electric motors so drivers can fill the tank, and some can use plant fuel. Rising gas prices helped the Toyota Prius pass one million sales by 2007.

10Most fuel cells split hydrogen atoms and join them with oxygen from the air. That reaction makes electricity to charge a battery, and the only exhaust is water, H2O. The Honda FCX Concept was a hydrogen fuel-cell car that worked this way. Hydrogen cars can fuel quickly and travel more than 300 miles on one tank. People once feared hydrogen was dangerous and hard to store, but safety and popularity have grown. The Toyota Mirai, sold since 2014, was the first mass-produced hydrogen car, and more are being developed. Gasoline and other fossil fuels will not last forever, so electric cars can stay if we make electricity. Rising prices, air pollution, and climate change raise demand, and power may come from known or new sources. Next time you are stuck in traffic, think of efficient, affordable, friendly travel.

11An alternative is another choice. Atoms are the smallest bits that still act like an element. A breakthrough is an important step forward. Capacity is the most something can hold. A chemical reaction turns one set of substances into another. Emissions are things given off. Exhaust is waste gas. The environment includes plants, animals, water, soil, weather, land, and air. A hybrid mixes different things, often two power sources in a car. Efficient means working well with little waste. Dangerous means able to cause harm. A nuisance annoys you. Pungent means a sharp smell. Mobility is moving from place to place. Prototypes are first models for later things.

21. France from Channel to Sea

1Western Europe’s biggest country is France. Belgium and Luxembourg lie to the northeast. Germany, Switzerland, and Italy lie to the east. Spain lies to the south. The English Channel separates France from the northwest. Across that water are the United Kingdom and Ireland. The Atlantic Ocean lies to the west. The Mediterranean Sea lies to the southeast. France includes Corsica, the large island lying off the coast to the southeast. Monaco, a tiny country, sits on that same southeastern coast. A map of the region also shows Paris, Dijon, Limoges, and Bordeaux. It shows Bayonne, Toulouse, and Marseille. It shows the Bay of Biscay and the Gulf of Lion.

2The Louvre began as a fort. It opened as a museum in the year 1793. Paris, the capital, is famous for beautiful architecture. Art and historic landmarks add to that fame. Millions of visitors come each year. The Eiffel Tower is one stop, said EYE-ful. Notre Dame Cathedral is another, said no-truh DAHM. The Louvre art museum is another, said LOO-vruh. The Arc de Triomphe stands over a main avenue. Say the arch as ARK du tree-OHMF. That avenue is the Champs-Élysées. Pronounce it SHAHM-zay, then lee-ZAY. A river called the Seine runs through the city. Say Seine as SEN. Parks open to the public are easy to find. Gardens are easy to find too. Residents of Paris number more than ten million. Business centers on this city. Culture centers on it too. Its reputation includes museums. It includes halls for concerts. It includes restaurants of high quality. Writers have lived here to use those chances to learn and to create. Artists have lived here for the same reason. Fashion is another fame. Huge shows each year display new clothing styles. July brings the Tour de France, and the race lasts three weeks. It is the hardest bicycle race on Earth. Crowds line the streets. The route crosses French land for thousands of miles. No yearly sports event in the country is larger. A trophy marks the race. People in the country number more than sixty-six million. Most of them live in urban areas. Service industries employ about 80 percent of workers. Tourism is by far the biggest of those industries. No country on Earth receives more visitors. Yearly tourists number more than eighty-two million. Factories employ others and turn out cars. They turn out machinery. They turn out aircraft. They turn out electronics. They turn out clothing and other products. Some plants make chemicals. Some process food. A large mining industry supplies jobs too. Daily life often moves at a slower pace than it does for Americans. Leisure time matters so much that the government acted in 2000. Weekly work for employees fell from 39 hours to 35. Paid vacation must last at least five weeks each year.

3Only about 4 percent of all workers farm the land. Farm output is still large. More than half the land is used for farming. Most farmers grow wheat. They grow corn. They grow other grains. Sugar beets are grown. Potatoes are grown. Dairy cattle are raised for cheese and for other dairy foods. Orchards in some regions grow apples. They grow pears. They grow peaches. Vineyards grow grapes for wine. Many kinds of wine make the country famous. Each kind of wine starts with its own kind of grape. A long history brought many groups to settle here. Ancestry is mixed. Celts are part of that mix. Romans are part of it. Germans are part of it. Vikings are part of it. North Africans are part of it. Most people are Christians. The Roman Catholic Church claims about three in every five people. Protestant churches claim some of the other Christians. A smaller share follows Islam. A smaller share follows Judaism. French is the main language. Several areas use different dialects. Landform maps show the English Channel and the Seine River. They show Paris and the Loire Valley. They show the Brittany Peninsula and the Paris Basin. They show the Loire River and the Jura Mountains. They show the Bay of Biscay and Mont Blanc. They show the Massif Central and the French Alps. They show the Ligurian Sea and the Cote d’Azur. They show the Pyrenees, the Gulf of Lion, and Corsica. They show the Mediterranean Sea. Hills, plains, rugged plateaus, and towering mountains all appear. Beaches appear. Massifs, which are older mountain blocks, appear too. Most of the land is still level plain or gentle hill. Several river systems flow through the land. The Seine runs through Paris. Together with smaller rivers, it drains the Paris Basin. That basin is the largest lowland. The Loire is the longest river. It flows 1,020 kilometers, or 634 miles, and empties into the Atlantic.

4Biking and hiking are popular in the Massif Central. Five mountain ranges rise in the country. Borders are where most of the ranges stand, and they wall the country off from its neighbors. Europe’s biggest mountain chain, the Alps, sends a section along the Swiss border. That section also follows the border with Italy. Mont Blanc is the highest peak, and it stands in the French Alps. The Pyrenees, said PEER-uh-neez, follow the border with Spain. The Massif Central is a wide, rugged plateau. Its area is about 86,000 square kilometers. That area is 33,200 square miles. An ancient mountain chain lies inside it. Extinct volcanoes are there. Deep river gorges are there. Beautiful beaches line the coasts, especially beside the Mediterranean Sea. Northern coasts face the English Channel with impressive bluffs of white chalk. Ancient France was called Gaul. Celts was the name for those people. Gaul entered the Roman Empire around the year 50 bc. Roman rule lasted nearly five hundred years. Latin spread through Gaul under the Romans. Many Celts took up Roman customs. They took up Roman ideas. By ad 400, Rome was quickly losing its grip on its regions. Neighboring tribes invaded Gaul. The Germanic Franks were among them. Around 486, the Franks took Gaul. They called it Regnum Francorum. In Latin, that name means kingdom of the Franks. The short name was Francia. Over time, Francia became France. Frankish kings of several dynasties ruled for hundreds of years. Clovis was the first king. He followed the Christian religion. He and his followers brought that religion into the land. Charles Martel, a king, halted an invasion in 732. Muslim armies were the invaders. Spain was where they came from. The first emperor, in the year 800, was Charles the Great. He is also called Charlemagne. Say his name SHAR-luh-mayn. He died in 814. Soon after his death, the Holy Roman Empire broke up.

5The tenth century brought a new settlement. A small area in the north, Normandy, was opened to Viking settlers. England fell to the Normans in the year 1066. William the Conqueror led them. Rivalry with England, and wars with England, then lasted for hundreds of years. By 1453, English forces had been pushed out. From the fifteenth century to the eighteenth, kings tried to grow the country’s power. They tried to grow their own power too. Those kings lived lavishly. Money went to palaces. It went to art schools. It went to other buildings. The people rebelled in 1789. That rebellion was the start of the French Revolution. A republic was declared in 1792. Turmoil filled the hundred years that followed. Rule passed at times to men who governed as dictators. Two more leaders were overthrown. A new government was set up each time. A republic governs the country today, and the democracy is strong. Among famous kings, Louis XIV stands out. His rule ran from 1643 to 1715. No king in Europe has ruled longer. His palace of Versailles, said vehr-SEYE, stands near Paris. Tourists visit it often. Holidays are many. A large share are tied to Christianity and to the Roman Catholic Church. Many people mark Fête des Rois on the Sunday that follows New Year’s Day. Say it FET DAY RWAH. The English name is Three Kings’ Day. Bakeries sell galettes, said gah-LETS. These pastries are round and flat. A paste of butter and almonds fills them. A small figurine is baked inside. A cardboard crown comes with the pastry. The person who finds the figurine wears the crown for that day. Bastille Day is another popular holiday. It falls on July 14. The day honors the start of the French Revolution. On that date in 1789, citizens stormed the Bastille. The Bastille was a prison in Paris. Prisoners were set free. The building was taken over. Freedom today is marked with parades. It is marked with speeches. It is marked with fireworks.

6Shopkeepers and outdoor vendors sell a wide variety of cheeses. Some regions make cheeses that are theirs alone. Food and cooking styles have made the country famous. Many people here count good food among life's greatest pleasures. Haute cuisine is a cooking style that has spread far beyond France. Say it OHT kwih-ZEEN. The words mean high cooking. Rich dishes in this style use butter. They use cream. They use eggs. They use spices. Delicious meals that are low in fat are cooked as well. Fresh ingredients go into those meals. Meat can be bought at a butcher, a boucherie, said boosh-REE. Bread can be bought at a bakery, a boulangerie, said boo-LAHN-zhuh-REE. The loaves are long and thin, and they are called baguettes. Crepes, pancakes rolled very thin, are sometimes served as dessert. Tarts, small pies of fruit or of eggs, are sometimes served as dessert too. Part of Normandy, in the north, is called the Alabaster Coast. White cliffs give it that name. France today is modern, and its history is long and fascinating. Invasions and warfare show in the ancestry of the people. Good food, art, and other leisure fill many lives. Architecture, art, food, and culture are known around the world. Rolling hills, plains, and mountains hold beautiful cities. They hold villages. They hold castles. They hold vineyards. Landmarks and a rich history help explain the crowds. No country is visited more.

7Population is 66,259,012. Land area is 620,427 square kilometers. The same area is 239,548 square miles. The capital is Paris. The primary language is French. The primary religion is Roman Catholic. The currency is the euro, said YUR-oh. Figures come from the Central Intelligence Agency in July 2014 and from the World Bank. Architecture means the design of buildings and the work of putting them up. A gorge is a long, deep valley with higher land around it. A basin is a low area in Earth’s surface. A massif is a group of older mountains that together make one large shape. A cathedral is a large church. Tourism is the business of serving people who travel somewhere for pleasure. Celts were people of ancient Britain and of Western Europe. Vikings were Scandinavian explorers, traders, and pirates. Between the eighth century and the eleventh, raids and settlements spread across much of Europe’s northwest. A dialect is a form of language used in a certain region or by a certain group. A dynasty is a line of rulers from one family. A vineyard is a field where grapes are grown. Fashion means clothing styles that are popular or current.

22. Frederick Douglass Wins His Freedom

1This book begins with bread traded for lessons. It tells how Frederick Douglass was born into slavery. It tells how he escaped. It tells how he wrote and spoke. It tells how he worked for a land of the free. Word meanings come last. Eight-year-old Frederick Douglass took a loaf of bread from the kitchen. He slipped out the back door. He was going to run errands. He was enslaved. Even so, bread was his to take in any amount he wanted. His meals were fuller than those of some nearby boys. He took food so he could trade with them. He had plenty of bread. What he wanted even more was something those boys had. They knew how to write. He walked the streets of Baltimore, Maryland. He thought about how unjust his life was. He was not allowed to read. Just the day before, a newspaper had been snatched away from him. Mrs. Sophia Auld had done it. She was his enslaver's wife. He had been trying to read the paper in secret. All he wanted was an education. Enslaved people were not allowed that in the 1820s. The law itself forbade it. Poor white boys in the city became his friends for that reason. Those boys had been allowed to attend school. At first he would tease them. He would bet that he knew more of the alphabet than they did. He would write a few letters in the dirt. Those boys then wrote the letters they already knew. Then he would learn to write those new letters. Food was now his trade for real lessons. The lessons were in reading. They were in writing.

2Up ahead, one of his friends was waiting to give him a lesson. Frederick hurried to join him. He would give up his lunch every day in order to learn. Frederick was born in February. The year was 1817 or 1818. His birthday was a date he never learned. His father, he knew, was a white man. He never knew who that man was. His mother was Harriet Bailey. His grandmother raised him. Her name was Betsey Bailey. When he was eight, he was sent away to work. He would be a houseboy. The work was for relatives of his enslaver. They were the Auld family. They lived in Baltimore, Maryland. Sophia Auld gave him help at the start of his time with the family. She taught him the alphabet. She gave him several lessons. Then her husband forbade her to continue. He said education and slavery were incompatible. Reading would leave an enslaved person unhappy with that condition. That person would desire freedom.

3The warning from her husband proved true. The more books Frederick read, the clearer his own thoughts became. Newspapers did the same. He came to despise slavery more. He grew more dejected. For some time he struggled with one word. The word was abolition. When an enslaved person ran away, people blamed abolition. When an enslaved person did something an enslaver disliked, people blamed abolition too. Frederick tried to look the word up in the dictionary. The dictionary only said it was the act of abolishing. He still asked what was being abolished. In 1831 he read an article about the abolition movement. The article was about people striving to end slavery. Now he understood. Ending slavery was a goal of some white people as well. Freedom for Black people, he learned, existed in some states. He began to dream of escape. Later in life he wrote about reading. He said that learning to read would leave a person forever free. He saw education and freedom as partners. Other enslaved people started getting lessons from him. Enslavers stopped his Sunday school. Among enslaved people he earned a reputation as a leader. Among enslavers he earned a reputation as a troublemaker. For years he had lived in the city. Farm work was where he was sent at age fifteen. He would be a field hand. The man he worked for was Edward Covey. Covey was brutal. He beat enslaved people into obedience. Loaning Frederick to Covey may have been a punishment from Auld. Frederick had tried to educate other enslaved people. He had tried to educate himself. His spirit nearly broke under Covey. The abuse lasted six months. Then sixteen-year-old Frederick fought back. Later he wrote about the fight. They were at it for nearly two hours. At length Covey let him go. Covey was puffing and blowing at a great rate. Covey said that if Frederick had not resisted, the whipping would not have been half so much. The truth was different. Covey had not whipped him at all. Serious escape plans started after that fight. He tried once. He was caught. Still he dreamed of freedom. He kept working on his literacy. He even joined a debate club. It was called the East Baltimore Mental Improvement Society. There he met Anna Murray. She was a free Black woman.

4Anna and Frederick planned his escape together. She sent him her savings. He could buy a train ticket with the money. She sewed him a sailor's uniform. He dressed in that uniform. He carried a freedman's passport. He traveled through Delaware. He traveled through Pennsylvania. He reached New York. Less than a full day was all the escape required. Later he wrote about that day. One free day, he said, held more living than a year in slavery. Years later he spoke to audiences. He said he stood before them as a thief and a robber. He said he had stolen his head from his master. He had stolen his limbs. He had stolen his body. Then he had run off with them. Escaping did not mean he was truly free. An enslaved person could reach a free state. That person could live as if free. Even so, the person could still be seized. The person could be taken back to a slave state. Anna was called to join him. They were married in 1838. He was a fugitive. Capture could send him back into slavery. They took a new name. The name was Douglass. The newly wed pair settled in New Bedford, Massachusetts. They had five children together. In the 1800s, abolitionists organized a secret network. The network helped escapees. It came to be called the Underground Railroad. Places with food were called safe houses or stations. Those places also had clothing and shelter. Many fugitives traveled on foot. The people who helped them were known as conductors or stationmasters. Their aim was a station at each fifteen-mile gap. That distance is 24 kilometers. Safe houses sheltered him right after the escape. Later he himself became a stationmaster. That work was in Rochester, New York. Fugitives got help from the pair on the way to Canada. A picture shows a safe house in Ripley, Ohio.

5Antislavery meetings became places where he spoke. Life under slavery was something he could describe from his own days. He could tell of his bold escape. The abolition movement gained a voice in him. His very existence answered an argument from enslavers. Their claim was that enslaved people did not have the intellect needed for free citizenship. His clear speech made some white people deny his story. They would not believe he had ever been enslaved. He wrote the first of three autobiographies. The title is Narrative of the Life of Frederick Douglass. It was published in 1845. Critics charged that the book was fake. They charged that Douglass was fake too. Eloquent writing, they argued, was beyond anyone who had been enslaved. Readers made the book a success right away. It persuaded many people of something important. A great mind could belong to a person who had been enslaved. Details in the book also put him in danger. Those details could have led to his arrest as a fugitive. To avoid capture, he went abroad for two years. He went on a speaking tour of Ireland. He spoke in England too. He was an electrifying speaker. He was a star overseas. Fans there raised the money for his freedom. That purchase is called manumission. He returned to the United States in 1848. He founded a newspaper. He also wrote thousands of speeches. He wrote editorials calling for social justice. He wrote that showing slavery in the open would destroy it. He called slavery one of those monsters of darkness. For such a monster, the light of truth is death. One of his most famous speeches has a long title. Its title asks what the Fourth of July is to the slave. He delivered it on July 5, 1852. He surprised his audience. He posed questions about Independence Day. He asked what that day meant for enslaved people. He asked what it meant for people once enslaved. He asked what he, or the people he represented, had to do with the nation's independence. He asked about the great principles in the Declaration of Independence. Those principles are political freedom and natural justice. He asked whether they were extended to the people he represented.

6When the Civil War came, no Black man in the United States was admired more. In 1863 he served as an advisor to President Lincoln. The work came during the drafting of the Emancipation Proclamation. That order freed the majority of enslaved people. Ending slavery was not a stated goal of the war. Again and again he pressed Lincoln to add that goal. Some historians think Douglass helped inspire a famous speech. That speech is the Gettysburg Address. They think he also helped inspire a Lincoln inaugural address. Douglass convinced Lincoln of another step. Black soldiers should be allowed to fight for the North. The Union Army's 54th Massachusetts Regiment was the first made up of Black soldiers. Two of Douglass's sons were in it. Guards from the 107th United States Colored Troops pose in a picture. They pose during the Civil War. Lincoln and Douglass did not always agree. Douglass was the keynote speaker at a memorial once Lincoln had died. The memorial honored Lincoln. People stood and cheered him. The president's widow gave him a gift. Her name was Mary Todd Lincoln. The gift was Lincoln's favorite walking stick. She gave it in appreciation. Douglass's house still keeps the walking stick inside. His writing helped end slavery. His speaking helped too. The end came with the Thirteenth Amendment. Passage came once the Civil War was over in 1865. Citizenship came with the Fourteenth Amendment three years after that. The citizenship went to people who had once been enslaved. The Fifteenth Amendment passed soon after, in 1870. It granted every male citizen the right to vote. Men who had once been enslaved were included. For the rest of his life, Douglass kept promoting equality for all Americans. Reaching its potential was what he wanted for the United States. For Black people, he wanted the country to live up to freedom. He wanted that for women. He wanted that for Native Americans. He wanted that for immigrants too. He said he would unite with anybody to do right. He would unite with nobody to do wrong.

7Douglass did not live to see universal suffrage. Universal suffrage is the right of every adult to vote. The fight continued until his death. States approved the Nineteenth Amendment in 1920. That amendment granted women the right to vote. He remained married to Anna for forty-four years. She passed away in 1882. Helen Pitts became his wife two years after that. She was an advocate for civil rights. Their marriage caused controversy. Pitts was a white woman. Douglass asked a question in a letter to a friend. He asked what business the world had with the color of his wife. Today people often call him the father of the civil rights movement. Seventy years after his death, that movement was at its height. An honor came from the United States Postal Service. He received his own postage stamp in 1965. Slavery was something the country thought about differently because of him. He changed the way it thought about race. He left behind words that still inspire Americans. His newspaper's motto was one of those lines. It said that right belongs to no single sex. It said that truth belongs to no single color. A picture shows him in the 1870s. These words have special meanings in this book. Abolition is the act of doing away with something or ending it. It can mean the act of making slavery illegal. A controversy is a disagreement over an idea or an issue. Editorials are articles in a newspaper or a magazine. They express the opinion of the publisher. An advocate is a person who actively supports a cause. A fugitive is a person running from something. The person is usually running from legal authorities. An amendment is a change or an addition to a document or a law. Articulate means able to communicate clearly and effectively by speaking or writing. It can also describe something that was clearly communicated. An inaugural address is a formal speech. A president gives it at the beginning of a term. Autobiographies are true stories about a person's life. That person writes them. Literacy is the ability to read and write. Principles are basic values or ideals. They guide an action or a decision. Civil rights are legal, social, and economic rights. They guarantee freedom and equality for all citizens.

23. From Cassius Clay to Muhammad Ali

1In 1963, Cassius Clay lets a punch land from a British boy named Patrick Power. Patrick is six. He is taking boxing lessons so he can defend himself against bullies. That photo opens the story of the same boxer. This book begins when he becomes a new champion. It then looks back at his childhood. It follows his hunt for Olympic gold. It tells why he refused to fight in a war. It tells of his comeback and of two astonishing matches. It ends with the years after he left the ring. Cassius Clay and Sonny Liston trade words before a title fight. The scene is the weigh-in. The place is Miami Beach, Florida. The date is February 24, 1964. In 1964, Sonny Liston was the heavyweight boxing champ, and he was ready to defend that title. Some boxers were afraid to meet Liston because of how tough he was. Almost nobody thought the newest challenger would win. A typical boxer planted his feet and swung with power. Cassius Clay did not. He danced around the ring and threw quick jabs. Most boxers let their fists speak. Clay made up sharp insults instead. One insult was a poem about Liston. The poem said a punch would send Liston off the planet like a person launched into space. Fans who came to the fight, the poem joked, would see that launch.

2Fans thought Clay was only talk. The insults were a plan. Anger was the goal. An angry Liston would slip up during the bout. Clay's quick style let him slip Liston's hardest swings. Quick jabs wore Liston down. Liston took punch after punch for six rounds. Then Liston quit. Cassius Clay was now the world heavyweight champion. His story was only starting. Soon he would take the name Muhammad Ali. History would remember that name. Clay's height was 6 feet and 3 inches. In meters, that height is 1.9. He weighed 215 pounds. In kilograms, that weight is 97.5. Even so, his speed was so fast that opponents struggled to hit him. The baby born in 1942 was named Cassius Clay. That child was Ali. The place was Louisville, Kentucky. His family was middle class. Even so, the Clays faced segregation every day. In that time, many restaurants in the South would not serve Black people. Many stores refused them too. Some movie theaters barred Black Americans. If a theater let them in, the balcony was their only seat. Black Americans who claimed equality, or who claimed the right to run their own lives, were often attacked. Angry crowds of white people carried out that violence. Cassius Clay started boxing at age twelve. A picture shows him as a boy boxer. A more personal hurt pushed him toward the sport. At twelve, someone stole his bike. Joe Martin was a police officer in the neighborhood. Cassius told him he wanted to thrash the person who took the bike. Martin told him to fight in a boxing ring instead. Martin became his coach. Training started, and before that year ended he had a win. The Golden Gloves had a novice group. He won it when he was fourteen. That contest was the country's best-known amateur boxing tournament. His school put on boxing matches. He was so sure of himself in the ring that the principal began introducing him in a special way. The principal called him the next champion of the heavyweight world.

3Clay was fast becoming America's best amateur boxer. The Golden Gloves championship was his at age seventeen. He also won the national title of the Amateur Athletic Union. The next year he fought in another country for the first time. Rome, Italy, hosted him in 1960 at the Olympic Games. He was eighteen. He had never been on a plane. He was so afraid that he packed a parachute. In the ring he was not afraid. He won the gold medal. A picture shows him fighting a Polish boxer at those Summer Games. After Rome, Clay fought for pay, not only for medals. Win followed win. He began to mock his opponents the way pro wrestlers did. He invented rhymes that bragged about a coming win. Jim Robinson was the next opponent. Clay predicted the night would be very short. He said Robinson would be finished, and that the end would come in round one. He was right. Clay knocked Robinson out ninety seconds after the first round began. Nineteen wins came one after another. Then he beat Liston and took the title. Two years before that title, Clay had watched Malcolm X speak about Islam. Clay left impressed. The two became friends. Malcolm guided Clay as a mentor while Clay explored a new faith. Clay also took up the civil rights movement. Winning the heavyweight championship made his name the best known in the sport. He no longer wanted people to call him by it. After the Liston fight, he converted to Islam. He took the name Muhammad Ali to honor Islam's prophet. He told reporters that Cassius Clay was a name tied to slavery. He said he had not chosen it. He said he did not want it. He said Muhammad Ali was a free name. He said the name means beloved of God. He said people must use it when they speak to him and when they speak about him. The young boxer's thinking was deeply shaped by two civil rights leaders. One was Martin Luther King Jr. The other was Malcolm X.

4Ali talks with reporters before his trial. Young men began to be drafted by the United States. The time was the middle of the 1960s. Vietnam, in Asia, was the country at war. When the draft called Ali's name, he would not go. He knew that refusal broke the law. He objected on religious grounds. His new religion, he said, taught against war. He also said this country did not treat him as a citizen with equal standing, so he should not fight for it. Racism had marked his whole life. He was ready to oppose it. He asked reporters a hard question. Why, he asked, should he drop bombs and fire bullets at brown people. At the same time, he said, Black people back in Louisville were treated terribly. In that question he used the word Negro. For much of the twentieth century, that word meant people of African heritage. Since the late 1960s, people have treated the word as offensive. Black and African American have mostly replaced it. On May 10, 1967, Muhammad Ali spoke at an anti-war rally. The place was the University of Chicago. Speaking against the Vietnam War made him a hero to many people. Speaking against racism in America did too. Boxing's leaders disliked controversy from the fighter everyone knew best. Officials removed his heavyweight title on the arrest day. The arrest was for draft refusal. They also banned him from boxing. While he was out of the sport, he spoke at colleges. He talked about the war and about the civil rights movement. People came to know his eloquent moral arguments as well as they knew his boxing. Conscience mattered more to him than keeping his career. Even some who disagreed came to respect that choice.

5Ali kept defending his refusal as the case rose through higher courts. After three years, a judge made a ruling. Ali had to be allowed back into boxing. His case at the Supreme Court was still not settled. He fought two warm-up matches. Joe Frazier held the heavyweight title then. Ali challenged him. Frazier was tough and relentless. He had never lost a match. Promoters called Ali's title challenge the Fight of the Century. The fight matched the loud promotion. For Ali, it was the Liston fight turned around. His speed could not answer Frazier's strength. Frazier's strong left hook shook Ali and knocked him down several times. The judges gave the match to Frazier. It was the first pro fight Ali had ever lost. A picture shows Ali low on the canvas in the eleventh round. He had slipped. Joe Frazier was the opponent in that 1971 comeback. A bigger win arrived for Ali once three months had passed. Refusing the draft, the Supreme Court said, was Ali's right. The court called him a conscientious objector. Other Americans who refused war for religious reasons had that same claim. Their beliefs deserved respect, and so did his. His moral stand moved Americans of many kinds to stand up for what they believed. A 1976 movie called Rocky took its story from Chuck Wepner. Wepner was a low-ranked fighter. A 1975 title bout against Ali left Wepner shockingly near a win. Rocky's on-screen foe was the charming champion Apollo Creed. That character was based directly on Ali.

6Ali kept fighting for three years. He won every bout but one. Then he met Frazier again. Ali won. George Foreman already held the heavyweight title that Frazier had lost. Foreman was young and powerful. Like Ali, Foreman had won Olympic gold. Like Ali, he had become champion without a loss. The challenge happened in Zaire. That land is now called the Democratic Republic of the Congo. The fight was named the Rumble in the Jungle. Some people rank that bout first among sports events of the twentieth century. Ali tried a new plan. He leaned on the ropes and blocked. Foreman tired himself out by punching. The name he gave the move was rope-a-dope. He teased Foreman. People had told Ali, he said, that George knew how to punch. Foreman fell for the trap and wore himself out. Ali could then knock him down. Muhammad Ali was champ again. A picture shows him leaning back on the ropes to use the rope-a-dope. The opponent is George Foreman. The place is Zaire. The year is 1974. The next year, Frazier tried to take the title back. The fight was in the Philippines. People called it the Thrilla in Manila. Ali used the rope-a-dope again. Frazier was the one who drained Ali's energy on this night. The fight was brutal. Ali said it brought him nearer to dying than he had ever been. Frazier said the punches he landed could have knocked a building down. In the fourteenth round, Ali used the last of his strength. He hit Frazier with a barrage of punches. Frazier quit. The towel came in. Both men were badly beaten. Ali later said they had arrived in Manila as champions and left as old men. Two and a half more years passed with Ali still the champion. Leon Spinks was the fighter who took it. A few months later he fought Spinks again and won. No other boxer has taken the heavyweight crown three times. He did not fight again for two years. When he returned, Larry Holmes took the title for good. Ali was thirty-eight. His long ring career was over. He won the rematch with Leon Spinks in 1978. That bout lasted fifteen rounds.

7After he left boxing, Ali traveled the world and spoke up for human rights. In 1984, doctors diagnosed Parkinson's. Muscles become hard for the brain to direct. As his health fell, speech grew hard for Ali, who had been eloquent. The Olympic Games saw him again in 1996. His job was to light the Olympic flame. The city was Atlanta, Georgia. Lifting the torch was clearly a struggle. The crowd cheered. He held the flame high. He was a fighter to the end. A picture shows him with the torch before he lights the flame at the Atlanta opening ceremony in 1996. In 2016, Muhammad Ali died. He was seventy-four. The champ who had once caused argument was now loved almost everywhere. President Obama praised his activism. Obama said Ali refused to give up his stand. Obama said that win helped people accept the America they know today. People can call him the greatest man to box, and still miss the rest. Belief drove him, and that struggle helped reshape the nation. Ali once said that he himself was America. He said he was the part people would not recognize. He told them to get used to him. He called himself Black, confident, and cocky. He said the name was his, not theirs. He said the religion was his, not theirs. He said the goals were his own.

24. From Field Songs to a Music the World Heard

1This book begins with the roots of the blues. One part visits Dockery Farms. One part meets Charlie Patton. One part meets the first women who recorded the blues. The music then goes north. After that the music goes overseas. A rich history closes the book. Word meanings close it too. The year was 1903. Tutwiler is in Mississippi. It had a train station. William Christopher Handy sat there. People knew him as WC Handy. He was a Black composer. He was a musician too. His train was late. He planned a nap while he waited. A man sat to his left. All at once that man played a guitar. The guitar was old and battered. A jackknife was the tool he played with. The sound was strange. The sound amazed Handy. It brought back old work songs. It brought back field hollers from his younger years. Those hollers grew from spoken music in West Africa. The work songs grew from that same tradition. Many enslaved people carried to America had been taken from that region. Workers in the fields made the songs up as they sang. They called across long distances. They hollered across long distances. A leader sang the opening line. The other workers answered. A strong beat came from that pattern. The beat set the pace of hard labor done again and again. The beat also helped the hours pass. Later, people widely called the pattern call and response. Historians see many likenesses between two old sounds. One is the Islamic call to prayer. The other is the field holler of the 1800s. The likeness is hardly a surprise. An estimated 30 percent of African people forced into slavery in the United States were Muslim. That forced movement ran from the 1600s into the middle of the 1800s. Handy had not planned to hear music that day. He had only planned to rest. The late train gave him time. The man with the jackknife filled that time with a sound Handy could not forget. The sound was rough. It was also full of feeling. It did not sound like the dance music Handy already knew. It sounded like people calling to one another in open fields. Handy sat and listened. The nap never came. The strange guitar stayed in his mind. Years of field songs sat in his mind too. This was the start of the story this book will tell.

2Call and response was used during hard hand labor. Enslaved people used it. Sharecroppers used it. Prison work crews used it. No known recording of a field holler comes from before the 1930s. Even so, some early blues records clearly grow from that tradition. Back home, Handy placed this unusual structure into dance music. He placed it into songs of his own. The year 1914 is when he published Saint Louis Blues. He took the name Father of the Blues. Historians of the blues think the Tutwiler guitarist may have been Henry Sloan. Sloan sharecropped at Dockery Farms, near Dockery, Mississippi. Some historians also think the blues we know began at Dockery Farms. The name blues likely comes from blue devils. Blue devils was old slang for low spirits or sadness. Many players say the blues is a feeling. They do not explain it with hard music words. A picture shows a Black sharecropper in Little Rock, Arkansas. The day is a Sunday in 1935, and he is resting from work. In the late 1800s and the early 1900s, most of Mississippi was rural. Music mattered deeply in that culture. It mattered especially on Delta farms and plantations. Dockery was such a place. The Seed House at Dockery Farms belongs to the old plantation there. William Alfred Dockery founded it in 1895. People called him Will. Land beside the Sunflower River was cleared so cotton could grow. Black laborers came looking for jobs. Some were sharecroppers. A sharecropper lived and worked on a piece of land. The pay was a share of the crop. The labor was hard. Dockery Farms had a good name. Workers arrived from every part of the South. Henry Sloan was one of them. Little is known about him. He is believed to have reached Dockery in the early 1900s. Other people there soon noticed a singing style that was not ordinary. They noticed guitar skill that amazed them. Charlie Patton was among those listeners. The farm was a place of long days and shared songs. People worked, rested, and listened. A new kind of music was taking shape there.

3Charlie Patton and Son House were two early makers of Delta blues who mattered. The Patton family arrived at Dockery Farms around the turn of the century. At ten, Charlie already played in a local ragtime band. Restlessness came quickly. He wanted music of other kinds. Meeting Sloan led to guitar lessons. Soon Charlie played next to Sloan at parties. Dockery often gave those parties for sharecroppers on the land. Sloan left by 1910. Patton kept Dockery as a home base. On his own, he became a popular solo player. He began to guide other local blues players. Robert Johnson was one. Son House was another. Their music would later be called Mississippi Delta blues. From 1929 until he died, Patton made nearly seventy recordings. Death came in 1934. He was forty-three. The year 1920 made blues history. Crazy Blues, the first vocal blues song, was recorded and put out. The singer made it remarkable for that time. The singer was a woman. Mamie Smith pioneered recorded blues. She sang in vaudeville, acted, danced, and played piano. Her voice was unusual. It had more power and more drive than the mild ballad singers common then. In month one, sales of Crazy Blues passed 75,000 copies. Six months brought sales of a million copies. That success earned her the name Americas First Lady of the Blues. Other labels rushed to sign Black women who sang blues. Harry Pace had been Handys business partner. In 1921 he started Black Swan. Black artists made the records, and a Black audience was the aim. Ethel Waters scored a hit with Down Home Blues. Bigger companies took notice. Competition grew too heavy. Black Swan had to shut in 1923. Those two short years produced 180 records. Doors opened for Black entertainers everywhere. A woman had led the way onto records. Men would follow later. For now, her strong voice filled homes that had never heard blues on a disc. Listeners bought the song again and again. The blues was no longer only a sound in a field or at a party. It was a record people could play.

4Three music companies turned Bessie Smith away. Columbia Records signed her in 1923. She recorded over 160 songs. In the 1920s she was the highest paid Black artist. That was true among men and among women. She was the most popular woman singing blues in the 1920s and the 1930s. By late 1921 the blues was a sensation from coast to coast. The next few years brought hundreds of new recordings. Women made a number of those records. Bessie Smith stood among the most popular of them. Her performances were emotional and intense in a way of her own. Fans who adored her called her The Empress of the Blues. Ma Raineys voice was deep and earthy. People called her the Mother of the Blues. She came along the path Smith had opened. She already had a large following. Gold teeth and flashy jewelry marked her style. Her songs spoke of heartbreak and of depression. She reached listeners in a deeply personal way. Many women singers who followed took her as a model. Victoria Spivey rose in this time. Ida Cox rose too. A classic boom in womens blues had arrived. By 1928 those careers were fading. Men playing guitar alone were growing more popular. Then the late 1920s and the early 1930s brought the Great Depression. Jobs were scarce. People waited in lines simply to get food. Blues was still being made. Buyers often had no money left. Sales of records dropped. Some companies that made records failed. The Depression also pushed many blues players north. Work was what they hoped to find. Hard times did not stop the songs. They did make it harder to sell them. A singer could still fill a room. A company could still fail. The women who had led the boom watched the crowd change. A man with one guitar now drew the loudest cheers. The road north began to look like the next chance.

5From 1916 to 1970, American history marks the Great Migration. More than six million Black people left the rural South. Racism, common there, was a reason many left. Cities in the Northeast, the Midwest, and the West took them in. They wanted better pay and better schooling. BB King grew famous for an electric blues sound. Delta players followed their listeners quickly. Electric power usually reached cities before it reached other places. Acoustic country blues began to give way to electrified city blues. City blues used loud amplification and electric guitars. The intense new sound became the main style for fresh pioneers such as BB King. Audiences went wild. Delta players slid a knife, a broken bottleneck, a brass ring, or polished bone along the strings. People called that way of playing slide guitar. A picture shows a musician using slide guitar. Hot spots for electric blues grew in Memphis, Detroit, Saint Louis, and Dallas. Chicago was the hottest spot. Higher pay pulled Delta players there. A direct train from Mississippi pulled them too. Chess Records formed in Chicago in 1950. Howlin Wolf and Muddy Waters, who would later be stars, found a home there. Delta blues then traveled across the United States. The style grew popular. A guitar could now be loud enough for a crowded city room. A player did not have to shout over the noise alone. The slide still cried like an old field song. The beat was harder and faster in the new towns. People who had left the South could hear home in that sound. They could also hear the city. The music had moved with them. It had changed on the way. Chicago gave many of them a stage, a wage, and a record company ready to listen.

6Younger artists at Chess Records changed the music in their own ways. Chuck Berry was one. Bo Diddley was another. They made tempos faster. They pulled in country and western music, and they pulled in swing. Then a huge wave of white rock and roll players recorded blues again. They recorded other popular Black music again. The form used a twelve bar progression. It used the classic scale of three chords from the blues. Rock and roll was built on that form. In 1958 Chess Records issued a run of albums by Muddy Waters and by Little Walter. The albums sold so well that Chess issued older songs once more. Howlin Wolf and John Lee Hooker were among those older artists. The records then crossed the ocean to the United Kingdom. Other parts of Europe heard them too. Nothing like that sound had been heard there. Muddy Waters toured England in 1958. The tour helped start a British craze for rhythm and blues. Rhythm and blues was dance music, and the beat was big. Young musicians in England formed bands of their own. The Rolling Stones was one name. The Yardbirds was another. Like Charlie Patton years earlier, they rejected the cleaned up pop music around them. They used the blues to say what was true for them. Early rhythm and blues by the Rolling Stones borrowed heavily from greats of the blues. Muddy Waters was one of those greats. A song born in the Delta could now fill a hall in England. Young players studied the old records. They learned the chords. They sped the beat for dancing. The blues had left one country and found new hands. Those hands did not replace the first players. They carried the sound farther. Fans who had never seen the South still felt the songs.

7In the middle of the 1960s, some players mixed blues with rock. The mix was called blues rock. A wider audience heard it than ever before. It helped form hard rock. It helped form Southern rock. It helped form heavy metal too. The history of the blues is rich. It runs deep in the center of American musical culture. From the start, and above all, the blues is a Black American art. It keeps changing. Music lovers around the world still draw inspiration from it. After midcentury, and on through the rest of that century, the blues clearly shaped popular music. Players and singers in many kinds of music still feel that pull. Radio spread widely during the 1920s. That spread carried Mamie Smith and her band across the United States and past its borders. Here are word meanings for this book. To popularize something is to make it liked, accepted, or widely known. Amplification means making an effect, or a strength, larger. Prevalent means more noticeable, more important, or happening often. Crews are groups of people working together. Released means made available, or issued for use. Enslaved means kept in slavery, owned, or forced to work for another person. Restless means unable to rest because of boredom or worry. Foundational means serving as a base or a support. Tempos are the speeds of a piece of music. Uprooted means forced to leave home. To influence is to affect people or things. Innovative means making something new and original. The blues began in hard work and shared song. It moved to records, to cities, and then across the sea. It is still a living music. New players still learn it. Old songs still matter. That is the rich history this book has followed from the field to the wide world.

25. Gems: Treasures from the Earth

1Many people think gems are Earth's most beautiful creation. They will spend thousands of dollars for even a small bit of that beauty. People wear sparkling gems on their fingers, necks, and wrists. Families pass gems down through generations. Gems can be found on royal crowns and on sacred religious objects. Many gems decorate the most beautiful and valuable jewelry.

2A gem is any beautiful stone that can be used in jewelry. Most gems sparkle or show beautiful colors, and they are hard enough for daily wear. Crystals, stones, and organic gems are the three major types. Crystals and stones are made of minerals, the natural nonliving substances in rocks. Crystals are very pure minerals with tight shapes, but stones are mixed and lack strict shapes. Organic gems come from living things, such as a natural pearl. Garnet is a crystal, and turquoise is a stone. In crystals, tiny atoms form neat patterns and flat faces, such as cubes or six-sided columns.

3Diamonds form deep inside Earth's mantle. Heat, magma, and pressure from gravity act in the crust and mantle. Volcanic eruptions bring diamonds to the surface. Diamonds form when rocks squeeze a mineral and force the atoms into the smallest shape. Sapphires form when a mineral melts from heat and slowly cools into a pattern. Opals form when minerals dissolve in water and the water evaporates very slowly. Rock salt, called halite, forms when salty seas evaporate. Dissolve salt in warm water, add a clean toothpick, and let crystals grow in the sun.

4Stones do not form orderly patterns, and other minerals may be mixed in. Layers called grain make streaks and lines that give stones beautiful surfaces. Pearls, amber, and coral are organic gems from living things. An oyster covers a trapped worm or shell bit with smooth nacre. Oysters shoot sand out with jets of water, so pearls do not begin as sand. Coral is polyp skeletons, and amber is ancient sap that may hold insects and spiders. Miners often chip rock by hand, and rarer gems are more valuable.

5A newly found gem is rough, dull, and bumpy, unlike a gem in a ring or necklace. It may have cracks, dark marks, bubbles, and other flaws. Lapidaries cut regular shapes that show the best parts and hide flaws. Cutters once used diamond-edged saws, and recently they began using lasers. Facets are flat surfaces that show color, pattern, and reflected sparkle. Diamonds often get a brilliant cut, while deep green emeralds often get a step cut. The rose cut has a rounded top and triangular facets, and it is common on older gems. The oldest cut, the cabochon, is rounded for opaque stones, while jade and coral may be carved.

6Gems are valuable because they are beautiful and rare, and prices change over time. Some gemstones have been called precious through the ages and in many cultures. Diamonds grew more valuable in the last century, even after large finds in South Africa. Diamond engagement rings and global marketing by sellers helped raise those prices. One gem's value depends on hardness and the four Cs of cut, carat, color, and clarity. A poor cut looks dull, and one carat weighs about the same as an unpopped popcorn kernel. Unlike rocks, gems have bright pure colors, and one different atom in a thousand can change a color. Clarity means few flaws, but a rare flawed emerald can be worth more than a flawless diamond.

7Hardness shows how pure and tight a mineral is, and how well a gem stands daily wear. Friedrich Mohs scratched one gem with another, because a gem can scratch only softer gems. Talc is 1 and can scratch nothing, while diamond is 10 and can scratch anything. Gypsum, calcite, fluorite, apatite, orthoclase, quartz, topaz, and corundum fall between them in that order. A fingernail is 2.5, a penknife is 5.5, and most gems must be 5 or above for jewelry. Diamonds form 140 to 190 kilometers down, and magma lifts them in volcanic pipes. They may be three billion years old, and a few were left by melting glaciers. Most are nearly colorless, and they are found in Russia, Canada, Botswana, Angola, and South Africa.

8The Hope diamond is famous for its intense sky-blue color, not for its size. Theft and ownership by royalty and wealthy people gave it a reputation for being cursed. The Cullinan of South Africa weighed over 3,100 carats and was as large as a pineapple. It was cut into nine gems, including the Greater Star of Africa at 530 carats. Graphite, or pencil lead, is the same mineral, but loose atoms rub off as a pencil mark. Rubies and sapphires are corundum, but only blood-red or pink stones count as rubies. Chromium makes that red, the name comes from Latin ruber, and other colors are sapphires. Titanium oxide can make an asterism, a bright star, in sought-after star rubies and sapphires.

9The quartz family is the most common of all crystals. Quartz is found all over the world in all colors of the rainbow. Most quartz is so common that nearly anyone can afford it. The largest quartz crystal was about 6 meters, or 20 feet, long. It weighed more than 44,000 kilograms, or 48 tons, more than a loaded eighteen-wheeled truck. Opal, a valuable quartz, shimmers with white, blue, and red-orange, mostly in Australia. Rare green emeralds were first found in Egypt thousands of years ago, and the finest come from Colombia. Beryl also forms aquamarine, while jade in many colors was carved in Asia into knives, swords, and axes.

10Blue-green turquoise from the deserts of Iran, Tibet, and the southwestern United States often has black spots. Much of the world's turquoise is set in silver, as the Navajo traditionally wore it. The Navajo believed turquoise was pieces of the sky that had fallen to Earth. Natural pearls are oddly shaped and rare, and an oyster may need two or more years to make one. Almost all round pearls in stores are cultured by people. Farmers insert a round shell bead, and nacre makes a round pearl in six months to several years. Cultured pearls come in white, pink, yellow, green, and even black. Gemologists are scientists who study chemicals to trace a gem's origin and spot synthetic gems.

11A strip mine clears an immense area of land. Beautiful gems are found around the world, and many stand for power and wealth. For centuries, people have killed and died for gems. In places such as West Africa and Colombia, the gem trade is still full of blood and violence. Mining with dynamite and strip mines is often dangerous to people and to Earth. Scientists can grow lab gems that match the finest natural gems. Those created gems can cost a thousand times less. In the future, they might meet our wish for beauty while protecting Earth and its people.

26. Giant Birds That Ride the Wind

1Air currents carry condors while they look for food. This book climbs from high flight, to hunting on the wind, to a taste for dead animals, to bare heads, to careful nesting, to people, to a last look, and then to words. High in the sky. A California condor soars at 15,000 feet, or 4,572 meters. Far below, it searches the ground for food. The wings stretch 10 feet, or 3.04 meters. Gliding looks effortless. Then something appears. It is the body of a dead rabbit. A slow drop begins. The bird weighs more than 30 pounds, or 13 kilograms. That weight puts it among the largest flying birds on Earth. Predators are little worry once it lands. Feeding on the dead animal starts at once. For many years, California skies showed very few of these birds. Extinction was close. Conservationists kept working. Government kept working. The giants are coming back. How big is one? An Andean condor can stand up to 4 feet tall, or 1.2 meters. Its wings can spread up to 10.5 feet, or 3.2 meters. Weight can reach 33 pounds, or 15 kilograms. A California condor can stand up to 4.6 feet, or 1.4 meters. Its wings can spread up to 9.7 feet, or 3 meters. Weight can reach 25 pounds, or 11 kilograms. A person in the chart stands 6 feet, or 1.8 meters. That person weighs 175 pounds, or 79.4 kilograms.

2Where condors live. A map shows South America and the Andes Mountains. It shows the Pacific Ocean and the Atlantic Ocean. One shade marks where California condors live. Another shade marks where Andean condors live. Searching on the wind. Only two condor species exist. The Western Hemisphere is home to each of them. California condors live in the southwestern United States. Andean condors live in western South America. Some birds of both species use the coast. Others use dry country inland. Rocky canyons are typical homes. Gorges are typical homes. Deserts, cliffs, and mountains are typical homes too. High perches are what these birds want. Those landforms give them the height. Experts lately found California condors settling high in giant redwoods. The birds use hollow spaces inside the trees. Redwoods are the largest trees on Earth. Flight begins at five or six months of age. Solo food searches begin at about two years. Any home region must meet one need first. The birds must be able to find food there. Thermal wind currents are required too. Updrafts are required. Those rising streams let the birds soar. A thermal is a column of warm air moving upward. Wind that crosses a mountain can make a strong updraft. Wind that crosses a cliff can do the same. In those conditions a condor flies in tight circles while it scavenges. A day of travel can cover 150 miles, or 241 kilometers. Speed can reach 50 miles an hour, or 80.4 kilometers an hour. Hours can pass with no wing flap at all. The bird simply rides the wind. When the dizzy heights are done, the usual seat is a high rocky ledge. Those homes are hard to reach. Eggs stay safer because animals that cannot fly cannot climb up.

3Condors eat together in a group. Colombia is one country where the Andean birds live. Bolivia is another. Ecuador is another. Peru is another. Chile is another. Argentina is another. A taste that has to be learned. Condors are vultures. Vulture is the word for every bird that eats carrion. Carrion means what is left when an animal has died. Rabbits are food for the California birds. They eat squirrels. They eat gophers. Larger animals are preferred. Deer, elk, and cattle top that list. Andean condors also eat both small animals and large ones. Local llamas are on their menu. Alpacas are on it too. Coastal condors clean beaches. Dead fish are part of that cleanup. Dead seabirds are part of it. Now and then a whale washes ashore and is eaten. One feeding can take in 4 pounds of flesh, or 1.81 kilograms. Days may then pass with no meal. Expert scavenging is required. Smell is not a strong sense for these birds. Eyesight is the outstanding tool. Food can be spotted from tremendous height. Other vultures circling are a signal. They have found food. The larger condor simply drops in. It eats the meal the smaller birds found. Bacteria fill carrion. Most living things could die from that bacteria. A condor's risk of bacterial sickness is low. It can eat infected dead animals and stay well. Recent studies compared stomach acid. Acid in a condor stomach beats human stomach acid by ten times. It can beat it by as much as one hundred times. The cleanup these birds do matters. Ecosystems where they live stay healthier because of it. Condors are normally social. They share food. One picture shows a California condor about to feed. The meal is what remains of a humpback whale.

4Pictures compare a male California condor with a male Andean condor. Excitement turns the head a deep pink. It can also turn the head orange. A bare head can be beautiful. Up close, beauty is not the first word most people would pick. The look still has a reason. Bare heads fit the way these birds eat. Few feathers mean less mess. The head goes deep into a carcass. Washing up after the meal is easier. Light bodies sit on sturdy, broad wings. That ratio makes the birds expert gliders. Males are usually larger than females. The species decides the other contrasts. Black is the main feather color on the California birds. Fluffier black feathers ring the neck like a collar. Andean condors are mostly black as well. White feathers at the neck make a contrast. Andean males carry a comb. A comb is a soft, fleshy growth on the head. They are born with it. Male chicks can be told from females right away. Wrinkles on those heads differ from bird to bird. Scientists have learned to name individual males by those wrinkles. Condors also belong with the raptors. Eagles are raptors. Hawks are raptors. A raptor's beak is sharp and hooked. The hook helps tear flesh. Raptors have strong legs. Most have talons sharp enough to catch live prey. Condors do not need to grab a living animal. Their talons changed with their diet. The talons are more like claws. They are less sharp. On the ground, the talons help the bird balance while it feeds. A surprise link. Storks look nothing like condors. Even so, storks are among their closest relatives. After a carcass meal, heads get wiped on rocks. They get wiped on tree branches too. Hours then go to smoothing feathers.

5Ecuador is the setting. A chick of the Andean species waits on a rock shelf that serves as a nest. Parents are due with a meal. Eggs that need great care. Two years is the usual gap. In that time a female produces a single egg. For an endangered species, that single egg matters enormously. The egg is vital to the species surviving. Even so, the mother builds no soft nest. She sets the egg on hard rock. A cliff can be the spot. A cave floor can be the spot. Both parents guard that precious egg with care. About fifty-six days pass. The chick starts to hatch. A full week can pass before it is all the way out. Both parents help with feeding. Food for the chick is stored in the throat. The parent carries it back to the nest. The food then passes from the parent's bill to the chick's bill. Growth and meals bring a color shift in the feathers. Gray slowly becomes black. Around six months, the young bird is ready to fly. Predators are few. A typical life lasts about sixty years. Falcons are the main wild danger. Eagles are a threat too. They try to snatch the eggs. Captive birds, such as those in zoos, live longer. Some have passed seventy-five years. What puts a condor in the bird group? The bill is strong. The bill is sharp. Wings can reach 10.5 feet across, or 3.2 meters. Feathers cover the body. The head has no feathers. Legs are strong. Talons are wide. Every bird has a backbone. Every bird has feathers. Every bird has wings. Every bird has a bill. Every bird lays eggs. Every bird is warm-blooded.

6People and condors. Both species are endangered. By 1973 the Andean bird was listed as endangered. The California condor has been on that list since 1967. Their struggles are not identical. This much is sure. People rank with the greatest threats these birds have faced. Counts of the California birds fell hard after newcomers arrived in the American West. That arrival was in the nineteenth century. Before then, the birds ranged the whole Pacific coast, up and down. Many people disliked the big birds. They called condors ugly. They shot them. Coyotes and wolves were targets too. People set out poison to kill those mammals. The poisoned bodies became condor food. Death followed for the birds too. Lead from bullets killed many more. A shot animal became a meal. Lead poisoning followed. More recent deaths came from flights into electrical power lines. Andean condor numbers fell from these same dangers. Extra perils hit them too. Some villagers in South America shoot condors. They believe the birds kill livestock. There was a time when wild California birds in the United States numbered under ten. Extinction was unbelievably close. In 1987, scientists rescued the remaining birds in a humane way. They carried the condors to places that could be guarded. There, the birds could raise young with help. Five years of hard work followed. Release into wild country came next for the California birds. Numbered tags went on the wings. The tags tell scientists how many birds exist. The tags also show whether the birds are managing. May of 2013 showed a free-flying California flock of more than 230. In South America, groups have secured reserves. They have taught the public about extinction. They have made clear that condors are not a hazard to livestock. A few thousand Andean condors are thought to survive in the wild today. Some wing tags hold radio transmitters. The signals show where the bird is.

7Utah holds a canyon scene. A rare California bird sails above it. A bird worth marveling at. Stretch your arms straight out to the sides. What do you think that reach measures? Whatever the number, it is probably only about half an average condor's wingspan. Few flying birds anywhere are more enormous than the California species. The Andean species shares that rank. They are very rare. A giant sailing past, full of grace, is a sight most people never get. They can only picture it. Earlier flocks were far bigger than today's flocks. A slow comeback is underway. Threats to these magnificent birds still exist. If people understand the danger, the birds may keep soaring. High sky could stay theirs for years to come. Words to know. Vultures are large birds of prey. They scavenge, and dead animals are most of their food. A thermal has to do with heat, or is caused by heat. Talons are foot claws. Owls have them. Hawks have them. Eagles have them. To scavenge is to find scraps, or animals already dead, and eat them. A reserve is a place where wild plants and wild animals are protected. Raptors are birds of prey. Inaccessibility means a place is impossible to reach, or very hard to reach. An ecosystem is a community of living things together with the places they live. A current is air or water moving in a set direction. A conservationist works to protect the environment. Animals, plants, and natural resources are part of that work. A cavity is an empty space inside a solid object. Adapted means changed to fit a new place or a special situation.

27. Groundwater

1In August 2014, a California well dried up. Ten days later, a woman received 300 gallons (1,136 L) of drinking water. Drought left little rain, so farmers pumped groundwater to water fruit and vegetable crops. About two thousand wells went dry, and some faucets gave only a gurgle. The county opened centers for drinking water and showers. Experts say the shortage will not end soon, even with rain. California is not the only place with a water crisis. Groundwater fills spaces and cracks between rocks, sand, and soil underground.

2Salt water is 97 percent of Earth's water, so people cannot drink it or use it on crops. Less than 3 percent of Earth's water is fresh, and ice locks up almost 70 percent of that. Rivers, lakes, and ponds hold only 0.3 percent of fresh water. Scientists say 99 percent of Earth's usable water is groundwater. This fresh water flows slowly under the land. Most of it is stored in aquifers made of rock, gravel, and sand. Rain and melting snow trickle down and feed aquifers that can stretch for thousands of miles. People pump this water faster than nature can replace it, so levels are falling fast.

3Since 1995, levels have fallen in almost two-thirds of wells across the United States. The Ogallala Aquifer under the Great Plains could be 70 percent depleted by 2060. It waters America's wheat fields, but that water cannot last forever. From 1950 to 2010, the level fell 36.7 feet in Texas and did not change in South Dakota. One Texas well dropped more than 120 feet (36.6 m). In Northern California, grape growers drill deep wells so their vineyards can survive. Smaller farmers blame them for drying shared shallow wells that they cannot afford to replace. Who owns groundwater, and how much use is fair?

4Pakistan uses 373,600 liters per person a year, while China uses 83,500. Pakistan's available water has dropped almost 75 percent in sixty years. Many blame Indian dams and say farmers now pump more groundwater. India says poor management in Pakistan is to blame. The rivals have fought four wars and now compete for fresh water. The United Nations says water crises will grow more severe. By 2030 the world could face a 40 percent shortfall, and nearly two billion people could face famine. In Syria, drought drove people into cities and helped start a civil war in 2011.

5It is easier to stay out of trouble than to get out of it. Dealing with the groundwater crisis now is easier than after aquifers are used up. Earth's average temperature is rising, and the ten hottest years on record have all come since 2000. Rainy seasons may shift, and water supplies could be badly disrupted. More than eight billion people live on Earth now, and eleven billion people are expected by 2100. About 65 percent of fresh groundwater pumped in the United States waters crops. Sensors can check soil moisture and turn irrigation on and off. Buried pipes can bring water closer to tree roots and waste less.

6Since 2002, NASA satellites called GRACE have tracked groundwater from space. About one-third of the largest aquifers were dangerously depleted from 2003 to 2013. Some Kansas and Iowa farms are switching from corn to wheat and sorghum. Record drought led California to pass a groundwater management law in 2014. Cities could fine people five hundred dollars a day for washing cars or watering lawns against the rules. From June to December 2014, daily use fell from as much as 140 gallons (530 L) to 67 gallons (254 L). Nationwide, 57 percent of homes use groundwater, and more than 90 percent of rural homes do. Shorter showers and full loads in washers can save hundreds of gallons each week.

7A 2016 study of 23,749 homes found that toilets use more water than anything else in the house. Newer toilets use less than toilets did in a similar 1999 study. Some families save water by not flushing every time. Owners can also replace thirsty grass with plants that need less water. In California, the motto for brown lawns is "Brown is the New Green." There is no quick, easy fix for saving groundwater. People still need to drink, and farmers still need to water crops. We can still be smarter about this water now and far into the future.

28. Hockey's Top Prize

1Jordan Nolan plays for the Los Angeles Kings. During 2012 he carried the great prize through the town where he grew up. The town is Sault Ste. Marie. Ontario is the province. Canada is the country. This book tells what the prize is. It names the man behind it. It follows the long road to a win. Famous moments from 1928, from 1942, and from 1970 come next. An octopus legend follows. An Oilers run follows. A closing cheer and a word list finish the book. A photo from 1996 shows Red Wings player Dino Ciccarelli, number 22. He is being pushed into Blues goalie John Casey. It is the seventh game. The series is the Western Conference final. Hockey's greatest prize. The year is 1996. The match is the seventh one. This series decides the West. Detroit's Red Wings face St. Louis. The other club is called the Blues. Whoever wins moves on. The next stop is the final round of the National Hockey League playoffs. People shorten that league name to NHL. The score is tied at 0 to 0. The period is the second sudden-death overtime. Tension could not be higher. Detroit captain Steve Yzerman takes the puck. His name is said EYE-zur-mun. He picks it up at center ice. He races to the blue line. Without warning comes the crack of a slap shot. The shot booms past the St. Louis goalie. Air seems to leave Detroit's Joe Louis Arena. Red Wings supporters, in the tens of thousands, burst into cheers. Plenty of fans rank that score among hockey's greatest thrills.

2Lord Stanley's cup. October is when the skilled athletes of the ice lace their skates. One aim fills their minds. They want the greatest prize in men's hockey. That prize is the Stanley Cup. April ends the regular season. Then the real season starts. For fans, the playoff weeks are the most thrilling of the year. Players skate. They shoot. They save. They check. They fight.
