All of it is for the cup they covet. The name honors Lord Stanley of Preston. Canada's Governor General is the post he held. The years ran from 1888 through 1893. In 1889 he watched ice hockey for the first time. The place was Montreal's Winter Carnival. Love for the game hit him immediately. He decided the top team should receive a trophy. Craftsmen finished the trophy in the year 1892. Its first name was the Dominion Hockey Challenge Cup. Canada's best amateur ice hockey club received it. His full name was Lord Frederick Arthur Stanley. Birth came in 1841. Death came in 1908. In 1915, two pro hockey groups made a deal. Each league would send its best club. Those two clubs would contest the trophy. Later years brought new leagues. Some leagues shut down. Some leagues joined together. A new title, the Stanley Presentation Cup, arrived in 1926. Another change came in 1947. From then on, people said Stanley Cup. The NHL then had only six teams. One club was in Toronto. Another was in Montreal. Detroit had a club. Chicago had a club. New York had a club. Boston had a club. All six still play in the NHL. The label historians use is Original Six. The reason is that more teams entered the league afterward. With all five bands in place, the cup stands 35.25 inches tall. That height is 89.54 centimeters. The first cup was silver. It stood 7.2 inches tall, or 18.5 centimeters. Its diameter was 11.4 inches, or 29 centimeters. The year 1963 is when the first cup entered the Hockey Hall of Fame. What players lift today is a stand-in. Among major sports in North America, only hockey skips a brand-new trophy every year. Champions hold the trophy only until the following season is finished. Five bands circle the cup. Winners' names are shown on the lowest band. When that band is full, the top band moves to the Hall of Fame. The other bands shift upward. A fresh band takes the bottom spot.

3A 2019 chart maps the conferences on the road to the cup. It labels the quarterfinals of each conference. It labels the semifinals. It labels the conference finals. It labels the Stanley Cup finals. Eastern clubs on the chart include Tampa Bay, Columbus, Boston, and Toronto. Washington, Carolina, and New York appear too. So do the NY Islanders and Pittsburgh. Western clubs include St. Louis, Nashville, Dallas, and Winnipeg. San Jose, Calgary, Colorado, and Vegas appear as well. The chart states that St. Louis won 4 to 3. The road to the cup is long. It is difficult. Fans would not want an easier path. Thirty NHL teams play now. They split into two conferences. One conference is the Eastern. The other is the Western. Inside the East are the Metropolitan Division and the Atlantic Division. Inside the West are the Central Division and the Pacific Division. Each division sends its first, second, and third clubs straight into the playoffs. Other spots remain. Those spots go to the next two highest teams in each conference.

4New York mayor Jimmy Walker handed the cup to the 1928 champions. Those champions were the New York Rangers. Famous moments. The year 1928, and a coach in the net. The Rangers were playing the Montreal Maroons. It was Game 2 of the finals. New York goalie Lorne Chabot got hurt. He had to leave. A spare goalie was rare in those days. Coach Lester Patrick scrambled for a last-minute fill-in. He asked the Maroons' head coach for a favor. Star Ottawa goalie Alec Connell was sitting in the stands. Patrick wanted to use him. Eddie Gerard coached the Maroons. Gerard refused. He wanted to keep his team's large edge. Patrick's players offered another plan. They asked their own coach to take the net. They promised harder play if he was the one in goal. A picture shows Lester Patrick, number 9. Across a career of twenty-four years he played for nine different teams. The Rangers were one of those teams. Moments later Patrick skated out. He wore a full set of goalie gear. He was forty-four. He was a retired defenseman. Goaltending experience was very thin. The cup was on the line. He had no other choice. Before he stepped on the ice, he spoke to his players. He asked the boys not to fail an old man. The result was remarkable. He stopped nineteen shots. He let in only one goal. The Rangers won in overtime. New York went on to take the Stanley Cup. Count his age that day as 44 years. Add three months. Add nine days. No older person has ever played in a Stanley Cup final. The record still stands. So does the story of that game.

5The year 1942, Maple Leafs against Red Wings. Original Six hockey had not yet held a cup final before this one. Toronto faced the worst odds. Detroit had already won the first three games. Game number 4 forced a jolt. Hap Day, coaching the Leafs, supplied it. He sat two star players down. Rookies took their places. He wanted the team to work harder. The plan succeeded. The Leafs won the next four games. The comeback was amazing. No club had ever erased a three-game hole in the Stanley Cup finals before. Someone asked Gaye Stewart, a Leafs player, what the series meant. A single word was his answer. That word was phenomenal. Beating Detroit in 1942 ended a dry spell for the Maple Leafs. Nine years had passed without a cup. A later Bruins win over the Blues returned the cup to Boston. Boston had waited twenty-nine years. The year 1970, and Bobby Orr's famous flying goal. It was overtime in Game 4 of the finals. The Boston Bruins were chasing a 4 to 0 result against the St. Louis Blues. The net of St. Louis was where captain Bobby Orr found himself. Derek Sanderson, a teammate, had just received a pass from Orr. Sanderson passed it straight back. Orr shot at the loose puck. He watched it cross the goal line. At that moment a St. Louis defenseman tripped him. The trip mixed with his excitement. Up he went, the way a comic-book hero might. Ray Lussier, a photographer, caught the moment. Hockey fans often name that picture the sport's best-known photograph. Orr is in the air. His arms are stretched out. He is celebrating a cup win.

6The Red Wings of Detroit added a habit in the year 2008. A giant inflatable octopus opened their games. The octopus legend. Hockey has some very strange traditions. None is stranger than the octopus story. Detroit is where it started. The date was April 15, 1952. The Red Wings were in a Stanley Cup playoff run. Only six teams existed then. A champion had to win two series. Each series was a best of seven. That meant eight wins in all. One night, a pair of local fans flung an octopus onto the rink. The animal had come from a seafood shop. Eight legs stood for the eight wins the team needed. Detroit won the cup that year. People treated the octopus as good luck. Year after year, Detroit kept the habit. A single game in 1995 saw thirty-six of the animals hit the ice. A second playoff night that same year brought a heavier animal. Two fans supplied it. The weight was 38 pounds. That weight is 17.2 kilograms. Twelve months later the ice received an octopus of 50 pounds. That weight is 22.7 kilograms. The habit still goes on. Twenty seasons in the NHL were Gretzky's career. A dynasty in Edmonton. From 1983 to 1990 the Edmonton Oilers looked like an All-Star team. Wayne Gretzky led them. Fans called him the Great One. His name is said GRET-ski. The club won five Stanley Cups in those years. He was that dominant. Terry O'Reilly, who coached the Boston Bruins, once spoke about him. O'Reilly called him the best. He joked that a league rule should send Gretzky to a different team each year. Gretzky was not the only star. The Hall of Fame also took in six more Oilers from those seasons. Official Dynasty status came from that Hall at a later date. Wins had piled up, and the span of years was fairly short. Since that award, the Hall has given Dynasty status to nobody else.

7Women's hockey has a second great trophy, called the Isobel Cup. The names are Isobel, Constance, and Mary. The family name is Gathorne-Hardy. The title is Lady. She is the person behind the trophy's name. She was Lord Stanley's daughter. Early women's hockey counts her among its first leaders. Amateur clubs were the first winners. The change arrived in 2016. From then on it honored the top pro women's club on this continent. A celebration photo shows the Boston Pride. The prize is the NWHL Isobel Cup Championship. March 27, 2021, is the day of that party. A game worth naming the best. Each year, hockey fans worldwide hunger for one picture. The dream is a favorite NHL club with the Stanley Cup in its hands. Players have chased that dream for over a century. They have scored. They have made saves. They have checked. They have fought. The reward is a drink from hockey's greatest prize. Playoff hockey can surprise anyone. Whichever club lifts the cup, the real winners are the fans. Those fans live all over the world. Words to know. Sudden death is an extra period after a tie. The first team to score in that period wins. A goalie is the defender who must keep the puck out of the net. A slap shot is a hard swinging hit that drives the puck with great force. A dynasty is a team, or a family, that stays successful or powerful for a long time. A semifinal round is a pair of games among four teams. The two winners move on. A deficit is a shortage, and the word is often used for money. Quarterfinals are four games among eight teams. The four winners move on. A conference is a group of sports teams. They play one another inside a larger league. A puck is the hard rubber disk used in hockey. To check is to stop a player who has the puck, or to take the puck away. Benched means kept out of the game. A playoff is a set of games after the regular season. Those games decide a champion.

29. How a Loose Union Became One Nation

1The Liberty Bell can be pictured at the start of this book. Another picture shows colonists in a fight with British soldiers at the Battle of Lexington. The story moves from the Articles of Confederation to the Treaty of Paris. Money troubles and the Constitutional Convention come next. Then come fights over land, the Louisiana Purchase, exploration, and war. Hard words are explained at the end. The year was 1776. North America's thirteen British colonies put their names on the Declaration of Independence. They said they were free from rule by Great Britain. They said they were independent. In the same moment they called themselves a union of states. The name they chose was the United States of America. That union then needed a system of government. People from the first thirteen colonies, by then called states, started writing a plan. Those people were delegates. War with Great Britain had not ended. Because the war went on, five years passed before the plan was firmly set in the new union. Forty more years would pass. Another war with Great Britain would come. Only then would the new union feel truly united.

2The year was 1777. Each state had named delegates to the Second Continental Congress. Those delegates agreed to send the new government plan to the states. The states would review it. The paper that laid out the plan became the Articles of Confederation. It refused the word nation for the United States. Its own phrase was a firm league of friendship. Thirteen states were in that league. A weak government for the whole country was set up. Strong governments for the states were set up. States liked the authority they held under the Articles. They liked the freedom too. Every state wrote a constitution of its own. Every state printed money of its own. A map of those first thirteen states shows Indigenous peoples on the surrounding lands. The names include the Algonquins, the Abenaki, the Ottawas, and the Chippewas. They include the Iroquois, the Hurons, the Illinois, and the Miamis. They include the Pottawatomies, the Delaware, the Powhatan, and the Shawnee. They include the Cherokee, the Chickasaws, the Creek, the Choctaws, and the Seminole. The Mississippi River is on that map. The first thirteen states were founded inside areas where Indigenous peoples lived. Those same areas bordered the states. Under the Articles, the federal government had no authority. It had no freedom to act. War had left the United States with debts. Congress was the only federal branch the Articles created. Congress could not tax anyone to raise money for those debts. No power to tax would prove a fatal flaw in the Articles of Confederation. Other flaws sat in the document too. No one executive stood in charge. A president was chosen to watch over the meetings of Congress. That president could not decide anything. Congress also could not change the Articles when the states found a part of the plan failing. Nine states out of thirteen had to agree before a change could happen. Several years after approval, the flaws finally showed. Indigenous peoples were pushed off their lands by the first thirteen states. A pattern began there. The same pattern returned in the westward expansion of the 1800s. Indigenous peoples resisted European colonists with diplomacy. They also resisted with warfare. Epidemics held their efforts back. The sicknesses came from diseases Europeans had brought.

3Almost five years passed before all thirteen states agreed to the Articles of Confederation. Major fighting in the Revolutionary War stopped in 1781. U.S. General George Washington defeated British General Charles Cornwallis. The place was Yorktown, Virginia. Two more years passed before the war was officially finished. Peace terms were discussed in Paris, France. The talkers represented the United States and Great Britain. Talks began in the middle of 1782. Talks closed on September 3, 1783, the day the Treaty of Paris received its signatures. That signature is what officially closed the war. Two important ideas were written into the peace treaty. The new United States was recognized as independent from Great Britain. Boundaries of the new country were set. Its land began at the Atlantic Ocean and reached west as far as the Mississippi River. New Orleans was left out. The Floridas were left out. Spain controlled both. The northern edge was set at the Great Lakes. It also followed the borders of Quebec and of Nova Scotia. Both of those places belonged to Canada, which Britain controlled. A map of this settlement can show Canada under British control. It can show land claimed at once by Great Britain and by Spain. It can show the Great Lakes, the Mississippi River, and U.S. states and territories. It can show New Spain, the Atlantic Ocean, and New Orleans. A wish for wealth drove most European colonization of North America. In the 1500s, trade paths between Europe and Asia were badly broken. The Ottoman Turks were fighting various groups. Lost profits pushed many European powers to seek new sea paths west toward Asia. From 1565 to 1625, colonies in North America were planted by Spain, Britain, France, and the Netherlands. The Treaty of Paris in 1783 closed the American Revolutionary War. Land was split between the United States and Great Britain. Rights of Indigenous peoples were ignored. Those peoples had lived on the lands since before recorded history.

4After the Revolutionary War and the signed Treaty of Paris, money troubles hit the United States hard. Soldiers went home poor and tired. The government held no money. It held no power to tax. Many soldiers therefore went unpaid. A larger money headache waited. That headache was the debt from the war. The nation owed more than 76 million dollars. Foreign countries were owed part of it. Individual Americans were owed part of it. State banks were owed part of it. Leaders had no clear way to pay debts that large. In Massachusetts, money pain at home set off a rebellion. Some farmers had fought for independence and then lost their farms. Prison took them because the debts could not be paid. The year was 1786. Daniel Shays had been a captain in the Revolutionary War. He marched with about a thousand farmers and other workers. They marched on Springfield, Massachusetts. Then they went toward Boston. There the U.S. Army traded gunfire with them. Some workers were tried and hanged. Others received no punishment. Shays' Rebellion convinced many citizens, and many leaders, that the federal government must be stronger. A picture shows a gold U.S. Half Eagle dated 1795. Fifty-five men came to Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, to face the country's many problems. The meeting place was Independence Hall. The month was May 1787. Their aim was to alter the Articles of Confederation. A stronger federal government was the goal. They would draft a constitution. An executive would lead the union. Congress would gain the power to tax. A way to change the governing paper would be created. Delegates came from twelve of the thirteen states. Each man was an influential leader at home. The men most involved in building the new union are sometimes called the founders. Every delegate was a wealthy, educated white man. That fact matched another fact of the time. In most states, the vote belonged only to white men who owned property. George Washington was elected to preside. The meeting took the name Constitutional Convention. It would run for several weeks.

5Tempers rose in debates about changing the Articles of Confederation. Two issues concerned power in the new government. Representation was the first issue. More seats in Congress would mean more power. Two plans were put forward. One plan tied representation to population. States with more people would get more representatives. The second plan gave every state the same representation. Slavery was the second issue. The people held in slavery were Black, taken from Africa, or born to people who had been taken. They were treated as property. Freedom was denied to them. Conditions were often terrible. They had to work, and they were not paid. Southern states wanted to keep slavery as an institution. They also wanted enslaved people counted when representation was set. Those people had no rights. They could not vote. George Washington, like many founders, gained because enslaved people labored on his estate. Southern delegates hoped a count of enslaved people would lift their states. They wanted footing closer to that of the Northern states. The Northern states had more people. Out of this came the Three-Fifths Compromise. It dealt with representation and with slavery. Congress would have two houses. The Senate would represent every state equally. The House of Representatives would follow population. On slavery, Congress was barred for twenty years from any change meant to control it. Each enslaved person would count as three-fifths of a person. That fraction would set how many representatives a state received. How much power a president should hold was debated too. The answer was a system called checks and balances. A power handed to one branch would be matched by equal power in the other two. Three branches were proposed. The legislative branch would hold two houses of Congress. The executive branch would hold a president. The judicial branch would hold courts that enforce laws. A method for changing the Constitution was written into the document itself.

6The date was September 17, 1787. Thirty-nine convention delegates signed the Constitution. It went out to the states for a vote. Across the country, people argued over whether the plan was a good one. The Federalists liked the Constitution. They worked so states would ratify it. The Antifederalists did not like it. They worked to keep it from becoming the law of the land. No democracy then existing in Europe could serve as a model. None existed. Delegates instead looked at some Indigenous nations. One example was the Haudenosaunee Confederacy. People sometimes call the Haudenosaunee the Iroquois. They had built a complex government for a confederacy of six nations. That government showed how ideas the delegates wanted might work in real life. A picture shows an Osage warrior, a Haudenosaunee man, and a Pawnee woman. The first U.S. presidential election came in 1789. In most states, the voters were only white men who owned land. After the Civil War, the Constitution was amended. The vote was widened more than once. People of every ethnicity can vote now. Women can vote now. Any citizen older than eighteen can vote now. Many people say voting-rights laws still discriminate. They say the harm falls unfairly on people of color. They say the problem is still serious in many parts of the country. Abigail Adams supported women's rights in America at an early time. Antifederalists said the Constitution must list rights that belong to the people. Government must not be able to take those rights away. The two sides later agreed. All thirteen states voted yes on the Constitution. The deal between Federalists and Antifederalists added ten changes. Those changes are the Bill of Rights. Free speech was guaranteed. Religious practice was guaranteed. Freedom from a religion run by the state was guaranteed. Other rights were guaranteed too.

7George Washington became president of the United States of America under the new Constitution. He took office on April 30, 1789. A great leader is what many people believed him to be. He served two terms. Each term lasted four years. His presidency ran from 1789 to 1797. In the first term he named Alexander Hamilton secretary of the treasury. Hamilton's fix for the nation's money problems included taxes on imports. It also included the first national bank. Faith in the government seemed to return. The economy grew. When Washington's second term ended, John Adams was elected president in 1796. Adams served from 1797 to 1801. Thomas Jefferson came next, in 1801. Jefferson served from 1801 to 1809. Another problem waited. What should be done with the huge lands won in the Revolutionary War? State governments claimed land to the west. Indigenous peoples had lived there since before recorded history. Some tracts were claimed by more than one state at the same time. The Land Ordinance of 1785 explained the split of this property. It would be split into townships and sections. Settlers would buy the sections. Some sections were reserved for the government. Some were reserved for public schools. In 1787 a Virginia delegate, Thomas Jefferson, designed the Northwest Ordinance. It gave rules for making new states inside the Northwest Territory. The United States would not hold colonies. Britain had held the thirteen colonies. The new country would not copy that pattern. Tecumseh led the Shawnee. They fought American settlers who were moving into the Northwest Territory.

8Homes went up as settlers moved west. The Land Ordinance and the Northwest Ordinance prepared a huge westward move of settlers. That move came in the early 1800s. Roads were built so the expansion could happen. Canals were built. Railroads were built. Growth was fast. The new government seemed able to match the pace. Debts were being paid down. Power in the new government was divided. The grim mood after the war had lifted. Prosperity and growth led leaders to make new plans. France now held ground that started at the Mississippi River and reached the Rocky Mountains. France also held New Orleans. Westerners in the United States could not send crops down the Mississippi for trade. They could not use the port of New Orleans for that trade either. The United States went to Napoleon Bonaparte, emperor of France. The hope was to buy New Orleans. Bonaparte had suffered major setbacks in a European war. He needed soldiers and cash. To pay for his war, he offered more than the city. He offered all of the Louisiana Territory. The price was 15 million dollars. In October 1803 the United States paid France's price. The nation doubled in size. Added land came to 800,000 square miles, equal to 2.07 million square kilometers. The added people numbered 200,000. Land area of the United States was nearly doubled by the Louisiana Purchase. Westward expansion was pushed farther. In many cases Indigenous peoples were destroyed or driven from their homes.

9May 1804 was when President Thomas Jefferson sent two army officers west. Their names were Meriwether Lewis and William Clark. A further group of 45 people went with them. They were to find a route across the continent. They were to explore the Louisiana Territory. They were to come back and report. Sacagawea, a girl of the Lemhi Shoshone, worked as an interpreter. Her help let the expedition succeed. More than two years of travel ended with their return. They carried journals. They carried drawings. They carried hundreds of plant and animal samples. The expedition prepared the way for taking land in the West. Many Indigenous groups already living there would be hit hard. A picture shows Lewis and Clark's group moving through the Louisiana Territory. While they explored, an old ally was fighting. France was in a raging war with Great Britain. President Jefferson hoped the United States could stay out. James Madison became president in 1808. He hoped for the same staying out. Staying out proved impossible. British ships seized U.S. ships and searched them. French ships did the same. Each side wanted to stop supplies from reaching its enemy. Britain also took the sailors off the captured ships. Those sailors were forced to serve Britain. Citizens of the United States had won independence from Britain. Now they were under Britain's tyranny again. In 1812 President Madison warned Great Britain. Searching and seizing of U.S. ships had to stop, or war would follow. The answer did not satisfy him. He declared war on Great Britain on June 18, 1812. During the War of 1812, the United States lacked a navy that could fight Britain at sea. An invasion meant to conquer Canada, then under Britain, failed. The failure showed that the army could not face that giant nation again. The war lasted two and a half years. A peace treaty was signed by the two countries on December 24, 1814. The U.S. ship Constitution grew famous in that war. It won a battle against the British ship Guerriere.

10The war was officially over. The United States still fought Britain once more. On January 8, 1815, Americans won a clear fight called the Battle of New Orleans. General Andrew Jackson led them. He did not know peace had already been signed. British dead numbered almost three hundred. U.S. dead numbered less than twenty. The win made something plain. The western part of the country was truly under the United States, not under Great Britain. Citizens felt as if the War of 1812 had been won. In truth, neither side had victory. The late win may have mattered most for another reason. The new United States of America felt, at last, like one united nation. A timeline of the American Revolution opens with the French and Indian War. That war is also called the Seven Years' War. It ran from 1754 to 1763. The years 1764 and 1765 brought the Sugar Act, the Quartering Act, and the Stamp Act. The year 1767 brought the Townshend Acts. The year 1770 brought the Boston Massacre. The year 1774 brought the Coercive Acts, also called the Intolerable Acts. The First Continental Congress met in 1774. The Revolutionary War began in 1775. The Second Continental Congress met in 1775. The Declaration of Independence was signed on July 4, 1776. The Revolutionary War ended in 1781. All thirteen states approved the Articles of Confederation in 1781. Shays' Rebellion came in 1786. The Constitutional Convention was held in 1787. All states ratified the Constitution in 1791. The Bill of Rights was ratified in 1791. War with Britain began again in 1812. The Treaty of Ghent, signed in 1814, ended the War of 1812.

11Authority is power to govern, to give orders, or to decide. Checks and balances split power among branches so no single branch can dominate. A compromise is a decision in an argument where each side gives something up. A constitution holds the basic laws that say how a state or a nation is governed. Debates are talks between people or groups who disagree on an issue. Debts are things owed to someone, and they are usually money. Executive describes the branch that leads a nation and carries out its laws. Expansion is growth in size, amount, volume, or scope. Guaranteed means promised, or made sure. Judicial describes the branch that checks whether laws agree with the constitution. Legislative describes the branch that makes laws. Prosperity means success, wealth, or good fortune. To ratify is to give official approval. A rebellion is a fight against authority or against power. Representatives are people picked to speak, to vote, or to act for someone else. Seizing means taking control by force. A treaty is a formal peace or friendship agreement between nations or groups. Tyranny means cruel or harsh acts done by someone, or by a group, that holds power.

30. How Flu Germs Invade and How We Fight Back

1This book tells what the flu is. It tells how viruses change and how the body fights back. It tells how to block the virus and how to treat the flu. It tells how flu has moved through history. A short close and word meanings come last. Have you ever shivered with chills? Have you burned with a fever? Has your throat hurt, or has your nose felt stuffed? Has your head pounded, and have your muscles ached? Have you felt very tired right after you woke up? Signs like these can mean flu germs have entered your body. Read on to learn about the illness and how to stay healthy.

2Another name for the flu is influenza. A virus is the cause. That virus can move quickly from one person to another. A virus is a tiny germ. Your eyes cannot see it. A little genetic material sits inside it. A hard shell of protein wraps that material and guards it. Scientists need very strong microscopes to see a virus. Even then, the picture must be one thousand times bigger than the real virus. Hundreds of thousands of viruses can sit on the head of a pin. A picture shows the avian influenza virus. Viruses are all around. Some never bother you. Some are dangerous, and some can kill people or other animals. In the northern half of the world, flu season runs from October through May. Viruses are widespread then. A virus needs a home if it is to live and make copies. For people and other animals, that home is inside us. A virus can exist on its own for a while, even for years. In the end it must find a host and enter that host's cells. Its hard shell punches a hole in a cell, and the virus goes in. It puts its own genetic information into the cell. Copies are made very fast. You may feel sick one to four days later. Those copies burst out and begin to enter other cells.

3A breathing diagram names the nose, the mouth, and the pharynx. It names the windpipe, also called the trachea. It names the larynx and the lungs. The respiratory tract is what flu viruses attack. Flu comes in many kinds. A specific virus causes each kind. The types differ, yet the symptoms look much alike. Flu infects the respiratory tract. You usually feel achy, feverish, congested, and fatigued. Your stomach may feel sick. Three basic types exist. They are Influenza A, Influenza B, and Influenza C. Influenza A can make humans and other animals seriously ill. Large outbreaks usually come from it. Influenza B is milder and causes smaller outbreaks. It affects only humans, and mostly children. Influenza C usually brings only a mild illness in humans. Every type can pass from a sick person to a healthy one through the air. Passage can also happen by touching objects such as utensils for eating or drinking. Saliva from a sick person's mouth often carries the virus. So do mucus droplets coughed or sneezed into the air. Cover your mouth and your nose whenever you cough or sneeze. Wash your hands after you use the bathroom. Wash them before you eat or prepare food. If you are infected, staying home is the best step. Keep away from other people so your germs do not travel. A sneeze fills the air with tiny drops of saliva and of mucus.

4Viruses often move from wild birds to chickens on farms. Those chickens meet people more often. Viruses can change. That change is called mutation. Among all viruses, flu viruses change as much as any. A virus that mutates out of another is called a strain of the first virus. Many influenza viruses begin in wild animals. Birds are the most common start. Farm ducks and farm chickens can catch strains that mutated from those viruses. After farm birds are infected, further mutation can happen. New strains can infect pigs. They can infect humans. On farms, chickens, pigs, and humans stand close together. That closeness can make the mixing and spreading of viruses deadly. The animal chain from wild animals to humans is not the same for every virus. One chain may hold animals as unlike as dogs and whales. A virus sometimes skips steps in the chain. The avian virus of 1997 did that skip. It went straight from birds to humans. One sample chain runs from a wild duck, to a chicken, to a pig, and then to a human.

5A body-fight picture shows a cell holding flu virus, plus white blood cells, antibodies, and viruses. The human body can fight an invasion by flu viruses. The immune system guards against harmful germs. It makes chemicals called antibodies. Those antibodies ride in the blood. They move through the body looking for invaders. Each antibody is set to destroy one kind of germ. When an invading germ is found, antibodies strike. They destroy cells that hold the virus. Change lets influenza become a terrible enemy. Your body may beat a virus, and the virus may still change into a new strain. You may have no immunity against that new strain. The body must begin again. It must learn, at the level of cells, how to fight the new strain. Scientists keep fearing that the next strain will kill more people than earlier ones. White blood cells are made to guard you from catching diseases. The body can notice invading germs in the blood. Antigens sit in the proteins of these diseases. Special immune cells can sense those antigens in small bits of virus that stick out of your cells. Finding an antigen opens many ways to attack. Some cells order other cells to make antibodies. Some call cells that eat germs. Some call killer cells that can destroy a cell a virus has infected. An artist's picture shows those killer cells striking a virus.

6Memory is one of the immune system's best gifts. A microbe fought once will be remembered. The body will know how to fight that microbe again. You may gain immunity to one particular microbe. The fight can grow so strong that a virus is fully destroyed before you feel sick. These are only some of the striking ways the body holds invaders off. Many kinds of cells are produced. The kind depends on the microbe that was found. Most of the time the body knows the right fight for an invading microbe. A weak immune system lets viruses take a stronghold. They then copy themselves faster than they can be destroyed. Flu symptoms follow. Without medical care, those symptoms worsen quickly. Scientists look for ways to help the body fight flu. Commonsense steps can keep a flu virus from getting in. Plenty of rest is one of the best ways to stay healthy. Eating well is another. Stay away from cups and utensils used by infected people. Wash your hands often. Keep fingers out of your mouth. These steps matter because a person can be contagious before any symptom shows. That person may not know about the exposure. Sickness may be about to start, and the person may not know it. People who sneeze or cough should be avoided. That avoidance helps keep the virus away. Frequent hand washing also helps you avoid the flu.

7Caution does not save everyone. Infection can still happen. Medical scientists did find a way to stop a flu virus from taking the body over. The way is vaccines. A vaccine holds flu viruses that are weakened or dead. It is injected before the person gets sick. The weakened viruses push the body to make antibodies. Those antibodies attack the weak or dead germs and destroy them. Once those antibodies exist, the body stays on alert. Stronger viruses that arrive in flu season can then be attacked. Every strain needs its own vaccine. Some flu vaccines hold several strains at once. Each injected strain makes the body build a matching antibody. Meeting many strains gets the immune system ready. The body can then face many virus forms that doctors expect in a given flu season. The first vaccine came from British physician Edward Jenner in 1796. He injected patients with cowpox virus. His aim was protection from smallpox, a similar virus that was far more dangerous. For most viral strains, vaccines prevent illness eighty percent of the time. That means immunity, and no sickness, in eighty of one hundred cases for a strain in the vaccine. The rate is high, yet it is not perfect. A vaccine does not guarantee that flu will stay away. If you did get the vaccine, the illness should be milder. Aches should be lighter. You should not feel so hot. A strain missing from your flu shot can still make you sick. Scientists must act like detectives. Each year they must guess which strains belong in the shot. Risk is not the same for every person. Some people become much sicker than others. The very young and the very old face a greater risk. People who already have diabetes or heart disease face a greater risk too.

8Flu cannot always be prevented. After viruses are inside you, several steps can still help. Pharmacies sell many over-the-counter medicines. Those medicines can ease flu symptoms. They can quiet aches in the head and in the muscles. They can bring a fever down. Lots of fluid helps, including water and fruit juice. Rest helps the body fight viruses inside its cells. Picture the work an immune system does during an infection. Pain and exhaustion make sense. A war is underway in the bloodstream. Water and rest let the body do that work. Most people get over the flu with no special medical treatment. Very serious cases can be treated with medicines doctors have. Antibiotic drugs do nothing to viruses. Some antiviral drugs do aim at flu viruses. A drug that beats one strain may fail against another. The same is true of other medicines. Over time, viruses can grow resistant to drugs. They can even grow immune to drugs. A picture shows a doctor in 1941 giving vaccine shots to children. History holds many influenza outbreaks. Some were mild and stayed limited. Others were severe, traveled the world, and killed millions. A new flu virus that infects a few people can spread fast. It can become an epidemic. Evidence suggests flu epidemics go back hundreds of years. They may go back thousands of years. Each one has killed thousands of people, or more than that.

9Late January and early February of 1918 brought severe sickness to Haskell County, Kansas. Soon after, soldiers at Camp Funston, an Army base in Kansas, showed the same symptoms. In a single week the Army hospital treated more than five hundred sick soldiers. Spring's end left forty-eight of those soldiers dead. At first the cause was unknown. Doctors did not know it. Scientists did not know it. Later, doctors traced the illness to one strain of influenza virus. That strain is called the 1918 flu. It killed more than six hundred thousand Americans. Worldwide, it killed twenty-five to forty million people. An outbreak of that size is a pandemic. A pandemic races around the world. A picture shows 1918 flu patients filling a makeshift ward. The 1918 pandemic was not history's only major influenza outbreak. About seventy thousand people in the United States died in the pandemic that ran from 1957 to 1958. It showed up first in China. It reached the United States in June 1957. A different flu strain was first found in Hong Kong, China. In 1968 that virus set off another pandemic. Deaths in the United States alone reached 34,000. A small city holds about that many people. H1N1, called swine flu, was in 2009 the first influenza pandemic for forty-one years. People in more than seventy countries were infected. A map can mark Russia, Mongolia, China, Japan, India, and the Pacific Ocean. Such a map can show how a pandemic may travel from one region to many. The flu of 1957 to 1958 is an example.

10Some influenza outbreaks caused wide worry without becoming pandemics. The Russian flu of 1977 is one. Avian flu in 1997 and in 1999 is another. Both avian strains began in parts of China. They then reached other countries. Doctors keep watching for the next major influenza outbreak. They believe speed of detection matters. Fast detection can stop an outbreak from becoming a flu pandemic across the world. A mask over mouth and nose cuts the chance of catching the virus. It also cuts the chance of passing the virus on. Influenza has existed for hundreds of years. Flu viruses can change into new strains. Because of that change, influenza may never be erased. If you have the flu, caring for yourself comes first. Keeping the illness from other people comes with that care. Drink water the way a camel drinks. Sleep the way a hibernating bear sleeps. Take a vaccine when one is available. Above all, fight back. Good food and rest give healthy cells a chance to win.

11Antibiotic describes medicine that fights body infections from certain microbes. Fatigued means tired or weary from work of the mind or of the body. Genetic material is the cell part that holds basic facts about an organism's traits. Antibodies are body chemicals that attack germs coming in. Antigens are substances that set off immune responses. Germs are microorganisms that often bring disease and sickness. Congested means filled or blocked, as when mucus fills the nose and throat. Hosts are organisms that other organisms live in or on. Immunity is a body's ability to resist infection. Contagious means able to pass from one organism to another. Intracellular means found inside cells. An epidemic is a disease outbreak that spreads fast through a group of living things. Invasive describes a move into a new area or body that harms or disrupts. A microorganism is alive but microscopic, for example a virus or a single cell of bacteria. The respiratory tract is the nose, mouth, throat, and lung passages that air uses during breathing. Mutating means changing into another form. A strain is a group of microbes that share a type. A pandemic is a disease spreading fast across the world. Submicroscopic means too small for an ordinary microscope. Parasites live inside other organisms and take nourishment from them. Vaccines are medicines built from weak or dead viral strains. The body learns from them how to fight a stronger virus of the matching type. Phagocytes are cells that eat microbes invading the body. A virus is a microorganism that enters a body, enters cells, copies itself fast, and causes illness. The word virus can also mean the illness that virus causes. Protein is a needed nutrient found in the cells of every living thing.

31. How Steroids Harm Athletes and Teens

1Police found anabolic steroids in someone's house. This book tells what steroids are. It tells their history. It tells how they can be bad medicine. It tells how athletes got caught. It tells about teens who use them. A closing thought comes near the end. Word meanings come last. Lance Armstrong is shown on the bike during the Tour de France. In the world of cycling, he seemed to fit the role of a real-life superhero more than anyone. A single bound over a tall building was beyond him. On a bike, though, his skill was clear. He won the Tour de France seven times. The wins came between 1999 and 2005. That race is the world's most grueling cycling race. No rider before him had managed a feat like that. Few sporting events demand as much as the Tour de France. Almost superhuman fitness is required of these cyclists. They must cover a course of 2,173 miles. That distance is 3,497 kilometers. The course crosses the mountainous French countryside. His feat was even more remarkable for another reason. He had earlier won a life-and-death struggle with cancer.

2The sports world had him at its peak. He had money. He had fame. He had medals. He had trophies. Then it all collapsed. His reputation was ruined. He was a cheater. He had illegally used steroids. The drugs improved his performance. They gave him an unfair advantage. The advantage was over cyclists who did not use steroids. For years he denied that he took them. In 2013 he admitted the truth. He called that mythic, perfect story one big lie. Days later, the honors were gone. The sport he once led later cast him out. He was also participating in blood doping. Blood doping increases the oxygen carried by the body's red blood cells. Extra oxygen lets a body run with greater efficiency. One method starts before a competition. Athletes remove some of their blood. They freeze it. By race time the body has naturally replaced the missing red blood cells. As the race is about to begin, they put the stored blood back. The body then has extra red blood cells. It has extra oxygen for game day. A picture can compare doped blood with normal blood. It can show red blood cells inside a blood vessel. Ben Johnson was first in that Olympic 100-meter race in 1988. A later test showed steroids in his body. He had to return his gold medal. The word steroids can refer to different things. Some steroids are made by the human body on its own. It does this as it develops. It does this as it goes through puberty. These natural steroids affect bone growth. They affect muscle growth. They play a role in many other changes. These steroids stay in a delicate balance inside the body. Other kinds of steroids are sometimes ordered by doctors. They treat health problems such as asthma. They treat skin disorders. These steroids can also help people who have cancer. They can help people with other serious diseases. They should be used only under a doctor's careful supervision. They can be very dangerous.

3The steroids some athletes use have a long name. They are anabolic-androgenic steroids. People shorten that name to AAS. Anabolic means they affect how the body builds tissue. Muscle is one kind of tissue. Body development is what androgenic refers to. That includes reproductive organs. It includes facial hair. People often call these drugs anabolic steroids for short. They are similar to the hormone testosterone. The human body produces testosterone naturally. It produces it in small amounts. Hormone balance in the body shifts when artificial steroids are used. The imbalance can help people become better athletes in the short term. Serious health problems can follow as well. When people exercise, their muscle fibers develop tiny rips. They develop tiny tears. The body repairs those tears in a natural way. The cells become larger. Muscles grow. Muscles become stronger. More exercise means more torn fibers. Then they are repaired. Repair goes faster when steroids are used. Strength comes faster for users than for people who skip those substances. An old engraving shows the Olympic Games in ancient Greece. Lance Armstrong is only one athlete in a long line. Many have tried to gain an unfair advantage with special substances. A special concoction was the drink of the first Olympians in ancient Greece. They believed it made them stronger. Roman chariot racers drank an herbal drink. The drink supposedly increased their stamina. Performance-enhancing drugs entered their modern era in the 1950s. John Ziegler was an American physician. He was also a weight lifter. He learned something about weight lifters from the Soviet Union. They were using artificial steroids. Those drugs helped them dominate international competitions. A synthetic steroid of his own was Ziegler's creation. Popular magazines for bodybuilders carried his account of the discovery. The steroid craze began. Soon athletes began using these substances. Other people began using them too.

4Major sports have rules about steroid tests and penalties. MLB uses tests for steroids. NBA uses tests for steroids. NFL uses tests for steroids. A first offense in baseball brings a suspension of 5 games. A first offense in basketball brings a suspension of 4 games. A first offense in football brings a suspension of 50 games. The football suspension is without pay. A second offense in baseball brings a suspension of 10 games. A second offense in basketball brings a suspension of 6 games. A second offense in football brings a suspension of 100 games. That suspension is without pay. A third offense in baseball brings a lifetime ban. A third offense in basketball brings a suspension of 25 games. A third offense in football brings a suspension of at least 1 year. So many athletes were using steroids that a ban followed. The International Olympic Committee organizes the Olympic Games. It banned steroid use in 1975. Today steroid use is banned in every major sport. The United States has passed laws against anabolic steroids. Many other countries have passed such laws too. The laws make use illegal. They make possession illegal. They make sale illegal. The chance of going to jail does not stop some people. They keep using anabolic steroids in spite of the risks. Recent years have made this much easier. Designer steroids have been developed. These specially made steroids are built so a drug test will miss them. Sports organizations then find it difficult to catch cheaters. Muscles may look larger after a person takes steroids. They may perform better than before. These artificial hormones still have a big impact on the body. They have a big impact on the mind. Short-term side effects can include bad breath that will not go away. They can include acne. They can include swelling of the legs and feet. Long-term use can ruin a person's life. It can even end a life. Blood cholesterol levels can shift because of steroids. Cholesterol is a fatty substance. Blood-vessel walls can collect this substance. A broken-off piece of this substance can block a blood vessel. That can cause a stroke. It can cause a heart attack. One large muscle is the heart. Steroid use can cause serious heart problems. Steroids can also destroy a person's liver. The liver helps remove waste from the bloodstream. If the liver is not working properly, waste builds up in the blood. That buildup can cause serious illness. Steroids can disrupt the brain's ability to function. A person may see sights that are not real. They can cause panic attacks. They can cause depression. They can cause an overly aggressive behavior. People informally call that behavior 'roid rage.

5Steroids can bring many harmful effects. Aggressive behavior is one. Severe acne on the face is one. Severe acne on the back is one. High blood pressure is one. Heart disease is one. Stroke is one. Blood clots are one. Nausea is one. Bloating is one. Aching joints are one. Liver damage is one. Urinary problems are one. Bowel problems are one. A higher risk of tendon injuries is one. Hormone levels can shift a great deal because of steroids. Women who take them may grow facial hair. They may develop a voice that is permanently deeper. Men who take them can develop noticeable breast tissue. Adolescents who use them can have severe side effects. Stunted growth is one of those effects. Quitting after steroid use has begun can be very dangerous. The body's chemical messaging system is thrown off completely. When a user stops the drugs, severe withdrawal can follow. Depression is one withdrawal effect. Muscle aches are another. Nausea is another. Irritability is another. Those are only a few. Muscles shrink within weeks after stopping. Strength diminishes within weeks too. The year 1998 was one for the record books. Two baseball sluggers battled each other. Mark McGwire played for the St. Louis Cardinals. Sammy Sosa played for the Chicago Cubs. Each hoped to break baseball's record for home runs in a single season. The 1961 record belonged to Roger Maris of the New York Yankees. He hit sixty-one home runs. McGwire broke that record. He slugged seventy home runs. The following season Barry Bonds hit seventy-three. Bonds played for the San Francisco Giants. The season looked spectacular at the time. It would soon be tainted. Sosa became mixed up in a huge steroid scandal. McGwire did too. Bonds did too. Other big-name players did too. Sports has rarely seen a steroid scandal so large. In the early 2000s, baseball officials revealed test results. They had tested 1,438 major-league players for drugs. Five percent of those players took steroids. Because of the link to steroids, some stars have been refused a high honor. The Baseball Hall of Fame has refused to induct McGwire. Bonds has been refused too. Refusal has also met other top stars. The Hall of Fame is the highest honor in the game. Each of these players has been branded a cheater. Their legacies are stained forever.

6Olympic athletes can have a legacy tainted by anabolic steroids too. The harm reaches athletes around the world. A world record in 1997 belonged to Chinese swimmer Wu Yanyan. The record came at the Chinese National Games. A test was taken at the Chinese Olympic trials. Anabolic steroids showed up on Wu Yanyan's test. Both she and her coach were fined. They were dismissed from the Chinese national swim team. A picture shows her after that positive test in 1997. World swimming rules required a suspension. She had to stay out of competitive swimming for four years. At that time she ranked among the world's best swimmers. The suspension kept her out of the Olympic Games in 2000. Human growth hormone is also called HGH. As the name suggests, it helps the body grow. Artificial HGH is injected every day by some people. Proper growth needs more of the hormone than their bodies make. Some athletes abuse HGH. They believe it increases muscle mass. They believe it makes them stronger. Researchers dismiss these claims. They say HGH is dangerous when it is abused. Teens using steroids has become a major problem. One study offers numbers. The study's figure for high school boys is 4.9 percent using anabolic steroids at least one time. It suggests that 2.4 percent of girls have done so. That is 375,000 young men. That is 175,000 young women. Caught teens usually lose the right to play sports. School can even expel them. This one poor choice can happen in middle school. It can happen in high school. It can cause problems for a person's entire future. Community members hold a memorial service in one picture. The service is for two young athletes. Those athletes died from steroid use. Researchers say teens take steroids for a reason linked to media. They are influenced by the media's idea of what a body should look like. They also take steroids because school sports are highly competitive. Many students in sports hope a steroid edge will bring a win. They are trying to enhance how they look. Or they are trying to enhance what their bodies can do. The sad result is harm to the body they hoped to improve. They damage their bodies.

7A picture shows professional baseball player Mark McGwire. He is sworn in before he testifies about steroid use. Steroids change who you are. A person may want a competitive edge. A person may want only larger muscles. The body transforms. The mind transforms. A price comes with this change. Athletes at all levels run a risk if they use steroids. They risk being labeled cheaters. They tear apart their bodies to earn an artificial victory. They tear apart their minds. Terrible side effects are what remain for them. Those effects may never subside. They are also left with a hard truth. The whole achievement was one big lie. These words have special meanings in this book. A cheater is someone who breaks rules for personal gain. A side effect is an extra result of a drug or treatment, and it is usually harmful. They go beyond the desired effect. Competitive means related to a rivalry. The rivalry can be between individuals, teams, or businesses. Stamina is the energy that lets a person keep going. A hormone is a chemical that steers how a body grows. Steroids are drugs that make body tissue grow. Athletes sometimes use them illegally to enhance performance. Illegally means in a way that a law or a rule forbids. Puberty is the stage when adolescents become physically mature. They become capable of reproduction. Stunted means limited in growth or development. Synthetic describes a thing people make by joining substances. It does not occur in nature. Reputation is the general way that other people think of a person or a thing. Scandals are behaviors, actions, or events that seem shocking or bad. They damage the reputations of the people involved.

32. How Women Fought for the Vote

1Election Day is when voters cast their ballots. In 1872, Susan B. Anthony stepped into a voting office. Rochester, New York, was the city. The presidential election was the contest she voted in. Arrest followed two weeks after that vote. A woman’s vote was illegal in most states at the time. Anthony knew the law was being broken. Unjust was her judgment of that law. Decades of effort for women’s rights already stood behind her. Harriet Tubman shared that effort. Lucretia Mott shared it. Sojourner Truth shared it. Marching was one of their tools. Protest was another. Congress felt their pressure. Anthony hoped a ballot would pull attention toward women’s suffrage. Suffrage means the right to vote. Notice did follow her act. Almost fifty more years still passed before the Nineteenth Amendment won approval in Congress. Women with U.S. citizenship gained the vote through that change in law. For most women of color, nothing ended there. U.S. citizenship did not close their struggle. That moment sat midway in a far longer suffrage battle.

2A 1913 parade shows a group of suffragists on the march. The United States adopted its Constitution in 1787. Voting rights were not set out in that document. Each state was left to decide who could vote. By 1807, the vote belonged only to free white men. Women remained unable to vote. Some rights still matched men’s rights for certain unmarried women. Marriage was the choice most women made. A wife had no legal voice in government. She had no legal voice in her own family either. Property she owned passed to her husband. Wages she earned passed to him if she held a job. Only the father had a claim to children she bore. Women who were enslaved held no legal rights at all. Marriage did not grant those rights. July 1848 brought about three hundred people together in Seneca Falls, New York. Women’s rights were the topic they met to discuss. That gathering is called the Seneca Falls Convention. Many historians treat it as the suffrage movement’s starting point. A model of equal rights for women stood close by. Artwork shows Elizabeth Cady Stanton addressing the Seneca Falls Convention. That town stood on ancestral land of the Haudenosaunee Confederacy. Few rights, and sometimes none, belonged to most women in the United States then. Inside their own nation, Haudenosaunee women held wide political power. A chief had to be a man. Choosing him was women’s work. Advising him was women’s work. Removing him was women’s work. Every government decision included women’s help. Any act of war could be blocked by women. Land stayed under the control of Haudenosaunee women. Food sources stayed under their control. Economic power followed from that control. Marriage did not strip their rights. Their property stayed theirs after marriage. Early suffragists took this as proof. Men and women could live as equals inside one society.

3Ideas from the Haudenosaunee inspired white suffragists. Most of those suffragists still did not demand U.S. voting rights for Indigenous women. Indigenous suffragists labored alongside white suffragists. One was Zitkála-Šá, said zit-KAH-lah-SHAH. Another was Marie Louise Bottineau Baldwin, said BOT-in-oh. Laura Cornelius Kellogg was another. White women’s voting rights stayed the aim of the mainstream movement. The early 1900s made Zitkála-Šá widely known. Her nation was the Yankton Dakota Sioux. Activism for Indigenous people and for women both carried her name. Music was part of her life. Teaching was part of her life. Writing was part of her life. National magazines ran her stories about Indigenous people. They also ran her articles. Washington, D.C., became her home in 1917. Protecting traditional Indigenous ways of life was her work there. She argued for U.S. citizenship for Indigenous people, so that voting could follow. The year 1926 was when she founded National Council of American Indians. She led the group as president. Indigenous people gained a shared front in the suffrage fight through her work. She helped them sign up as voters. Advocacy for Indigenous rights continued until her death in 1938. Women’s suffrage grew from abolition work that had begun in the 1830s. Speaking against slavery was how many early suffragists began. Writing against slavery was another start. Organizing against slavery was another. The year 1865 brought the end of slavery. Abolitionists then aimed at the vote for white women and for every Black person. A split soon opened among suffragists. The Fifteenth Amendment was the point of disagreement. Black men would gain the vote under that amendment. Support for the amendment was the view of Frederick Douglass. Lucy Stone shared that view. Both still meant to keep working for women’s suffrage. Anthony would not back an amendment that left women out. Elizabeth Cady Stanton would not back it either. They opposed giving greater power to Black men they regarded as uneducated. They opposed the same shift for immigrant men. Passage of the Fifteenth Amendment came in 1869. Many Black men still found voting nearly impossible under some state laws. The South was where that barrier was sharpest. Artwork shows a celebration by men in Baltimore, Maryland. The year of that celebration was 1870, after the amendment had passed.

4Women of color were shut out by Stanton and by other leaders inside the movement. Black women faced that exclusion most of all. Racism was a problem Black suffragists wanted named. Sexism was a problem they wanted named too. Addressing racism did not interest many white suffragists. They feared politicians would step away. They feared white women in the South would step away. Important leaders in the vote fight still included many Black women. Mary Church Terrell was among them. Ida B. Wells was among them. Nannie Helen Burroughs was among them. They saw the vote as a route to laws shielding Black Americans from racial violence. Demonstrations were organized by them. Women’s clubs were founded by them in cities nationwide. The East held many of those clubs. The Midwest held many of them. Black suffragists could be mobilized through the clubs. In the 1900s, Nannie Helen Burroughs was widely known as a suffragist. She was an educator. She was a speaker. She was an activist for women’s rights. Alice Paul, a suffragist, helped plan a march for women’s suffrage in 1913. Washington, D.C., was the setting. The date was the day before Woodrow Wilson became president. More than sixty years of suffrage work already lay behind women. A national event for the movement had not happened before that march. Streets filled with more than five thousand marching women. Men in the crowd tripped the marchers. Some of those men were police. Marchers were grabbed. Marchers were shoved. Marchers were spat on. Marchers were insulted. The country paid attention. A new generation of activists drew energy from the day. Tougher tactics in the suffrage fight became easier to imagine. Mexican Americans in the Southwest faced limits from racial violence. Segregation limited them as well. Spanish-speaking women were rallied by bilingual leaders. In New Mexico, Nina Ortero-Warren spoke in Spanish. She also gave out fliers in Spanish. California heard the same kind of work from Maria Guadalupe Evangelina de Lopez. Texas journalist Jovita Idár printed articles that favored suffrage. Those articles ran in newspapers written in Spanish. Work of this kind was crucial to passage, in 1920, of the Nineteenth Amendment. Literacy tests were later used by some lawmakers to block women of color. Poll taxes were used for that same block. Decades of shared work with white suffragists did not prevent a new isolation. Suffragists of color then had to seek their own voting rights without that partnership.

5August 26, 1920, was the day the Nineteenth Amendment became law. No Indigenous person could cast a ballot in a U.S. election then. U.S. citizenship was not granted to Indigenous people. A 1924 law changed that status and granted citizenship. Indigenous women gained the vote with citizenship. State officials still shut many Indigenous voters out for decades. Living on a reservation was treated as proof someone was not a state resident. Belonging to a tribe was treated the same way. Asian American women born inside the United States did receive the vote in 1920. Immigrants who were the first Asian generation in their families did not. Older laws remained active. Nearly every Asian immigrant was blocked from U.S. citizenship by those laws. Voting rights did not exist without citizenship. Years of work for women’s suffrage still left some Asian American suffragists unable to vote. Dr. Mabel Ping-Hua Lee was one such suffragist. The year 1920 did not include a ballot for her. A law in 1952 lifted that limit. Asian immigrants could then obtain both U.S. citizenship and the vote. Barriers kept being built by many lawmakers. Even women of color who already held U.S. citizenship were stopped by those barriers. Poll taxes were one tactic. Tests were another. Intimidation was another. Threats were another. Violence was another. A 1963 march showed activists demanding voting rights. Before 1965, one common tactic was a poll tax. The tax was a fee due before a person could vote. A literacy test was another common tactic. Passing it was supposed to prove a voter could read. The tests were written, on purpose, to confuse people. Failure was easy. A citizenship test demanded proof that a voter understood government. A voter might have to recite from the Constitution of the United States. A voter might have to write pieces of it. A voter might have to explain those pieces. Voters of color were the people these tactics were built to hinder. A grandfather clause opened a way around the requirements. The way opened if a grandfather had held voting rights before 1867. Most places allowed only white people to vote before that year. White voters who could not pay a poll tax could still vote under the clause. White voters who could not pass a test could still vote under it too.

6True suffrage was still being fought for in the 1960s. Fannie Lou Hamer worked without rest for stronger protection of voters of color. Ella Baker did that work. Diane Nash did that work. President Lyndon B. Johnson signed the Voting Rights Act in 1965. The act banned tactics aimed at blocking voters who were people of color. Further protections arrived in a 1975 amendment. People facing voter discrimination because English was not their first language gained those protections. Annie Devine, Fannie Lou Hamer, and Victoria Gray were civil rights activists with a crucial part in this work. Voting rights for people of color were better protected because of them. Devine appears on the left of a well-known picture. Hamer appears in the center. Gray appears on the right. Hawai‘i was a kingdom for a long time before 1900. In that year it came under the United States as a territory. A monarchy ruled the kingdom. Americans overthrew that monarchy in 1893. A new government was set up by them. Women were barred from voting under the new government. Before the overthrow, women had held wide political power. Families looked to them as important leaders. Communities looked to them as important leaders. The kingdom looked to them as important leaders. Work to win women’s votes back began at once. Clubs were founded by Hawaiian suffragists. Speeches were made. Rallies were held. Articles were written for newspapers in the Hawaiian language. Articles were also written for newspapers in English. By 1919, the wait felt far too long. Wilhelmina Kekelaokalaninui Widemann Dowsett led the protest. Say the long middle of her name as keh-keh-lou, then kah-lah-nee-NOO-ee. Five hundred suffragists followed her. Their banners read Votes for Women. They walked onto the floor of Hawai‘i’s House of Representatives. The next year, the Nineteenth Amendment brought women’s suffrage back. Full voting rights for Hawaiians waited until statehood in 1959. An important piece of the Voting Rights Act was removed by the U.S. Supreme Court in 2013. Before that removal, certain states needed permission from the federal government. Any new voting law required that permission. Those states had discriminated against voters of color in earlier years. Harmful laws could be stopped before damage was done.

7Each year, stickers reading I Voted are placed on the grave of Susan B. Anthony. The stickers honor her work. New voting laws have appeared in many states since 2013. Advocates say the laws lower the risk of voter fraud. Voting is made harder by some of the laws. People of color carry the heavier burden. One example is a smaller number of voting stations. The cuts fall in areas where residents are mostly people of color. Lines grow long at the stations that remain. The wait to vote can stretch a long time. Sometimes that wait passes ten hours. Women still meet voting barriers across wide stretches of this country. The Nineteenth Amendment did not finish the fight for women’s suffrage. The Voting Rights Act did not finish it either. Large progress has still been made. Suffragists and civil rights activists produced that progress. The record runs from the first meeting in Seneca Falls, New York, up to the present. An advocate is a person who works openly for a cause. A movement is many people organized to reach a goal or to cause a change. The people and groups inside that effort are also called a movement. An amendment is a change added to a document or to a law. A barrier is a rule, an obstacle, or a problem that stops progress. Suffrage is the right to vote. A suffragist supports giving the vote to people who lack it. The Constitution is the written set of rules and principles under United States law. A convention is a political meeting of delegates. Plans are formed there. Goals are set there. People are chosen there to run for office. Unjust means unfair. It can also mean undeserved. It can also mean dishonest. Wages are the regular pay a worker earns. The amount of time worked sets that pay. Discrimination means treating someone, or a whole group, in an unfair way. Race can be the basis. Age can be the basis. Sex can be the basis. Religion can be the basis. Other differences can be the basis. An election is the act of picking someone for government office by voting.

33. Japan, a Chain of Islands

1In Kagoshima, Japan, women dance for the Ohara Matsuri Dance Festival. Japan is an archipelago, which means its land is a chain of islands. The chain runs more than 2,400 kilometers, a distance of 1,500 miles. Asia’s eastern shore is nearby, and the islands sit in the Pacific Ocean. The country does not take up much space. California covers more ground than Japan does. Four large islands form the core, and thousands of small islands join them. China is one close neighbor. North Korea is another. South Korea is another. So is Russia’s Siberian region. The Sea of Japan separates Japan from Asia. People also call that water the East Sea. The East China Sea lies off the southwest. Open Pacific water lies off the eastern side. Maps of this part of the world also show the Yellow Sea, the city of Tokyo, and the continent of Asia.

2Bullet trains full of riders cross Tokyo's busiest station, and that traffic points to a larger pattern. The biggest island is Honshu, pronounced HON-shoo, and it is home to more than 80 percent of the people. The other main islands are Hokkaido, Shikoku, and Kyushu. Honshu holds the biggest cities, and nearly all of them stand on the east coast. The capital is Tokyo, pronounced TOH-kee-yoh, and it stands on Honshu as well. More than thirty-two million people live and work in this sprawling metropolis. Business and trade reach it from many countries, and few cities on Earth hold more people. Millions more live in the districts around it, a wider area often called Greater Tokyo. Those commuters ride very fast bullet trains, slower trains, and subways, which makes Tokyo the country's main hub for transportation. A railway station called Shinjuku, pronounced SHEEN-ju-ku, is said to have more travelers pass through than any other station on Earth. Beyond the capital, the population tops 127 million, and cities and towns hold over 90 percent of residents. Service jobs are common, including work in banks, restaurants, hospitals, and other service trades. Factory work is another path. Those plants turn out high-tech electronics, including cell phones, computers, and televisions, along with motor vehicles and machines. Japan leads in electronics production. Coastal cities also support a massive fishing industry, and the fleet of fishing ships is one of the world's largest. A cell phone goes by the name keitai, pronounced KAY-i-tie. Because cities are crowded, many people live in tall apartment buildings. Suburbs more often have houses and shorter apartment buildings. Where room exists, some homes have Japanese gardens kept with great care.

3Home life has its own order. In some homes, thick mats woven from rice straw cover the floors. The mats are called tatami, pronounced tah-TAH-mee. Shoes come off at the door, and that custom is widely kept. Some people sleep on a thin bed called a futon, pronounced FOO-tahn, which is unrolled onto the floor. In some houses, thin paper or bamboo screens split rooms instead of solid walls. Manners are ordered too. Rules cover greetings and the serving of guests. People bow rather than shake hands, and social status decides how many bows are given and how deep each bow goes. Language is just as layered as daily custom. The main language is Japanese, and the writing system is very complex. Hiragana is one script, and katakana is the other. About fifty symbols sit in each script, and each symbol stands for a sound. Kanji are used too. Those characters were borrowed from Chinese writing. Letters of the Latin alphabet, the same letters English uses, show up as well. A kanji picture can suggest fire, a mountain, or rain. Landform maps then fill in the high ground. They mark the Hidaka Mountains, the Ou Mountains, the Japanese Alps, and the Chugoku Mountains. They mark the Shikoku Mountains, the Kyushu Mountains, the Ryukyu Islands, and the Izu Archipelago. They also mark Mount Fuji. Rugged land, high mountains, and the water around the islands define the country. Mountains cover most of it, so nearly every city stands on flat coastal ground. A few islands are too steep and too dangerous for anyone to live on. Mount Fuji is the tallest peak, and that volcano stands on Honshu's south coast.

4The land is restless as well as steep. Geology here is among the least stable on Earth. More than seventy volcanoes are active, and about one thousand earthquakes strike each year. A quake under the sea can build a huge wave called a tsunami, pronounced tsoo-NAH-mee. Harbor wave is the Japanese meaning of the word. Water shapes the quieter land too. Fast rivers drop from the mountains onto the flats, and rice farms on the coastal plain draw water from those rivers. Some lakes sit near the ocean. Other lakes sit inside volcanic craters called calderas, which rain has filled over time. Climate shifts with the seasons and with place. Monsoons steer the climate and bring strong winds that may be stormy and wet or dry. Heat and cold differ a great deal from place to place. Cooler weather belongs to the north, and warmer weather belongs to the south. A similar spread of temperatures appears along the eastern side of the United States. Fall is the rainy season, and typhoons are a threat then. Northeastern Honshu includes the city of Ishinomaki. Pronounce it ih-SHIH, followed by noh-MAH-kee. The 2011 tsunami left that city in ruins. Belief and names reach just as far back as the landforms. Nippon is what the Japanese call their country, and sun's origin is what the name means. An old belief said the Sun's first rays fell on Japan, so another name is Land of the Rising Sun. The civilization goes back thousands of years, and strong influence came from Asian neighbors to the west. Some historians think the skill of growing rice arrived from China thousands of years ago. In the sixth century, Buddhism spread into Japan from Korea. Unique customs grew inside Japan as well. Shinto stands as one of the main religions, and plenty of people keep Shinto customs and Buddhist customs together. Shrines for Shinto and temples for Buddhism are spread through the land.

5Fights over land were common in the past, and fights over power were common too. Even so, the Yamato dynasty is the family that has supplied every emperor. That line has lasted more than two thousand years, and no other monarchy has run so long without a break. Control shifted in the twelfth century when a warrior took the country. That warrior was the first shogun, a kind of military king, and rule by shoguns lasted for centuries. Emperors from the imperial family were still allowed to serve, but their real power was scant. Samurai belonged to the same age, and the word is said SA-muh-rye. They were fierce in war. Honor, under a strict code, guided them, and skill in fighting was another mark of the samurai. Feudalism organized society then. A lord owned a piece of land and held power over its residents. Staying on that land meant peasants had to labor for the lord. Democracy arrived in the nineteenth century. Voters chose leaders for the parliament, which is called the Diet. An emperor remains in place today, but after World War II ended, his role became ceremony, not politics. The calendar turns from that political past to shared festivals. Rice planting is celebrated, harvest is celebrated, children's health is celebrated, and ancestors are honored. January 1 is New Year's Day. A full week of celebration, among the biggest holidays, leads up to it. House cleaning is a common task in that week. Debts are paid off, and cards wishing good luck are sent. Special toys go to children. Kimonos, the traditional robe, are often worn on January 1. Foods prepared for the day are eaten, and prayers for luck in the coming year are offered at a shrine nearby. Summer brings Obon, also called the Lantern Festival. Families use Obon to honor ancestors. Lit lanterns are placed on rivers, and lit candles are placed on lakes. The lights drift slowly off. People believe those lights lead the souls of ancestors home to rest.

6Rice matters greatly in daily meals. Small round cakes of rice are called mochi, pronounced MOH-chee, and people eat mochi as a snack. Food also comes heavily from the sea around the islands. Many dishes include fish, and many dishes include other seafood. In the city, fast food often means sushi, pronounced SOO-shee, or sashimi, pronounced sah-SHEE-mee. Raw fish is in both. Looks matter as much as flavor, in the view of many people. Several small dishes usually make up a meal. Soup is a frequent dish, and noodles sometimes appear.
