Chopsticks are the usual tools, as they are for neighbors in China. Wood is the material, and the sticks are long and thin. A sushi roll often wraps sticky rice around raw fish and around vegetables. Strict rules shape how some foods are cooked and how they are set out. Tea is the focus of one old ceremony. Every step is ordered, and the style is elegant. A room built for nothing else usually hosts the ceremony. That care for form sits beside a very modern capital. Tokyo's Imperial Palace has modern buildings pressed along the edge of its grounds. Mountains cover this country of islands, yet crowded cities still bustle with high-tech industry. Earthquakes shake the land, volcanoes blow, and typhoons storm through, but people continue to thrive. Long centuries taught them to use both soil and sea fully. Small fields yield tons of rice, and the ocean yields large catches of fish and of other seafood. Ancient custom sits beside the newest machines. The old world and the modern world are both at home here.

7The figures come first. Population stands at 127,250,000. The land area is 364,485 square kilometers, or 226,480 square miles. Shintoism and Buddhism are the main religions. Yen is the money. The Central Intelligence Agency gave these figures in July 2014, as did the World Bank. The glossary then names ideas used in the account. Buddhism is both a faith and a philosophy, drawn from Siddhartha Gautama, who taught in ancient India. A kimono is a Japanese robe or gown, often chosen for formal events. A metropolis is a city that is huge or especially important. A monsoon is a storm tied to the season, sometimes with heavy rain. Shinto is Japan's traditional faith, honoring many nature gods. In early Japan, a shogun was the ruler who held military power. A shrine is a holy or special place set up to honor a god or a person. A tsunami is an ocean wave big enough to destroy. The cause may be a quake under the water, a slide of land, or a volcano. A typhoon is a spinning storm, fierce with wind and rain, near the Indian Ocean or the western Pacific.

34. John Muir and the Wild Land

1This book follows a love of nature, then an early life, then a break into freedom. It moves to the High Sierra, to writing and public action, and to the Sierra Club now. A word list closes it. Settlers began, long ago, to colonize land. That land is the west of what we now call the United States. Many of them talked about conquering nature. Indigenous peoples in that region had cared for the land and guarded it for thousands of years. The settlers wanted the land cleared. They wanted natural resources used with no limit. John Muir walked in nature to draw nearer to what he called the world's heart. Few settlers shared his wish to keep nature safe. That love pulled him away from crowded places. For years he lived in cabins far from towns. It also pulled him to walk across huge parts of California. He traveled to many other places in the world as well. His picture of a clean, untouched wilderness shaped what he thought about nature. He strongly backed protection of the environment. He backed the idea of setting land aside so national parks could be made. He was an eloquent writer. He was an eloquent speaker too. He used those skills to move other people into the conservation movement. That movement could harm Indigenous peoples.

2John Muir's birthplace in Scotland is a museum now. The museum is about his life and his work. April 21, 1838, was his birthday. The town was Dunbar, in Scotland. Daniel, his father, worked in business. Daniel had also been a soldier. His mother, Anne, kept the home. She painted, wrote poems, and loved nature. John was the oldest son. The family had eight children. In 1849 John was eleven. Wisconsin became their new home inside the United States. Daniel wanted a farm and more money. Daniel was a strict disciplinarian. He made John and the other children work long hours at farm labor. They were allowed only one small meal each day. Their health suffered because of that. During those years John had no formal schooling. He still hungered to learn. Each morning he got up at 1:00 a.m. He was thrilled. Those hours gave him five extra hours every day for his own interests. On his own he studied literature and philosophy. He also studied mathematics and geometry. Daylight that was free was spent outdoors near the farm, among trees and open ground. In his teen years he became a skilled inventor. Some of the early-morning time went to his inventions. Those included water wheels and tools for reading the weather. A later invention was a rough alarm clock. At the set time, the machine got him up. The clock was also a desk. It was fixed to a bed that could collapse. At the chosen moment the machine lit a lamp. Then it set John down on the floor with a gentle drop.

3Young adulthood left Muir restless. He wanted to follow his loves and get off the farm. A neighbor had an idea when Muir was twenty-two. Muir should show his inventions at a fair. Its name was the Wisconsin State Agriculture Fair, and the year was 1860. The city was Madison. The inventions were a huge hit. The alarm clock stood out most. Jeanne Carr was one of the judges. She would later be his mentor. She gave him a cash prize. For the first time, Muir had some independence. He did not go home. He stayed in Madison and went to college. Jeanne Carr was a writer and an amateur botanist. Ezra Carr was a professor of geology. Both became Muir's friends and teachers. In the fall of 1860, his new life was going well. He studied botany, the science of plants. He later wrote that this study sent him rushing into woods and meadows, full of wild excitement. He also studied geology and chemistry. He did not finish a degree. A picture shows him as a young man. In 1863 he left Madison. He said he was going to the University of the Wilderness. He walked around Canada. He walked through parts of the northern Midwest. He watched nature closely. Odd jobs paid his way. In 1866 he took work as a mechanic. The city was Indianapolis, in Indiana. The next year an injury at work hurt his eye. For a time he could not see. He feared the blindness would last. Sight came back over about a month. Once he could see, he understood something. Inventing was keeping him from the thing he loved most. He quit the job. His promise was simple. The years ahead would be lived outdoors.

4Muir set out on September 1, 1867. He walked about 1,000 miles toward Florida. In kilometers, that distance is 1,609. In his writings he recalled the route he chose. It was the wildest path, the leafiest path, and the path with the fewest feet on it. Each day he walked 20 miles or more. In kilometers, a day was at least 32. He took detailed notes on plants during the trip. A map of the 1867 journey marks Indianapolis. It shows travel by train, by foot, and by boat. The map names Ohio, West Virginia, Indiana, and Kentucky. It also names Virginia, North Carolina, Tennessee, and South Carolina. Alabama, Georgia, the Atlantic Ocean, Florida, and Cedar Keys are on it too. The Sierra Nevada region is marked as well. He reached the Gulf Coast in a little more than seven weeks. A few days later he became very sick. He had caught malaria while crossing the swamps of Florida. The next January he was only partly well, and his health was still poor. He boarded a ship for Cuba. He hoped to reach the Amazon later and study the plants that grow there. He went to San Francisco instead. He arrived on March 28, 1868. He asked a person to point out the nearest way out of town. The man asked where Muir wanted to go. Muir answered that any wild place would do. On foot, he headed for California's Sierra Nevada. The beauty of that country filled him with awe. He was lonely without other people. He was still ecstatic to be among woods and fields. If he had to choose people or nature, nature usually won. A map shows California, San Francisco, and the Pacific Ocean. It also shows Ahwahnee, also called Yosemite Valley. His first trip into the High Sierra was short. He came back the next year. For a short time he worked as a shepherd. Then he ran a sawmill near Yosemite Falls. Say the place name yoh-SEH-muh-tee. Free hours went to exploring the land and to celebrating nature. Ralph Waldo Emerson's books gave him ideas. Emerson was a famous writer and lecturer. He often wrote about why people should connect with nature.

5In the early 1870s, Muir offered a theory that caused argument. He said moving glaciers had carved Yosemite Valley. Glaciers fascinated him, so he went far into the mountains seeking proof. An article on glaciers came from his pen. The month was October, and the year was 1871. The New York Tribune printed it. Over time, people confirmed his theory. Readers and scientists were starting to notice Muir and what he saw in nature. His Yosemite cabin drew other naturalists who wanted to meet him. Emerson came in 1871. Settlers used the name Yosemite for a place that already had a name. Ahwahnee Valley means a place like a gaping mouth. Say it ah-WAH-nee. It is the homeland of several Indigenous tribes. Those tribes include the Ahwahneechee, the Miwok, and the Paiute. A militia band drove out many Indigenous people by force. Yosemite, as a name for the area, came from that band. El Capitan is one feature of the valley. Yosemite Falls is another. Once Muir knew Yosemite so well, he gave more time to writing about what he had lived. Oakland, California, became his home in 1873. Magazine articles from there made him famous across the United States. He wrote with passion. He wanted readers to care about natural places. In April 1880 he married Louisa Strentzel. People called her Louie. He moved to her family's fruit ranch. The town was Martinez, California. He was a devoted husband. He was unhappy with the work the ranch demanded. He still longed for far-off places. With Louie's encouragement, he traveled again. A trip to Yosemite in 1889 led to two articles that many people read. The articles described the area's beauty. They also argued for keeping it safe. Other people helped by urging Congress to protect Yosemite. Those efforts built public support for conservation, and they worked. The next year Yosemite became a national park of the United States. It was the third. Yellowstone was earlier. Sequoia was earlier too. Muir and other conservationists saw a need. Yosemite needed a group to defend it. So did the rest of the Sierra Nevada.

6Muir helped start the Sierra Club in 1892. He became its first president. The club's purpose now includes guarding nature areas and wildlife across the United States. President Theodore Roosevelt wanted a meeting with Muir. The year was 1903. Roosevelt wanted to understand what far-off wild areas in the West needed. Muir felt honored. He took Roosevelt camping in Yosemite. Muir used the trip to argue for setting natural land aside as named parks. Because of that trip, more of Yosemite joined the national park. Roosevelt also protected many other natural places. Crater Lake National Park was one. Wind Cave National Park was another. Writing about where he had traveled filled more of his later years. Four universities gave him honorary degrees. One picture gathers him, Louisa, and the couple's two daughters. He also led a fight that lasted twelve years. He wanted to stop a planned dam from flooding Yosemite's Hetch Hetchy Valley. People tried to stop the project. Muir wrote a letter to Congress. Even so, the project was approved in 1913. The fight still woke the public to the need to protect natural land. It also helped bring about the National Park Service in 1916. That service gave stronger management to every national park in the United States. December 24, 1914, was the day John Muir died. He was already dead when the National Park Service began. His work still helped make that service real. A picture shows him as an older man. The word wilderness can suggest a natural place that nobody cares for and nobody lives in. Many places Muir called wilderness had people in them. Indigenous peoples had lived there and managed them since before written history. Muir did not always understand Indigenous ways of caring for land. He did not always agree with them. Indigenous peoples had used controlled burns for thousands of years. A controlled burn is a planned fire that keeps a forest healthy. Muir believed the only protection was to stop every fire. Evidence now shows that controlled burns work and that they matter.

7Many nature places carry John Muir's name. So do other establishments. Muir Woods National Monument is one example. It lies north of San Francisco. Environmental conservation in this country owes a great deal to John Muir. His drive to protect natural places helped create national parks. Those parks still keep beautiful land safe for people not yet born. Today the Sierra Club works on many kinds of environmental problems across the United States. It also works on climate problems around the world. The group works with Indigenous communities. Together they protect land and animals. The aim is to keep loved places safe and healthy for years ahead. To advocate is to support something or to recommend it. Speech or writing that is eloquent stays simple. It is also strong and elegant. Avid means showing a strong interest or a strong wish, and it can mean eager. Glaciers are large bodies of ice and packed snow that stay all year and creep downhill. Conservation is the protection of wild land and of the living things there. A mentor is an experienced teacher and adviser. Controversial means causing a lot of disagreement. A naturalist studies nature and its history. Plants and animals are the usual focus. If something is designated, people have marked it, named it, or picked it for a special job. Passion is intense enthusiasm or strong feeling. Ecstatic means feeling huge happiness, excitement, or delight. Remote means far away or cut off, and it can also mean in the distant past.

35. Jonas Salk

1Signs warned people to stay away from places hit by polio. In the spring of 1953, parents in the United States feared another summer of polio. The summer before, polio had sickened nearly fifty-seven thousand people. It left twenty thousand people, mostly children, unable to walk well or at all. Another three thousand people died. The virus often caused cold-like signs, but it could paralyze arms, legs, or chest muscles. Police quarantined homes and kept sick children in hospitals for months or years. Dr. Jonas Salk told the nation that a polio vaccine was on the way.

2Jonas Salk was born in New York in 1914 and entered Townsend Harris Hall at twelve. By sixteen he was studying at City College of New York and wanted to fight diseases. He finished his studies as the United States entered World War II. A flu epidemic had killed more than forty thousand U.S. soldiers in World War I. Dr. Thomas Francis hired Salk, and the U.S. Army began flu shots in 1943. Antibodies bind viruses, and a vaccine makes them without usually causing the disease. Salk's blood test worked only if the killed virus kept its outside shape. The flu vaccine saved thousands of lives, and at thirty-two he led a virus lab in Pittsburgh.

3The National Foundation for Infantile Paralysis raised money for polio survivors. Basil O'Connor gave Salk money because he was eager to get things done. If polio had many types, like the common cold, one vaccine would not protect people. Only humans get polio naturally, but monkeys can be infected in a lab. Old tests took months or years, but a light dose made antibodies and did not cause illness. If a virus grew after a known type was given, the strain was a new type. Albert Sabin and most scientists scoffed, yet the new method was faster and used fewer monkeys. With tissue cultures, the team found only three types in one year instead of three years.

4In 1950 most scientists believed only a live weak virus could give lasting protection. Salk trusted a killed virus, like his flu vaccine, because it could never cause polio. The foundation funded both methods, and fifty researchers tried to kill the virus safely. They used the same chemical used to kill the flu virus. They tried different temperatures and strengths. A million viruses can line up in one inch, so one live virus could cause the deadly disease. By 1952 the vaccine worked on monkeys, but 1930s vaccines had given children polio. He first tested survivors, who could not get polio again, and their antibodies increased. Then children who had never had polio made antibodies and had no ill effects.

5Visitors made work hard, so Salk explained the project once on the radio and became famous. Sabin, who worked at the University of Cincinnati, and others were furious. They said he was taking all the credit and that real scientists do not speak on the radio. Salk ignored them as polio sickened another thirty-five thousand people in 1953. Sure that it was safe, he vaccinated himself, his family, and lab workers. Salk agreed to a huge trial, because a delay could mean polio for thousands more children. He taught drug makers his method, but only two of five could make vaccine in time. Nearly two million children were in the 1954 trial, including vaccine, placebo, and control groups.

6On April 12, 1955, millions heard that Salk's vaccine was safe, effective, and potent. People cheered, yet some scientists never forgave him for forgetting his team. Vaccine distribution stopped after some children who got the shot developed polio. One maker had not killed the virus properly, so that vaccine was pulled and shots went on. By 1962 United States cases were fewer than one thousand, but the vaccine cost a lot. That year Sabin's cheaper live vaccine was approved and given on a sugar cube. By 1979 most remaining cases came from the live vaccine, and after 2000 only the killed vaccine was used. Salk had developed the first polio vaccine, and it finally eliminated the disease in the United States.

7In 1960 Salk founded the Salk Institute for scientists working for humanity. He studied cancer and HIV/AIDS until he died in 1995. The institute does cutting-edge research and has produced several Nobel Prize winners. Salk did not get a Nobel Prize, and he was never elected to the National Academy of Sciences. Sabin called the work pure kitchen chemistry and said Salk did not discover anything. He brought together years of polio research and new ideas to make the first vaccine. Time named him one of the hundred most important people of the twentieth century. The world thanked him for saving thousands of children, and he had helped humanity.

36. Kenya from Highland to Coast

1Five countries border Kenya, a nation on the continent of Africa. North of Kenya is Ethiopia. South of Kenya is Tanzania. Say Tanzania as tan-zuh, then NEE-uh. West of Kenya is Uganda. The northwest border is with South Sudan. East of Kenya is Somalia. Southeast, the shore meets the Indian Ocean. Kenya is crossed by the equator. Temperature does not swing enough to mark the seasons. What changes instead is how much rain falls, and how long it falls. Place still changes the temperature. Higher mountains are the cooler spots. Lower places are warmer. Nearby labels on a map include Lake Turkana, Lake Victoria, Nairobi, and Mombasa. A wider map also takes in Europe, Asia, and the rest of Africa.

2The capital is Nairobi. Say it nie-ROH, then bee. Railway builders created the town as a station on the new rail line. Today the city holds more than three million residents. Industry for the whole country centers here. Clothing comes from its factories. Furniture comes from them. Bread comes from them. Cornmeal comes from them. Other processed foods come from them. East African farms sit close enough that raw crops reach the food plants without a long haul. A wildlife park in the backyard is rare for a capital. Nairobi National Park is that park, and it is a large game reserve. The park begins just 8 kilometers out, a trip of 5 miles. Lions can be seen there. Giraffes can be seen there. Reptiles and birds can be seen there. A hotel close to Nairobi offers something unusual. Breakfast at Giraffe Manor may be shared with Rothschild’s giraffes. Dinner may be shared with them too. This rare giraffe has a large sanctuary, and the hotel sits on it. Windows are no barrier. The giraffes push their heads inside. Visits often come in the morning. Visits often come again in the evening. People in the nation number more than forty-five million. That count places Kenya thirty-first among countries. Tribes, meaning ethnic groups, number more than thirty. African ancestors are the background of nearly every Kenyan. Each tribe has its own language, and people speak it. A national language is spoken as well, at least one of them. English is a national language. Swahili is the other, said swah-HEE-lee. English is the language of government. It is the language of higher education. It is the language of large business corporations. Swahili is the language of primary schools. It is also used in many smaller businesses. Only a few Kenyans have ancestors in the Middle East, in Europe, or in Asia. Soccer, which many also call football, is a sport people love to watch and to play. International races have made Kenyan runners renowned. Olympic gold medals have been won by those runners. Young and old both play bao. The game uses a wooden board. Small cups in the board hold seeds or stones. Board is the Swahili meaning of bao.

3Fibers come from sisal after the plants are crushed and then dried. Weaving joins those fibers. Countryside life is the pattern for about 75 percent of the people. Farming is how most of them earn a living. Family meals come from food they grow. Crops are also raised to sell. Tea is one sale crop. Coffee is another. Corn is another. Sweet potatoes are another. Wheat is another. Flowers are grown as well. A natural insecticide is made from many of those flowers. Sisal ranks as another major crop. Ropes and mats are made from its strong fibers. Income also comes heavily from tourism. Yearly visitors number over a million. Clothing such as the kanga is made from cotton, another important crop. A kanga is a large cloth full of color. Women use it as a shawl. They use it as a headscarf. They use it as a skirt. A child can be carried in a kanga. A proverb is printed on many kangas. One of them warns that digging a well can leave the digger inside it. Mapped landforms include Lake Turkana and the Chalbi Desert. They include the Great Rift Valley, Mount Kulai, and Mount Elgon. They include the Merti Plateau and Mount Kenya. They include Lake Victoria and Nairobi. They include the Loita Hills and the Yata Plateau. They include the Chyulu Range and the Nyiri Desert. They include Mount Kilimanjaro, the Indian Ocean, the Taita Hills, and Mombasa. Western Kenya is highland country. Those highlands are split by the Great Rift Valley. Earth’s crust is cracked there for 6,437 kilometers, or 4,000 miles. Width of the crack runs about 50 to 130 kilometers, or 30 to 80 miles. The floor holds lakes. It holds volcanoes. It holds deserts. Escarpments, which are cliffs, form the walls. Height of those walls reaches 2,700 meters, or 9,000 feet, above the floor. Farming rain falls in the highlands. Only one other area gets enough rain for crops. The highlands are therefore home to most Kenyans. Inside the highlands, Mount Kenya outranks every other peak.

4Farming rain also falls on the coastal lowlands. Coconuts thrive in that coastal belt. Cashews thrive there too. Beaches of white sand follow the shore. Forests of mangrove follow it too. The Sahara is larger than the deserts of northern Kenya. They are not as large as other deserts in Africa. Nomadic tribes, few in number, live in these dry lands. Sheep and goats are their main herds. The deserts’ western edge holds Lake Turkana. Fossils found there belong to the earliest humans. Lake Rudolf was the lake’s former name. No desert lake on Earth is larger. The savanna is a landscape Kenya is known for. Wildebeests graze in large herds. Zebras graze in large herds. Gazelles graze there, along with other animals. Grass on the savanna can reach 1.5 meters. That height is 5 feet. Flat-topped acacia trees, thorny ones, are scattered across the view. Say acacia as uh-KAY-shuh. Grasslands lie on both the western side of the highlands and the eastern side. Early humans from this region did not all stay. Some left and settled in other places. Those who remained were largely cut off from the wider world until the first century ad. Trade in East Africa was then opened by people from the Middle East. People from Asia opened trade there too. Hundreds of years of coastal trade included enslaved people. Ivory was traded. Other goods were traded. Elephants are one source of ivory. Hippos are another source. Selling ivory is banned by many countries today. The late 1400s brought people from Europe. British Empire rule became official in 1920, when Kenya was made a colony. Anger followed when much native land was handed to white settlers. Some of those settlers came from Great Britain. Others came from South Africa. Independence became the fight in 1952. British rule ended in 1963, and the country was free. Presidential power was very large for a few decades after that. The government had a single political party. An end to that inequality was what many Kenyans wanted. Less power for the president, and more for the people, came with a new constitution in 2010.

5Safari vans, with guides, carry tourists who search for animals to watch and to photograph. Rare animals are part of why the wildlife is famous. Protection comes from more than fifty parks and reserves. Safaris draw millions of tourists each year. Rhinoceroses are among the animals they come to see. Lions are among them. Leopards are among them. Hippos are among them. Elephants are among them. Elephants here come in two kinds. Highland rainforests are home to the forest elephant. Many settings, deserts included, are home to the savanna elephant. Rhinoceroses also come in two kinds. One is called the white rhino. The other is called the black rhino. Gray is the real color of both. Eyesight is poor. The sense of smell is sharp. The sense of hearing is sharp. Weight averages about 3,600 kilograms. That weight is 8,000 pounds. Running speed can reach 45 kilometers in an hour. That pace is 28 miles in an hour. Grazing animals, including zebras and gazelles, crowd the savanna. A yearly trek of wildebeests and zebras is among the most amazing sights in Africa. Wildebeests on the move number about 1.5 million. Zebras on the move number about 300,000. Distance covered is more than 1,600 kilometers, or 1,000 miles. The path leaves the Serengeti Plain and reaches Kenya’s savanna. Say Serengeti as ser-en-GEH-tee. Food is the goal. Water is the goal. Hardship marks the journey. Deaths among wildebeests reach about 250,000 a year. Hunters follow behind the herds. The followers include lions. They include leopards. They include cheetahs. They include hyenas. They include wild dogs. Few sights match the herds as they cross the Mara River. Hippopotamuses live in the Mara and in rivers like it. A hippo moving in water can seem serene. It can seem harmless. Human deaths in Africa are caused more often by hippos than by lions. Aggression and a strong sense of territory define them. Water is where they stay for most hours. Night is when they leave the water to feed. Grass is eaten. Other plants are eaten. Crops are sometimes eaten, and people then come into conflict with the animals. Danger is increased by the size of their teeth.

6Wildlife is not limited to the savanna. Lake Nakuru, inside the Great Rift Valley, draws flamingos. Both greater flamingos and lesser flamingos come. Counts reach the thousands. Counts sometimes reach the millions. Heads go down into the water as these wading birds feed. Tiny plants are the food. Tiny animals are the food. Most hours of a flamingo’s life are spent in water. Land is the only home of a different African bird. No bird on Earth is larger than the ostrich. No bird lays a larger egg. An egg may weigh 1.35 kilograms. That weight is 3 pounds. Stretching flat on the ground is one way an ostrich hides from predators. Running is another way. Speed can reach 70 kilometers in an hour, or 43 miles in an hour. Powerful kicks are the defense if hiding and running both fail. Large predators in Kenya are several, and they are dangerous. Humans are harmed most by two animals that are actually tiny. About one third of Kenya is infested by the tsetse fly and the mosquito. Say tsetse as TSEE-tsee. The tsetse fly carries sleeping sickness. The mosquito carries malaria. The name sleeping sickness comes from a symptom. Deep sleepiness takes hold of a sick person. A bite transfers a very small organism. Disease follows from that organism. Severe illness follows from malaria. Severe illness follows from sleeping sickness. Government buildings are numerous in Nairobi. Museums are there. Several universities are there. Fame rests on safaris and on the savanna, but Kenya holds much besides. Some animal species here are found in no other place. Industry for the entire country centers on the capital. Exports leave that city for markets around the world. Natural beauty is found in abundance. Fascinating cultures are found in great number.

7The population figure is 52,573,973. Area of the land is 569,140 square kilometers. The same area is 219,746 square miles. Nairobi is listed as the capital. Swahili is listed among the primary languages. English is listed among them too. Christianity is listed as the primary religion. The Kenyan shilling is listed as the currency. Both the Central Intelligence Agency, in 2019, and the World Bank are the sources of these figures. Moving about with no lasting home is what nomadic means. Small yellow flowers, or white ones, mark the acacia, a tree of warm climates. Wild plants and wild animals are protected inside a reserve. Halfway between the North Pole and the South Pole, an imaginary line circles Earth. That line is the equator. Searching for wild animals is the purpose of a safari. Two levels of land can be split by an escarpment, a steep slope or cliff. A sanctuary is a place of safety. Tusks of elephants, and of other animals that have tusks, are made of a white material called ivory. Few trees stand on a savanna, which is flat grassland. Ropes use the strong fibers of sisal leaves, and the plant is grown for those leaves. Shallow salt water or swamps are where a mangrove, a tropical tree, can grow. Branches of a mangrove send out roots. Living things that move in a regular, predictable way, from one location to another, are on a migration.

37. Labor Day

1Labor Day is the first Monday in September. It is one last time for summer fun before school starts for many children. Banks and government offices, such as post offices, are closed. Many people have the day off and may have a party in the backyard. The United States government set the day aside to honor workers from the past and the present. The labor movement is workers joining together to improve their lives and demand change. Marchers carry a banner honoring union workers in the New York City Labor Day parade.

2Horses provided much of the energy used in farming before machines. During the Industrial Revolution, machines did many hand jobs faster. Farmers used machines to plant and harvest, which often raised profits but put many small family farmers out of business. Factories made cloth more cheaply than home businesses, so many people left small towns for city factory jobs. In 1925 a farmer used a tractor to plow a field, and in 1911 women and children worked as spinners in a Mississippi factory. Recent immigrants and formerly enslaved people often faced low pay and unsafe work, and workers who objected were easily fired. No laws limited work hours, and the average factory worker worked 100 hours a week. The labor movement began because workers wanted higher pay, fewer hours, and safer conditions.

3Four children worked in a tobacco factory in 1917, where they earned around $1.20 a day. Child labor rose because children could run machines, be paid less, be easier to control, and fit in tight spaces. By the early 1800s about two million children worked in mills, often from poor families who relied on their pay. Children often started before age ten, worked six days for up to eighteen hours, missed school, and many became ill. Workers formed unions to bargain for better hours, wages, conditions, and benefits, and they sometimes called strikes. Early unions were for all workers in theory, but later they split by trade, and race and gender often separated jobs. One group wanted a union for people who ran and fixed machines but settled for a white-only union. In 1902 Black workers were only 3 percent of union members, and union seals show which goods union workers made.

4In 1869, President Ulysses S. Grant guaranteed government employees an eight-hour workday and a stable wage. He encouraged private sector workers to seek the same guarantee. Workers marched on strike for an eight-hour day in New York City in 1872. Many employers refused after Illinois signed an eight-hour law for all workers except farm workers. On May 4, 1886, police used heavy force on eighty thousand people at Haymarket Square, later called the Haymarket Affair. Women were mostly left out of the early movement. New York textile workers formed the Ladies Garment Workers Union and showed that women belonged in it. President Roosevelt later listened to activists. The Fair Labor Standards Act limited hours and set a minimum wage. That wage was 25 cents in 1938. As of 2025, the federal minimum was $7.25. Union membership dropped after the 1980s. Strikes rose from 2020, and about 9.9 percent of workers were union members in 2024.

5The act also required extra pay for hours beyond a usual week. Overtime means one and a half times usual pay for each hour over forty. The rule was meant to discourage overwork and encourage more hiring. The act also set safety rules and banned most kinds of child labor. The minimum age was sixteen during school hours, fourteen for some after-school jobs, and eighteen for dangerous jobs. A worker paid $15.00 an hour earns $600 in a forty-hour week. With ten overtime hours, that worker earns $825. Earlier strikes had already shown the cost of low pay. In the 1894 Pullman Strike, railroad workers struck for over two months because wage cuts hurt their families. Mother Jones helped striking coal miners and worked to end child labor. A. Philip Randolph organized sleeping car porters, called for a march on Washington, and helped inspire the civil rights movement. Eugene Debs helped found major unions and ran for president five times. Dolores Huerta helped a 1965 strike of 5,000 grape farmers win safer conditions.

6Workers marched in a Labor Day celebration in Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, in 1882. That was the same year the holiday was first celebrated, in New York City. The Central Labor Union of New York organized the day to give working people a day off. In 1887, Oregon became the first state to make Labor Day an official holiday. Thirty more states passed similar laws before the United States made it a nationwide holiday in 1894. Canada also celebrates Labor Day on the first Monday in September, and Canadian ironworkers march in a parade in Toronto. More than sixty other countries celebrate International Workers' Day on May 1. That day was inspired by the Haymarket Affair. Like Labor Day, it honors workers, and activists teach people their rights and work to stop unfair labor practices.

7The American Welding Society was founded in 1919. Welders use thick leather gloves and a special helmet to protect themselves. Today, labor movements still work to improve how workers are treated and paid. The COVID-19 pandemic arrived in the United States in early 2020. It brought attention to essential healthcare, food service, and retail workers. Many people have called for a higher federal minimum wage to support these workers. In 2022, the United States raised the minimum wage for federal workers to $15 an hour, but it stayed $7.25 for other workers. On Labor Day, take a moment to honor workers who build better lives for themselves, their communities, and the country.

38. Leonardo of Vinci, Maker and Questioner

1This book follows a prankster who never had an ordinary class. It tells his childhood, his many skills, and his questions. It tells what he invented, how he learned by looking, and how notebooks were lost. Hard words are explained at the end. Leonardo was born in 1452. The place was Vinci, a small village in Italy. He liked pranks. Curiosity sent him on adventures even while he was small. Once he shaped bizarre creatures from soft wax. He filled them with air. He set them loose in a garden. The people there were surprised and frightened. Another time he painted a monster on a wooden shield. He hid the shield in a dark room to scare his father. Father walked in. Window light fell only on the monster. Father believed the monster was alive. He fled.

2One picture is a self-portrait of da Vinci. His life lasted 67 years. Other children his age were not like him. Charm was one difference. He was gentle. He was handsome. Animals were dear to him. He loved them so much that meat stayed off his plate. Vegetarians were very rare then. People found his refusal of meat strange. Other differences showed too. Math ideas far beyond his years made sense to him. Even as a child, his music already sounded adult. His art already looked adult. Home, in childhood, was with his grandparents and his uncle. His father, Ser Piero, was a businessman with big plans. Most of Ser Piero's time was spent 40 miles away, in Florence, Italy. Visits to the family home near Vinci were rare and short. With father gone so often, Leonardo stayed with Francesco, his father's brother. The uncle let him roam fields and woods by the farm. Francesco was patient. He tried to answer every question. He taught plant names and herb uses. He taught signs that weather was coming. He taught how local wildlife behaved. Reading, writing, and basic math came from the village priest. All of this left Leonardo thirsty for still more knowledge.

3A picture shows markets on the Ponte Vecchio Bridge. It also shows a view from the Duomo in Florence. Teenage Leonardo went to Florence so he could live near his father. The father had just married. Florence held a major place in Europe's rebirth. People called that rebirth the Renaissance. The Renaissance took place in the fourteenth to seventeenth century. The city held artisans and sculptors, and it held poets and musicians, all living and working there. Leonardo wanted the life of an artist. Father agreed he could serve a local artist as an apprentice, which means an assistant. The shop belonged to master Andrea del Verrocchio. There Leonardo learned many methods of painting and of sculpting. He even mixed his own paints from pigments and egg whites. He rose to head assistant. Soon his painting passed Andrea's. Respect as an artist followed. Painting pleased him. People in Florence sent many commissions, meaning requests, for portraits. Some complained that he did not treat the jobs as serious. They said one task could not hold him long enough to finish. Customers grew upset while paintings sat unfinished. Leonardo was unbothered. New projects filled his mind.

4Art was only one of his gifts. Solving problems was another. Uncle Francesco had taught him to ask questions and hunt for answers. If trouble appeared, a solution should be hunted too. Leonardo followed that teaching. Painting brought problems, and he set out to solve them. Candlelight and oil-lamp light were too dim for the work. He built a new lamp. Water in the lamp magnified the flame. Light then filled the whole room. A sketch shows that oil lamp. Fine detail strained his eyes and hurt his sight. He changed his eyeglasses so he could see better. Other inventions answered other problems. He made pliers. He made a wrench. Larger projects had parts that needed tightening and loosening. The new tools did that work. No one had seen either tool before his time. He designed a clock that showed minutes, not hours alone. The clock would keep him on schedule. The prankster in him also built a very funny alarm clock. Here is the way it worked. A rope was tied to a sleeper's ankles. The rope's other end held a bucket hung from the ceiling. Overnight, water slowly filled the bucket. A full bucket was heavy enough to yank the sleeper's legs straight up. Waking up was certain. What he made was often written, described, and drawn in notebooks. The notebooks open a window on his thinking. They show his way of solving problems. A picture shows a replica of the alarm clock.

5Problem-solving was a great strength. Curiosity is what made the strength so great. The childhood wish to explore never left him. He had explored that way in fields and woods by his uncle's farm. Study delighted him. Once he wanted a tree's age. He studied the tree. He found that rings in the trunk can tell anyone how old the tree is. Another time he asked whether solar power was possible. Concave mirrors bounced sunlight and heated water. Flight stayed a lifelong question. Birds were among his favorite notebook subjects. He studied them and sketched them. If he could grasp how a bird flew, he thought the knowledge could be used. A machine might then let a person fly. Sketches of an early helicopter followed. A child's toy was the base of those designs. He also drew a machine like a bird. That machine would have been far too heavy to leave the ground. A parachute was sketched too. His best flight design was a hang glider. The hang glider appears in his books. Some people think he really tried a flying machine on January 2, 1496. The Wright brothers' first flight came four hundred years later.

6An early sketch shows the hang glider. Not every invention was built. Ideas were drawn with care in the notebooks. Precise details and instructions were written for each one. He believed a drawing was the fullest way to understand a thing. So he drew objects from many angles. Forms showed in the drawings. Functions showed too. Some sketches are so careful and so beautiful that books today use them as examples. Learning never stopped being a passion. He read every book he could reach. He borrowed books. Libraries held him for hours. Unknown words were written down while he read. He could learn them afterward. One notebook held over nine thousand words. The words ran backward, in his own mirror writing. Some experts think the backward hand kept ink from smearing. He was left-handed. No teacher forced his other hand to do the writing. Some left-handed people did face that force. He simply wrote in the way that felt easiest.

7Every study opened more questions. It opened more interests. Light, and the way light lets a person see, led him to the human eye. The eye led him to the human body. Birds led him to flight. Mathematics led him to mechanics. Currents of air and of water led him to power and to energy. Each new fact made him want another. Why and how were favorite questions after he finished a book. The questions started fresh thinking. They sent him toward new interests. People still study the notebooks. Modern builders have made prototypes, meaning models, from those notes. A number of the prototypes really work. A museum of his work might show a prototype of the clock. It might show a miniature replica of a cathedral design. It might show a small helicopter model. It might show the many hydraulic machines, meaning machines run by water, that he drew. The drawings in the notebooks are careful and faithful. One picture is a cathedral design. In 1993, and in later years, scholars gathered to test the hang glider designs. The prototypes flew beautifully. Modern materials that are light made them work especially well.

8People of the 1400s could hardly imagine many of his inventions. Today a door that opens by itself is ordinary. In the fifteenth century, a door yanked open without warning would have been a novelty. His design used ropes and weights for that pull. It might even have felt a little scary. He could throw an object's image onto a wall. He needed a box, plus a small lens and a candle. People were amazed. His camera obscura let images be caught on paper. Feats like these led some of his own students to accuse him of witchcraft. The ideas were so unfamiliar that the work turned controversial. Probably for that reason, the pope in Rome would not commission anything from him. As a master craftsman he signed on with the guild. He then trained apprentices of his own. Students often finished paintings he had left undone. The arrangement suited him. He would rather invent gadgets and improve them. He would rather study what he loved. Painting portraits all the time did not appeal. When he was not painting and not inventing, he was watching. Birds, water, people, and anything else of interest held his eye. He believed knowledge comes from knowing how to see. His ideas were not always right. Wide watching of how the world works still produced creations. For his time, those creations were amazingly accurate. He also drew animals that lived only in his imagination.

9Some ideas stayed private. Submarine designs were among them. He feared a submarine might be used to kill people who had done no wrong. Much of his knowledge was shared anyway. For years, one theory carried the name Leonardo's law. His theory said an object stays at rest or in motion unless a force acts on it. Sir Isaac Newton later wrote a mathematical formula for the same principle. He did that work in the seventeenth century. People then called it Newton's first law of motion. A picture can show Isaac Newton. Leonardo meant to publish his writing as an encyclopedia. He stayed so busy that the book never appeared. Death came on May 2, 1519. He was 67. Count Giovanni Francesco Melzi was a devoted student. Melzi inherited the notebooks and the documents. He inherited many other belongings too. He tried to sort thousands of papers into the books his master had dreamed of. Only one book was finished. Its title was A Treatise on Painting. It held Leonardo's thoughts about art and about the artist. Publication came many years after Leonardo's death. When Francesco Melzi died, the papers went to his son. The son cared nothing for them. They sat in the younger Melzi's attic for years. Later he gave much of the work away or sold it to collectors. The papers scattered across the world. A picture shows a page from A Treatise on Painting.

10For a long time, most people knew Leonardo da Vinci only through his art. The Last Supper was one masterpiece. La Gioconda was another. Americans more often call La Gioconda The Mona Lisa. Nothing pushed people to see him as more than a wonderful painter. The only book of his in print was about art. During the Industrial Revolution of the 1880s, the documents began to surface again. People met ideas that were new and original. Many creations of his had, by that point, been built again by later people. Other people received the credit. Fields of study could have moved ahead centuries sooner because of his ideas. That chance was lost. Research and drawings filled 13,000 pages, and half of those pages had disappeared. Famous people have studied the art and the notebooks, or written about them. The playwright Oscar Wilde is one. The poet William Butler Yeats is one. The psychologist Sigmund Freud is one. The scientist Albert Einstein is one. The artist Andy Warhol is one. A biographer wrote that Leonardo has always been studied more than he has been understood. Journals say little about his private life or his feelings. Most works and notebooks carry no date. So the timing of events in his life is confused. The reasons for those events are confused too.

11The biographies disagree with one another. Fascination with his life and his discoveries still continues. He made over 1,500 careful drawings of the human body. That output was a great achievement. His gift to science through anatomical pictures alone cannot be priced. His life was a hunt for knowledge. He took on problems and solved them himself. One of his sayings shaped that life. The saying was that knowledge of all things is possible. Anatomical means related to how a body is built. An apprentice is a beginner who learns a skill or a trade from a master. A camera obscura is a dark, closed space. Outside images pass through a small lens and land on a facing surface. Commissions are jobs someone asked for and paid for. A guild is a group of people who share a trade. It is a union. Hydraulic means run by water. The Industrial Revolution shifted how goods were made. Hand work based in homes gave way to large factory production. Innovative means new and original. A novelty is something new and unusual. Prototypes are first forms used as models for later making. The Renaissance was a historical time marked by a return of classical art, literature, and architecture. A replica is a copy of a work of art, often smaller than the original.

39. Living and Working Far Above Earth

1Astronaut Robert L. Curbeam Jr. smiles for the camera. He is aboard the International Space Station. The smile comes after a successful space walk. This book tells about weightless living. It tells about the race to space. It tells about space stations. It tells about an astronaut's life. It tells about sleeping. It tells about eating. It tells about using the toilet. It tells about bathing and cleaning teeth. It tells about free time. It tells about exercise. It tells about the return to Earth. Word meanings come last. Sunrise is the moment you wake. Earth spreads outside a small window. It is a bright blue sphere. It takes a moment to get free of the sleeping bag. The space around you is small. You open the door. You grab a handle. You flip yourself over. A gentle push sends you flying. The push carries you across the room. Another crew member floats by. The crew member hands you a packet of coffee. The greeting is good morning, spoken in Russian. You thank the crew member. You yawn. You are tired because you have not slept much. Your stomach has not adjusted yet. The cause is the lack of gravity. You are also incredibly excited. A space shuttle is shown docking with the International Space Station. You are about to begin your first full day of work aboard that station. You have trained for years. Even simple activities are complicated without gravity. Eating is one of them. Bathing is one of them. Going to the bathroom is one of them. It is almost as if you must learn them all over again. The transport back to Earth is not coming for another six months. Starting now is the sensible choice.

2The race toward space took off in the 1950s and the 1960s. The first person in space was a cosmonaut from Russia. His name was Yuri Gagarin. Russia was then part of the Soviet Union. He orbited Earth once. He landed safely after 108 minutes. The day was April 12, 1961. In August of that same year, another Soviet cosmonaut flew. His name was Gherman Titov. He orbited Earth more than 17 times. The flight lasted about 25 hours. Technology advanced quickly. People could go farther. They could stay longer. In 1968, United States astronauts aboard Apollo 8 orbited the Moon. The flight was a success. Apollo 11's crew set foot on the Moon on July 20, 1969. Technology kept advancing. Space programs built vehicles that let people stay in space for days. Some stays lasted weeks. Scientists were also working on something more lasting. They wanted a structure in space where people could live for months. They wanted stays that could last years. The Soyuz spacecraft were first created in the 1960s. They are still used to carry people to the International Space Station. They also carry people home. 1971 was the year the Soviet Union sent up the first space station. It was called Salyut 1. Three cosmonauts stayed aboard for three weeks. It spent 175 days in space. Then it fell out of orbit. It burned up while reentering Earth's atmosphere. Skylab went up from the United States in 1973. Skylab supported three missions. It was abandoned in 1974. Its fall back to Earth came in 1979. A picture shows a space shuttle docking with Mir. Mir's first piece went up in 1986 from the Soviet Union. Mir is said MEER. Only then did a space station keep working for more than a few years. Fifteen years was how long Mir circled Earth. That was three times longer than first planned. It hosted scientists from many different countries. Russian scientist Dr. Valeri Polyakov spent 438 days in space in a row. The stay covered 1994 and 1995. He lived on Mir during that record stay. He orbited Earth 7,075 times. His record still stands.

3Work on a newer station was already underway before Mir's 2001 fall. The new station would be larger. Countries from all over the world worked together. The United States was one. Russia was one. Japan was one. Canada was one. The first section of the International Space Station was launched into orbit in 1998. People call the station the ISS. Later years brought more sections. Future plans call for still more sections. By 2012 its length was 357 feet. That length is 108.8 meters. It weighed almost 1 million pounds. That weight is 453,592.3 kilograms. It is the largest human-made object in space. People can see it in the night sky. They do not need a telescope. People have stayed on the ISS without a break since November 2, 2000. Over the years, more than 240 people have visited. They came from nineteen countries. The station has more living space than the average house with five bedrooms. The time an astronaut spends there varies. Many stay for about three to six months. That can mean six months of living without gravity. It means six months of working without gravity. The space is enclosed. Three to six other people share it. Crew members might come from different countries. They might speak different languages. They still have to work together as a team. This can be extremely stressful for the mind. It can be extremely stressful for the body. An astronaut practices a repair on an underwater model of the ISS. Space walks are practiced ahead of time by working underwater. Astronauts attend school for many years before training begins. They often earn the highest degree in their field. Physical training includes a special airplane flight. The flight simulates weightlessness. A space suit can be on them for as long as seven hours. They work underwater in a huge pool. The pool holds 6.2 million gallons. That volume is 23.5 million liters. They work on life-size models of space vehicles. Once they are on the ISS, they still must adjust. Life there is completely weightless. When astronauts first arrive, bodies need time to adjust. Minds need time too. Illness hits some people because of the change. The ISS comes with special bags for astronauts who feel ill. One side of the bag is cloth for wiping up. An extra bag is attached. That bag can be sealed for easy disposal.

4Without gravity, astronauts cannot jump into bed. They cannot curl up under a pile of blankets. The ISS has six small sleep pods instead. Each pod contains a sleeping bag with armholes. Each bag is tied securely to the wall. The tie keeps the astronaut from floating away while asleep. The lack of gravity often causes a certain kind of dream. Astronauts dream of falling. Because of the station's orbit, the crew sees many sunrises. They see many sunsets. The number is sixteen of each every day. Night is the usual time for people to sleep. They are used to being awake during the day. The extra sunrises and sunsets can cause serious sleep problems. The station has a shades-down period. During that time it is dark. It is quiet. The quiet dark lets the astronauts get the sleep they need. A picture shows astronaut Alvin Drew getting ready to sleep on the ISS. Another picture shows breakfast on the station. The meal is eggs and sausage on a tortilla. Coffee is part of it. Early astronauts ate gels. They ate pastes. They ate freeze-dried food. The food had the nutrition they needed. It did not taste very good. Today astronauts eat many foods that most people enjoy. Chicken is one. Fruits are one. Peanut butter is one. Brownies are one. The ISS has no refrigerator. All food must be packaged so it will not spoil. An oven is available on the station. Some food can be eaten as it is. Peanut butter is an example. Other food needs water added. Spaghetti is an example. Salt comes in liquid form. Pepper comes in liquid form too. Dry particles would float away. They could damage the delicate equipment. Once the food is ready, swallowing still feels strange. Astronaut Piers Sellers compared it to eating while lying on one side.

5Things float around in space. The bathroom is not simple to use. Toilet use takes special training for astronauts. Two parts make up each ISS toilet. A tube with a nozzle handles liquid waste. For solid waste, they must stay in place. Foot straps help. Handles help. They position themselves over an opening that is 4 inches wide. That width is 10 centimeters. An average toilet on Earth has a much wider opening. The opening is 12 to 16 inches. That is 30.5 to 40.6 centimeters. The space toilet uses light suction. The suction moves the waste from the opening or the tube. The waste goes to storage in another part of the station. Liquid waste goes into a tube on the wall. Solid waste goes into a base set on the floor. The ISS has two toilets. They have stopped working more than once. Earth has to send the parts before a toilet can be fixed. Those parts can take months to arrive. Hair washing on the ISS is demonstrated by astronaut Karen Nyberg. Astronauts wash their bodies with water and special soap on a cloth. The hair shampoo they use needs no water. They clean their teeth with toothpaste they can swallow when they finish. Earlier stations had an actual shower. Skylab was one of them. An enclosed space was where those astronauts stepped in. A tube squirted water. Soap and a cloth were used for the wash. A second tube pulled the dirty water away.

6Free time is very scarce for astronauts on the ISS. They still find ways to entertain themselves. They might play cards. They might read. They might write. They do other things too. Keeping in touch with loved ones is important. Astronauts can make calls. They can use video chat. Sometimes they hold live video chats. Questions from students worldwide are answered by the crew. They give tours of the station. When they have nothing else to do, many simply look out the windows. They take photos. The view is pretty amazing. It is hard to blame them for looking. A guitar is played on the ISS by astronaut Chris Hadfield. During his last visit he even made a music video. For astronauts, exercise matters a huge amount. On Earth, bones and muscles must work against gravity during exercise. That work makes them stronger. In space there is almost no gravity. It takes very little strength to move things. Those same things might be extremely heavy on Earth. It takes very little effort. Because movement is so easy, bones weaken over time. Muscles weaken too. Daily workouts are required if the decline is to slow. The workout lasts at least two hours. The ISS gym has special equipment. Straps on a treadmill tug the runners downward. Other equipment uses resistance. The resistance helps them stay strong. Most astronauts still lose muscle after that hard daily workout. Muscle mass drops by 20 to 30 percent. The loss comes during a six-month stay. A picture shows astronaut Sunita Williams. She exercises on a treadmill on the ISS.

7Coming home from the ISS does not end the work. Recovery from a long time in space takes a long time. A six-month stay calls for about six weeks of rehabilitation. The goal is to regain lost muscle. Bones can take a whole year to fully recover. Astronauts must endure hard work. They must endure inconvenience. They still know the time in space is worth it. They know the work there is worth it. A 2021 picture shows Japanese astronaut Soichi Noguchi with his thumb up. He has just returned to Earth. His spacecraft home was a Crew Dragon capsule. During the time in space, scientists monitor the astronauts. They monitor them during rehabilitation too. They watch physical health. They watch mental health. The information has already helped astronauts live healthier lives. It has helped them live happier lives. We are looking farther into space. Some places may take months to reach. Some may take years. Some may take decades. This vital information might mean success for future exploration. It might also mean failure for future space exploration. These words have special meanings in this book. Astronauts train for travel and work off Earth. Orbited means revolved around another object. A cosmonaut is trained for space travel and space work. The word is Russian for astronaut. Rehabilitation is the process of restoring health or normal activity. Gravity is the natural force that pulls objects toward each other. One example is the pull toward the center of Earth. Resistance is a force that slows motion. Simulating means modeling or imitating how something looks or what condition it is in. A space station is a spacecraft where people can live for a long time. It is used for scientific research. Grueling means extremely difficult. It means exhausting. Launched means used force to start something moving. Technology means using science or tools to make things or get work done. Operational means a thing is ready to work.

40. Mexico from Shore to Highland

1North America’s southern lands include the country of Mexico. The United States stands on its northern side. Central America stands on its southern side. A shared border runs between Mexico and the United States. Guatemala shares a southern border with Mexico. Belize shares a southern border with Mexico too. Some islands belong to Mexico. Those islands sit along the western coast. Other islands sit along the eastern coast. West of the mainland is the Pacific Ocean. East of the mainland is the Gulf of Mexico. A second gulf lies between the main body of land and a long, fingerlike stretch. That second gulf is called the Gulf of California. That stretch juts out from the northwest coast. It is called the Baja Peninsula. A map of the region also marks the Yucatán Peninsula. Mexico City is marked as well. Honduras appears on the wider map. El Salvador appears there too. South America appears beyond the southern neighbors.

2Mexico City’s center holds the Metropolitan Cathedral. Builders began the cathedral in the late 1500s. Few cities in North America are older than Mexico City. It is Mexico’s capital. More than nineteen million people live in it. Mountains ring the basin where the city rests. A lake bed once covered the ground under the city. That lake was drained so building could go forward. Old structures and new ones stand side by side. The mix gives the city both a historic face and a modern one. Hundreds of buildings fill the center. The oldest among them come from the 1500s. Floating gardens are part of the city. They are islands people made, not islands nature made. The Aztecs, an ancient Indigenous people, built those islands. Corn was one crop grown on them. Beans were another. Beautiful parks also fill Mexico City. Alameda Park is the oldest of them. Its creation dates to 1592. Chapultepec Park is the largest. Fountains are found there. Museums are found there. A zoo is found there. Lakes are found there. For thousands of years, thriving Indigenous civilizations lived on land that is now Mexico. Spanish explorers colonized Mexico in 1521. About two thirds of the people descend from both Indigenous ancestors and European ancestors. Mestizos is the name for these people. Latin America has few Indigenous populations more diverse than Mexico’s. Indigenous groups still form about one sixth of the population. The country now holds more than 120 million people. Spanish is the language most Mexicans speak. No other country has more Spanish speakers. Indigenous languages are spoken by some people. Náhuatl, said NAH-wah-tuhl, is one of those languages. Three fourths of the people in Mexico, plus still more, live in cities. Art in Mexico reaches far back and runs deep. Stunning art was made by ancient Indigenous peoples. Temples and other buildings wore that art. In the middle of a Mexico City museum stands a god carved long ago by the Aztec.

3Painting, sculpture, and architecture still carry older traditions forward through modern artists. The mural ranks among Mexico’s best-known art forms. Beautiful jewelry was also made by ancient Indigenous peoples of Mexico. Gemstones went into that jewelry. Precious metals went into it too. The craft of making jewelry has lasted into modern times. Mexico leads the world at producing silver. Many jewelry artisans today shape that precious metal. Sports in Mexico have a long history, and the history is a serious one. People today use a short name for one of Mexico’s oldest sports. They call it the ball game. The invention dates back over two thousand years. Stone courts used for the game still stand in some places. Soccer draws crowds in Mexico today. Baseball draws crowds too. Fronton draws crowds too. Fronton resembles handball. A Mexican soccer player is shown getting ready to put the ball in the goal. Mexico’s land is full of geographic contrasts. Mountains rise here. Canyons cut here. Deserts spread here. Rainforests grow here. There is mainland. There are islands. The west holds mountain ranges. The east holds mountain ranges. The south holds mountain ranges. An unstable zone called the Ring of Fire runs along Mexico’s edge. Volcanoes make up some of its mountains. The country’s highest mountain is itself a volcano. It reaches 5,700 meters. That height is 18,701 feet. Terrible disasters can follow earthquakes around the Ring of Fire. September 1985 brought an earthquake to Mexico City. More than nine thousand people died. Eruptions came from volcanoes in the 1990s. Eruptions came again in the 2000s. A landform map names the Sonoran Desert. It names Copper Canyon. It names the Chihuahuan Desert. It names the Baja Peninsula. It names the Sierra Madre Oriental Mountains. It names the Gulf of California. It names the Mexican Plateau. It names the Gulf of Mexico. It names the Yucatán Peninsula. It names the Pacific Ocean. It names Mexico City. It names Pico de Orizaba. It names the Sierra Madre del Sur Mountains.

4Canyons and gorges cut the other way from the mountains. They slice deep into Earth’s crust. Copper Canyon, in northern Mexico, is known as larger than the Grand Canyon. Depth in places reaches 1,400 meters. That depth is 4,600 feet. Northern Mexico is crossed by the Sonoran Desert. The Chihuahuan Desert crosses it too. Native vegetation in the Sonoran Desert comes in more types than in any other desert of North America. The count is about two thousand species. California would fit inside the Chihuahuan Desert. No North American desert covers more ground. Dry weather marks the north. Wet weather marks the southeast. Thousands of animal species live in the rainforests there. Thousands of plant species live there too. East of the mainland coast, two islands rise from the sea. One island is Cozumel. The other is Isla Mujeres, said EES-lah moo-HAIR-es. Bright tropical fish, full of color, live in the ocean around both islands. Two peninsulas push into the oceans on Mexico’s two sides. The Baja is one. The Yucatán is the other. Limestone forms the Yucatán Peninsula. Over thousands of years, that limestone has dissolved in places. Water-filled caves are the result. The caves are called cenotes, said sih-NOH-teez. The first people on this land were Indigenous. Advanced civilizations grew among several groups. The Maya were one group. The Aztec were another. Great cities rose under them. Beautiful works of art were made. Their calendar was accurate. It showed when crops should be planted. The Maya are the people who developed that calendar. About 700 years ago, Aztec people reached central Mexico. Lake Texcoco held an island, and the capital stood there. The capital’s name was Tenochtitlán, said tay-nohch-teet-LAHN. Water entered the city by aqueducts. Aqueducts are waterways. Canals joined every part of the city. By 1519 the Aztec empire was huge. Five to six million people lived in it. Life, the Aztec believed, was the most precious thing a person had. To their gods, a human life was the gift with the deepest meaning. People were sacrificed to those gods because of this belief. Experts think the sacrifices may have numbered in the thousands each year. Hernan Cortes, a Spanish explorer, reached Tenochtitlán in 1519. Within two years, Aztec enemies joined the Spaniards. Together those forces conquered the Aztec. Mexico became a colony of Spain in 1521.

5Conquerors arrived in Mexico in the 1500s. Spanish calls them conquistadores, said kohn-kees-tah-DOH-res. Smallpox came with them. Measles came with them. Indigenous peoples had never met these diseases. The effect was devastating. Scientists think about twenty million people lived in Mexico when Spain conquered it. By 1600, barely one million people remained. Spanish rule lasted the next few hundred years. Independence from Spain came in 1821. Years of war led to that win. Fair treatment did not reach every Mexican person just because the country was free. Farming was the work of most people. Farmers held hardly any of the land. Poverty was the condition of almost all farmers. Rights for them were few. Improvement began with the Mexican Revolution. The years of that revolution were 1910 to 1920. Mexico today is a democracy. Life has improved for many people. Two main social classes still divide the country in a general way. The rich form one class. The poor form the other. Pancho Villa led in the Mexican Revolution. Emiliano Zapata led as well. Villa is pictured on the left. Zapata stands to Villa's right. Tourists from around the world favor Mexico as a place to travel. Ancient ruins let a visitor walk back through the history of Mexico. The best-known Mayan ruin is Chichen Itza, said chee-CHEN eet-SAH. Southeastern Mexico is where it stands. Two large cenotes mark the site where Chichen Itza was built. Religious offerings were thrown into those cenotes. Precious gems were among the offerings. Metals were among them too. The gifts honored the Mayan god of rain. Building of the city began around the 500s. A finished city held an observatory for studying stars and planets. It held a ball court. It held temples. It held a pyramid now called El Castillo. El Castillo, also called the Castle, rises 24 meters. That height is 79 feet. Ninety-one steps run up each of the four sides. With the top platform, those steps come to 365. A full orbit of Earth around the Sun is counted here as 365 days. The Maya had already left Chichen Itza by the time the Spanish reached Mexico. Jungle hid the city across the three hundred years that followed. People found the city again in the 1800s and began to study it. Another name for El Castillo is the Pyramid of Kukulkan. The Maya knew this snake god by that same name.

6Palenque, said pah-LEN-keh, is a Mayan ruin that looks over the jungle around it. A mountain forest is its setting. Toucans are among the wildlife that live in that forest. Monkeys live there too. Architecture at Palenque is remarkable. Sculpture there is remarkable. Wall carvings there are remarkable. Tombs of ancient rulers are there. A water system is there as well. Scientists learned the city’s history from writing cut into tombs and into buildings. Carved symbols helped too. Teotihuacan, said tay-oh-tee-wah-KAHN, stands near Mexico City. Scientists do not know where its people lived before the city was built. They do know the city ranked among the world’s largest at the time of building. Two main pyramids rise at Teotihuacan. The larger pyramid carries the name Pyramid of the Sun. Its pair, called the Pyramid of the Moon, does not rise as high. Great temples stand in the city. Palaces stand there. Apartment complexes stand there too. Study of the site shows a practice shared with the Aztec and the Maya. The builders sacrificed people to their gods. Visitors notice the Sun pyramid before any other feature at the site. Across Mexico, many people enjoy folk dancing as a pastime. Traditional clothing is often what the dancers wear. Mexico’s story moves from Native civilizations to conquest by Spain. It moves on through revolution and into democracy. The history is rich. Artistic traditions tell that long, fascinating story. Ancient ruins tell it too. Geographic contrasts mark the land as well. Desert is here. Rainforest is here. Mountain is here. Canyon is here. All of them belong to this amazing country.

7The population count is 127,575,529. Land covers 1,964,375 square kilometers. That area equals 758,450 square miles. Mexico City is the capital. Spanish is the primary language. Roman Catholic is the primary religion. The currency is the peso, said PAY-soh. Figures come from the Central Intelligence Agency for 2019 and from the World Bank. An aqueduct is a channel people built so water can travel from one place to another. An artisan is a person with skill in a craft or a trade. A basin is a low spot, a depression, in Earth’s surface. A cenote is a deep well made by nature. It appears when limestone at the surface falls in and groundwater shows. Conquistadores is Spanish for conquerors. They seized land for Spain during the sixteenth century in both North America and South America. A gorge is a long, deep valley with higher land around it. Indigenous describes what is native to a given region. A mestizo is a person whose ancestors are both European and Indigenous. A mural is a large artwork, usually painted, made right on a wall. A peninsula is a long arm of land with water around nearly all of it. The Ring of Fire borders the Pacific Ocean. Active volcanoes and frequent earthquakes mark that region. Ruins are what remains after something has been destroyed.

41. Morocco

1Morocco is a country on the coast of northwestern Africa. It has shores on the Mediterranean Sea and the Atlantic Ocean. Morocco is a little smaller than Spain. Algeria lies to the east and the southeast. Western Sahara lies along the southern edge. The Strait of Gibraltar separates Morocco from Spain. This narrow channel joins the two seas. At its narrowest point, Spain is only about 13 kilometers, or 8 miles, away.

2Rabat, the capital, sits on the Atlantic at the mouth of the Bou Regreg River, next to Salé. Its history reaches the twelfth century. French and Islamic buildings show that past. Silt blocked the river mouth, so Rabat is no longer a major port. For centuries, sea traffic there was heavy, and not all of it was legal. From the seventeenth to the nineteenth centuries, Rabat and Salé were a pirate hub. The pirates ranged near Spain, Italy, England, Ireland, and Iceland. A wall surrounds Old Rabat. Music is important in Moroccan life, and musicians perform in the streets. A little more than 33 million people live in the country. About 60 percent live in cities, and Casablanca has about 3.5 million. Tourism employs more people than mining or manufacturing. Farming employs about one-third of rural workers. Mild weather and farmland help Morocco grow crops. Soccer has replaced horsemanship as the top sport.

3Most Moroccans are Berber, Arabic, or both, and almost half are under twenty-four. Islam is the official religion, 99 percent of people are Muslim, and the law is both Islamic and secular. In 2004, new laws gave women more rights, so gender equality is high among Arab countries. People speak Arabic, Berber, and French, as on a school sign named for a president's daughter. Much of the land is high above the sea. The Atlas Mountains rise in the middle, and the Rif Mountains rise in the north. Mount Toubkal is the tallest peak, at 4,165 meters. Rain and snow feed many streams, coasts stay about 18°C to 28°C, and inland summers often pass 35°C.

4The sharqi is a hot wind from the Sahara. It blows in late spring or early summer and can bring heat of 41 degrees C. The land is widely varied. It has mountains, waterfalls, beaches, desert, and forests. Milder places are covered by forests. Argan trees grow only in Morocco, including some high mountain forests. In the Sahara, few plants grow except in oases, where people grow date palms. More than twelve hundred years ago, Idris I came from the Middle East. He was a descendant of Muhammad. He took control from the Berbers and began a Muslim dynasty. Later Muslim kingdoms held parts of Spain until Catholic rulers forced them out in 1492. Spain took the coast in 1884. France ruled from 1912 to 1956 and promised military strength and protection for the royal house. Today Morocco has a king and a prime minister. It claims most of Western Sahara after fights over that mineral-rich desert.

5Morocco has a rich variety of birds. At Merja Zerga, a lagoon on the Atlantic, huge flocks of herons, flamingos, and ducks gather, especially in winter. Single-humped camels, or dromedaries, thrive here and carry tourists on treks. The Barbary macaque is a forest monkey, and males help care for the young. These macaques also live across the strait in Spain and are the only monkeys in Europe. Fennec foxes, the world's smallest foxes, live in the Sahara and stay active at night. Their large ears help release body heat. The flic-flac spider flips forward or backward, even uphill, and can cover 2 meters a second.

6People around the world enjoy Moroccan food, with Spanish, French, Berber, and Arab flavors. Couscous is a tiny round pasta, and bread comes with meals. Many dishes cook in a clay pot called a tagine. Meat is plentiful, with kebobs, a lamb stew in Ramadan, and b'stillah, a sweet pigeon pastry. Coastal people often eat fish, with tomatoes, onions, peppers, and eggplant. Garlic, olives, lemons, saffron, cumin, coriander, and cinnamon flavor meals, often with mint tea or coffee. That famous food draws visitors. They also find an ancient history, snowy mountains, hot dunes, beaches, and many animals. Tourists see a bridge to Africa and a gateway to Europe, with a mild north and strong crops.

7About 33,322,699 people live in Morocco. The land covers 446,300 square kilometers, or 172,317 square miles. The capital city is Rabat. The primary languages are Arabic and Berber. The primary religion is Islam. The name of the currency is the dirham. These facts come from the Central Intelligence Agency World Factbook for 2016.

42. Mysterious Shipwrecks

1A map shows North America, Europe, Africa, the Atlantic Ocean, and the Pacific Ocean. It marks eight ships: Antikythera, HMS Victory, Mary Celeste, HMS Erebus, HMS Terror, SS Valencia, USS Cyclops, and SS Edmund Fitzgerald. Marks show a wreck, a last known place, or a ghost ship sighting. The United Nations estimates more than three million shipwrecks on coasts and sea floors. Archaeologists study wrecks hundreds or thousands of years old, and treasure hunters seek rumored riches. Some ships vanished with no clues at all.
