A wreck rests on the floor of the Red Sea in Jordan. Stories tell of danger, death, treasure, and intrigue, yet the sea may hide the clues forever.

2In 1900, divers near Antikythera found a wreck they were not seeking, partly under sand 55 meters down. No one knew how it got there, but the bigger question was when. A diver brought up a bronze spear from a large statue. The ship was more than two thousand years old and held art and luxury goods. One piece in an Athens museum is the earliest known computer, with gears that showed stars and planets, maybe for navigation. Teams still dive and study the wreck more than 120 years later. In 1708 a British warship sank the Spanish galleon San José, with gold and emeralds for Spain’s war with Great Britain. Found off Colombia in 2015, Spain and Colombia dispute goods worth as much as 17 billion dollars.

3A painting by Thomas Butterworth shows HMS Victory in wild seas. Launched in 1737, it was Britain’s finest warship and carried 110 cannons. In early October 1744 a storm in the English Channel separated it from the fleet. The ship vanished with about 1,150 people. Islanders heard cannons call for help, but the seas were too rough for rescue. Some wreckage washed up days later, yet the hull and deck were gone. The loss baffled people for more than 250 years. In 2008 sonar found it 100 kilometers away, a bronze cannon was raised, and the oldest naval ship still in service shares its name.

4Not every lost ship sinks, and the 30.1-meter Mary Celeste met a stranger fate. On November 7, 1872, it left New York for Genoa with Captain Benjamin Briggs, wife Sarah, two-year-old Sophia, and seven crew. A month later the British ship Dei Gratia found it drifting in the Atlantic with nobody aboard. A lifeboat and navigation tools were missing, yet there was no violence, gear was stowed, the cargo remained, and the log looked normal. Briggs, his family, and the crew were never seen again. Few clues explain the exit, though later tales added rebel crew, murder, and sea monsters. Cat Emmy fled the Empress of Ireland twice the day before it left Canada, and people called it a bad omen. The next day the ship hit another vessel and sank in fourteen minutes, killing more than one thousand people, Canada’s deadliest wreck.

5In 1845 Sir John Franklin led 129 people on HMS Erebus and HMS Terror to find the Northwest Passage. They hoped for a shipping shortcut between Europe and Asia, and the refitted warships carried three years of supplies. Whalers last saw the ships in Baffin Bay on July 26, 1845. After two silent years, searchers found gear, graves, and notes on empty islands. Pack ice trapped the ships in September 1846, Franklin died the next June, and the sick crew waited in vain. In April 1848 the survivors tried to hike south to the Canadian mainland. Inuit told of Europeans who starved on the coast, and evidence showed cannibalism. Sonar found preserved Erebus in 2014 under 12 meters of water, and Terror in September 2016 on a bay floor off King William Island.

6In January 1906 the passenger steamer SS Valencia left San Francisco for Seattle with 173 people. Near midnight on January 22 its iron hull ran aground near Vancouver Island. Pounding waves trapped the ship, most lifeboats flipped, and people drowned in freezing water. High winds blocked rescue, and about thirty-six hours later a huge wave swept the upper deck. Only 37 of 136 passengers survived. Investigators blamed bad choices by the captain and crew. In 1910 newspapers reported a ghostly Valencia, and a cave rumor of eight skeletons was never confirmed. In 1933 a lifeboat was found in a nearby bay, and its nameplate is in the Maritime Museum of British Columbia.

7The USS Cyclops was a U.S. Navy supply ship. In Brazil during World War I, Captain W. Worley took on heavy ore and reported that one engine was not working. He was told to fix it in the United States, and the ship left on February 22, 1918, with 309 people. It vanished in the Atlantic, and no wreckage has ever been found. Some guessed a German submarine sank it, or that Worley, born in Germany, gave it to the enemy, but there was no proof. Experts blame overload, low engine power, and a March 10 storm for this largest non-combat U.S. naval loss of life. It disappeared in the Bermuda Triangle, where some ships were later sunk on purpose to make reefs for sea life. Legend places it near Bermuda, Puerto Rico, and Florida and tells of strange powers, yet losses are no higher than in other busy seas.

8The SS Edmund Fitzgerald was over 213 meters long, the largest and longest ship ever built for the Great Lakes. On November 9, 1975, it left Superior, Wisconsin, full of iron ore, under Captain Ernest McSorley. A storm was forecast, but the veteran captain was not worried. On November 10 the Coast Guard warned ships to seek shelter, so the Fitzgerald headed for Whitefish Bay, Wisconsin. At about 7:00 p.m. McSorley said the ship was taking on water, but radioed, "We are holding our own." Moments later it was gone from radar, no lifeboats launched, and none of the twenty-nine crew were found. Within days the Coast Guard found the ship snapped in half and recovered life rings. Investigators suspect three rogue waves, sudden giants made by currents and wind that can flip freighters, sank it with no distress call.

9Between the 16th and 19th centuries, as many as 1,000 ships carrying enslaved people were lost at sea. Most of those wrecks are still undiscovered and undocumented. The Slave Wrecks Project, founded in 2008, works to find the ships and the history they hold. In 2014 its first major recovery was the sunken São José near Cape Town, South Africa. Oceans are vast, weather can turn fast, and rocks and reefs hide near coasts. Modern tools make shipping safer, yet dozens of wrecks still kill about two thousand people a year. Ship work is among the most dangerous jobs, and sometimes fate is only luck. Some mysteries can be solved and old wrecks can show the past, but often the sea keeps its secrets.

43. Otzi: The Iceman

1About 5,300 years ago, a man got ready for a hunt. A small brown dog yapped at his feet. He pushed the dog aside and spilled a basket of grain. He smiled at the puppy, but he was annoyed. He had a lot to do. Winter was closing in, and he had no time to waste.

2A tall, dark-haired boy scooped up the spilled grain. The man was proud of his son and told him to care for things while he was gone. The boy said he would. They put the grain back in the basket, and the dog played nearby. The boy stored the grain in an underground pit for winter. On the way home, the man rubbed his legs. Colder weather would make food scarce, and men might fight over it. He packed and left to hunt red deer, ibex, and wild boar for his family.

3The man had not gone far when he killed an ibex and carried the meat. An arrow flew past his ear, and another hit the back of his right shoulder. He could not pull the arrow all the way out. He walked up a hill while men behind him took the meat. He lay down and closed his eyes for the last time. His body froze, and later people called him Otzi, the Iceman, after the Otztal valley. This is only one possible story. We do not know for sure, so a theory is a guess based on facts.

4A map marks Switzerland, Austria, France, and Italy near where Otzi was found. Labels name a dagger and sheath, a grass cape piece, a quiver, a birch-bark container, a bow, an ax, and a backpack frame. In September 1991, German hikers Erika and Helmut Simon found a hairless frozen body in the Tyrolean Alps. They were more than 10,000 feet up, near the border of Austria and Italy. Only the head and part of the shoulders showed, and they thought he was a modern climber. Rainer Henn saw a yellow, dry body and said it had been frozen a long time. The Iceman was about 5,000 years old. Tests showed he was about five feet tall, weighed about 110 pounds, had wavy brown hair and probably a beard, lived to about 46, and had a bad back, arthritis, worn joints, and a bad stomach.

5From his worn teeth, scientists think Otzi ate mostly bread and grains for many years. He also likely ate crab apples, berries, acorns, hazelnuts, and meat from hunted animals such as ibex. Finds included a copper ax, a bow, arrows, a quiver, a knife, and a grass sheath. One arrow had blood from two people, and his knife and hide jacket had blood from another person. He had a stone disk on a fringed leather string, mushrooms on a leather string, flint, felt strips, and a birch-bark container. He also had a grass cloak, a wood backpack frame, a leather pouch, leather pants, waterproof shoes, grass socks, and a fur hat. Other finds were a bone awl, a stick with a deer antler tip, ibex meat, a sloe berry, and tattoos. Tests showed arthritis and back pain, and he had cuts on his hands and wrists.

6At first, some thought Otzi was caught in a storm, fell asleep, and died from the cold. Later ideas say he was killed, because blood was on his clothes and wounds were on his body. Many scientists think an arrow hit his back and a knife cut his hands. Theory one says other hunters shot him by accident while he was hunting, and then he was buried. His clothes and tools suggest a hunt. Scraps of leather, fur, grass, bearskin, cowhide, and tree bark were on his body. They think he wore leather pants and a long jacket or vest of deer, goat, and ibex hide, with the fur side out. He may also have worn a long woven grass cloak.

7Scientists think his wide waterproof shoes had bearskin soles, cowhide or deer on top, and netting of tree bark. Soft grass worked like socks, and grass laces held his size-six feet under a dry leather flap. His hat was sewn from fur pieces, probably bearskin, with two straps that may have tied under his chin. These clothes fit a long hunting trip. He carried a copper ax, a bow, arrows, a quiver, and a tiny knife. The bow and arrows were for deer and ibex, the knife cleaned them, and the ax cut firewood. Felt strips may have started fires and been carried in a birch-bark container. A pouch held two flints, a short antler-tipped stick may have sharpened them, and some scientists think a crossfire hit him by accident.

8Some of the items found with Otzi support a second theory. This theory says he was killed on purpose in a fierce fight with other hunters. While he hunted ibex, starving hunters did not want him to take the kill, so they shot arrows and he shot back. Dr. Tom Loy thinks Otzi shot two enemies and pulled his arrows out each time. That idea explains blood on his knife, two traces on one arrowhead, and blood on his coat. The attackers may also have used a knife. Cuts on his hands and wrist may show that he tried to defend himself. Many scientists accept this idea, but some look for another reason.

9A third theory says his community killed Otzi, a shaman, because he had grown too powerful. Walter Leitner, a historian in Innsbruck, Austria, thinks Otzi may have been a shaman. A shaman is a healer of body and spirit who may travel to the spirit world for medicine. Scientists found many strange tattoos, including stripes, a cross, and small bars. The marks were not for show, because clothing would have hidden them. Fifty-seven rough tattoos were on his ankles, knees, and calves. They sat on or near points used for arthritis and back pain, so they may have been old acupuncture. One tattoo can be seen on his ankle.

10A small hollow was found in Otzi's right earlobe. Did that mean he wore an earring? A fringed leather string ran through a two-inch white stone disk found nearby. Some scientists think this may have been a necklace for a medicine man. Two mushrooms on a piece of leather were also found near his body. That kind of mushroom can fight sickness, so he may have carried medicine the hunters needed. These are only a few theories, and they change when the clues change. Sometimes the clues stay the same, but scientists read them in a new way.

11The book names Rainer Henn, Walter Leitner, and Tom Loy. It lists the Iceman's age, looks, clothes, health, diet, and given name. It also lists his finders, the place he was found, and the things he carried. Death ideas in the list include an accident, murder, and the weather. A made-up account of his last days is part of the story too. Scientists still do not agree on who he was. For now, Otzi keeps the true story a secret.

44. Painters of a Passing Moment

1Claude Monet painted Boulevard des Capucines. The caption lists 1873 and 1874. This book starts with new art for a changing Paris. It then tells how painters rebelled against old habits, changed their subjects, and tried new methods. It looks at pictures and cameras, then closes with a word list. Paris, France, was changing in the middle of the nineteenth century. Its art world was changing too. A group of artists chose to rebel against the art scene that was already in power. They did not want formal paintings in the realistic style people preferred then. They wanted brief moments from ordinary days. A map places Paris in France, with the Atlantic Ocean, Europe, the Mediterranean, Asia, and Africa around it. Art was shifting while the city itself was being rebuilt and modernized. Paris had stayed much the same since the Middle Ages. The city had grown more and more crowded. Narrow streets wound through it. Those streets were filthy. Disease was everywhere. As the city changed, streets too narrow for carriages were taken out. Long boulevards lined with trees replaced them. Builders added cafes. They added theaters. They added parks in the open air, and they added squares. They also added a grand opera house. Wealthy people came to enjoy this new Paris.

2Paris was a major home of culture. Many artists lived and worked there. Collectors liked to search its galleries and build art collections. Each year Paris hosted an art event. No show of art anywhere in the West drew more people. People called it the Salon. It was an exhibition. Paintings covered the walls from the floor all the way to the ceiling. Work shown in the Salon was wanted by collectors at once. The artists who picked the Salon art were conservative. They especially liked formal portraits. They also liked subjects from history, myth, and religion. One favored kind of picture is the Princesse de Broglie. Say that title PRAHN-sess DU BROY. It is a portrait of a wealthy woman from France. Jean-Auguste-Dominique Ingres painted it in 1853. The sounds of his name start ZHAHN oh-GOOST. Then comes doh-mee-NEEK AN-gruh. Fine brushwork and sharp detail make it realistic. People prized that style then. Another picture, by Renoir, is Luncheon of the Boating Party, from 1880 to 1881. People of different classes pass time in a café. Paris had been made over. Many young artists wanted people's ideas about art made over too. They did it by challenging old traditions. They left behind styles they felt were out of date. The Salon did not often pick their work. When it did, the pieces were often hung so poorly that nobody noticed. That was frustrating. The young artists hoped rich people would become their patrons. A patron gave an artist money. Then the artist did not need another job. The artist could focus on making art. A patron could also present an artist to other people who collected art and had money.

3Thirty artists who were struggling joined together. Claude Monet was one. Say his name moh-NAY. Edgar Degas was another. Say his name du-GAH. Mary Cassatt was another. Say her name kuh-SAT. They wanted to change the Salon's settled art scene. In 1874 they held their own exhibit. Their subjects included sunlight striking water at one time of day. They also showed people laughing while they ate with friends in a café. Monet's exhibit piece was a painting. Its name was Impression, Sunrise. He had made it in 1873. A small rowboat sits in it. Behind the boat, ship masts and smokestacks appear in a haze. One reviewer attacked the lack of detail. He said the work offered nothing beyond simple impressions. Monet did not paint the scene exactly as it looked. He painted the personal effect the sunrise had on him. The reviewer's cutting words stuck. The artists in the group did not all paint the same way. Together they became known as the Impressionists. They would define a new movement in art. Impression, Sunrise helped start that new movement in art history. Style was only one challenge to old ways of painting. Many Impressionists refused the usual subjects. They stepped away from scenes of history and religion. They stepped away from realistic portraiture too. They wanted the world as their own eyes saw it. They wanted their own points of view. They wanted both the beauty and the flaws of ordinary life. Portraits and religious scenes no longer boxed them in. Subjects could come from a wide range. Landscapes of countryside in France were a frequent subject for Camille Pissarro. Say his name pi-SAR-oh. Alfred Sisley often did that too. Say his name SIS-lee. Pierre-Auguste Renoir focused on sunlight. Start his name pee-AIR. Finish it oh-GOOST ruh-NWAH. He studied how that light fell on flowers and on figures. He also made informal portraits. One example is a picture from 1892. Its title begins Two Young Girls. The rest is at the Piano. It shows that a portrait, an old kind of picture, could be painted in a new way. In that painting Renoir caught a moment that was already passing.

4Short-lived moments had to be painted fast, so the Impressionists learned speed. Monet used a method called wet-on-wet. He put a new layer of color on top of another layer. He did not wait for the lower layer to dry. Older painters had to wait. Each layer had to dry before more color could go on. Oil paint can need a full day to dry. A large painting could then take months. It could even take years. The Impressionists did not lay down wide strokes of color. They tried small touches of paint. Those touches held the color and light they saw. They used pure pigments. A pure pigment is paint that has not been mixed with another color. They set these colors next to each other. They mixed them as little as they could. The contrast made each color look bolder and more vivid. One 1899 picture of that subject begins with the words Bridge Over a Pond. The rest is of Water Lilies. Monet made eighteen paintings of it. The River Thames in London was another subject. He made over one hundred images of it. Many of them show the Houses of Parliament. Many Impressionists, Monet among them, painted en plein air. Say that phrase ON PLEN AIR. It means outdoors. Before this era, working outside was uncommon. Artists had usually worked in indoor studios. Indoors, they could control the light more easily. Outside, they could watch natural light and color and play with both. Some painters returned to one scene many times. Each visit caught a different moment of light, color, and time. The British Houses of Parliament became a series in Monet's hands. That series is one example. He worked on it for three years. Different hours of the day appear in those views of the building. He also showed it in different weather.

5Some Impressionists, Edgar Degas among them, liked to paint indoors. They still showed moments of daily life. His paintings featured ordinary people of Paris. Restaurant diners appear. Musicians appear. Ballerinas appear. He often sketched ballerinas backstage in their dance halls. He also sketched them in his studio. After many sketches of the same pose, he was ready to paint. He used the sketches. He used what he had learned by watching how the dancers moved. Those figures were reshaped into a scene ready for paint. In The Ballet Class, made from 1871 to 1874, no one person stands out as the subject. The picture feels spontaneous. The ballerinas are backstage, practicing. Some stretch. Some chat. They are caught in an ordinary moment of their lives. They are not posing. They seem to have no interest in the artist. They do not even look out of the canvas. The painting shows how well Degas could catch a gesture that lasts only a moment. In the middle of the 1800s, modern photography was growing. As the new tools improved, more people had a photographer take their portrait. A photograph took less time than a painted portrait. It cost less. It could match a person's looks more closely. The Impressionists did not try to beat photography at its own job. They wanted viewers to see a kind of work a camera could not replace. At the same time, photography inspired them. By the 1880s, cameras were easier to carry. Photos became more candid. A photo could freeze a spontaneous moment. Painters tried to do that too. A subject might be ordinary. People waiting on a train is one example. It might be people resting in a park. Édouard Manet was never an official member of the Impressionists. Say his name e-DWAR ma-NAY. Some of his paintings still show their influence. The Railway, from 1873, is one.

6Cameras also taught artists to frame a scene in a striking way. That method is called cropping. In a photograph, an artist could cut off the edges to frame the subject better. The Impressionists understood cropping. They used it when they composed their own works. Their pictures show a strong debt to photography. From 1881 to 1886, Renoir painted The Umbrellas. People carrying umbrellas fill the group. Rain is falling as they move along a street. The edge of the canvas cuts off many figures. The center does not hold a single focal point. The viewer's eye jumps from place to place. It moves to the woman on the left. It moves to the girl on the right. Both of them look out of the canvas. X-rays show a later change. The left-side figures were painted again later, and the new handling is crisper. Degas's painting Waiting, from around 1882, holds many contrasts. Young meets old. Light meets dark. Glamour meets dreariness. In art history, Impressionism had a wide influence. When these painters began, people did not take their art seriously. The Salon rejected them. Acceptance was hard to win. They left traditional realistic styles behind, and that let them chase new ideas. They focused on moments that flee. They worked en plein air. They used some methods from photography. They kept a style of their own. Buyers now pay millions of dollars for pictures by Monet, by Degas, and by other painters from this group. People call them masterpieces. Art museums around the world display them. These painters broke the rules of traditional art. That break opened a road for later movements. Because of their work, artists still experiment. They still make us question what we think art is.

7Each art movement builds on movements that came before. By challenging old painting styles, the Impressionists opened a door. Other artists could keep experimenting. After the Impressionists, some artists tried to put deeper memories and feelings into their work. Color and shape carried those feelings. This was Post-Impressionism. Vincent van Gogh was a famous Post-Impressionist. Say his name VAN GOH. The Starry Night is one of his paintings. In the middle of the 1880s, Neo-Impressionism appeared. Georges Seurat was one of these artists. Say his name ZHORZH su-RAH. He used a new method called pointillism. The paintings were made of dots, or points, left by a brush. This work took time. It was not spontaneous the way Impressionist work was. One such painting is titled A Sunday on La Grande Jatte. Say the place name GRAHND ZHAT. Expressionists did not try to copy the natural world. They tried to catch emotions. Edvard Munch painted The Scream in 1893. It is among the best-known pictures of that time. A screaming figure stands on a bridge. The figure carries intense anxiety. The last part of the book is a glossary. Conservative means traditional and slow to change. To have criticized something is to have found fault in it, or to have judged it with disapproval. Depicted means showed or portrayed something. An exhibition puts artwork, or other interesting things, on show for the public. A focal point is the place that holds the center of attention. In art, an impression shows the basic traits of something. It often aims to suggest a feeling or an effect. Modernization updates something. It can also make that thing fit newer technology, ideas, or style. A portrait can be a drawing of a person, a painting of a person, or a photograph of a person. Something realistic looks like real life, or it is accurate. A person who rebels actively fights an authority that is already in place, including a government. Sketched means made a rough drawing or an outline. Spontaneity means something happens on its own, or springs from a sudden impulse.

45. Printing Objects, Not Just Pictures

1This book starts with a first look. Later parts cover stamps and laser light. Flat sheets become solid forms. A new method spreads. Hearts and livers can be printed. One house is unlike the rest. The last parts look ahead and list words. Johannes Gutenberg removes the first page proof from his printing press. A picture shows shoes planned for one person and built by a printer that makes solid objects. A first look. A designer who plans goods for industry is deep in work on a computer. She is shaping a shoe no shop carries. Her eyes stay on the screen. A program made for this job lets her adjust the shoe's shape. She uses the same program to set the shoe's size. The design now satisfies her. She tells the machine to begin. On the other side of the room, the printer comes alive. A whirr fills the air. A hum follows. The result is more than a flat picture of footwear. A shoe you could hold is what the machine builds. You have entered three-dimensional printing. A shorter name for it is 3D printing. It is the newest way to form a model of nearly any object a person can picture. Such a model is called a prototype. The object that leaves a 3D printer is not like the sheet that leaves an ordinary computer printer. The two machines still rest on one shared idea. They add material one coat at a time. People call that method layering. Layering is what finishes the product.

2From stamps to lasers. Ask most people what printing is, and they picture words. They also picture pictures. The marks lie on something smooth. Paper is one such surface. Cloth is another. Ink, laid on as a coat, makes the marks. A hand can spread that ink. A machine can spread it too. History holds many ways of putting ink on a surface. Xylography goes back about two thousand years. People in Eastern Asia were the ones who used it. Another name for xylography is woodblock printing. The work began with a drawing on wood. Carving came next, and the carvers removed wood from around the drawing. Ink then covered the raised part that was left. That inked part was pushed onto a flat surface. Up came the block. What remained was a thin ink coat shaped like the raised picture. A stamp pad and a rubber stamp do this same job. Anyone who has stamped ink has tried xylography. Covering a whole page with many stamped pictures would eat up the hours. A machine invented in 1450 ended that long wait. The machine was the printing press. Its inventor was Johannes Gutenberg. His trade was blacksmithing, and his country was Germany. For the first time, books could be printed in huge numbers. One picture shows a lithographic press moving images onto the pages of a book. The new press did not stamp a page one mark at a time. Pieces of type, which are many small stamps, sat together in a tray. Paper met that tray under pressure. A single push put inked type across every part of the page. Copy after copy of one page became far simpler to produce. Hundreds of years passed, and printing kept changing. Lithography is one later method. Water plays a part in it. Oil plays a part too. On a lithographic press, images move onto the pages of books. Picture areas and blank areas share one flat plate. They sit at the same level. A treatment on the plate makes the picture area pull in inks made with oil. Water stays apart from oil. Because of that split, the blank areas drive the ink off. The year 1796 is when this lithographic method was worked out. Magazines still come off such presses. So do many books.

3The 1950s brought the earliest printer that could work at high speed. It was paired with a computer named UNIVAC. Data for that computer sat on reels of magnetic tape. A typewriter, changed for the job, was hooked to the reels. Words stored on the tape came out through that typewriter. Numbers came out the same way. No person could match that speed. By the 1980s, computers sat on desks in many homes and offices. Inkjet machines let those desk computers put digital pictures on paper. The computer sends facts about each shape. The printer reads those facts. Heating comes first, inside a cartridge packed with ink. Warm ink raises the pressure in the cartridge. Ink then bursts out through an opening called a nozzle. Jet is another name for that opening. Coat after coat of ink lands on the sheet. Work on laser printing began in the 1970s. Homes could not buy those printers until 1984. A laser printer resembles lithography. One big difference sets it apart. Oil is not painted onto the surface. Instead, a laser strikes the paper and leaves an image that carries an electric charge. Particles of dry ink move across the sheet. The charged image pulls those particles in. A roller then squeezes the particles against the paper. The page is printed. Flat sheets become solid forms. Charles Hull, in 1984, created a method he named stereolithography. That method is what people now mean by 3D printing. His job was design engineer. Long waits made him angry at his work. Plastic prototype parts took weeks to develop. Testing them took months. Redesign took still more time. He went looking for a fix. Prototypes, he believed, should be ready much sooner. A stack of layers, he realized, could grow into a solid thing. From that idea, 3D printing started. A picture shows Charles Hull. Stereolithography takes tools from inkjet machines. It also takes tools from laser machines. Those tools build objects with height, width, and depth. They do not stop at a flat picture. Software on a computer draws the image first. The drawing is saved as data. That file travels to the 3D printer. Think of the file as a map of roads. The map guides a part called the print head. The print head looks like a very small box. A nozzle, also called a jet, sticks out from the box. Metal bars form a grid, and the print head hangs on them. Some bars run the length of a platform. Other bars run across it. That platform is the print bed, and the object grows there.

4A diagram points to the bed, to the material, to a nozzle, and to the moving head above them. Plastic thread is the stuff this printer lays down. Small objects grow from that thread. Above the bed, the head slides forward and back. It also slides left and right. Material leaves the nozzle in a narrow stream while the head travels. Plastic is a common choice for that stream. The stream drops onto the bed. Instructions come from the image file on the computer. Those instructions stack the material in layers too thin to see one by one. People call those layers microlayers. Up and down goes the bed as the stack grows. A fresh layer lands. Heat from a laser meets that layer. The heat sticks it to the layer already there. Little by little, a solid prototype climbs off the bed. Each added coat brings it closer to done. Look at the monitor, and the rising object matches the picture there. Names and dates in printing. Bi Sheng, a printer from China, made the earliest known movable type. The time was about the year 1040. His letters were formed from clay. Gutenberg's press arrived in 1450. He was the German blacksmith already named in this book. Printed books could then be turned out in great numbers. Alois Senefelder brought lithography into the world in 1796. He wrote, and he also acted, in Germany. Plays were what he hoped to print without high cost. Lord Stanhope, a scientist from Britain, finished a press of cast iron in 1800. No cast-iron press had come before his. Richard Hoe, an inventor from America, built the cylinder press in 1846. Eight thousand sheets could leave that press in a single hour. Ottmar Mergenthaler invented the Linotype machine in 1886. He was born in Germany and later lived as an American. Before the Linotype, no device could line up a full row of type for the press. Chester Carlson invented electrophotography in 1938. Xerox is the other name people use for that invention. Carlson studied physics in America. He also worked as an attorney. Remington-Rand, a firm in America that made office machines, built a fast printer in 1953. That printer served the UNIVAC computer. It was the first printer of its speed for that machine. Gary Starkweather, an engineer from America, made a laser printer for sale in 1976. His was the first laser printer that businesses actually used. In 1984 Hull both built stereolithography and took out a patent on it. He was American. Design engineering was his field. The patent covers the method we call 3D printing.

5A new method takes off. Once the twenty-first century was young, factories chose stereolithography first. They used it when a prototype part had to be made. Design, building, and testing could all finish inside hours. Sometimes the work fit inside a few days. The old pace had been weeks. Sometimes it had been months. Plastic was the usual stuff in those early solid prototypes. Better machines arrived later. Ceramics joined the list of materials. Metals joined it too. Seoul, a city in South Korea, hosted a design show. There, a 3D printer formed a shape like a sculpture. More materials meant more things a printer could attempt. Workers in many jobs saw a shortcut to custom pieces. The wait shrank. A designer might form a jewel that exists only once. An artist might form a solid sculpture no one had shaped before. The price stayed fair. Only the material inside the object gets used. Nothing extra is poured away. Photos follow Beauty, a bald eagle, from before a new beak to after it was fixed on. Printing for the birds. Custom solid objects, made at a price people can pay, have found surprising jobs. One engineer walked into a place that nurses hurt wild animals. Beauty, the bald eagle there, stopped him cold. Her upper beak was gone. A hunter's shot had taken it. Hunting was impossible for her. Eating was impossible. Preening her own feathers was impossible. He thought a printed part could answer her need. An X-ray of her head was his starting point. On a computer he drew a plastic stand-in for the beak she had lost. The printer turned that drawing into a solid beak. Surgery locked the new beak on for good. Drinking is possible for her now, as it is for any healthy eagle. Eating is possible too. So is washing her feathers.

6Have a heart, or have a liver. Real human organs are the new goal for some engineers. Scientists share that goal. Living cells can be fused by the same layering used for plastic. The fused cells become human tissue. Cells taken from one patient go into a gel. Layers of that gel copy the organ's shape. Time in an incubator lets the cells grow up. They fasten to each other as they mature. Ears have already been grown this way. Lungs have been grown. Livers have been grown. A heart is the project on the bench now. Natural tissue would be part of it. Artificial material would be the other part. Rejection is the trouble many transplant patients face. An organ from a printer might spare a person that trouble. Put something foreign into a body, and the immune system sees a danger. It fights the newcomer. Medicine after a transplant tells the immune system to hold back. Without that medicine, the new organ would be destroyed. The medicine has a cost. Infection becomes more likely. Cells from the patient change the odds. An organ grown from those cells is less likely to be rejected. Less medicine may be enough. The body can mend faster. Fewer extra problems show up during recovery. Medical classes use a solid model of a human heart made by this method. Amsterdam, in the Netherlands, is home to the 3D Canal House. A printer made one block for that build. The left-hand picture shows the block in the walls. The right-hand picture is a drawing of the house when the work is done. One house stands apart. Some engineers have aimed 3D printing straight up. The height is real, not a figure of speech. Architects in Amsterdam run a printer big enough to move from place to place. Their project is a plastic house. It will stand three floors high. Melted plastic, laid in coats, hardens into bricks. The bricks lock into one another. Thirteen rooms will be built from those bricks. Three years is the planned build time. Ordinary houses go up faster than that. The team wants the trial to change how people think about homes. Factory methods, including this kind of printing, might shape the next house.

7Move clockwise, and the first object is a work of art. Next comes a lollipop made of chocolate. Then a lamp. Last, a dinosaur's head. A 3D printer made each of them. The years ahead. No one can count every use still waiting for this technology. People can already tailor goods to one user. Braces for teeth, formed from plastic, are one good. Furniture big enough for a whole apartment is another. A full building is another. The machines keep getting better. Odd settings may be next. A trip through space that lasts a long time is one odd setting. The craft could carry a printer. A broken part could be replaced on the spot. New tools could be drawn up out there. Those tools could be built to make life in space possible. Staying alive on a strange world might depend on this invention. Words to know. Call something customized when it is made for one person alone. Call a thing innovative when it is new and when it did not copy an older idea. A laser is a tool that throws a tight, strong beam. Waves in that beam are alike. Data means facts, including sizes and counts, that help a person study a problem or make a plan. A particle is a speck of matter. Digital means the facts are saved as ones and zeros in a row. A computer, or a similar machine, can read that row. A prototype is the first form of an object. Later copies follow that first form. Stereolithography names a way of printing. A digital plan goes in, and a solid object comes out. An engineer designs machines. An engineer may also design a building, a bridge, or some other structure. Building and repair can be part of the same job. Technology means using what science knows, and the tools science builds, in order to make something or carry out a task. Industrial points to the making of goods. Machines often do that work. Automation and other technology often do it too. Three-dimensional describes an object that has height. It also has width. It also has depth. The short label is 3D.

46. Russia

1A friendly snowball fight takes place in St. Petersburg. Russia reaches from Eastern Europe to East Asia. It covers eleven time zones and 16,377,742 square kilometers of land. Russia is twice the size of the United States and is the largest country in the world. Seas and oceans surround Russia on two sides. The Arctic Ocean lies to the north, and the Bering Sea, the Sea of Okhotsk, and the Sea of Japan lie to the east. Russia shares borders with Finland, Ukraine, and Kazakhstan, and also with Mongolia, China, and Japan. Across the Bering Strait, its far eastern tip is only 88.5 kilometers from Alaska.

2Moscow, the capital, is a large city near the western border. More than twelve million people live there. The Kremlin was once a castle or fort. Today it holds palaces, cathedrals, and government offices. It is the center of government and the president's home. Red Square borders it at one end. The Kremlin and Red Square are important to Russia and the world. They will always be protected and restored. St. Basil's Cathedral was built in the sixteenth century. It is one of the most famous sights in Russia. Russia ranks tenth in population, with about 142 million people. About three-fourths live in cities. People speak about one hundred languages. Most use Russian and its thirty-three Cyrillic letters. Russia has about one fourth of the planet's forests. It is one of the biggest lumber producers. Its fishing industry is booming.

3Russia also has huge amounts of coal, oil, and natural gas. It is among the top three producers of oil and natural gas. Soccer and ice hockey are popular, along with swimming, tennis, volleyball, snowmobiling, ice fishing, sledding, and hot springs. Fishermen haul in a net of salmon. Many of the world's best chess players have come from Russia. The arts flourish. Many famous authors, poets, and composers were born in Russia. Russia is a world leader in ballet. Tchaikovsky wrote Swan Lake, which the Moscow Ballet performs. The land has plains, plateaus, and mountains. The West Siberian Plain covers more than 2.6 million square kilometers. The Ural and Sayan Mountains are major ranges. Lake Baikal, at 1,620 meters, is the deepest lake in the world.

4Always frozen tundra covers the north and about one-tenth of Russia. South of it, taiga forests of spruce and pine support the lumber industry. Oak, maple, and birch grow in warmer areas, and the south has steppe grasslands. The climate is very cold, and Oymyakon in Siberia set a record low of -71 C. Peter the Great ruled from 1682 to 1725 and modernized Russia's military and economy. Catherine the Great ruled from 1762 to 1796 and took control of parts of Poland and Turkey. Czars and czarinas ruled until the 1917 revolution ended the monarchy and the USSR formed in 1922. The USSR was the first communist country, and the government owned land, housing, and companies to share wealth equally.

5Russia controlled USSR countries, including Ukraine, Belarus, Georgia, Azerbaijan, and Armenia, and Moscow was the capital. Economic and political problems left many people in poverty. In the Soviet Union in the 1930s, poor women shared a bowl of soup. The USSR broke up on December 31, 1991, and Russia was no longer a communist country. Today businesses compete in a market economy, and elected presidents and a parliament rule Russia. Russia has no single official religion, but the Russian Orthodox Church is its main institution. Many Russians celebrate Christmas on January 7 and Easter with baked goods and painted eggs. A large Muslim population eats only after sunset during the holy month of Ramadan. Independence Day is on June 12, Victory Day on May 9 marks the end of World War II, and Maslenitsa brings blinis, snowball fights, and dancing bears.

6Borscht is a famous soup of beets, cabbage, onions, and carrots. The beets give the soup its red color. People serve it hot or cold. It is popular in Russia and famous around the world. Some people make kvass at home. They soak stale bread in water with yeast, and sometimes raisins or mint. They let the mixture sit for a few days. The drink has a tangy, sour flavor. People use kvass in okroshka, a soup with cucumbers, sausages, salami, and boiled eggs. People serve okroshka cold and eat it in summer. Before the twentieth century, people salted, pickled, or dried food so it would last through long, cold winters. Today, some people still do this to honor tradition.

7The population of Russia is 142,423,773. Its land covers 16,377,742 square kilometers, or 6,323,500 square miles. The capital city is Moscow. The main language is Russian. The main religions are Russian Orthodox and Muslim. The name of the currency is the ruble.

47. Samuel de Champlain and the French Start in the North

1A picture made in 1613 shows St. Croix Island. Samuel de Champlain drew that picture. St. Croix Island was the earliest French settlement in Acadia. Acadia is now part of Maine. This book tells how the English held Champlain as a prisoner. It tells who he was. It follows his first journey to North America. It follows his search along the St. Lawrence. It tells of bitter days in Acadia. It tells how Quebec began. It tells of his final trip back to New France. It tells what remained after his life. Word meanings come at the end. A map marks Indigenous lands on his path in 1629. The Labrador Sea is on that map. Hudson Bay is on that map. Canada is on that map. The Wabanaki Confederacy is on that map. The Atlantic Ocean is on that map. Four Indigenous nations formed the Wabanaki Confederacy. Each of those nations had its own system of government. Nitassinan was the homeland of the Innu people. The Montagnais in the south belong to the Innu. The month was July. The year was 1629. The place was Quebec. Quebec was a trading settlement. Samuel de Champlain had started that settlement. He started it on the river he called the St. Lawrence. He stood at the edge of the region he called New France. New France was a huge area. People later called that area Canada. He also stood at the edge of the Wabanaki Confederacy. He stood at the edge of Nitassinan. Those places were long-standing homelands of different Indigenous peoples. This part of North America was land France hoped to possess. England wanted to own it too.

2English ships rode at anchor in the river next to the fort. David Kirke was in command of the vessels. He was a private English sea captain. He was also an adventurer. His brother Lewis was with him. The Kirke family had five brothers in all. England had chosen the brothers to seize New France. The siege of Quebec was one piece of a larger war. France and England were fighting that war. Five hundred years of off-and-on fighting lay behind the two countries. Now the fighting had spread into North America. Both countries were trying to lock down control of Indigenous nations' lands. The Wabanaki Confederacy was one of those nations. The Haudenosaunee Confederacy was another. England wanted quick wealth from those lands. France wanted that wealth too. The wealth they sought was timber. It was also furs. It was also minerals. Champlain had tried to hold a French foothold. That foothold sat in Wabanaki lands. It sat in Wyandot lands. It sat in Innu lands. He could see that hope was gone. Fewer than one hundred people were in the fort. Fighting men among them numbered no more than eighteen. Almost every bit of food was gone. The people defending the fort were starving. The day was July 16. Champlain handed over the fort. He did it so the suffering would stop. David Kirke was asked for leave to sail the colonists home to France. Kirke refused that request. The captain made Champlain a prisoner instead. Champlain and the other French colonists were put aboard the English ships. Those ships set sail for England.

3A true likeness of Champlain was never made. Most drawings of him come from a portrait of a different French official. That portrait was made in 1654. The drawing on this page is one of those later pictures. What we know about Samuel de Champlain is incomplete. The early years of his life are the least clear. Brouage is the town of his birth. Brouage lies near the western coast of France. His birth was sometime around 1567. His father was a sea captain. Ocean navigation was a skill his father taught him. Several sea trips took Samuel out with his father. He also sailed with his uncle. His uncle was another seaman in the family. Those trips helped him polish his navigational skills. During the same years he became an expert mapmaker. Army service in France filled most of his twenties. In 1598 he began long voyages with his uncle. Those voyages went to Spanish territories in North America. Those territories are now the Caribbean. On every trip he sketched local plants. He sketched local animals. He wrote notes that described what he saw. He was back in France in 1602. He gave King Henry IV a book of sketches and descriptions. The king was impressed with the young sea voyager. Henry believed Champlain could help make France stronger in North America. The king wanted more colonies. More colonies would keep French access to the riches of the continent. French people would populate the colonies. Military force would hold the colonies against other nations. The French king also wanted Indigenous peoples converted to Christianity. He wanted their lands seized for the Crown. That pair of goals was a common strategy of colonization. Champlain could draw with skill. He was a cartographer as well. His sketches show Indigenous peoples of North America.

4The year was 1603. The trip to New France was ordered by King Henry. Champlain went with a group that wanted to trade with Indigenous peoples. The trade they wanted was in furs. Getting furs was a leading reason for posts in North America. Beaver pelts were the main fur they sought. For centuries, people in Europe had prized beaver pelts. Hats made from those pelts were popular fashion items. By the early 1600s, beavers in Europe had been nearly wiped out. The demand for beaver hats had caused that loss. North America still had huge numbers of beavers. King Henry told Champlain to find a good site. The site would be for a fur-trading colony. Henry gave him a second task as well. Champlain was to search for the Northwest Passage. That passage would be a water route through North America. It would be a shortcut to Asia. By the early 1500s, explorers had learned something important. The Americas formed one unbroken land mass. The land stretched from the Arctic down to South America's tip. For Europeans, the best path west toward the Pacific Ocean would pass through North America or above it. The same path would lead on to Asia. Many explorers hunted for a water route across the continent. The route stayed unfound. It did not exist. Many European explorers wanted a northern sea lane toward Asia's markets. People called that lane a Northwest Passage. Ships from western Europe usually reached Asia by rounding the Cape of Good Hope. It also went around the southern tip of Africa. The path around Africa was long. Huge waves in the southern latitudes made it very dangerous. Merchants had long wanted a shorter route to Asia. They wanted a safer route too. In 1492 Christopher Columbus was searching for such a route. He ran into the Americas instead. If someone had found the route, the finder would have kept it. It was not something to share. It was something a country would seize for its own exclusive use. About four hundred years later, melting Arctic ice opened a real Northwest Passage. Ships can use that passage during the summer months.

5Champlain's group left in two ships. They set sail in March 1603. Violent storms tossed them during a voyage of ten weeks. Then they entered a wide river. That river is now known as the St. Lawrence. A harbor is where the ships stopped. The harbor is where the St. Lawrence meets the Saguenay River. Saguenay is said SAG-uh-nay. The next day Champlain met Indigenous people of the area for the first time. About a thousand people had gathered. The far bank of the Saguenay River was where they stood. They sang. They played music. They were celebrating a victory. The victory was over a shared enemy. That enemy was the Haudenosaunee. People commonly called them the Iroquois. Iroquois is said EER-oh-kwaw. Champlain crossed the river to meet the group. This was not their first meeting with Frenchmen. In 1534 another French explorer had claimed lands to the north for France. His name was Jacques Cartier. Cartier is said kar-tee-YAY. Cartier tried to found a colony in New France. That attempt failed. He did open the French fur trade. One tribe is not what the Haudenosaunee are. In that era the league held five nations. Later the league had six nations. They spoke related languages. They had formed an alliance with the English. When Champlain arrived they numbered about 12,000. Smallpox took at least half their people by the 1640s. Indigenous peoples in this land were skilled at trapping animals for fur. They traded those furs with the Europeans. The Europeans wanted more than money from the fur trade. They wanted to seize Indigenous land. They wanted to convert Indigenous peoples to Christianity. They wanted to block the English from moving farther into the area. Before this, Europeans were still new to most Indigenous peoples. They had little immunity to European diseases. Many Indigenous people died. Those huge drops in population helped European colonizers. Lands once full of Indigenous people now had fewer living there. An Indigenous man who spoke French aided Champlain. With that help, Champlain met an Innu chief. The southern Innu people are the Montagnais. Montagnais is said mon-ten-NAY. In French the name means mountain people. The chief's hope was French help to vanquish that enemy. That enemy was the Haudenosaunee. Champlain saw that an alliance with these tribes offered great possibilities. He might side with them against the Haudenosaunee. In return they might help him grow the fur trade. They might help him explore this land. In time they might help him acquire it for France.

6A picture from 1777 shows Indigenous men trading furs. They are trading with European sailors. Champlain's men explored much of the Saguenay River. After that they set out to explore the St. Lawrence Valley. A stop came at one spot as they traveled upriver. The Montagnais called that spot Kebec. Kebec means where the river narrows. To the French, the place became Quebec. An Indigenous town had once stood there. A French settlement, he believed, fit that spot perfectly. On several later expeditions he went on exploring the St. Lawrence. He ventured some 2,500 kilometers. That distance is 1,550 miles. He was sure the rivers and lakes of New France held a water route to the Pacific Ocean. That route would be the Northwest Passage. He could not find such a route. He never would. In mid-August of 1603 he set sail for France. He arrived in late September. He traveled to Paris. He made his report to King Henry. The king was pleased. Henry's trust showed when he gave Champlain the title Vice Admiral of France. At this time Champlain published a book. Three more books about his time in New France would follow this first one. In 1604 he returned to New France. About eighty male colonists came with him. The group included laborers. It included doctors. It included carpenters. It included missionaries. The missionaries hoped to persuade Indigenous peoples to give up their faith traditions. They also hoped Indigenous peoples would give up an entire way of life.

7The colonists settled in Acadia. The Mi'kmaq lived in much of that land. Mi'kmaq is said MIG-maw. They are one nation in the Wabanaki Confederacy. The French named their island Sainte-Croix. Sainte-Croix is said sawnt-KWAW. They built a storehouse and homes. They built other buildings too. Then a hard winter came. Wood and water were hard to find. The men ate dried food and salted meat. They had no fruit or vegetables. They got sick with scurvy. Scurvy comes from a lack of vitamin C. By spring, thirty-five of the men had died. Citrus fruit can stop scurvy. In the Canada of the 1600s, they could not get it. In winter, scurvy was the most common sickness among the Haudenosaunee. They boiled parts of the eastern white cedar. People came to call that tree the tree of life.

8Champlain came back to the St. Lawrence Valley in June 1608. Thirty-two colonists were with him. July was the month they anchored at Quebec. Here he planned a permanent settlement in the valley. He also planned a fur-trading post. He put the colonists to work at once. They began building a group of houses. They built other buildings too. He called the group the Habitation of Quebec. As the work went on, several tribes visited the settlement. The visitors included the Wyandot, also called the Huron. They included the Anishinaabe, also called the Algonquin. They included the Montagnais. His promise was what they brought up to Champlain. He had promised to help them fight the Haudenosaunee. A drawing of the Quebec settlement comes from Champlain's own sketches. Winter came early that year. It was a bitter winter. Illness started spreading among the men inside the Habitation. Scurvy struck many of them, as it had before. Twenty-eight colonists stayed for the winter. Twenty of them died. Spring came. It was time for Champlain to keep his promises to his allies. In July 1609 he and his men joined the Indigenous nations. They carried early firearms called arquebuses. Arquebuses is said ar-kway-BOOS-es. Together they raided the Haudenosaunee. They defeated them that time. A long lake appeared on this journey south. He named the lake for himself. Today that lake is a natural border between New York and Vermont. People still call it Lake Champlain. In later years Champlain and his men joined their Indigenous allies in other battles. Those battles were against the Haudenosaunee. The Haudenosaunee were allies of the English. The Indigenous nations at war shared far more than they differed. The French and the English were enemies too. They also had more in common with each other. The Europeans wanted to own the land. They wanted to exploit it. North America would change hugely under that view of nature. Unlike Europe, North America had stayed largely intact up to this time.

9Champlain went back to France often. He reported on his work in Quebec. He recruited new colonists. The title governor of New France came to him in 1620. Under his administration, Quebec grew. Quebec prospered. He wanted the settlement's defenses to be stronger. He built a fort on Cap Diamant. Cap Diamant is a rocky cliff. It overlooks the Habitation. A few years later he knocked that fort down. He built a larger fort in its place. The new fort was stronger. In 1629 he and his fellow colonists were in that fort. There they finally surrendered to the Kirke brothers. The Kirkes had taken Champlain prisoner. He landed in England in mid-October 1629. He then heard news he had not expected. France and England were no longer at war. A peace treaty between the two nations was already six months old. That meant the Kirke brothers' conquest was illegal. The conquest covered Quebec. It covered other parts of New France. Any territory the Kirkes won after the treaty was signed would go back to France. King Charles of England agreed to a return. Quebec should be given back to France. Other territory the Kirkes now ruled should be given back too. From the Indigenous point of view, both European claims were illegal. Indigenous laws said the same thing. King Charles accepted another treaty in March 1632. That treaty returned all of New France to the French. Champlain was free at last to go back to Quebec. He returned the following year. He took up his duties as governor of New France again. He was now in his sixties. He was tired. He was feeling his age. Two years later he suffered a stroke. One month after that stroke, he died. The day was Christmas Day. The year was 1635.

10Champlain traveled through lands that are now six Canadian provinces. He also traveled through lands that are now five American states. He started a small settlement where the river narrows. That place became Quebec City. Quebec City is the capital of the province of Quebec. He hoped New France would last as a French colony. It did not. From 1689 to 1763, about seventy-five years passed. In that time France and England fought four more wars. They fought for control of New France. After 1707, England was part of Great Britain. France lost. Its lands in North America came under British rule. Quebec became part of the British Empire. Even so, many French ways still last. In Quebec City, shops have signs in French. Indigenous people live on there too. The Montagnais are among them. They are part of the Innu Nation.

11These words have special meanings in this book. Administration means management. Appointed means chosen by one person or a group to do a job. Colonists are people who live in a colony. They can also be people who founded a new colony. Conquest is the act of invading another area or group. It includes taking over. It includes ruling. Convert means to change from one religion to another. It can also mean a change of belief or view. To exploit is to use a person or a thing for gain. It can mean making use of someone or something. A habitation is a home or a living space. Indigenous means native to a particular place. Missionaries go to another place so they can spread a religion. Navigate means to find one's way over a long distance. It also means to steer a course toward a destination. Populated means that people were added. A settlement is a new place where people live. In a siege, an enemy is surrounded and held that way for a long time. During a siege the enemy is kept from food and other supplies. A site is the ground where something is found or happens. It can be the place where something was found or happened. A treaty is a formal agreement of peace or friendship. It is made between two nations or groups. Vanquish means to defeat in battle. Ventured means dared to go to a dangerous place. Voyages are long trips or journeys. They are especially trips on a ship.

48. Satellites

1A satellite is any object in space that orbits another object. To orbit means to travel around that object. There are natural satellites and artificial satellites. The Moon is a natural satellite of Earth. The eight planets orbit the Sun, but they are not natural satellites. They are Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune. People make artificial satellites.

2Artificial satellites are high-tech tools that orbit Earth. They do jobs that people cannot do. They have been important since Sputnik I launched in 1957. They help with phones, TV, ships, and storm forecasts. Thousands of satellites now orbit Earth. Space probes explore planets, moons, or asteroids and send data back. The payload holds cameras, antennas, and other tools. The bus holds the parts, and solar cells turn sunlight into power.

3Downlinking sends a radio signal from a satellite to Earth. Dishes called Earth stations receive those signals. Uplinking sends a signal from Earth up to a satellite. Computers act like a brain, and cameras act like eyes. Sensors notice light, heat, water, minerals, and gases. In 2010, GOCE data showed tiny changes in Earth's gravity. Speed and gravity keep a satellite in orbit. In low orbit, about 200 to 500 miles up, satellites fly near 17,000 miles per hour.

4Space holds junk such as gloves, bolts, rocket parts, and meteoroids. A paint fleck dug a pit in a shuttle window. Junk can hit spacecraft at 170,000 miles per hour or more. NASA has charted over 370,000 pieces of space junk. Some satellites use an oval orbit to see Earth more closely. Polar satellites fly from pole to pole about eighteen times a day. Other satellites orbit over the equator about 22,300 miles up. They turn with Earth, so they seem to stay over one spot.

5Scientists design satellites for the data they need. Communication satellites carry phone, email, TV, and radio signals. In 1962, Telstar sent the first live broadcast across the Atlantic. Signals can now bounce from one satellite to another. Some satellites watch forests, rivers, oceans, fires, and volcanoes. They also record pollution and other changes on Earth. Spy satellites have taken pictures of Earth since 1960. They watch other nations, weapons, armies, and secret messages.

6In 1983, President Reagan announced a space defense plan called Star Wars. Defense satellites would spot enemy missiles so they could be destroyed in the air. That system was never finished. Imaging satellites help find oil, minerals, drug crops, and pollution. Research satellites map Earth and track meteors that come too close. One research satellite image shows New York City. In disasters, satellite signals can still get through. Mobile dishes send news up, and satellites show the size of the damage.

7Weather satellites track storms and can save lives. They warn people about hurricanes and typhoons. They also measure wind, rain, and air temperature. Two weather satellites circle the poles, and others orbit the equator. GPS began as a military tool. It uses radio signals from at least four satellites to find a place. Cars, ships, and planes use it, but storms or power failures can disturb it. Other satellites study the Moon, stars, and galaxies, even in dark space.

8The Hubble Space Telescope launched into low orbit in 1990. Later repairs gave it new cameras, batteries, and other tools. It flies at about 17,000 miles per hour. It has helped scientists study stars, black holes, and galaxies. Isaac Newton had the idea for an artificial satellite in 1687. Sputnik I launched in 1957 and was about the size of a basketball. It circled Earth in 98 minutes and began the space race. Sputnik II carried the dog Laika, but she died in space.

9Space stations let astronauts live and work while orbiting Earth. The first crew boarded the International Space Station in 2000. Before that, Russia's Mir was the largest space station. Mir weighed 130 tons, stayed up for 15 years, and had six parts. By 2010, the ISS was about four times larger than Mir. It has nearly an acre of solar panels. Sixteen countries share the station, and Canada built a robotic arm. It orbits about 250 miles up, where crews study gravity and Earth.

10Satellite rescue grew easier after the shuttle Columbia launched in 1981. Astronauts could move outside with units like chairs on their backs. Robot arms could grab satellites and move them. The U.S. shuttle program ended in 2011, so NASA tested new repair tools. No world law says old satellites must leave orbit. Above 500 miles, objects can stay for thousands of years. Many old satellites fall back and burn up in the air. New systems help send data, track farms, and study space.

11You can learn more about these topics on the Internet. Use www.google.com to search for words from this book. You can also search for glossary words or index words. One search to try is satellites. Another search is Global Positioning System. You can also search for Hubble Space Telescope.

49. Seeds of Revolution

1More than 200 years ago, colonists started a war against an empire and won. They lived in what is now the United States, and they fought Great Britain. Great Britain was a powerful country. It tried to expand its empire and secure land in the New World. The New World is North, Central, and South America today. Other European countries wanted land there too. In 1750, claims included New France, British Canada, Louisiana, the British colonies, Florida, and New Spain. Some land was disputed, and the oceans shown are the Atlantic and the Pacific.

2The New World had raw materials and land, but Indigenous peoples had lived there for thousands of years. Spain began colonizing in the 1400s and enslaved and killed Indigenous peoples. By the 1700s, European colonizers had killed millions of Indigenous peoples through violent conflict and the spread of disease. Great Britain took land by force and set up thirteen colonies across the Atlantic Ocean. Colonists had charters with the king and legislatures elected by property owners. Their independent spirit fueled the Revolutionary War, though they had once been allies with Britain. In the 1750s, France and Britain both wanted the same land in North America. The French explored from Newfoundland to the Great Lakes and the Ohio River Valley and set up fur posts. Some nations, including the Cherokee, the Catawba, and the Iroquois Confederacy, allied with Britain, while many others allied with France.

3The Delaware, the Shawnee, and the Ottawa allied with France, and both sides built forts in the Ohio River Valley. In early 1754, the French finished Fort Duquesne at a key spot where Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, is today. The fort stood near the Ohio, Allegheny, and Monongahela rivers. George Washington failed to make the French leave. He, his men, and allies from the Seneca Nation then fought a group of French scouts. They built Fort Necessity about 60 miles, or 96.5 kilometers, away, but the French captured it in 1754. Washington surrendered, escaped, and later aided General Edward Braddock. French soldiers and Indigenous allies ambushed the British, killing Braddock and more than half his soldiers. Many call this the first real battle of the French and Indian War, part of the Seven Years' War.

4In 1756, Great Britain declared war on France, though many battles had already been fought. At first France seemed likely to win because of Indigenous allies and better frontier warfare. By 1758, Britain had almost 42,000 troops and attacked Fort Niagara, Lake Champlain, and Quebec. The French were cut off from supplies in the Ohio River Valley, the Mississippi River, and New Orleans. Britain won, and the Treaty of Paris in 1763 ended French claims east of the Mississippi River. Colonists could enter old French lands, including Canada, and were pleased for a short while. After the war, British land included Canada, the thirteen colonies, Florida, and land reserved for Indigenous peoples. King George III and Parliament then raised taxes and made colonists house and feed British soldiers.

5In 1764, Parliament passed the Sugar Act, an expansion of the Sugar and Molasses Act of 1733. It taxed Spanish and French molasses and sugar, and it added taxes on coffee and some textiles. It also limited where colonists could sell their products. Cheap foreign sugar became so costly that colonists had to buy British sugar. This raised money for war debts and hurt the colonial economy. In 1765, the Quartering Act said colonists must house 10,000 British troops in private homes without rent. King George III had become king in 1760 at age 22, and he wanted the colonies to stay loyal. Most colonies refused. The British government felt it had supported the colonists for too long.

6Colonists were angry about taxes when they had no voice in Parliament. They cried, "No taxation without representation." King George did not like being told what to do, but the colonists began to work together. Virginia's elected assembly, begun in 1619, backed Patrick Henry. Henry said only Virginians had the right to tax Virginians. Some called him a traitor, but protest spread through the 13 colonies. Samuel Adams also spoke out, and colony supporters were called Patriots, while king supporters were called Loyalists. The Sons of Liberty and the Daughters of Liberty urged boycotts, and some stamp agents were attacked.

7In October 1765, nine colonies sent representatives to the Stamp Act Congress in New York. The congress asked Parliament to cancel the Stamp Act, and King George agreed in 1766. In 1767, the Townshend Acts taxed glass, paint, lead, paper, and tea. Colonists refused to buy British goods. In 1770, Britain repealed every Townshend tax but the tax on tea. On March 5, 1770, rowdy colonists in Boston fought British soldiers, who fired into the crowd. The soldiers killed five colonists armed only with snowballs, sticks, and stones. Samuel Adams called it the Boston Massacre, and Paul Revere's picture enraged many colonists even though it was not a true account.

8Crispus Attucks lived in Boston and worked as a whaler on ships in Boston Harbor. He is believed to be the first person killed in the Boston Massacre. Speeches at his memorial honored his bravery, and he believed strongly in freedom from Great Britain. In 1773, the revised Tea Act said colonists must buy tea only from the East India Company. That December, colonists dressed as members of the Mohawk Nation and dumped 342 chests of tea into Boston Harbor. They hoped the disguise would keep them from being blamed. In 1774, the Coercive Acts, called the Intolerable Acts, closed Boston Harbor to punish the colonists. General Thomas Gage brought 4,000 troops to Boston, and colonists had to house and feed them. Twelve colonies, but not Georgia, sent delegates to the First Continental Congress, yet Parliament refused their demands and declared mutiny.

9The delegates knew war was possible, so they agreed to meet again in 1775. They urged Massachusetts to stockpile weapons and stop all trade with Great Britain. They trained minutemen, who could be ready in a minute and often wore no uniforms. Colonists were preparing to fight for independence from a country many no longer trusted. On April 18, 1775, General Gage sent 700 soldiers to seize weapons at Concord, about 20 miles west of Boston. He also planned to arrest Samuel Adams and John Hancock in nearby Lexington. Doctor Samuel Prescott warned the militia that the British were coming. At Lexington, an unordered shot started the Revolutionary War, eight militiamen died, and ten were wounded.

10Paul Revere is famous for warning the troops that the British were coming, but that part of the story is a tall tale. He sent a spy to hang one lantern for a land march or two if the British crossed the Charles River. Revere saw two lanterns in the church tower that night. He rode with William Dawes, and Samuel Prescott joined them. British officers stopped Revere and Dawes, but Prescott escaped and warned the militiamen. The British destroyed some supplies at Concord, but most weapons had been hidden. Nearly 500 militiamen attacked at the North Bridge and fired from behind trees and walls as the British retreated. At Lexington and Concord, 273 British soldiers and 93 militiamen died, and the Revolutionary War had begun.

11Allies join together for a common cause. A charter sets out rights and duties, and a legislature makes or changes laws. Colonists live in a colony, and delegates represent other people. Parliament is the lawmaking body of Great Britain. A militia is an army of ordinary citizens, not trained soldiers. Mutiny is a refusal to follow orders, and a revolution removes a government by force. Raw materials are basic substances, and textiles are cloth. To migrate is to move to another region to live or work, and to stockpile is to keep a large supply.

50. Seeing What Eyes Miss

1A credit card has a raised number. Magnification shows tiny flaws in that number. This book starts with things barely visible to the eye. It then tells how the first simple microscopes worked. Next it explains compound microscopes. Later pages step through stronger views. One step is twenty to thirty times. Another is fifty to two hundred times. A further step runs from two hundred times up to fifteen hundred times. One page reaches one million times. A closing page and a word list finish the book. A green blowfly's body would look strange if you could shrink yourself very small. Measure a human hair and the width is about a tenth of one millimeter. In inches, that width is 0.0039. A hair that thin is among the smallest things a human eye can see. Picture yourself shrunk until the hair looks as thick as your arm. In a second picture, that same hair is as thick as a tree trunk. What would meet your eyes? Hairs on a fly's legs would seem like huge spikes. The fly would seem like a monster with giant eyes. A microscope gives that kind of close view. It changes the way we see the world.

2People have spent thousands of years wanting to inspect their world more closely. The ancient Greeks filled glass globes with water. Objects seen through that water looked bigger. Roman makers, during the first century ce, found a new trick with glass. The useful glass was thicker at the center than at the rim. Things looked larger through it. That glass was the first magnifying glass. In Europe in the 1600s, people learned to make better glass lenses. The microscope was invented then. At the start, a microscope was only a fancy magnifying glass. One glass lens sat above a holder for specimens. Most magnifying glasses make objects look about five to ten times larger. People write that power as 5–10×. The first simple microscopes had that same power. A simple microscope from 1780 folded into its case. A person could carry it in a pocket. A modern compound microscope has named parts. They are the eyepiece lens, the body tube, and the objective lens. This kind of microscope has three objective lenses, each with a different power. It also has a focus knob, a stage, a mirror, and a base. Early users learned they could line up two magnifying glasses. The second lens enlarged the image that the first lens had already made. That step multiplied the power. Two lenses in one instrument created the compound microscope. Multiply the two lens powers and you get the total magnification. Picture a first lens at ten times, written 10×. Picture a second lens at forty times, written 40×. The specimen is then seen at four hundred times its real size, written 400×. Today a compound microscope can make an object look up to fifteen hundred times larger than it is. People write that power as 1,500×.

3Some views are only twenty to thirty times life size, written 20–30×. Robert Hooke, a British scientist, was among the first people to use early microscopes. He improved the early compound microscopes. Those tools could enlarge objects only about twenty to thirty times their real size. He was also among the first to write careful notes on what he saw. In 1665 he published those notes in a book called Micrographia. No scientist had written a book about using a microscope before this one. Hooke's Micrographia includes drawings of a louse up close and a flea up close. He drew a fly's compound eyes with more detail than anyone had shown before. He sketched the separate hairs that cover a fly's body. He sketched the delicate antennae. He sketched the complicated mouthparts. A detailed flea drawing from that 1665 book is one picture from his work. Plant sections and seed sections were in his sketches too. He looked at a thin slice of cork. He found that the cork was made of hundreds of tiny chambers. He called those chambers cells. Monks lived in small rooms inside a monastery. Those rooms came to his mind. Those rooms were called cells too. Later, people used cell for the tiny blocks that build every living thing. A magnified slice of cork still shows those many tiny cells. Today, thin plant slices show how seeds form. They show how cells build the veins and tissues of leaves. They show the cells that let water and nutrients move in stems. From that view, we can see how a plant stays alive and how it grows.

4Stronger views reach fifty to two hundred times life size, written 50–200×. Historians believe Hooke's Micrographia stirred another early user, Antonie van Leeuwenhoek. Say his name LEY-vun-hook. He was a Dutch cloth merchant. He was not a trained scientist. He used magnifying glasses to study the fabrics he bought and sold. Hooke's drawings of the tiny world moved him to grind lenses himself. He found a way to make better lenses. An object under his microscopes could look over 200 times as large as it really was. People write that power as 200×. He built more than five hundred microscopes. That work happened in the late 1600s and the early 1700s. With these stronger tools he found red blood cells. He also found tiny living things called microorganisms. They lived in lake water. They also lived in scrapings from a person's teeth. Before his time, nobody knew microorganisms existed. They are living things too small for the naked eye. Microscopes showed that these tiny lives are everywhere. Pond water, seen through a lens, can show them.
