A classroom microscope today likely enlarges about as much as his early tools did. Two hundred times may not sound huge. It is still enough to show details the naked eye misses. Even sand looks new under a lens. Without magnification, sand looks plain. Magnified sand from the ocean's edge holds microscopic shells. Pieces of shells are in it. Coral is in it. Volcanic rock is in it. Ordinary beach sand is really many tiny pieces with color.

5Still stronger views run from two hundred times to fifteen hundred times, written 200–1,500×. Early compound microscopes had a problem. Their lenses often bent the picture into distortions. It was very hard to know which part was the real image. The rest could be a false effect. Light passing through the lenses caused that effect. Joseph Jackson Lister solved this in the early 1800s. He was a scientist from Britain. He built a new kind of lens. His lens fixed the distortion problem. His work made microscopes stronger and more reliable. Older microscopes could stretch a picture the way a funhouse mirror does. After the fix, microscopes opened new finds in science and in medicine. Louis Pasteur, a biologist from France, worked in the mid-1800s. He found what made some drinks spoil. Say his name pass-TUR. Microorganisms in the air could get into beer or milk and spoil it. Around the same time, better microscopes let doctors see microorganisms inside sick people. Scientists linked microorganisms to disease for the first time. That link changed how medicine was practiced. Photos made by Snowflake Bentley in 1902 show another discovery. Each snowflake has its own beautiful shape. The early compound microscope helped prove that no two snowflakes match. Wilson Alwyn Bentley was an American photographer. People called him Snowflake Bentley because of his snowflake work. He fastened a camera onto his microscope. The years were the late 1800s and the start of the 1900s. He photographed more than five thousand separate snowflakes. He showed that a snowflake mirrors itself in six directions. Salt forms very specific crystals. Sugar does too. So do some other materials. Compound microscopes showed this. Salt contains only two kinds of atoms, spaced in a regular way. Because of that, salt forms cube-shaped crystals that repeat. Sugar, also called sucrose, is built from two simpler sugars that are not the same. Its crystals slant at each end. They are more oblong than salt crystals. Sugar crystals take a regular rectangular form.

6Natural hooks on a cocklebur gave people a model for a fastener. The fastener is called Velcro. Tiny details can show how things work. George de Mestral was an engineer from Switzerland. Cockleburs made him curious. The burrs stuck to his clothes and to his dog's fur. He wanted to know why the grip was so firm. He put the burrs under a microscope. He saw tiny hooks. The hooks caught loops in the cloth of his pants. Nature's hook-and-loop fastener gave him the idea. He tried to copy that design in fabric. Years later, Velcro was the result. Velcro uses two cloth pieces. Thousands of tiny hooks cover one piece. Thousands of tiny loops cover the other. The hooks and loops let the two pieces zip together. A tiny dust mite is another magnified subject. Flakes of skin from people are its food. Light itself sets a limit on how far a compound microscope can enlarge. During the last century, scientists built microscopes that light a sample in new ways. One way uses ultraviolet light. Another sends energy beams made of particles called electrons. A microscope that lights a sample with electron beams is an electron microscope. Electron microscopes enlarge more than any other kind now in use. They can enlarge a sample up to one million times. People write that power as 1,000,000×.

7A fish scale under the microscope shows details the naked eye cannot catch. Each step up in magnification shows more about the tiniest structures. Those structures belong to plants, animals, and materials. Nearly every science field has gained countless finds from microscopes. They turn the invisible into something we can see. They let us explore the world in a wholly new way. Plenty of cells are transparent. Other small objects are too. Transparent means see-through. That makes them hard to see under a microscope. Since the 1800s, stains and dyes have helped. They make an object stand out from what is behind it. The object is then easier to see. Stain on a sick human liver, seen up close, shows a change. Healthy cells, colored orange, are being replaced. Damaged cells, colored purple, take their place. Some animals have thin feelers on their heads. Those feelers are antennae. They help those animals touch and smell. An electron is a particle in an atom. It orbits the nucleus and carries a negative electrical charge. Artificial means human-made, not found in nature. A microorganism is a living thing so small it takes a microscope to see. Viruses fit this word. Bacterial cells fit it too. Compound means made of two or more parts. A microscope is a device for viewing tiny objects. To contaminate is to add something harmful, or something unwanted, so the item is no longer safe or usable. Living things need nutrients if they are to stay alive, keep healthy, and grow. A crystal is a solid whose atoms sit in a regular, tight pattern. In math, multiply two or more numbers and the answer is the product. Specimens are examples kept for comparison, study, or display. Distortions are twisted or untrue pictures of something.

51. Space Camp

1In the summer of 1990, sixth grader Kate Rubins went to camp in Huntsville, Alabama. It was not a camp with campfires or canoes. It was space camp. The program taught the science and excitement of space exploration. Kate told her parents she wanted to be an astronaut, a biologist, and a geologist, in that order. She joined science and engineering activities with great energy. She loved real astronaut training and team missions that were only practice. Campers stay in a habitat at United States Space Camp.

2In 2016, young trainees at that same camp watched a live rocket launch. A giant Russian rocket carried supplies and crew to the International Space Station. The station orbits more than 322 kilometers, or 200 miles, above Earth. One of the three astronauts was Dr. Kate Rubins, a space camp graduate. She had worked as a biologist, then trained with NASA. She served four months as flight engineer and did biology experiments. Her childhood dream came true. The Pathfinder, a space shuttle model, stands near the Alabama camp. Space camps teach history, robotics, and real astronaut equipment. Trainees also practice teamwork in machines that copy a spacecraft. The Alabama camp is near the United States Space and Rocket Center. Trainees can see rockets and capsules that helped NASA reach the Moon in 1969. More than 750,000 trainees have come since 1982, from all fifty states and more than sixty countries.

3Some universities and museums in the United States host space camps in summer. Children can go to the Frontiers of Flight Museum in Texas. They can also go to Camp Kennedy Space Center in Florida. In Washington, D.C., the Smithsonian Association runs day camps for children as young as kindergarten. These programs feed interest in science, technology, engineering, and even science fiction. In Quebec, Canada, the Cosmodome hosts three-day camps. Space Camp Turkey is in Izmir, one of Turkey’s biggest cities. Russian Space Camp is at the Gagarin Cosmonaut Training Center. The center is named for Yuri Gagarin, the first human in space, in 1961. Camps are also in Dallas, Huntsville, Merritt Island, Laval, and Star City. Some camps let young children come with family. Older children are grouped by age. Some sessions help trainees with visual or hearing needs. Scholarships help more space fans attend. Rockets built at camp can be powered by water. A sample day includes breakfast, simulators, team building, lunch, rocket building, a movie, mission practice, dinner, history, engineering, and bedtime.

4Space camps focus on the history, science, and technology of space travel. Many trainees build and launch model rockets. Others study spacecraft design and design a ship for a mission. Robotics programs are common too. The Opportunity and Spirit robotic rovers explored the surface of Mars. Trainees build and program robots to solve real problems. Teams may compete at the end of camp. A NASA astronaut and an educator tested a rover built in a camp program. Astronaut food is dried so it takes less space, packs easily, and stays fresh longer. Alabama’s camp has a weeklong aviation program about flight and military aircraft. The earliest NASA astronauts began as military test pilots. At the Cosmodome, trainees sleep in bags like those on the space station. Other camps let trainees try the special meals astronauts eat. Adults come from around the world too. Some science teachers go home with new lessons for young explorers.

5Simulators are among the most popular activities. Astronauts must practice every situation before they enter a spacecraft. Trainees feel some of those challenges, and counselors always watch them. The Multi-Axis Trainer was used in the early 1960s. It helped the first astronauts practice taking control of a tumbling capsule. A trainee is strapped in a metal ring inside a larger ring. The rings spin wildly in different directions. The trainer is made so riders should not feel sick or dizzy. The 1/6 Gravity Chair lets trainees feel bounding on the Moon. Moon gravity is one-sixth of Earth’s gravity. A person who weighs 27 kilograms, or 59.5 pounds, on Earth would weigh 4.5 kilograms, or 10 pounds, on the Moon. The Space Shot shoots riders up at four g’s, four times Earth’s gravity. A rider who weighs 23 kilograms, or 50.7 pounds, feels like 92 kilograms, or 202.8 pounds. The G-Force Accelerator spins fast and reaches three g’s. That is the same force astronauts feel at launch.

6Some trainees work inside simulators and use controls as if they are flying a shuttle. Simulated missions are another highlight, and some work much like video games. Teamwork and leadership matter for real astronauts. One mission has trainees launch a shuttle, fly to the space station, and dock. They talk on headsets and follow checklists so tasks stay on schedule. Some act as astronauts, and others work in Mission Control back on Earth. Some practice trips go to the Moon or take place on models of a station or spacecraft. The crew must think fast if the simulator makes an emergency, such as an engine problem or a meteor shower. For older trainees, a few camps practice a future mission to Mars. In Alabama, crews use life-size models of the Orion capsule and a Mars lander. Real astronauts will live on Orion on the planned trip to Mars and back. They will use the lander to reach the surface and return to Orion in orbit. Campers also repair a model of the Hubble Telescope.

7Dr. Leroy Chiao is a veteran NASA astronaut and now heads the Houston Association for Space and Science Education. He visits students and trainees around the world by live stream. Bobak Ferdowsi is an engineer planning a robotic probe to Europa, a moon of Jupiter. Samantha Cristoforetti was a space camp trainee and later a fighter pilot for Italy. In 2014 and 2015, she served 199 days on the space station. Robert Gibson, called Hoot, supports space camps and flew five shuttle missions in the 1980s and 1990s. Michael Lopez-Alegria flew four shuttle missions and has spoken at Space Camp Turkey. Like Kate Rubins, today’s trainees may be tomorrow’s astronauts. NASA and other programs hope to send a mission to Mars as soon as the 2030s. Current trainees could ride Orion. They could be among the first people to leave footprints on the Red Planet.

52. Teotihuacan

1Teotihuacán is in Mexico, in North America, near Mexico City. Its largest structures are the Pyramid of the Sun and the Pyramid of the Moon. Almost two thousand years ago, ancient people built these great pyramids. No one knows who built them, but the site was made to inspire awe. The Avenue of the Dead is as wide as an eleven-lane highway. Massive stone temples and palaces stand on both sides. In 1320 the Aztecs named the ruins Teotihuacán, the place where the gods were created. Locals told the Spanish that giants from the heavens had built them.

2Teotihuacán was one of the world’s early city centers and one of the biggest. One to two hundred thousand people lived there at its peak. It was the largest city in the Americas and probably the sixth largest on Earth. Every building, avenue, road, and plaza lined up on a grid. Builders changed the San Juan River so it crossed the Avenue of the Dead at a right angle. The avenue is 95 meters wide in places, and the Citadel is about 400 meters on each side. The Pyramid of the Moon is 43 meters high, and the Pyramid of the Sun is the third-largest ancient pyramid. The Aztecs named the Avenue of the Dead after they mistook nearby buildings for tombs.

3The Pyramid of the Sun is the city’s largest structure and the world’s third-largest ancient pyramid. Its base is about 220 by 230 meters, and it is more than 66 meters high. It stands where the Sun sets on August 13 and faces 15.5 degrees east of true north. The whole city shares that line, and one idea is that builders followed the Sun. In 1971, archaeologists found a cave under it that had been a sacred shrine. People believed caves were where the gods were born. Work may have begun around 100 CE and took more than one hundred years. Workers hauled 764,000 cubic meters of rock by hand, without wheelbarrows or animals.

4The Pyramid of the Moon is the second-largest structure in Teotihuacán. It sits at the top of the Avenue of the Dead, before a plaza that could hold thousands. Fat Hill stands behind it, and streams from the hill supplied precious water. The Great Goddess, linked to rain, once stood in front and is now in a Mexico City museum. Bound, sacrificed people were buried inside with jade and obsidian. Archaeologists also found sacrificed pumas, rattlesnakes, a wolf, eagles, a falcon, and an owl. People believed these sacrifices would make the gods bring rain. Begun around 1 CE, it was rebuilt perhaps eight times and later stood about 46 meters high.

5The people who built Teotihuacán are a mystery. We call them Teotihuacanos, but we do not know their language, rulers, or city name. They used glyphs, a picture writing that no one can read yet. Settlers arrived around 400 BCE, and the community stayed small for hundreds of years. The Basin of Mexico had good soil and water for corn, beans, squash, and other crops. Traders used a mountain break to reach the Maya in the south and cultures on the Gulf Coast. They exchanged cotton and shells for obsidian from Teotihuacán. Popocatépetl erupted in the first century CE, and Xitle around 320 CE, bringing new people and skills.

6By 200 CE, a massive building program began for the huge population. More than two thousand apartment compounds were built along the city grid. They housed farmers, merchants, laborers, and immigrants, not only the wealthy. One grand door opened onto courtyards, shared kitchens, a temple, and fifty or more rooms. Sixty to one hundred people lived in each compound, with water from a reservoir and drains. Ordinary people lived very well in these spacious stone homes with bright murals. Potters from southern Mexico, Gulf Coast textile workers, and Maya families had their own neighborhoods. Neighborhoods competed to make the finest goods and offer the rarest imports.

7Immigrants brought feathers, shells, incense, and cacao beans used to make chocolate. By 500 CE the city covered 20 square kilometers, and trade reached Honduras and Guatemala. No military buildings were found, so archaeologists first wondered if the city was peaceful. Murals show plentiful warriors with deadly weapons, large shields, and the dress of deadly animals. Common men and wealthy men both fought in large numbers. After more than 650 years, drought, hunger, and overuse of wood may have cut the population. In 550 CE temples and palaces were burned, perhaps by invaders or by rebels inside. Teotihuacán lived on as a small town, but its great pyramids lay in ruin for over a thousand years.

8In 1905 archaeologists began a major restoration and cleared dirt and weeds from the Pyramid of the Sun. Other big structures were slowly restored through the 1900s. Millions of visitors come each year and climb 248 steep steps up the Pyramid of the Sun. Each step is about 30 centimeters high. For safety, tourists stop at the Moon pyramid’s first platform, which still gives a grand view. Only 5 percent of the city has been excavated, and archaeologists are still at work. In 1987 Teotihuacán became a UNESCO World Heritage Site, and it is the most studied site in Mesoamerica. In 2004 a developer built on old city ground, hid artifacts, and faced years of legal battles.

9In 2013 archaeologists sent a small robot with a camera into a tunnel under the Temple of Quetzalcoatl. Photos showed a chamber filled with hundreds of spheres that glowed gold. A mineral powder covered the spheres, and the walls and ceiling glowed too. Under torchlight long ago, the whole chamber would have looked brilliant. Masks covered with crystals and jade were there as well. Archaeologists still do not know the purpose of this golden chamber. In other tunnels they have found obsidian knives and carvings of coiled jaguars. Perhaps one day they will learn who lived and ruled in ancient Teotihuacán.

53. The Amazing Undersea Food Web

1The ocean is a huge buffet for sea life and for many land animals, including humans. You may think of great white sharks eating their prey. You may think of blue whales gulping tons of krill. You may think of giant seaweed eaten like an underwater salad bar. More than 90 percent of all sea creatures are eaten by other sea creatures. Almost every ocean organism gets eaten at some point. Almost every sea creature eats other sea creatures. The blue whale is the largest animal on Earth.

2These tiny phytoplankton are called diatoms. They are almost always eaten, but they do not eat anything themselves. They are the smallest ocean species, yet they sit at the center of the food web. Being food for others is a powerful job. Phytoplankton are algae, almost like a very tiny plant, but they have no roots. They float with the currents near the sunny surface of every ocean on Earth. They make food by photosynthesis, using chlorophyll to turn seawater nutrients into food. Chlorophyll makes them green, but that color shows only when billions of them gather.

3A bloom of phytoplankton and zooplankton can look like light streaks from space. Thousands of phytoplankton can live in one cup of seawater. Their photosynthesis supplies well over half of the oxygen in the air. Scientists see large patches of them from space when pollution makes them struggle or die. These patches also show the health of the ocean. Tuna eat mackerel, which eat herring, which eat zooplankton. Zooplankton are tiny animals such as krill and the young of crabs, jellyfish, shrimp, and fish. One kilogram of tuna needs about 10 kilograms of mackerel, then 100 of herring, 1,000 of zooplankton, and 10,000 of phytoplankton.

4A cross section shows the ocean zones from the surface to the deep trenches. Otters feed and live in the sunlit zone. The euphotic zone is the top level and reaches about 200 meters down. Strong sunlight makes this zone good for phytoplankton and ocean plants. Most animal species in the ocean can be found here. Red and green algae cover much of the shallow ocean floor. Giant kelp grows in thick forests and shelters many sea animals. Sea urchins live at the kelp base, and otters eat them and wrap kelp around their bodies while they sleep.

5Almost all seafood people eat comes from the euphotic zone. Clams, mussels, and oysters live on shallow ocean floors. Lobsters and crabs scuttle in coral reefs and seaweed beds. Most fish, including salmon, tuna, mackerel, cod, and swordfish, hunt in the sunlight. Most squid and octopuses that people eat come from this zone too. Coral reefs hold most ocean species and form from coral polyp skeletons in clear tropical water. Sea fans, brain coral, many fish, eels, sharks, crabs, and lobsters make reefs busy and colorful. Anemones sting zooplankton, jellyfish feed turtles, and reefs shelter young open-ocean animals.

6People love snorkeling and diving in the euphotic zone, especially at coral reefs. They take thousands of tons of fish for food and for tropical aquarium pets. This activity and pollution threaten these waters, and boats, anchors, and divers can break coral. Pollution, boat engines, and divers' feet can kill coral polyps. When corals die, the rest of the reef life suffers. Overfishing can drop whole fish populations dangerously low. A tide pool is a small pool left when the tide goes out. You may see mussels, sea stars, urchins, clams, barnacles, snails, an octopus, or a hermit crab in a shell.

7Below the sunlit zone, the water gets darker, colder, and heavier. This twilight zone is also called the disphotic zone. It begins near 200 meters and goes to about 1,000 meters. The pressure would crush a person, but the life here has adapted. Light is too weak for plants, yet octopuses, squid, hatchet fish, and viperfish live here. Whales visit, and many dark fish wait with huge mouths and curved teeth. They do not hunt, but hold their mouths open in the dark. The angler fish is small but scary, and the giant squid of this zone remains a mystery.

8Octopuses live in both the euphotic and disphotic zones. Many animals here eat what scientists call marine snow. Marine snow is a name for dead phytoplankton and zooplankton. When we die, our bodies drift down through the ocean layers like snow. So phytoplankton are eaten even where we do not live. Tiny bioluminescent animals glow in the pitch-black depths.

9The midnight zone is totally dark and holds 90 percent of the ocean's water. It holds almost none of the ocean's life at first glance. Some life is there if you look closely. This aphotic zone runs from 1,000 meters to 5,000 meters and below. The pressure can crush most submarines, and the temperature is near freezing. Lantern fish and others glow by bioluminescence, the same process used by fireflies. These lights help animals see, communicate, find mates or food, and look beautiful. The dark abyssal zone covers deep plains, mountains, valleys, and canyons. Trenches at colliding crust faults can reach 10,000 meters, and black smokers there spew hot minerals for crabs and tube worms.

10Animals in this zone use the warmth and the nutrients. Special bacteria use those nutrients the way phytoplankton use sunlight. Tube worms, shrimp, and giant clams feed on the bacteria around the chimneys. These chimneys were discovered only recently. Only a few submersibles are strong enough for the pressure at the bottom of a trench. Such a submarine can withstand that enormous pressure. Phytoplankton live only at the top of the sea. Reef beauty, the abyssal mystery, and tasty fish and chips all depend on them.

11The euphotic zone is the sunny top zone and holds almost all ocean life. The disphotic zone is the dim middle zone, with no plants and few animals. The aphotic zone gets no sun, and the abyssal zone is its deep ocean floor. Trenches are long ditches in the seafloor, and faults are cracks in Earth's crust where movement occurs. Phytoplankton are single-celled algae, and zooplankton are microscopic animals in the water. Photosynthesis lets plant cells use sunlight to turn water and carbon dioxide into food. Chlorophyll is the green substance that takes in light, and bioluminescence is light made inside a living thing. Anemones look like flowers, snorkeling uses a short breathing tube, and submersibles are small deep vessels.

54. The Balloon Brothers

1A map shows early balloon flights near Paris, France. It also shows England, Germany, Italy, Spain, the Atlantic Ocean, and the Mediterranean Sea. Marked places include Versailles, Saint Denis, Belleville, Gonesse, and the Tuileries Garden. The map dates are August 27, September 19, November 21, and December 1, 1783. On the afternoon of September 19, 1783, a crowd of 130,000 came to the Palace of Versailles, just outside Paris. The brothers Joseph-Michel and Jacques-Étienne Montgolfier built the balloon, and King Louis XVI and Queen Marie Antoinette watched. The silk-and-paper balloon had gold designs on a blue background. The dangers of flight were unknown, so a sheep, a rooster, and a duck rode in the wicker basket.

2These animals were part of a science test. The sheep was close to a person, the rooster could not fly high, and the duck could fly high. Hot, smoky air from a fire in a pit filled the balloon. Soon after 1:00 pm, it rose about 460 meters, or 1,500 feet. Eight minutes later, it landed 3.2 kilometers, or 2 miles, away, and the animals were safe. The age of flight had begun, and the brothers deserved much of the credit. Joseph and Étienne were two of sixteen children of Pierre and Anne Montgolfier, in southern France. Pierre made paper for King Louis XVI, and in the 1770s the brothers ran the business and studied buoyancy, first explained by Archimedes.

3Joseph thought a light gas in a light cover might lift a craft. He tried hydrogen, then called inflammable air, inside small paper balls. In 1766, English chemist Henry Cavendish named the gas, and in 1783 French chemist Antoine Lavoisier called it hydrogen. The balls rose only a few meters, because tiny hydrogen atoms leaked through the paper. Elements are made of atoms, and one oxygen atom with two hydrogen atoms can make water. One day, Joseph saw shirts puff up over a smoky fire. In early 1783, damp straw and chopped wool made the most smoke and a terrible smell. The brothers named the lift Montgolfier gas, but heated air expands, grows less dense, and makes a balloon rise.

4The brothers still believed in Montgolfier gas and kept making foul smoke. Their test balloons worked, so they saw no reason to doubt the idea. They tried silk, paper, and linen for the envelope, and they made the balloons bigger. On June 4, 1783, a crowd in Annonay, France, watched an empty balloon of cloth lined with paper. It rose about 2,000 meters, or 6,560 feet, flew ten minutes, and landed about 2 kilometers, or 1.2 miles, away. These flights started a balloon craze in France. The name balloon came later from the French word ballon, a large ball used in games. The French Academy of Sciences waited to approve the brothers, while rival Jacques Charles planned a hydrogen balloon, though safer helium was not found until the 1880s.

5In July 1783, Charles began work with the academy’s approval and money from the public. Engineers Nicolas-Louis and Anne-Jean Robert helped him build a small silk balloon coated with rubber to hold hydrogen. King Louis invited the Montgolfiers to Paris, gave them government money, and wanted a grand balloon. The two teams raced to carry people, but the contest stayed fairly friendly. They followed the scientific method by asking, researching, testing, and sharing results. On August 27, 1783, a crowd gathered where the Eiffel Tower now stands. Benjamin Franklin, the U.S. ambassador to France, was among the watchers. The balloon was about 4 meters, or 13 feet, wide, with red and white stripes turned yellow by rubber.

6Late that day, the empty balloon flew northeast for less than an hour and landed in Gonesse, about 15 kilometers, or 9 miles, away. Scared villagers struck the strange craft with pitchforks. After the animals landed safely, the king wanted prisoners to fly, but Jean-Francois Pilatre de Rozier and the Marquis d'Arlandes volunteered. On November 21, their balloon rose from the edge of Paris, standing 23 meters, or 75 feet, high. It had gold designs, the king's two L's, lilies, sun disks, zodiac signs, and eagles. After 25 minutes, it landed about 8 kilometers, or 5 miles, away between two windmills, and the men were heroes. Franklin reportedly answered, "What good is a newborn baby?" On December 1, Charles and Nicolas-Louis Robert flew a hydrogen balloon from the Tuileries Garden for over two hours, about 36 kilometers away. The academy later honored the Montgolfier brothers. The king honored the Montgolfier brothers too.

7The love of ballooning soon spread across Europe. In June 1785, de Rozier and his copilot, Pierre Romain, were killed while trying to cross the English Channel. Even so, people kept making new balloon designs after the craze of the late 1700s. In the 1800s, lighter-than-air craft were given rudders and motorized propellers. By the 1930s, huge airships carried passengers back and forth across the Atlantic Ocean. The golden age ended in 1937, when the German airship Hindenburg caught fire and crashed while landing in New Jersey. Today, airships mostly show ads over stadiums, gas balloons gather weather data, and hot-air balloons are mostly for fun. Many festivals fill the sky with hundreds of colorful hot-air balloons.

55. The Banker Who Sent the Children to Safety

1The year on the map of Europe is 1939. This book begins with a girl who leaves home. It tells how a British banker came to Prague. It tells about Hitler and his plans. It tells how the banker asked the world for help. It tells about the trains he arranged. It tells how a secret was found. It ends with his own words. Word meanings come last. German soldiers rode military motorcycles into Prague in 1939. Prague was the capital of Czechoslovakia. Czech people stood stunned. They watched in silence. The German army's invasion is a day Vera Gissing still remembers. The day was March 15, 1939. Vera Gissing was Jewish. She was still young. She woke to the sounds of tanks. She woke to German soldiers marching through the streets. Soldiers even took over rooms in her family's home. They ordered the family to speak only German. Her father refused. A soldier spat in her father's face while she watched. It was bad. It was only the beginning.

2The Eberstark girls left the Prague train station that day. They never saw their parents again. Vera Gissing's story was like theirs. The day her parents sent her off is still in her memory. They hoped the trip would save her life. The day came shortly before her eleventh birthday. Dozens of other children went with her. Her best clothes were what she wore. A numbered tag hung around her neck. They stood at Prague's main train station. Engine steam rose and wrapped the families. Parents hugged their children. They kissed them. They whispered words of love. They whispered words of hope. In 2002 Gissing spoke about that morning. It was sixty-three years earlier. She said she would never forget the anguished look on her parents' faces. A 1938 picture shows a German Jewish girl holding a small bag. She clutches a doll too. She has just arrived in England. The children got on a train next. The train was bound for England. As it pulled away, Gissing tried to keep her eyes on her parents' faces. She did not know then that she would never see them again. She did not know that her parents would soon be sent away to die. The same fate waited for most other parents there. She also knew nothing of a stranger from Great Britain. He opened his heart to save her. Nearly fifty years passed with his actions kept secret.

3In 1938 Nicholas Winton was a banker in London. He was twenty-nine years old. He had big plans for his Christmas holiday. He was going to ski in the Alps. His good friend Martin Blake was going with him. The pair never reached the Alps. Blake's request to switch the trip to Prague came just before Winton left England. Organizations in Prague were where Blake was working. Those groups were giving food to thousands of Jewish families. They were giving other kinds of help too. The families had fled their homes. Germany had taken over a part of northern Czechoslovakia. That part was known as the Sudetenland. Christian organizations worked beside Jewish organizations. Youth Aliyah was one of the Jewish groups. The Children's Aid Society was another. The Kindertransport was another. They all worked to provide aid. Winton later explained why he went. He and others had talked a great deal about events in Europe. They had talked about it almost daily. The last thing he thought was that he was going there to work. This work was personal for him. He was of German-Jewish descent. His parents had decided to move to England. They had converted to Christianity. Without those choices, he and his family could have been in a desperate situation. The takeover of the Sudetenland was part of a secret plan. The plan belonged to Germany's leader, Adolf Hitler. Hitler had once been a soldier in the army. Germany's loss in World War I left him angry. That loss came in 1918. Part of the blame, in his view, sat on the Jewish people. He also blamed Black people. He blamed disabled people. He blamed LGBTQ+ people. He blamed Roma people. Jewish people were an inferior race in his belief. He believed they needed to die so they would be removed from society. Hitler took part in forming the Nazi Party once the war ended. That group wanted to restore Germany's power in the world. Hitler was an emotional speaker. He could excite a crowd. He became a popular leader. In time he and the Nazis grew so powerful that he was named Germany's leader. Control of the government passed into his hands. Then he started to build up the military. He also made a secret plan. His plan was to regain land taken from Germany after its loss in World War I. A picture shows him in Austria in 1938. He shouts. He gestures with great energy as he speaks to a crowd.

4Some people in the Sudetenland welcomed the German troops. They greeted them with a salute when the troops entered. Fear drove others out of the area. Hitler put his plan into motion in 1936. He took control of Austria. Leaders in other European countries objected. No one moved to stop him. After taking Austria, he wanted the Sudetenland. That area lies along the border of Germany and Czechoslovakia. Many German-speaking people lived there. To get it, he met with leaders in 1938. The leaders were from France. They were from Great Britain. They were from Italy. All three countries were friends of the Czechs. They were allies. They had promised to protect the country. They did not like Hitler's actions. They also feared another war. His demands were met. By 1940 his forces had moved into land once given to France. Leaders objected again. No one moved to stop him. Many people distrusted Hitler. A wider takeover of Europe was what they thought he planned. Jewish people were frightened. Germany, under Hitler, wrote many laws that targeted them. Lawyer was no longer a job Jewish people could hold. They could no longer work as doctors. They could no longer work as journalists. Those jobs are examples. Public hospitals were closed to them. They could not go to public schools after age fourteen. Marriage to a person who was not Jewish was blocked by other laws. Many photos from World War II show Jewish people wearing six-pointed stars on their clothing. Men wore them. Women wore them. Children wore them. The star is often made from two interlocking triangles. Another name for it is the Star of David. Judaism has used it as a symbol for thousands of years. During World War II the Nazis made a rule. The stars were required for every Jewish person. Then non-Jewish people could identify them easily. Shame was the purpose of those stars. They were meant to encourage discrimination against Jewish people. The rule applied to every Jewish person over the age of six. It applied in any country Germany controlled.

5Next, Hitler ordered the army to gather Jewish people who had been born in Poland. He ordered the same for Jewish people born in Russia. They were to be removed from Germany. Homes were taken from Jewish people by force. They could take only the belongings they could carry. They were loaded onto trucks. They were loaded onto wagons. They were driven to the border. They were left there. Soon after, the army kidnapped 30,000 German Jewish people. They removed them by force. They placed them in Nazi concentration camps. Prison is the closest word for what the camps were. Jewish people were sent there to live. They were sent there to work in torturous conditions. Then Hitler ordered their execution. A picture shows a family sitting in a wagon. They wear armbands that mark them as Jewish people. The removal shown here is from Krakow, Poland. These events frightened Jewish people across Europe. Leaving home was the choice many people made. They were trying to escape the danger. City camps in Prague held Jewish citizens of Czechoslovakia. The camps were short-term shelters. Rounding them up was easy for the Nazis because of that. The Nazis could take them to concentration camps. A school in Czechoslovakia was used as housing. Refugee families from the Sudetenland stayed there. Winton saw that conditions for Jewish people were terrible. New homes for Jewish people were the aim of some aid groups. He noticed where their focus sat. It sat on elderly people. It sat on sick people. The Jewish children of Czechoslovakia had no one working for them. Winton decided he would try to save them. In a 2002 film about his life, he described the situation. He said it was bad. The refugees felt the days were numbered. The helpers felt that too. They expected the Germans to arrive in the rest of Czechoslovakia. The people asked how they could save themselves. They asked what they could do. They asked where they should go. They were stuck.

6He began by basing himself in a hotel. The hotel was on Prague's Wenceslas Square. A dining-room table was his seat every day. Parents seeking safety for their children met with him. Safe homes for the children were the heart of his plan. The homes would be with families outside Czechoslovakia. A program in Germany was already using trains. The same program ran in Austria. It was called the Kindertransport. It took thousands of Jewish children to safety. Winton thought he could copy that program. Then he could save thousands of Czech children too. Word spread quickly about the Englishman of Wenceslas Square. Hundreds of Czech families arrived at the hotel. They sought his help. He hired two helpers to work with the families. Then he returned to England. Living places for the children still had to be found. He needed to raise money for their travel. In London he began writing letters. Governments in many countries received the letters. He asked them to take the children. Many countries refused. Children without parents were barred by those laws. In the end, only two countries agreed to help. One was Sweden. The other was Great Britain. England still had strict rules about bringing the children in. Winton had to find a family to take each child. The British government also said he must pay a fee. The fee would cover a later trip home for the children. That would be when they could return to Czechoslovakia. The fee was fifty pounds per child. Back then, such a fee was a small fortune. To find families, he placed ads in newspapers across Great Britain. He talked with churches. He printed pictures of the children. He sent the pictures all over the country. He hoped families would want to help once they saw the children's faces. At the same time he worked on permission. He needed the German government to let the children enter England. He needed the British government to allow it too. Sometimes the governments moved too slowly. Then Winton and a small team of helpers made fake permits. He said they only sped the process up a little. The pound is Great Britain's form of money. It is the currency. Fifty pounds counted as a small fortune in 1939. Fifty pounds then had a value above $3,400.

7In 1939, seven trainloads of children left Prague. They traveled to the coast of Holland. There they boarded a ferry. The ferry crossed the English Channel. Then they boarded a second train. That train took them to London. Winton's hard work paid off in those train journeys. Seven trains packed with children departed across the next six months. They left from Prague's Wilson Railway Station. Holland was where those trains carried the children. They reached the coast. There the children boarded a boat. The boat crossed the English Channel. The journey ended in the arms of new families. That meeting was at a London train station. A smiling Winton looked on. A 1939 photograph shows him holding one of the young children. He had rescued that child from Czechoslovakia. Safety was reached by 669 children in all. Some carried keepsakes from home. Some carried letters of thanks. Parents wrote those letters for the new families in Britain. Families took in most of the children. A Czech boarding school became home for many of the others. The school was in Wales. An eighth train was in Winton's plans. September 3, 1939, was the planned departure from Prague. It would carry 250 more children. On that day Hitler's army invaded Poland. All borders under German control were closed. The train disappeared. No one saw those children after that. A horrible military struggle came next. It lasted nearly six years. Nations around the world were pulled in. It became known as World War II.

8A barbed-wire fence splits male prisoners from female prisoners. The place is a German concentration camp. A guard is posted there to watch. In 1941 Hitler decided that all Jewish people must be killed. That decision was part of his war effort. Concentration camps were where millions were forced to go. Work continued until weakness killed them. Later the Nazis judged death in the camps too slow. They began murdering them instead. People call Hitler's effort to wipe out all Jewish people the Holocaust. Shoah is another name some Jewish people use. Shoah is a Hebrew word. It means a whirlwind of destruction. The camps were the place where six million Jewish people were murdered. More than a million of them were children. Non-Jewish people were murdered there too, in the millions. Among human crimes, the Holocaust stands as one of the most atrocious. The war brought a sudden end to Winton's rescue mission. Other ways to help were what he looked for. His first role was with the Red Cross. That group is a relief organization. The Royal Air Force was his next service. He became a pilot. After the war he went back to banking. He got married. He had a family. The children he had struggled to save never came up in his talk. Then, in 1988, his wife found his secret by accident. Her name was Grete. In the attic one day she came across a dusty leather briefcase. She opened it. Inside was a worn old scrapbook. It was filled with pictures of the children. Beside each photo was the child's name. There was information about the child's family in Czechoslovakia. There was a note of the British family who had taken the child in. The scrapbook also held letters. It held other papers. The papers described the work Winton had done. His story came out because Grete pressed him to speak. Soon a newspaper ran a story about him. That same year a British television show did a program about him. The show was called That's Life. As a surprise, rescued children were in the audience. More than two dozen of them had come to thank him.

9That emotional reunion included Vera Gissing. She is an author. A biography of Winton is one book she later wrote. She has written a book about her own experience. She was a child who lived through the war. She has said most Jewish children of her generation in Czechoslovakia owed their rescue to him. Very few of them met their parents again. The parents perished in concentration camps. If the children had not been spirited away, they would have been murdered alongside their parents. Descendants of those 669 children now number as many as 5,000. Those children rode his trains to safety in 1939. The children are now old. Many still call themselves Winton's Children. A picture shows some of them at a London train station. They have come to meet their rescuer. The day marks the 70th anniversary of their evacuation. In 2002 Winton got down on his knees. He was receiving one of his country's highest honors. The honor was knighthood. Once, knighthood was an honor and a title reserved for soldiers. In modern times it recognizes achievements of many kinds. Artists can receive it. Athletes can receive it. Politicians can receive it. Humanitarians can receive it. Inventors can receive it. Scientists can receive it. Other people can receive it too. The monarch performs the ceremony. Another member of Great Britain's Royal Family may perform it instead. Recipients kneel in front of the monarch at the ceremony. A sword taps each shoulder. They are also given a medal. They are given a title. Citizens of Great Britain receive a special title. The title sir is given to men. Women are given the title of dame. Winton's work has earned him many honors. The honors come from the government of Great Britain. They also come from the government of the Czech Republic. In 2002 Queen Elizabeth II made him a knight. That award is given for acts of bravery. It is given for service. It is given for success. A picture shows him with the medal from his knighting ceremony.

10Winton stands beside a train in one picture. The train repeated the last leg of the historic trip from Prague to London. In 2009 people marked seventy years since his last train. A trip repeated the journey his children had made. The path ran between Prague and London. The same path was the one that train used. Many of the people he had saved were on board. He greeted the group himself. The place was London's Liverpool Street station. He greeted them with open arms. The trip came a few months after his 100th birthday. From childhood, Vera Gissing thought of him as her savior. She just did not know who he was. For many years he felt he had not done anything that special. He felt it was not important. Only after he met Gissing did that view start to change. He met some of the others he had saved. Only then did the size of what he had made possible become clear to him. In 2002 she co-authored a book. The title is Nicholas Winton and the Rescued Generation. In it she writes about a chain of ifs. War clouds were gathering. Her parents needed courage to send their only children away. They needed strength. The children would go to unknown people in a foreign country. British families had to be found to take them in. Without those pieces, hopes and efforts would have been fruitless. Willingness to help would have been fruitless too. Nicholas Winton was the piece that made the rest matter. Because of him, she can sit in her garden. She watches her grandchildren playing. She listens to their laughter. She hears her daughter's voice calling them in for tea. It is an everyday family scene. It is a scene she can never take for granted. A candid photo shows Gissing and Winton together.

11Winton laughs with a boy in one picture. The boy is the grandson of a girl he saved. That rescue was 70 years earlier. After his secret was revealed, he spoke often. Saving the Czech children was a choice he talked about often. Nothing special was what he said he had done. He claimed that he did nothing heroic. He said that is why he never talked about it. He once said he only saw what was happening. Then he did what he could to help. Some of the people he saved wanted to thank him. They gave him a ring. A line is inscribed on the ring. The line comes from a book of Jewish laws. That book is known as the Talmud. The line says that to save one life is to save the world. July 1, 2015, was the day Sir Nicholas Winton died. That day was the 76th anniversary of a train journey. More children left Prague on that journey than on any other. The number was 241. These words have special meanings in this book. Inferior means lower in quality or rank. Anguished means filled with grief or pain. Jewish describes the people, the culture, or the religion. Atrocious means extremely bad, evil, or cruel. A concentration camp holds people who cannot choose to leave. The conditions are usually harsh. People are held because they belong to a specific ethnic group. They may be held as a minority. They may be held for political reasons. A keepsake is saved so an event, a person, or a place will be remembered. Perished means died. The word is used especially for a sudden, violent, or unexpected death. A generation is all the people born and living at about the same time. The word can also apply to other animals. A refugee has fled war, famine, persecution, or a natural disaster. They often have no definite place to go. Hebrew relates to the earlier people of today's Jews. Those ancestors lived in the area around Jerusalem. Spirited means smuggled or carried off secretly. During World War II the Nazis carried out the Holocaust, a planned killing aimed especially at Jewish people.

56. The Biggest Fish in the Sea

1Inside: a huge fish, a spotted pattern, life at sea, tiny meals, giant babies, staying alive, one more meeting, and words to know. A fish bigger than you expect. Picture yourself in the water off western Australia, with a snorkel on. A dark mass rises out of the deep. Closer now, the shape is clear. The animal is a shark. A school bus would match it for size. Your heart hammers. Fear is the wrong response. The shark in front of you is a whale shark. No fish on Earth grows larger. Size, for this animal, is not the same as danger. People swim next to whale sharks and come to no harm. The sharks are that calm. Swimmers speak of them as elegant. Majestic is another word swimmers use. One fact still stands. Among animals, whale sharks count as giants. How large is one? Length can reach 40 feet, which is 12.2 meters. Weight can reach 40,000 pounds, which is 18,144 kilograms. Set a person beside that shark for scale. The person in the chart stands 6 feet, or 1.8 meters. The same person weighs 175 pounds, or 79.4 kilograms.

2A giant checkerboard. See a whale shark once, in the sea or in a photo, and you will know it again. Other fish do not share this look. The head is wide. The head is flat. The snout is blunt. The mouth is huge. Swimming often happens with that mouth held open. Spots and stripes on one shark never match another shark exactly. Scientists tell individuals apart by that pattern. Rough skin is a shark trait, and whale sharks have it. Sharp scales cover them. The scales are dermal denticles. Those words mean skin teeth. Dark color covers the back. Blue can be the dark shade. Gray can be the dark shade. Brown can be the dark shade. Pale spots cross that dark back. Pale stripes cross it too. Some people compare the pattern to a checkerboard. Others compare it to a domino. Why the pattern exists is still unknown. Sunlight at the surface may be too strong, and the pattern may act as a shield. That is one idea. Camouflage is another idea. Adults grow so large that hunters seldom threaten them. Young whale sharks may still need the pattern. Blending into the water around them could keep the young ones safer. What puts a whale shark in the fish group? The whole body pushes this shark forward. Other sharks rely more on the tail and the rear of the body. A tall fin rises from the back. The tail fin is large as well. Each side of the body carries five wide gill slits. The mouth opens at the front of the head. On other sharks the mouth sits under the snout. Cartilage, not bone, forms the skeleton. Every shark is built that way. Most fish share a backbone. Most fish take oxygen through gills. Most fish spend their lives in water. Most fish have a tail. Most fish have fins in pairs. Most fish are cold-blooded. Most fish wear scales. Most fish lay eggs.

3Away from shore, and near it. Warm seas are home to whale sharks. Tropical water suits them. Warm temperate water suits them too. The Atlantic holds them. The Pacific holds them. The Indian Ocean holds them. Open water is one place they cruise. Water close to land is another. Watchers often find them at the surface. They also drop as far down as 6,000 feet. That depth is 1,829 meters. Strength is not in doubt. Speed is another matter. The shortfin mako is the fastest shark. A mako can pass 30 miles in an hour. That pace is 48 kilometers an hour. A whale shark is slow beside a mako. Long trips are still possible. The cruising pace is 2.4 miles an hour, or 3.9 kilometers an hour. A whale shark usually travels alone. Remoras often ride along anyway. A remora is a fish with a pad like a sucker. The pad grips the shark's body. Other big fish sometimes share the route. Those companions eat in a similar way. Parasites are fewer when remoras stay attached. The remoras gain a meal for their work. They also gain safety. Where these sharks live. A map marks Europe, Asia, and North America. It marks the Pacific and the Atlantic. It marks Africa, the equator, and the Indian Ocean. It marks South America and Australia. Shading shows where whale sharks live. Shading also shows how far they migrate. The journeys cover great distances. Nobody is sure why they go. Breeding may be the reason. Food may be the reason. Other needs may send them off as well. A common trip runs past 1,240 miles. That is 2,000 kilometers. One tracked shark covered 8,077 miles. That is 13,000 kilometers. More than three years passed during that trip. Gathering places are scattered worldwide. Scientists call them aggregation sites. The biggest site is the Afuera. Spanish speakers use that word for out, or for outside. Mexico's Yucatan Peninsula has this site off its coast. Counters once found over 400 of these sharks in that one place at a single time. Fish eggs, in huge amounts, were their food that day. Another big gathering showed up beside reefs. That water is called the Red Sea. These meetings are a classroom for scientists. Behavior is what they hope to read. Feeding grounds are one question. Breeding grounds are another.

4A huge body, a tiny meal. Most sharks chase prey. A whale shark does not. Predator is the wrong label for it. Plankton is the meal. Those are ocean plants and animals too small to see. They drift rather than swim hard. Micronekton is a second menu. Those animals are still tiny, but they can swim. Krill ranks as a favorite. A krill looks a bit like a shrimp. Small fish can enter the diet. Squid can enter it. Plants can enter it. Fish eggs can enter it. Now and then a bigger fish, such as a tuna, is eaten too. The feeding method has a name: filter feeding. The mouth opens. Seawater streams in and brings food with it. Flow continues toward the gills. Five pairs of pads, soft like sponges, catch the tiny plants and animals. Water then leaves by the gill slits on the outside of the body. A bit of food only 0.08 inches across can still be caught. That width is 2 millimeters. The pads do the catching. Gill slits let the water back out. Two main styles of filter feeding are known. One style is passive feeding. Ram feeding is its other name. The shark stays calm. The mouth stays open while it swims. The head swings from one side to the other. Whatever drifts in gets swallowed. Trash in the ocean gets swallowed too. The stomach can be turned inside out. It comes out through the mouth. That is how the trash leaves. Suction feeding is the second style. During suction feeding the gills pump. During passive feeding they do not. Think of a vacuum cleaner. Great gulps of water are pulled in. A thick cloud of food makes this style useful. Too much food can arrive at once. A cough may follow. Extra bits piled on the pads get cleared by that cough.

5Hard to believe. An open mouth on this shark can span 5 feet. That span is 1.5 meters. A person could fit inside that opening. The longest whale shark on record measured 66 feet. That is 20 meters. Line up a school bus and add half of another, and you match that length. Filtering power is just as startling. One Olympic pool holds 650,000 gallons of water. The same pool holds 2,460,517 liters. This shark can strain that much water in four hours. Feeding groups show up at the surface when the sun is setting. They also show up after dark. Plankton is abundant then. Smell is the likely guide to food. Scientists reached that view by studying where the nostrils sit. They also studied where the eyes sit. The nostrils sit high, close to where the mouth begins. Food passes close to that spot. The eyes are small. They sit farther back on the head. A placement like that hints at a smaller job for sight during feeding. Three hundred rows of tiny teeth line the mouth. Eating does not use those teeth. Young and grown. A newborn's length, multiplied by twenty-five, can equal an adult. Pups of giant size. Egg-laying was the old guess. The year 1995 changed the story. Live birth is what whale sharks do. A female died while she was pregnant. Inside her were more than three hundred young. People call the young pups. Each growing shark measured somewhere from 16 inches to 24 inches. That range is 42 to 63 centimeters. Some pups were further along than others. The further-along pups would have been born first. Human birth and whale shark birth differ in a key way. No shared blood supply links a pup to its mother. Nutrients do not pass straight from her body into the pup. An egg case wraps each baby. Yolk inside that case is the food supply before birth. The pup grows until it is ready. It breaks the case. Birth follows that break. A newborn can live without help. It is ready for the sea at once.

6A pup is a small copy of the adult. The pattern and the shape already match. Only size sets them apart. No scientist has watched a birth. Guesswork is all anyone has. Open ocean is the suspected birthplace. Shore water is not the leading guess. A newborn adds length faster than an older juvenile does. A female adds length faster than a male. Full-grown females are the larger sex. Maturity arrives around age thirty. Lifespan is a blank. Sharks in general last a long time. This species is enormous. A century of life is what some scientists expect from that size. Mexico's Sea of Cortez holds a scene of two snorkelers. They glide beside a young whale shark. West Papua, in Indonesia, holds another scene. A whale shark skims the surface and takes in plankton. The fight to survive. Hunters want the meat. They want the skin. They want the fins. They want oil from the liver. Surface swimming makes the hunt easy. Ships hit these sharks for the same reason. Nets set for other fish catch whale sharks by mistake. Dirty water is a further danger. Lost habitat is a further danger. Climate change adds more danger. Endangered is not the label yet. Vulnerable is the label. That word means the species could become endangered. A few countries cap the number that hunters may take. The cap is meant to slow the drop in numbers. Ecotourism adds another kind of help. It shows people why these animals matter. They are treasures of the planet.

7Small baitfish pack into a tight ball around a whale shark. Predators are the threat they are trying to dodge. A fish this large. The snorkel goes on again. This time the water is the Gulf of Mexico. A whale shark of great size comes near. Fear stays away. Wonder takes its place. The mouth opens. A bicycle, set on its length, would match that opening. Seawater rushes in. Huge gill slits pump as you watch. Tiny food is what those slits are gathering. Elegance shows even in a body this big. A close look at such a gentle giant feels like a privilege. Luck and local names. Japan treats the whale shark as a sign of good luck. Vietnam's name for it is Sir Fish. People there pray to the shark and ask for protection. Mexico's name is domino. Spots on the body are the reason for that name. Madagascar's name means many stars. Words to know. An aggregation is a crowd of many things, or of many animals, gathered in one place. Distinctive describes a trait that stands out and is easy to notice. Endangered describes a living thing that might vanish completely. To migrate is to leave one home region for another. The move comes at the same season each year. Passive describes something that is not taking action. Plankton are very small plants and animals. They live at the ocean surface or just under it. A predator hunts other animals and eats them in order to live. Reproduction is how living things make young. A snout is the nose and mouth together on an animal's face. Solitary describes one animal on its own. Temperate describes a place with mild weather. Vulnerable describes something that can be harmed with ease.

57. The Corps of Discovery Heads West

1Canada, the United States, and Mexico all appear on a map of this journey. So do the Atlantic Ocean and the Gulf of Mexico. Missouri is the state where St. Louis marks the start. Fort Clatsop marks the far western camp. The way west belongs to the years 1804 and 1805. The way home in 1806 did not follow one single path. Borders of today's states can be drawn on that same map. The opening of this book is the start of the journey. Later parts take up the many nations of North America. The Louisiana Purchase comes next, and so does Jefferson's plan. Then come the Corps of Discovery, the Great Plains crossing, and the winter stop. A short close and some word meanings finish the book. The date was May 25, 1804. Travelers known as the Corps of Discovery went past the edge of the United States. Lands governed by Indigenous peoples lay on the other side. White men in the group numbered about forty. An enslaved Black man traveled with them. Later, people gave the journey the name Lewis and Clark Expedition. About two years passed before the trip was done. The miles they traveled numbered more than 8,000 (12,875 km). St. Louis, Missouri, was the starting city. The Pacific Ocean was the far point. After that, the travelers turned around and came back. More than fifty Indigenous tribes met them on the way. Notes were kept about the land. Notes were kept about plants. Notes were kept about animals. To the U.S. government, the trip was a bold look at land soon to be opened for settlement. For Indigenous peoples, what followed was nearly a century filled with invasion, with war, and with forced assimilation. Exploration made the expedition an adventure. Science made it a survey. The army made it a military mission. In later years that mission helped the United States push farther west. A new chapter of colonization began.

2Indigenous nations named for this story can be placed on a map. One name is Jiwére, also called Oto. Numakiki, also called Mandan, appears with Kaw, also called Kansa. Hiraacá, also called Hidatsa, is on the map. So is Apsáalooke, also called Crow. So is Niutachi, also called Missouri. Oceti Sakowin, also called Sioux, includes the Dakota, the Lakota, and the Nakota. Tsitsistas, also called Cheyenne, is named as well. Canada, the United States, and the Atlantic Ocean frame the map. Borders of today's states can be shown too. Lands long used by these nations often lay on top of one another. Those lands also moved as years passed. By 1803, North America's political map was not the map we know. The United States then covered only a little more ground than the first thirteen colonies. Other nations stood along its edges. British colonies called the Canadas lay straight north. Only the southeasternmost part of modern Canada was inside those colonies. Indigenous peoples still held the rest. Nations now called the Five Tribes bordered the United States to the south and the southwest. The five were the Cherokee, the Chickasaw, the Choctaw, the Muscogee, also called Creek, and the Seminole. Parts of European life had been mixed into their own ways. Those parts were European government, money, religion, writing, and slavery. Hundreds of other Indigenous nations lived to the west, and each was independent. Each one had a political system of its own, and those systems were complex. The French Empire claimed the eastern half of this huge region governed by Indigenous peoples. The claimed region carried the name Louisiana Territory. Europeans had settled only a tiny share of it. Most of that land was still home to Indigenous peoples. A second map can show the United States before the Louisiana Purchase. It can show the land added by that purchase. Borders of today's states can sit on it too.

3Control over Indigenous nations in the West was what U.S. President Thomas Jefferson wanted. He was afraid France would stand in the way. He hoped to cut some of France's power in North America. His question to France was about one city. Would France sell New Orleans, the key port, to the United States? France came back with a larger offer. The whole Louisiana Territory was for sale. The price was 15 million dollars. In today's money that price is about 310 million dollars. Enslaved Black people had just won a revolt. That revolt pushed France out of the land now called Haiti. France had almost no money left. Cash mattered more to France than land did. Indigenous peoples were living on the land that was sold. They did not accept France as the owner. France's real offer was a promise. France would not step in when the United States tried to take control of the Indigenous nations there. Jefferson did not wait to plan a look at the new territory. Money for the Lewis and Clark Expedition was approved by Congress before the purchase was even finished. The approved path ran all the way to the Pacific Ocean. That ocean sat well outside the Louisiana Territory. Taking Indigenous land in the West would be easier after this expedition. The men had orders to hunt for a water path. That path would join the Atlantic Ocean with the Pacific Ocean. Careful maps were also required. Plants they met had to be written down. Animals they met had to be written down. Congress wanted to know how the lands might earn money. Congress also wanted those lands for U.S. settlement. Jefferson had one more aim. He wanted Indigenous peoples to start coming under the control of the United States. At each new meeting, the men said the people must obey the laws of the Great Father. The Great Father was their name for the president of the United States. Gifts were handed out. Guns were then fired into the air so military strength would be plain to see. Coins called Jefferson Peace Medals went from the corps to Indigenous leaders.

4Jefferson's way of dealing with Indigenous peoples shaped these orders. He hoped talk would lead Indigenous peoples to give their lands up for U.S. settlement. He also hoped they would leave traditional cultures behind. European Christian customs were what he wanted in their place. In private he spoke of the army. If they resisted, the army would drive them off their lands or kill them. The gifts had a purpose of their own. Jefferson wanted Indigenous peoples to see good in goods made in the United States. He hoped they would then let trading posts into their communities. Those posts were a trick that pulled Indigenous peoples into debt. Officials at the posts let Indigenous customers take goods. Payment could wait, the customers were told. Later the officials said the sums owed were huge. The army then forced the debts to be cleared. The payment demanded was land, which had to be given to the United States. Jefferson picked his own secretary to lead. That man was Captain Meriwether Lewis. The group he led would be known as the Corps of Discovery. Lewis asked Captain William Clark to lead beside him. Clark was a friend, and he had once commanded Lewis. Both men had already fought Indigenous peoples. Those fights were wars to take more land after the United States broke free from Britain. The expedition was organized like an army mission. Soldiers and hired boatmen made up the members, with one exception. The exception was York, a Black man. Clark held York in slavery. Military uniforms stayed on every enlisted member for the whole trip. Lewis chose only men who were not married. He chose men whose health was good. He chose men who could stay alive in wild country. To get ready, Lewis studied medicine, astronomy, plants, and animals.

5York's parents were enslaved on a plantation in Virginia. He was born there in the late 1770s. In 1799 the legal claim to York passed to Clark. Claims to other enslaved people passed to Clark in that same year. York had to join the Corps of Discovery. His job was to serve as Clark's body servant. He was the expedition's only Black member. Hostility and aggression from the other men were regular parts of his days. Even so, the trip opened chances other enslaved people did not get. Training with firearms was one of those chances. At first meetings with Indigenous peoples, York often stood as a representative. Louisville, Kentucky, is where a statue of York stands. Camp Dubois in Illinois was the official starting camp. The date was May 14, 1804. Boats headed up the Missouri River. Eleven days later, the farthest west U.S. settlement on the river was behind them. Indigenous lands lay ahead. Bad behavior showed up in the early days. Some men slept while they were supposed to be on duty. Some men tried to run off. Order was kept with harsh physical punishments from Lewis and Clark. In the first months, the nations they met already knew white people well. Those white people were usually trappers or merchants. Many people there understood English. Little of note happened at those meetings. The lands they passed belonged to the Kaw, the Jiwére, the Niutachi, and the Dakota. Dakota lands meant the corps had entered Oceti Sakowin territory. Two-thirds of the Great Plains, on the northern side, lay inside that confederacy. Deeper in those lands, Teton Lakota leaders asked for a toll. The toll was the price of crossing their territory. Payment was refused by Lewis and Clark. The Lakota also had a suspicion. They thought the corps meant to sell firearms to Lakota enemies farther upriver. An argument followed. Weapons were drawn on both sides. Calm returned because of Lakota chief Black Buffalo. Three days passed with the corps among the Lakota. An agreement then let them continue up the river.

6Oceti Sakowin means The Seven Council Fires. The Lakota, the Dakota, and the Nakota make up this confederacy. Outsiders often use the name Sioux Nation. When Lewis and Clark traveled, this confederacy was one of the Great Plains' strongest powers. The strength was political, economic, and military. Their life followed bison herds that moved with the seasons. Across North America, few riders matched their skill or their fame. In the United States today, the Oceti Sakowin ranks among the largest Indigenous nations. Their languages are still kept. Their cultures are still kept. They still care for lands that have always been theirs. One picture shows a Lakota boy. He is protesting building work on sacred Lakota land. A month after the Lakota meetings, the corps reached two allied nations. Those nations were the Numakiki and the Hiraacá. A huge trade network had its center there. Its reach started at the East Coast and continued past the Rocky Mountains. White traders who brought enslaved Black people were already a familiar sight. The Numakiki and the Hiraacá treated the corps in a friendly way. Winter was close. The corps chose to stay until spring. They built a stronghold and gave it the name Fort Mandan. At that fort, Lewis and Clark asked what the river ahead would be like. They asked the Numakiki and the Hiraacá. They learned the shape of the upper Missouri River country. They learned that English would probably not be spoken in the lands ahead. French would probably not be spoken there either. Help with talking to later Indigenous peoples came from a hired man. His name was Toussaint Charbonneau. He was a French fur trader who lived in the Numakiki town. Later it became clear that his languages were only English, French, and a little Hiraacá. Clark's journal says Charbonneau had two Indigenous women. The journal says he had purchased them. One was a girl of 17. Her name was Sacagawea. The language she spoke was Newe. She would matter a great deal in the rest of the expedition. Before the corps left Fort Mandan, she gave birth to a son. The boy's name was Jean Baptiste Charbonneau. No one on the expedition was younger than he was.

7Fame may put Sacagawea on the same level as Lewis and Clark. Even so, little about her life is sure. Shoshone people say her birth nation was the Lemhi Shoshone. Most historians who are not Indigenous say the same. They say a Hidatsa raid took her when she was 12. Many Indigenous people do not accept a hero story in which she helped the United States grow. A traitor is what some of them see. Others see an enslaved woman who did what she could for her people. By then the distance traveled was 1,500 miles (2,414 km). Harder country still lay ahead. Only the Great Plains had been crossed so far. The Rocky Mountains would come next. The Great Basin would come after that. Forests of the Columbia River Basin would come too. In the months ahead, death would come close to the Corps of Discovery many times. Each time, Indigenous peoples would pull them back from that danger. Those peoples would care for them until they were well. Sacagawea was not the only reason the Pacific Ocean was reached. Every Indigenous people Lewis and Clark met helped make that arrival possible. A second book tells the rest of the journey. Assimilation means taking on a country's main culture, or being forced to take it on. Cultures are the ideas and customs of particular groups. A debt is something owed, and it is usually money. Colonization means taking a new territory for one's own use. Encountered means met suddenly or by chance. A confederacy joins people, organizations, states, or countries for a shared purpose. Enslaved means kept in slavery, owned by someone, or forced to work for that person. To conquer is to take control, usually by force, of a person, a group, a place, or a thing. An expedition is a group that goes on a journey or a voyage. Indigenous describes the earliest peoples of a place, or something related to those peoples. The Corps of Discovery was a unit of the U.S. Army. Meriwether Lewis and William Clark led it. From 1804 to 1806 it explored land gained in the Louisiana Purchase. A stronghold is a place with strong protection.

58. The Gettysburg Address

1The fifth version of the Gettysburg Address is the only one President Lincoln signed. The American Civil War was a long fight between two sides of the country. Men from the same town, and even the same family, sometimes fought on opposite sides. Many officers had trained and fought together earlier, and some were close friends. The Union was the Northern states, and the Confederacy was the Southern states. The war raged from 1861 until 1865 and cost the lives of 620,000 men. One of the war's most famous battles was the Battle of Gettysburg. Friends Lewis Armistead and Winfield Scott Hancock were wounded there as opposing generals, but Hancock lived and Armistead died.

2On the morning of July 1, 1863, Union soldier Rudolf Schwarz saw his brother among Confederate prisoners. The brothers hugged, happy to meet after leaving Germany, then parted and never met again. Rudolf was killed in action that afternoon. How did the United States end up at war with brothers fighting brothers? Why is the Battle of Gettysburg famous, and why is a 272-word speech more famous still? Slavery was legal in fifteen Southern states and illegal in eighteen Northern states, so many slaves tried to escape north. Lincoln opposed its spread beyond the South, and many feared he would abolish it in every state. Plantation owners needed slave labor and thought ending slavery would destroy their way of life.

3The economy was different in the North, where factories were common and did not use slave labor. Many people there thought slavery should end. After Lincoln's election, eleven slave states left and formed the Confederate States of America. They were Alabama, Arkansas, North Carolina, South Carolina, Florida, Georgia, Louisiana, Mississippi, Tennessee, Texas, and Virginia. Missouri, Kentucky, Maryland, and Delaware stayed, and only West Virginia formed by leaving Virginia. Lincoln took office in March 1861, war began a month later, and he had served two years by Gettysburg. Gray-clad rebels, or Johnny Reb, fought for independence, while blue-clad Yankees fought to keep the Union. Lee won many victories and led troops through Maryland into Pennsylvania, yet Lincoln had kept the nation together.

4The Union marched more than 93,000 soldiers into Gettysburg, Pennsylvania, and the Confederates more than 71,000. When the fighting stopped three days later, nearly 51,000 were dead, wounded, or missing. Thirty percent died or were injured, and more men died there than in any other Civil War battle. The battle ended Lee's advance north, but it devastated both sides and the town. Public buildings and homes became hospitals, every farm became a graveyard, and the war lasted another year and a half. Four months later, on November 19, 1863, Soldiers' National Cemetery was dedicated as the first national cemetery. Lincoln was asked for a few remarks, but Edward Everett, a former congressman and senator, spoke for two hours. Everett praised Lincoln's two minutes, and Lincoln was glad the speech was not a total failure.

5Days before, Lincoln told a journalist the speech would be short, short, short, and he kept his word. Brevity can move us more than a long speech, which is what less is more means. He wrote with care on the train, that morning, and until it was time to go to the cemetery. A picture shows President Lincoln in August 1863, a month after the Battle of Gettysburg. The battle was a turning point, but few understood that even four months later. Confederate leaders saw a defeat, not a disaster. Some in the North were sick of the war and wanted to let the South go. Lincoln knew victory was not near, so he needed a great speech to keep the Union strong and fighting.

6Today the Gettysburg Address is called one of the most inspiring speeches in American history. No recording exists, so there is no proof of his exact words. Reporters wrote what they heard and sent the words by telegraph to newspapers. Five copies in Lincoln's own handwriting still exist, but they differ a little. He spoke loudly and clearly, yet slowly and carefully. The slow pace may have shown how solemn the words were, or that the speech was not long. He was falling ill, looked sad and mournful, and his secretary called him ghastly and haggard. He felt weak and dizzy, went home feverish with a bad headache, and was later diagnosed with smallpox.

7The final signed copy is the most popular version and is worth almost half a million dollars. The original hangs in the Lincoln Room of the White House. Lincoln said a new nation was born in liberty and dedicated to the proposition that all men are created equal. He said the Civil War tested whether that nation could long endure. They met to dedicate a resting place for men who gave their lives so the nation might live. He said the living could not hallow the ground, because the soldiers had already made it sacred. The world might forget the words, but it could never forget what the soldiers did there.
