The dead must not have died in vain, and government of the people, by the people, for the people must not perish.

8Four score and seven means four sets of twenty years, plus seven more. The year was 1863, so he meant 1776, when the Declaration of Independence was signed. He said the founders left an old system for the freedom and chance of a new democracy. Our fathers meant men like Thomas Jefferson, who wrote that all men are created equal. In 1863 the South treated people unequally, so Lincoln urged them to uphold that founding ideal. He said the war was testing those values and the nation's strength to endure. The North's push for equality had strained a nation divided against itself. Gettysburg was among the bloodiest battles, yet Confederate leaders would not quit, so the war was not over.

9Gettysburg National Cemetery is the resting place of more than 3,500 Union soldiers from the battle. In the 1870s, the remains of 3,200 Confederate soldiers were sent to cemeteries in the South. A few Confederates remain at Gettysburg, along with soldiers from later wars. Saying they gave their lives stresses sacrifice, and Lincoln hoped to unite mourners on both sides. He said a cemetery was right, but the soldiers had already made the ground sacred. Hallow means to honor as holy, and consecrate means to make sacred. He said the world will little note what we say here, but it can never forget what they did here. The living must finish the work so the nation has a new birth of freedom and people's government does not perish.

10He called the soldiers noble and honored, and the last full measure means they gave their lives. He asked listeners to recommit to freedom, win the war, end slavery, and save the United States. The famous ending says that government of the people, by the people, for the people shall not perish. Simple word changes remind us that this government is all about people. A tablet with the phrase is in Elihu Vedder's 1896 mural Government at the Library of Congress. Today the handwritten copies are national treasures, and the speech is carved in the Lincoln Memorial. The memorial was designed like a Greek temple and opened in 1922. A 19-foot statue of Lincoln sits inside, and forty-one years later King spoke from its steps.

11Their reaction to the speech was quiet. Some say the applause was delayed and scattered, barely enough to be polite. One paper called his remarks silly and hoped the nation would forget them. Others described a hushed scene and a dignified silence. Some historians say there was no applause because people were so impressed. In just ten sentences, he summed up the war and the spirit of a nation. Over time, people came to revere the speech for saving equality and the cause the dead fought for. Lincoln, the sixteenth president, is for many people today the most beloved president of all.

59. The Judicial Branch

1The Supreme Court courtroom has nine seats for justices. In 1951, Black parents sued the school district in Topeka, Kansas, for their children. One child was third grader Linda Brown. Topeka schools were segregated, so Black children could not attend with white children. The case Brown v. Board of Education went to the Supreme Court, the country's top court. In 1954, the court voted unanimously to end legal school segregation across the country. A mother and daughter celebrated this victory on the steps of the Supreme Court. The judicial branch oversees courts and interprets laws fairly, like a referee who explains the rules.

2Leaders signed the Constitution in September 1787 and ratified it in 1788. The Constitution has seven articles and still guides the country. It divides government into executive, legislative, and judicial branches. The president leads the executive branch and manages operations. Congress, the Senate and House, makes laws and represents citizens. The judicial branch oversees the court system. Article III lets Congress create lower federal courts. The Supreme Court is in charge of the federal courts, and branches may cooperate or oppose.

3Federal courts take cases involving the U.S. government, states, the Constitution, federal laws, bankruptcy, copyright, patents, and maritime law. State courts hear a wider variety of cases, such as robbery, traffic violations, and family disputes. Federal courts may get involved if a state case involves federal law, and states cannot defy federal laws. Criminal law deals with acts like theft, assault, and vandalism, and guilty people may go to prison. Civil law deals with harm between parties and may end in money, not prison. Most cases are decided without a trial. Cases can move up from district courts and state courts to the Supreme Court. Judges, lawyers, and the Federal Judicial Center, set up in 1967, help run the federal courts.

4There are eighty-nine district courts in the states and five in territories such as Puerto Rico, for ninety-four in all. Each district has one judge, and federal cases start there. Some cases use juries of randomly selected citizens, while bench trials are decided by the judge. A losing side may appeal, and the number of appeals depends on the state and court level. Judge Neomi Rao was sworn in as a U.S. Court of Appeals judge. Thirteen appeals courts exist, two of them in Washington, D.C. Three judges review whether the case was fair, without redoing the trial. They may uphold the ruling, overturn it, or send it back to be retried.

5The Supreme Court is the last appeals court and has eight associate justices plus one chief justice. Each year thousands ask for a hearing, but at least four justices must vote yes. It grants a writ of certiorari, Latin for to be more fully informed, and studies the case. About 7,500 requests come in each year, and the court usually hears fewer than 150, or 2 percent. John Jay was the first chief justice, and John Marshall served the longest as chief justice. William Howard Taft is the only person who served as both president and chief justice. Thurgood Marshall was the first Black justice, Sandra Day O'Connor the first woman, and Sonia Sotomayor the first Hispanic justice. Decisions become precedents for later cases and can change society, as seen with Earl Warren and Ruth Bader Ginsburg.

6Most real court cases are less dramatic than TV shows, and many never reach trial. Some cases still take years and can change society when they reach the Supreme Court. In 1965, three teenagers in Iowa wore black armbands to protest the Vietnam War and were suspended. Their parents sued, and in 1969 the Court said students may express themselves if they are not disruptive. Mary Beth Tinker and her brother John showed their armbands in 1968. In 1961, Clarence Gideon was arrested in Panama City, Florida, got no lawyer, and received five years in prison. He wrote the Court and cited his right to counsel under the Sixth Amendment. In 1963 the Court ruled 9-0 for Gideon, he was found not guilty at a new trial, and many others in Florida were helped.

7In 2003, Massachusetts and other states asked the EPA to regulate gases that add to global warming. The EPA refused, so the states sued under the Clean Air Act of 1963. The Supreme Court ruled 5-4 that the EPA must monitor and regulate these gases. The EPA said 14 percent of global greenhouse gas emissions in 2010 came from transportation. The Constitution uses checks and balances so no branch can control the government alone. Congress can create lower courts, and the president nominates federal judges. The Senate must confirm those judges and can block the president's choice. President Obama named Sonia Sotomayor to the Supreme Court in 2009.

8Federal judges serve for life and cannot be fired for political reasons. The Senate can impeach and remove them for illegal or unethical behavior. Only fifteen federal judges have been impeached, one of them a Supreme Court justice, and eight were removed. Judge Harry Claiborne was impeached for tax evasion in 1986. Courts can strike down laws or actions that violate the Constitution. Lady Justice carries scales, a blindfold, and a sword. She stands for weighing facts, avoiding prejudice, and acting for justice. A strong court system helps people settle conflicts without force, and Lady Justice comes from Roman art.

9The judicial branch checks the other branches if they stray from the Constitution. It protects free speech, religious freedom, the right to organize, and the right to be secure at home. The American justice system is not perfect. Wealthy people can sometimes use money and good lawyers to avoid punishment. Hiring a good lawyer can change whether someone is found guilty. Sometimes criminals go free because of constitutional protections. Still, the principles of the judicial branch are well-founded. When applied well, they can bring true justice, and Supreme Court arguments are open to the public on a first-come basis.

60. The Lakota

1The Dignity of Earth and Sky Statue in Chamberlain, South Dakota, honors Dakota and Lakota women. The Great Plains stretch from the Rocky Mountains to beyond the Missouri River, and from Canada nearly to Mexico. The Lakota are part of the Oceti Sakowin Nation, one of the largest Native nations in the United States today. The nation has three groups based on language, culture, and place: the Lakota, the Dakota, and the Nakota. Each group has smaller bands, and the Lakota are the largest group with a long history on the Plains. Before the 1800s, Native peoples were most of the people on the Plains. Some lived in villages and grew crops, while others moved often to follow bison. The Lakota were one of the largest nomadic tribes, and Pine Ridge is an Oglala Lakota reservation.

2The Oceti Sakowin once lived farther east, on lands now in Minnesota and Wisconsin. The Ojibwe pushed them west in the 1700s. Outsiders call them Sioux, which may mean snake or enemy in the Ojibwe language. They call themselves Lakota, Dakota, or Nakota, words for allies, friends, or those who are united. Men and women had different jobs, but both were crucial, and women led the family and tribal decisions. Women owned the goods, animals, and tipis, packed the camp, and gathered, cooked, and dried food. They made clothes and bison robes with paint, quills, shells, and beads that European traders wanted. Tipis had wooden poles and bison-hide walls, stood up to wind, stayed warm or cool, and moved easily.

3Lakota men were skilled horse riders and earned a name as fierce warriors. Before the 1700s they hunted on foot, but trade and raids brought horses by the late 1700s. Brave deeds in war could bring wealth, honor, and higher status. It was a great honor to touch an enemy first or to capture an enemy's horse. They were strong traders who knew the land and bison, so they got hides faster than European traders. At fairs they traded bison robes for tools, beads, fabric, and weapons. Tatanka, or bison, are sacred, and bones, horns, hair, hides, and meat all had uses. Hunters once lured bison over cliffs or used bows, and bison gifts and symbols still mark ceremonies.

4Some old Lakota ceremonies are still held, though they may have changed. The Sun Dance lasts four days in late spring or early summer. They build a ritual circle and a center tree for the Great Spirit and the universe. Dancers look at the sun from sunrise to sunset to renew ties with the land and all beings. Making of Relatives once built alliances, and now it joins families or adopts a child. These ceremonies were forbidden while the U.S. government tried to end Lakota culture. From 1860, boarding schools far from home punished children for their language and customs, and some never saw family again. Settlers and soldiers killed millions of bison, so only a few hundred remained by the end of the 1800s.

5Lakota leaders signed many treaties that set borders with the United States. The government broke those treaties so white pioneers could settle on Lakota land. The Lakota were pushed onto smaller reservations, and war with the U.S. Army lasted for decades. In 1875 gold was found on sacred land, and the army invaded despite the treaties. Thousands left the reservations and joined Sitting Bull and Crazy Horse in Montana. In June 1876 at the Battle of the Greasy Grass, about three thousand warriors led by Crazy Horse overwhelmed U.S. troops. More than two hundred soldiers died within an hour, but years of war and lost resources forced a hard peace. On reservations they hid children, shared oral history, and kept ceremonies secret until the American Indian Religious Freedom Act of 1978.

6Billy Mills set a world record in 1964 and was the first American to win Olympic gold in the 10,000-meter race. Eagle Woman was an activist, diplomat, and translator, and the only woman recognized as a Lakota chief. Luther Standing Bear helped film studios hire Native actors, and Mary Brave Bird wrote and worked with the American Indian Movement. Dr. Beatrice Medicine fought for Native rights, and Tokata Iron Eyes travels to teach people about climate change. Today the Lakota gather for festivals, dance contests, and ceremonies that honor their heritage. In the 1990s the Brave Heart Women's Society revived a four-day First Moon Ceremony for Lakota girls. In 2007 Red Cloud Indian School in South Dakota and Indiana University started the Lakota Language Project. In 1991 the Lakota and over sixty tribes formed the Intertribal Buffalo Cooperative, raising bison above twenty thousand.

7A young Lakota woman spoke at the United Nations Youth Climate Summit in 2019. Students practice writing words in the Lakota language. Young members of the Standing Rock Sioux Tribe met President Barack Obama. Despite war, discrimination, and a two-hundred-year campaign to destroy their way of life, the Lakota have survived. Today they protect ancestral land and resist building that would harm the environment. They also work to increase bison populations. They teach young people and non-Native people their history, language, and traditions. As teachers, doctors, activists, artists, and politicians, they help their community and the world.

61. The Largest Bird Alive

1The parts of this book name the bird, then look from claw to small head. Later parts link it to dinosaurs, ask where it began, and visit the plains. Plants, stones, enemies, and group life come next. A closing look and a word list finish the book. How big is one? An ostrich can stand 9 feet tall, which is 2.74 meters. Weight can reach 350 pounds, which is 158.8 kilograms. A person in the same chart stands 6 feet, or 1.8 meters. That person weighs 175 pounds, or 79.4 kilograms. What kind of animal is this? Birds, in most minds, are small. They seem delicate. They fly. They sing sweet songs. They nest in trees, in homes that are tiny. Then you meet the ostrich. Hide the rest of the body and show only the legs. A viewer might guess reptile. A viewer might guess some animal from before written history. So what is an ostrich, exactly? It is the largest bird alive anywhere. Flight is not in its skill set. Small and fragile do not fit it at all. Its calls can be loud enough to raise the hair on your neck. Even so, the ostrich is a bird. Grown birds tower over the tallest people. These so-called little birds are strong. Fierce wild animals can meet a defense that works.

2A close look, from the claw up to a small head. Four toes is the usual count on a bird foot. An ostrich breaks that rule. Each foot has two toes. A hidden weapon sits on the inner toe. That weapon is a claw. The claw lets the bird dig into the ground. A firm grip helps it run. No other animal on two legs is faster. The legs are extremely powerful. A sprint can hit 45 miles an hour. That pace is 72.4 kilometers an hour. A longer run can hold 30 miles an hour. That pace is 48.2 kilometers an hour. One stride covers 10 to 16 feet. That reach is 3 to 5 meters. Feathers on the wing and on the torso set this bird apart in another way. Flying birds keep their feathers locked tight. Tight feathers are what make flight possible for them. An ostrich never flies, so its feathers stay soft. They are fluffy rather than stiff. Wings serve as balance. Balance makes the run smooth. Sharp turns become possible. Feathers also block sunburn. A picture shows a kick thrown at an enemy. The kick is powerful. A long neck helps in many ways. Extra height lets the head stay high. From up there the bird can scout new ground. It can search for food. It can spot danger coming. No land animal has larger eyes. Each eye measures 2 inches across. That is 5 centimeters. Long, thick lashes frame the eyes. Almost every part of an ostrich is oversized. The head is the exception. The brain is the other exception. Brain size is about the same as one eye. For a creature this strong, that brain is small. You could call the bird a little empty-headed. What puts an ostrich in the bird group? Its eyes are five times the size of a person's eyes. The neck is nearly half the bird's full height. Every bird has a backbone. Every bird has feathers. This bird cannot fly, yet the feathers still hold warmth. Wings help it stay balanced while it runs. Every bird has wings. Every bird has a bill. Every bird lays eggs. Every bird is warm-blooded. No bird egg is larger than an ostrich's. No bird egg has a thicker shell. Two toes, and only two, meet the ground on each foot.

3Running costs an ostrich about half the energy a person would spend. The reason sits in the leg tendons, which are tissues that connect parts. Those tendons are twice as springy as a person's. Ostriches and dinosaurs. Speed, size, and power are plain to see. Where the bird came from is not plain. Why flight was lost is not plain. How it grew so huge is not plain. Nobody truly knows. Theories are many. One recent idea looks back millions of years. Early relatives were much smaller then. They could still fly. Some scientists think these birds were a favorite dinosaur meal. Flight let the birds get away. That escape was their good luck. Dinosaurs later died out. Enemies grew fewer. More time was left for grazing. Across a long stretch of years, the birds changed. They became the huge flightless birds we know. And the winner is. Living relatives of T. rex do not get much closer than ostriches. An ostrich can average 30 to 45 miles an hour. That range is 48.3 to 72.4 kilometers an hour. T. rex was guessed at about 10 to 25 miles an hour. That range is 16 to 40.2 kilometers an hour. A newer study noticed how much an ostrich resembles Tyrannosaurus rex. A link seemed necessary to some scientists. Leg bones of the bird and leg bones of T. rex look extremely alike. Researchers wanted the dinosaur's speed, so they had an ostrich run on a moving belt. The study settled one point. A fit ostrich would beat a heavy T. rex in a race.

4Where ostriches live. A map shows the Atlantic Ocean, Africa, the equator, the Indian Ocean, Asia, and Europe. Shading marks the ostrich range. Ranches on many continents keep ostriches today. Africa is the only place they are native. Ostrich lands. Where the line began. The true starting place on Earth is still unsettled. The bird looks almost as if it came from another world. Fossils of ancient animals like ostriches have turned up in China. More have turned up in Europe. More have turned up in Canada. Those fossils let us make careful guesses. Could early relatives fly? Where did they start? How did they reach Africa? No one can answer for sure. Home on the plains. Today the birds live on the eastern plains of Africa. They also live on the southern plains. The land is dry. Scrubby grasses grow in plenty. Trees are few. That mix suits these birds. The savanna swings from one extreme to another. Days can be very hot. Nights can be quite cold. A year averages 68 to 86 degrees Fahrenheit. That range is 20 to 30 degrees Celsius. Winter runs a little cooler. Feathers become a fan in the daytime heat. The fanning keeps the bird cool. At night the feathers drop over the legs. That cover helps when the air turns cold. The Latin name is Struthio camelus. The words mean camel-like. A camel and an ostrich both carry a long neck. Both have eyes that stand out. Both can last a long time with no drink of water.

5Plants supply much of the water an ostrich needs. A long stretch can pass with no drinking. Greens, plus a side of stones. Almost any plant will do as food. Ostriches are omnivores. Most of the day goes to grazing. Leaves are on the menu. Seeds are on the menu. Fruit is on the menu. Roots are on the menu. An insect turns up now and then. A lizard turns up now and then. Teeth are missing, so stones do some of the chewing. An adult carries about 2.2 pounds of stones. That mass is 1 kilogram. The stones sit in the gizzard, which is an organ for digestion. Digestive fluids work with the stones. Food is ground before it moves on. The next stop is the intestines. Those intestines are astonishingly long. They stretch 46 feet, which is 14 meters. A person's intestines are about half that length. Enemies of the ostrich. Lions hunt them in the wild. Cheetahs hunt them. Leopards hunt them. Hyenas hunt them. Vultures steal eggs and eat them. Jackals do the same. A long neck and large eyes make the bird extremely alert. Danger can be seen from far off. Escape often follows that sighting. When a fight is the only choice, the bird has strong moves. A kick from those legs can do serious harm. The claws can do serious harm too. People once hunted ostriches for the plumes. The feathers were beautiful. Hats used them as trim. Dresses used them as trim. The hunting pushed the birds close to dying out. Plumes are used far less today. A male makes a deep booming call when a predator appears. The letters in one picture spell out that boom.

6Living on a large scale. An ostrich may live apart from others. It may also join a herd. A herd can hold five birds. It can hold fifty. It can hold still more. Time alone is what they prefer. They will still share grassland in peace with other grazers. Zebras are among those neighbors. Antelopes are among them too. The ostrich pays the neighbors back with noise. A loud call warns that danger is near. One herd usually has a single adult male in charge. That male is called the rooster. Several females, called hens, join him. Chicks join the group as well. The male courts more than one female. He decides which females may enter the herd. He also decides which ones may not. Once the herd is set, he digs. The dig is a shallow dent in the ground. That dent is the nest. The top female lays about six eggs there. No bird lays a larger egg. Each egg weighs about 3 pounds, or 1.4 kilograms. Other hens may share the nest, because the top hen allows it. Her own eggs stay safest in the center. Sitting on the eggs is shared work. Night duty goes to the top male. Day duty goes to the top female. A picture shows a male lifting his feathers in a mating dance. About forty-two days pass. A chick then hatches. The baby is about as big as a grown chicken. Running starts almost as soon as the shell opens. Wild-country skills pass from the male to the chicks. Luck can carry an ostrich to age fifty. Some live even longer. Six months is enough for a chick to stand near adult size. A myth says a scared ostrich buries its head in sand. The truth is different. The bird drops low. Its neck lies close to the ground. That pose hides it from predators. These birds are not cowards. They will fight when they must.

7On the plains, an ostrich keeps up with many other fast animals. An odd bird, and a wonder for science. Ostriches are grand. They are also mysterious. Their pace is quick. Their running skill is high. Predators do not automatically win. The land around them is hostile. It is dangerous. Flight was never their way of coping. They adapted anyway. Scientists stay fascinated for many reasons. The creature is swift. It cannot leave the ground. Studies will go on. They ask what makes this bird one of a kind. They also ask which traits it may have shared with Tyrannosaurus rex. That sharing, if it happened, was millions of years ago. Words to know. Adapted means changed so a living thing fits a new place or a special situation. To court is to try to win a mate. Defensive describes an action that protects the actor from harm or from attack. Dominant describes the one that is more important, more effective, or more powerful than the others. Grazing means feeding on grass and on other plants that grow in open land. Hostile means very unfriendly. An indentation is a shallow hole, or a dimple, in a surface. Omnivores eat plants and also eat animals. A plume is one feather. A group of plumes is a cluster worn by a bird. Prehistoric points to the time before people wrote history down. A savanna is flat grassland with few trees. The torso is the main part of an animal's body. Arms, legs, and the head are not part of the torso.

62. The Largest Lizards on Earth

1Dr. Tim Jessop studies wild animals for a living. Komodo dragons are his specialty. His first wild dragon, in 2002, caught him off guard. He was just starting out. Could a lizard truly grow this big? That was the question in his mind. A huge male lay in the sun on a rock. Jessop watched. For a moment his eyes seemed to be tricking him. Years of watching, study, and handling came after that day. He now knows these giant reptiles are entirely real. How big is one? Length can reach 10 feet, which is 3 meters. Weight can reach 176 pounds, which is 80 kilograms. A person in the chart stands 6 feet, or 1.8 meters. That person weighs the same 176 pounds, or 80 kilograms.

2A younger dragon fights a stronger male. The prize is females. The prize is also food. Muscle makes up nearly the whole animal. Almost any rival can be overpowered. Two males may clash over a female. They rise up on their legs. Thick tails keep them balanced. Each one tries to throw the other down. Bony plates sit in the skin. Bites from a rival meet those plates. Scratches meet them too. Teeth are the greatest weapons. The teeth are sharp. The edges are serrated. Huge claws join the teeth as weapons. Prey dies by those tools. Other dragons meet them in a fight as well. Blood is a likely result when two of these lizards clash. What puts a Komodo dragon in the reptile group? Skin is rough. The color is black, or it is yellow-gray. Bony plates lie in that skin. About sixty sharp teeth are ready to tear flesh. The tail is long. Strong muscle runs through the tail. The tongue is forked. Claws are long. Claws are curved. Every reptile has a backbone. Every reptile has scales or bony plates. Lungs are how every reptile breathes. Eggs are what most reptiles lay. Most reptiles are cold-blooded.

3A map places these dragons in Asia, on the Lesser Sunda Islands. Waters nearby include the Indian Ocean, the Flores Sea, and the Savu Sea. Indonesia is the country. Island names include Komodo, Flores, Padar, and Rinca. Gili Motang and Gili Dasami appear as well. One shade shows where dragons live now. Another shade shows where they lived in the past. Every wild Komodo dragon lives in Indonesia. That country is in Asia. Five islands of the Lesser Sundas hold them. Those islands sit northwest of Australia. They also sit just south of the equator. The islands are small. Volcanoes built them. Hills are rugged. Forest mixes with grassland. Heat lasts all year. Humidity runs high from December through February. Those months are the rainy season. A typical day reaches about 91 degrees Fahrenheit, or 33 degrees Celsius. The high can touch 110 degrees Fahrenheit, or 43 degrees Celsius. Night averages 73 degrees Fahrenheit, or 23 degrees Celsius. Midday heat sends dragons into shallow burrows. Cool nights send them there too. A dragon may dig its own burrow. It may also use a burrow another dragon dug. Most of the time each dragon lives alone. Hunting fills the hours. Eating fills them. Napping fills them. Swimming is a strength. A dragon can cross to a nearby island. Even so, most stay on the island they call home. Beaches are a common walk. Dead fish washed ashore are what they seek. The old range was wider than the range today. Food has disappeared in places. Habitat has disappeared too. The living area has shrunk over time. Dragons on two islands are much smaller than the rest. Those islands are Gili Motang and Gili Dasami. Less food and a harsher home changed them. Their average weight is 44 pounds, or 20 kilograms. On the remaining three islands the average weight is 176 pounds, or 80 kilograms. Many burrows are barely large enough for the animal inside.

4Prey includes Timor deer, water buffalo, and wild pigs. Animals follow game trails, old paths through the forest, to reach food and water. Komodo dragons are carnivores. The menu is wide. They hunt live animals. They also scavenge. Carrion is a clear favorite. The main foods start with deer. Wild pigs belong on that same short list. Water buffalo belong on it too. People brought all three of those animals to the Lesser Sunda Islands. Rats are eaten too. Snakes are eaten. Bird eggs are eaten. Dead fish are eaten. Young of their own kind are eaten as well. That makes them cannibals. About one-tenth of an adult diet is young dragons. Scientists worked out that share. A chase is possible. Ambush is more common. The dragon hides beside a game trail. The wait can last hours. An animal passes, and the attack comes. Most predators fail more often than they succeed. Dragons are like that. A bite still lands often, and the bitten animal gets away. Death usually follows the bite. Sometimes death is quick. Sometimes death waits a few days. Bleeding from the injury alone can kill the prey. Harmful bacteria live in a dragon mouth. A killing infection can grow from bacteria that live in the mouth. Hurt prey may later stand in dirty water where animals drink. Bacteria in that water can infect the wound and kill the animal. Dragons usually share a kill. Fights still break out at mealtime. Venom is a recent discovery. Venom is a kind of poison delivered in a bite. Evidence says the venom makes blood leave the body faster. It keeps blood from clotting as it should. It can also send the animal into shock. More study is needed. Scientists still need to sort out what venom does. They also need to sort out what the bacteria do.

5Wounded prey may wander off and die. A long forked tongue is the tool that finds the body. The tongue flicks out and tastes the air. Molecules floating in the air stick to it. The tongue pulls back into the mouth. Both tips touch a special organ. That organ sits on the roof of the mouth. Odor molecules from a nearby dead animal can be read there. Messages go to the brain. The brain compares the two tips. One tip holds a stronger dose of molecules. That tip points the way to the meal. Dr. Jessop once saw a dragon kill a water buffalo. He watched it eat. Other dragons arrived as time passed. They joined the feast. Several days later almost nothing remained. Bones were left. Horns were left. Hoofs were left. Those leftovers prove how huge the appetite is. One meal can pass three-quarters of an adult own weight. Very little of a carcass is wasted. About 12 percent goes unused. Lions leave 25 to 30 percent unused. An extra hinge in the jaw lets the dragon swallow a piece bigger than its head. An egg is about 4 inches long and 2 inches wide. Those measures are 10 centimeters by 5 centimeters. At hatching, body length is just past the length of a ruler. Females do not all use the same kind of nest. Some nests are dug in the ground. Some sit on a hillside. A mound built by a bird may be used instead. That bird is the orange-footed scrub fowl. It lives on the ground. Age decides how many eggs a female lays. The count runs from ten to thirty-five at one time. Laying takes a great deal of energy. It also takes responsibility. Six to nine months pass before hatching. The female must guard the eggs the whole time. Predators are the threat. Decoy chambers are sometimes built inside the nest. The false rooms trick predators. Other dragons are among those predators. A female does not lay every year. The off years let her regain energy.

6Babies break out of the eggs when the rains are finishing. The month is April. Insects are plentiful then, and the babies need that food. Mothers give no care. The young are on their own at once. Trees are the safe place. Adult dragons are the danger below. Hawks are a danger too. Other birds of prey hunt the babies. Two to four years can pass in the trees. Insects are food. Geckos are food. Rodents are food. Birds are food. Older young also take leftovers from adult meals. They stay careful. They do not want to become the meal. A common baby color is green. Black stripes and yellow stripes mark them. Spots mark them too. Males and females grow at the same pace at first. They stay the same size until adulthood. Adulthood arrives around age seven. Males then pull ahead in size. Males also live much longer. Sixty years is possible for a male. A female may live only about half that long. Nest building takes extra energy. Producing eggs takes extra energy. Guarding eggs takes extra energy. Scientists think that load makes females age faster. Wild numbers run from 3,000 to 5,000. Breeding females are few. The count of those females may be as low as 350. The conservation status is vulnerable. Vulnerable means the animal could become endangered. People compete with dragons for food. Wild dogs compete too. That contest threatens survival. Humans are also taking the habitat. Villagers sometimes kill a dragon that attacks livestock. Indonesian law protects the dragons. International law bans selling them. It also bans trading them. Poachers still kill some. People who study these lizards, in country after country, are trying to stop extinction. Indonesia set up Komodo National Park in 1980. The park shelters these rare reptiles. Dr. Jessop is Australian. Scientists from Italy joined him. Scientists from Indonesia joined him. The year 2007 is when their Komodo Survival Program began. The program studies wild dragons. It looks for ways to manage them. It looks for ways to conserve them and the land they need. Zoos in Europe keep captive dragons. Indonesia has zoos in the work. Canada has zoos in it. The United States has zoos in it. Their goal is a captive group with wide genetic variety.

7For a short burst, a Komodo dragon can reach 11 miles an hour. That pace is 17.7 kilometers an hour. These reptiles are remarkable. They are supersized. No lizard in the world is larger. A zoo visit makes people stare. A video does the same. The body looks perfectly fitted to the life it leads. Tools of the hunt are always on the animal. Teeth are razor sharp. Claws are long and strong. Watch one move, and the damage it can do is easy to imagine. A meal can set it prowling. A mate can set it prowling too. Earth biodiversity includes these dragons. They matter. Dr. Jessop asks people to help. Help can go to these lizards. Help can go to other wild animals as well. Support for conservation groups is one path. Another path is balance. People have needs. Other living things have needs. A balance between those needs can keep these huge lizards alive. Solitary describes an animal that likes to be by itself. Serrated describes an edge cut into points, the way a saw is cut. Conservation is the work of guarding wild places and the creatures that live in them. Carrion is meat that is rotting after an animal has died. Clotting is the change of a liquid into a thicker or solid form. People use the word most often for blood. Scavengers search out animals that died already, then eat them. A molecule is the tiniest piece of a material that can stand alone. Two or more atoms join to build each one. Basking is a rest taken while sunlight warms the body. Genetically has to do with traits passed down, and with how living things differ. Bacteria are one-celled life. Some kinds make a body sick. A decoy pulls a threat away from the real goal. It can be a person or an object. Adapting is a change that helps a living thing fit a new setting.

63. The Leader from a Log Cabin

1Washington, D.C., holds the Lincoln Memorial. It is one of the monuments Americans love most. This book opens with his life, then follows the early years, law and politics, a nation split apart, the war, and a word list. A beginning. Few presidents matter more in the story of the United States than Abraham Lincoln. He led the country through the Civil War. Northern states and Southern states were fighting over what the nation would become. People call him the Great Emancipator. Enslaved people across the South gained freedom because of what he did. Peace left the country united once more. It has stayed one nation ever since. Pictures often show him tall. They show him lanky. They show him solemn. Memory paints him as a common man. Regular schooling was slight. A log cabin in Kentucky was his birthplace. Even so, he became a great lawyer. He became a great speaker. He became a great political leader. Speeches on freedom are cut into the stone of the Lincoln Memorial. So are speeches on justice. So are speeches on bringing all Americans together. Washington, D.C., is where that stone memorial stands. The belief he held was plain. He said he would not be a slave. He said he would not be a master either. That pair of refusals, he said, was his idea of democracy. A cabin in the picture is built of logs. People believe those logs came from the cabin where he was born. Visitors find the cabin at a national historic site named for his birth. Hodgenville is the town. Kentucky is the state.

2The early years. Thomas Lincoln and Nancy Hanks Lincoln were his parents. Birth came on February 12, 1809. The place was a small farm of log cabins near Hodgenville, in Kentucky. The family later moved to Knob Creek, which was nearby. Abe had an older sister named Sarah. The two children went to school only in short winter stretches. Abe was seven when the family left for Indiana. They hoped life would be better there. Tom built a new cabin of logs. Two years after that, Nancy grew ill. She died. Abe and Sarah grieved for a mother who had worked hard and loved them. Their father soon married again. The new wife was Sarah Bush Johnston. She was a widow. She already had three children. Tom had known her back in Kentucky. Love and care from her made a warm home for Abe. Sarah, his sister, shared that warmth. She encouraged Abe while he grew into a tall youth. The youth was awkward. Friends spoke of a moody quiet. Even so, Abe told stories that felt homey. The stories were funny. School still lasted only a few weeks each winter. The walk was 18 miles a day. That distance is 29 kilometers. Most of his learning came from books he borrowed. Newspapers were borrowed too. Neighbors lent them. Travelers lent them. Pioneer tales fed his mind. Daniel Boone was one of those pioneers. Stories of Johnny Appleseed did too. The books he read did the same. A world bigger than the backwoods began to seem real. A picture shows Abe on a flatboat bound for New Orleans. As a teenager he stood 6 feet 4 inches tall. That height is 2 meters. The flatboat rode the Mississippi River. New Orleans was the busy port at the end of the trip. There, for the first time, he saw Black men held in chains. He saw Black women in chains. He saw Black children in chains. White enslavers were buying them and selling them. Rivers of misery. His feelings about ending slavery changed across his life. Hatred of the institution itself never changed. A steamboat trip in the year 1841 carried him along the Ohio. That river was a border. Free states lay on one side of several stretches. Slave states lay on the other. A dozen enslaved people were on board. Shackles bound them together. Lincoln called the sight a torment that did not let up. Every later meeting with that river brought the same sight. The same sight met him at every line that separated free people from enslaved Black people.

3Work as a young man earned him the name rail splitter. At age 22 he moved to New Salem, in Illinois. Jobs came one after another. He kept a store. He worked as a surveyor. He worked as a carpenter. People knew him as a wrestler. They also knew him as a skilled speaker. That speaking club in New Salem was a debating society. A race for the state legislature of Illinois came next. The 1832 race was a loss. Two years later he won. John Todd Stuart was a lawyer. Stuart was also a legislator. Law study was the path Stuart urged. Books of law were what Abe read. March of 1837 is when the exams were behind him. He joined Stuart's law practice. Springfield became his new home. The Illinois legislature met there. Law and politics. Lincoln's views rested on one strong belief. Each person mattered. Wealth did not change that. Privilege did not change that. Respect came to him inside the Whig Party. Whigs wanted a strong central government. That government sat in Washington, D.C. Democrats were the other leading party. They wanted states to run their own affairs. They did not want Washington stepping in. At age 30 he met Mary Ann Todd. She would become his wife. The wedding came three years later, in 1842. A year after the wedding, their first son was born. The son's name was Robert. In 1846 Lincoln won an election. He would represent Illinois in the U.S. Congress. The family moved with him to Washington, D.C. A picture shows Mary Todd Lincoln in 1846.

4Lincoln sat in Congress while North and South grew more divided. Slavery was the issue. The North relied on workers who were paid. Those workers were in factories. They were also on small farms. A powerful government at the center was the Northern view. In those states, holding people as slaves was illegal. The Southern economy turned on King Cotton. Large plantations grew that cotton. Enslaved people did the labor. Ownership treated them as property. Daily life for most was very poor. Personal rights were denied. Civil rights were denied. Laws in the Southern states allowed slavery. Those laws protected it. The United States was growing. Western territories asked to join. Would a new state enter free? Would it enter with slavery? Atlanta, in Georgia, had a building where enslaved people were sold. Slavery was something Lincoln opposed. As Illinois's man in Washington, he still held a legal view. State law in the South, he believed, protected the practice there. He did fight the spread of slavery. The western territories were where he fought it. Two years in Washington ended. He went back to Springfield. For the next few years he shared a law practice. The practice did well. His partner was William Herndon. People came to know Lincoln's honesty. They knew his skill with the law. They knew his wit. They knew his fine public speaking. Early campaigns had already made him a skilled communicator.

5A nation split. Power was the prize in a fight between slavery's friends and slavery's foes. The year 1854 brought a law from Congress called the Kansas-Nebraska Act. An older law had banned slavery in those territories. The new act cancelled that ban. Residents could now choose. A territory might become a free state. It might become a slave state. Letting the people decide was called popular sovereignty. Stephen Douglas introduced that idea. Lincoln and Douglas had been rivals in politics for a long time. By then Douglas held a Senate seat. The state was Illinois. A map shows the United States after that act, in 1854. It marks the Nebraska Territory and the Utah Territory. It marks the Kansas Territory and the New Mexico Territory. It marks free states and free territories. It marks areas allowed to choose. It marks slave states. It marks unorganized territory. Lincoln decided the time had come to speak. He would speak against slavery's spread. A new try for office was part of the plan. Membership came in a young party called the Republicans. That party opposed slavery. In 1858 the party nominated him for senator. Facing him was Douglas, the senator who had long been his foe. The campaign included the Lincoln-Douglas debates. The whole country paid attention. Lincoln's speaking skill was great. A crisis for the whole nation, he said, was what slavery was creating. Split a house against itself, he warned, and it will not last. He did not believe this government could last forever half slave and half free. He argued that Black people were owed the right to life. They were owed liberty. They were owed the pursuit of happiness. White people already held those rights. In Douglas's reading, the Constitution offered equality to white people and to nobody else. Choice of slavery or freedom, he believed, belonged to each state. A picture shows Lincoln in 1858. The photo came two weeks before his last debate with Douglas. Another picture shows Stephen A. Douglas.

6Lincoln lost the race for the Senate. The debates still made him popular. The Republican Party felt that popularity most. By 1860 the party wanted him for president. Rallies and parades filled the campaign. People called him Honest Abe. They called him a homespun man of the people. Equality was his stand. Freedom was his stand. November of that year made him president. Joy broke out in the North. Western territories shared the joy. Outrage was the Southern answer. His inauguration was set for March 4, 1861. Even before that day, seven states of the South voted to quit the country. The word for that leaving is secede. Pro-slavery states set up their own government. They called it the Confederate States of America. Jefferson Davis was their president. They prepared for war. Lincoln took the oath on March 4, 1861. The place was the U.S. Capitol. Workers had not finished the building. Years of war. Lincoln still hoped to avoid war. His inauguration speech put a heavy question in other people's hands. He told unhappy fellow citizens that the huge issue of civil war was theirs. It was not his to settle alone. Events moved fast. April 14 arrived with a surrender. Northern troops left Fort Sumter in enemy hands. South Carolina had turned cannons on that fort. Speed marked what each side did next. The Union called for volunteers. The Confederacy called for volunteers. Both sides gathered weapons. Both sides gathered supplies. Lincoln's home and office were in Washington, D.C. That city stood with the Union. Across the river he could see Virginia. Virginia stood with the Confederacy. Southern generals were the stronger leaders at first. Bull Run was the first battle, and the Union lost it. Finding commanders with enough strength was a struggle for Lincoln. The search lasted through the war. General George B. McClellan led Union armies to some successes. By 1862 those armies were stopped. The stopper was Confederate general Robert E. Lee. Union forces did control New Orleans. They controlled the Mississippi River. Victories were still few. More of the war plans shifted into Lincoln's own hands. The North was calling for action. Huge numbers of young soldiers died. Others were wounded. Others went missing. The war moved into its second year.

7A picture shows President Lincoln talking with General McClellan. The place is Antietam. McClellan would soon be replaced. Personal loss hit Mary and Abraham Lincoln hard. Fever took Willie, their third son. The month was February 1862. Deep despair followed. Mary never fully recovered from the grief. Abraham carried his own sadness into meetings with families. Those families were mourning people killed in the war. Bull Run was fought a second time, and the Union lost again. Antietam brought the war's bloodiest clash, and both armies paid. Senators from the Republican Party, and powerful ones, pushed the president. They wanted ending slavery to become a goal of the war. A successful fight, they argued, required removing the issue that had caused the war. Lincoln at last became convinced. The presidency, he decided, gave him power to command abolition where the South held sway. New Year's Day of 1863 started the Emancipation Proclamation. Every enslaved person inside the Confederacy was declared free. The freedom was meant to last forever. Freedom for some. The proclamation freed many enslaved people. Some enslaved people were left out. Freedom on that January day covered enemy territory. Black men just freed inside Confederate land could join the Union fight. The North badly needed those soldiers. The war's meaning changed. It became a crusade against slavery. The order did not cover the border states. Delaware was one of those states. Maryland was another. Kentucky was another. Missouri was another. Why skip them? They held enslaved people, but they never seceded. Lincoln feared a different result if he freed people there. Joining the Confederacy was the risk he feared. A loss of the war would then be almost sure for the country. It would lose the South. It would lose the chance to end slavery in every state. Enslaved people who remained would wait two more years. A map of border states in 1863 uses four labels. One label is Union free state. One is Union slave state. One is Confederate slave state. One is territory. Names on the map include Maryland and Delaware. West Virginia is there. Missouri is there. Kentucky is there. Only West Virginia was created by breaking away from a state that had joined the Confederacy. That Confederate state was Virginia. From 1863 to 1865 the new Union state ended slavery bit by bit.

8Another people under oppression. Manuelito was a Navajo chief. The year was 1864. The land was taken, and his people had to leave. That land was in northeastern Arizona. Many people call Lincoln the Great Emancipator. Many Native peoples remember a harsher record. Westward growth was huge while he held office. His policies harmed Native peoples who already lived in those places. The Homestead Act came in 1862. The Pacific Railway Act came in the same year. A railroad across the continent followed from those laws. Land vanished for many Native peoples because of that railroad. It meant lost natural resources. It meant the loss of a whole way of life. Lincoln forced Native peoples onto reservations. In doing that, he continued harsh policies. The policies were discriminatory. Earlier presidents had used the same kind of policies. Fort Sumter fell, and free Black men hurried to sign up for the Union's army. At first they were turned away. By the middle of 1862 the need for volunteers was pressing. The government then began to take them. Men newly freed in the South signed up too. When the Civil War closed, Black men in uniform numbered nearly 200,000. Their service was as soldiers for the United States. A picture shows Company E of the 4th U.S. Colored Infantry. The company mixed newly freed men with other free Black men. Summer of 1863 brought a Union victory at Gettysburg. That battle was a turning point. President Lincoln dedicated a cemetery. Dead soldiers, and there were many, were the people that ground was meant to honor. His talk there is called the Gettysburg Address. Two minutes was the whole length. People remember it for simple beauty. They remember it for eloquence. He called the war a test of survival. Could the country remain a democracy? Living people, he said, had to complete what the dead had begun. The aim was a government of the people. It would be a government by the people. It would be a government for the people. That government, he said, must not perish from the earth. General Ulysses S. Grant led Union armies to victories. Those victories came in the West. They also came in the South. Lincoln began to hope the war would end. Confederate troops were beaten in Georgia late in 1864. They were beaten in Virginia in that same season. Voters re-elected him president. Early in 1865 he cheered a vote in Congress. The change was the Thirteenth Amendment. It was added to the Constitution of the United States. Slavery inside the country was banned.

9April 9, 1865, brought the end. The fighting had lasted four years. Lives lost came to 600,000. Survival went to the Union. Slavery was abolished. Six days after the peace, the president was gone. Abraham Lincoln had been killed. An assassin's bullet had killed him. John Wilkes Booth fired the shot. Booth sympathized with the Confederacy. Lincoln was at a play. The theater was Ford's Theatre. The city was Washington, D.C. A funeral train carried his body back to Illinois, the state he loved. Mourners along the road saluted in silence. They were saluting a great American hero. One of the last photographs of Lincoln was taken in February 1865. Words to know. Abolition means bringing something to an end. Here it means making slavery against the law. Momentous describes something of huge importance. An orator speaks in public, and the best ones speak with power and clarity. Civil rights cover the law, social life, and money matters. They exist so every citizen can have freedom and equal standing. A privilege is an honor, or a special gain that only some conditions allow. Eloquence is clear, strong speech or clear, strong writing. To secede is to leave a group in a formal way. An emancipator sets other people free. Solemn means grave, or full of sadness. Sovereignty means you are free to run your own affairs. Equality means all people hold the same rights. Homespun describes a humble person whose life began in a simple rural place.

64. The Legislative Branch

1In the summer of 1964, Congress discussed a controversial new law. The Civil Rights Act would end segregation in schools, libraries, pools, theaters, and restaurants. It had passed the House but still needed to pass the Senate. Some senators stalled the vote with long speeches. This is a filibuster, also called talking a bill to death. Senator Robert Byrd of West Virginia spoke for fourteen hours and thirteen minutes. He packed his briefcase after that speech on the Senate floor.

2Most senators soon voted to end the filibuster. Nine days later the Senate passed the act, and President Lyndon Johnson signed it. Thomas Jefferson, the third president, held a contest for the Capitol design. Congress is the Senate and the House, the center of the legislative branch. Members work in the Capitol in Washington, D.C., and write and change laws. The Constitution, adopted in 1788, divides government into three branches. Only Congress can make or change laws, borrow money, set taxes, fund the military, and declare war.

3In 2018 a senator's yearly pay was $174,000. Each state has two senators, so the Senate has one hundred members. Senators serve six years and may then run again. The Senate approves treaties and some presidential appointments, including Supreme Court judges. The House has 435 members elected for two-year terms. Seats are divided by state population. California has the most representatives. Montana and Delaware have one seat each, and Lisa Blunt Rochester is Delaware's only representative.

4Six nonvoting members come from D.C., Puerto Rico, American Samoa, Guam, the Northern Mariana Islands, and the U.S. Virgin Islands. The House elects the Speaker, who would become president if the president and vice president could not serve. Citizens may contact representatives or speak at town halls. Anyone may write a bill, but a member of Congress must sponsor it. Cosponsors are other members who support it. Committees research bills, hold hearings, and ask experts for help. A subcommittee studies the bill more deeply before a full committee vote.

5In March 2018, hundreds of thousands marched on Capitol Hill against gun violence. If most of a full committee will not recommend a bill, discussion stops. Members often bring bills back again. Supported bills go on a calendar, but many wait months or are never taken up. A term of Congress lasts two years, and bills that do not pass are dead. Members debate, change, and vote, and a passed bill moves to the other chamber. If the versions differ, a conference committee writes a compromise both must approve. An early 1860s picture shows House members debating.

6Both chambers must approve a bill before it reaches the president. The president may sign it, or let ten days pass so it becomes law. A veto rejects it, but two-thirds of both chambers can override the veto. From 2011 to 2013, Congress introduced 6,845 bills and only 561 became laws. So 6,284 did not become law. In 1936 a representative put a bill in the hopper on the House floor. Standing committees are permanent, and select committees are temporary. In 2017 to 2018 the Senate had sixteen standing committees and the House had twenty.

7In 2016 a senator spent about $10.4 million and a representative about $1.5 million on a campaign. Members raise money for staff, travel, ads, posters, stamps, and stickers. Representatives run every two years, so fund-raising helps them keep their jobs. In election years they may spend a quarter of their work time raising funds. They use calls, speeches, and interviews to tell voters their plans. Often only a few senators are on the floor. Senators may speak until a time limit is set, and bells and lights call them to vote. Members must win the votes of the people they hope to represent.

8Members must be in the Capitol to vote. House members use a card at one of forty-seven stations. Green means yea, red means nay, and yellow means present. A yellow vote does not decide the bill, but it counts toward attendance. Senators may answer yea or nay in a roll call. In a voice vote, the presiding officer judges which side is larger. In a division vote, members are counted one by one. The House has used electronic voting since 1973. In a voice vote, names and exact totals are not counted.

9The statue Freedom crowns the Capitol dome. The bronze figure wears a helmet, holds a sheathed sword, and holds a laurel wreath. She weighs about 15,000 pounds, or 6,804 kilograms. She wants peace but is ready to defend freedom. She reminds politicians of the country's founding pillars. Congress can write and change laws, so the legislative branch is integral to government. Senators and representatives are meant to give citizens a voice. They should see that people are fairly represented.

65. The Living World of Fungi

1This book asks what fungi are and how many kinds exist. It looks at fungi that harm and fungi that help. It tells how to hunt mushrooms and how fungi can be fun. Word meanings and a topic list come last. White fuzzy mold on stale grapes and small brown mushrooms in a neighborhood park share something. Itchy, scaly skin on a person's feet shares something with yeast that makes bread rise. All four are organisms of the fungi kind. Flowers are often more colorful than fungi. Animals move around far more than fungi do. Fungi can still catch your eye in other ways. Shapes and sizes vary widely. Their ways of living are their own. They matter in health care. They matter in making food. They matter in other human work too. A common belief says fungi are plants. Plants make food by photosynthesis. That process uses sunlight, it uses water, and it uses carbon dioxide gas. Fungi cannot do photosynthesis. They must get food another way. Chemical study makes fungi look closer to animals than to plants. That likeness is why fungal diseases are hard to cure. A medicine that harms a fungus can harm a human. It can harm another animal too.

2Mycologists may study fungi with computers. Laboratory tools and indoor collections are other tools. Outdoor searches are another way. Food comes from chemicals called enzymes, which fungi release around themselves. Complex molecules nearby break down because of those enzymes. Simpler chemical nutrients are the result. Long, threadlike cells called hyphae then take the nutrients in. Most fungi share one more trait that many plants lack. They build tiny structures called spores. Wind can carry spores. Water can carry them. Animals can carry them. New fungi grow from the spores. A mycologist is a scientist who studies fungi. More than 100,000 species have been identified. Yeasts are fungi made of one cell each. Most other fungi are made of many cells. Molds, mildews, and mushrooms are in that many-celled group. A field of mushrooms is one kind. Mold on fruit is another. Bracket fungus on a tree is another. Athlete's foot on skin is another.

3The next sandwich you eat may deserve a thank-you to yeast. Yeast is a fungus of a single cell. Bread making uses it. Bubbles of carbon dioxide gas come out of the yeast. Bread rises into the soft, fluffy loaves you eat because of those bubbles. Beer and wine are made with yeast too. Some yeasts make new yeasts by forming spores. Others use budding. In budding, a small bulge called a bud forms on the cell. A hard wall grows around the bud. The bud then breaks away from the old cell. It becomes a yeast cell of its own. A picture shows a small bud on the side of a yeast cell. That bud will leave and grow into a new cell. A crusty patch on a rock may be gray, yellow, orange, or brown. The patch may be a lichen. Lichen can form where fungi and algae grow as partners. Algae make food the fungi use. Fungi take in water the algae can use. Rocks and tree bark are places lichens grow. Many lichens live where it is cold. Other life finds very little to eat in those places. Reindeer eat lichen. Caribou eat it. Other animals eat it too.

4A microscope can show mold on stale bread up close. Tiny sporangia may appear. Spores are made by sporangia. You may remember reaching for fruit, such as an orange, and finding blue-green and white fuzz. That fuzz was mold. Mold is one type of fungus. Grapes, peaches, and grapefruits can grow it. Bread, cheese, and other foods can grow it too. Many molds send hyphae straight up, like pins. Hyphae are what let fungi absorb nutrients. The pinhead tips are called sporangia. Spores come from those tips. The fuzzy look of mold comes from the pinheads. Mildews are parasitic fungi. They live in or on the organisms they eat. Powdery blotches on leaves are a common sign. Apple trees, cherry trees, rose bushes, and pea plants can show those blotches. Bathroom-wall scum is often called mildew. What bothers most bathrooms is actually bacteria, not a fungus. A picture shows fungi coating an apple that hangs in an orchard. Mushroom species number more than 5,000. Colors include white, yellow, orange, red, and brown. Most grow in woods or in grass. They live on dead matter that is decaying, such as dead grass. A mushroom's hyphae tangle into a mass called a mycelium. The mass sits underground, just under the surface. Years can pass while the mycelium grows and spreads down there. The umbrella-shaped parts above ground last only a few days. They last just long enough to make spores.

5Fly agaric mushrooms are poisonous. Enoki mushrooms, also called straw mushrooms, can be eaten. A blue whale may seem like the largest living thing. A giant redwood may seem largest instead. An underground fungus makes both look small. It grows in Malheur National Forest in Oregon. The mycelium covers 3.5 square miles, which equals 5.6 square kilometers. That spread is bigger than 1,600 football fields. Few people have looked at this giant. Only clusters of small golden-brown fruiting bodies rise above the soil. They appear now and then. A mushroom comes out of the ground on a stalk. Stalk height, depending on the kind, runs from 3/4 inch (1.9 cm) to 15 inches (38 cm). A round cap sits at the top. The biggest caps can reach 18 inches (46 cm) across. Stalk plus cap is the fruiting body. Under the cap, many mushrooms have rows of thin blades called gills. Fish use gills to breathe. These gills do not. Cells in the gills make spores. Some mushrooms make spores in tiny tubes under the cap. A diagram can name the cap, the gills, the annulus, the stalk, the volva, and the mycelium. A life cycle is the set of stages a species passes through. The stages run from first growth, through death, to the start of a new organism. Every fungus species has its own kind of life cycle.

6A typical mushroom's life cycle starts when a spore lands where food and water are enough for it to germinate. Hyphae branch out under the ground. A mycelium forms. Hyphae from two different mushrooms sometimes join inside one mycelium. Tiny knots called buttons grow on that mass. Buttons sprout and become fruiting bodies. After a heavy rain, those bodies may shoot up fast. Once spores are released, the fruiting body withers. The mycelium stays alive a long time. More fruiting bodies can be made. A cycle diagram can show a mature fruiting body, spores, and fungal spores. It can show a button sprouting after rain. It can show buttons starting to form, plus a button, hyphae, a mycelium, and a spore. Many fungi harm plants. They can harm animals, people included. They can harm things people make. In damp places, mildews and some other fungi grow in books, in clothing, and in wood. The items decay because of the fungi. Other parasitic fungi sicken crops. Cereal crops are at special risk. Wheat, rice, oats, and corn are examples. Spores sometimes enter the seeds of these grains. When grain falls, the spores move into the new plants that grow from those seeds. A fungus causes Dutch elm disease. Bark beetles, a kind of insect, carry it from tree to tree. A large elm can die in four to eight weeks. Chestnut blight, a similar fungus, killed nearly every chestnut tree in the United States. The early 1900s were when that happened. In the 1840s, blight, a fungal disease, killed most potato plants in Ireland. Potatoes were most of the food for people there. The blight was so wide that about one million people in that small country starved to death. Millions more left Ireland. They went to the United States, to Canada, and to England.

7Hot, sweaty feet do not look appealing, unless the viewer is a fungus. Some fungi too small to see prefer warm, moist skin. They grow between toes and on the soles. The disease they cause is athlete's foot, and it is infectious. Skin turns red, scaly, and itchy. Clean, dry feet help you avoid it. Socks that let fresh air through help too. Ringworm names several skin diseases caused by fungi. They show up as red, scaly rings. The neck, the back, the chest, or other skin can carry the rings. Spread from person to person is easy. Touching infected skin can spread it. Almost any object that touched the sore spot and then touched skin can spread it too. Red, itchy rings mark the skin of people who have ringworm. One picture shows ringworm over most of an ankle. Other fungal diseases hit the lungs, the kidneys, the throat, or other inner parts. Chemical solutions called fungicides can kill harmful fungi. Harm is not the whole story. Some fungi help doctors cure disease. Certain molds make chemicals used as antibiotics. An antibiotic is medicine that weakens or kills disease-causing bacteria. Penicillin is one of the more common antibiotics from fungi. It treats pneumonia. It treats many other bacterial diseases. Other molds go into cheese. Added mold sets off chemical reactions. Flavor changes. The cheese ripens. Fungi help food in further ways. Farmers count on fungi to decompose dead plant material. Chemicals released into soil become nutrients. New crops grow with the help of those nutrients. Blue cheese is one cheese made with blue mold. The mold makes the cheese ripen.

8Penicillin was the first antibiotic used on people with serious disease. The find was an accident in 1928. Sir Alexander Fleming, in England, made it. He saw green mold in a lab dish that also held bacteria. Every bacterium around the mold was dead. The mold was giving off a chemical that hurt the bacteria. Later researchers learned how to use that substance, penicillin, against bacteria in people. Going out to look for fungi is a fun way to learn more. Mold or mildew may turn up here and there. Hunting mushrooms is likely to be the most fun. Do not put any mushroom in your mouth. Wash your hands after you handle fungi. Some mushrooms are safe to eat. Others hold poisons that can make you very sick. Telling the safe kinds from the poisonous kinds is hard. Only a hunter with a great deal of experience can do it. Always go with a hunter who has that experience. The best hunting times are fall and spring, after heavy rain. Before you start, learn which mushrooms grow where you live. A library can help. A wider search can add facts about mushrooms near you. Mushrooms named at the end of the book show some of the many fungi of North America. People once killed pesky flies with sugar sprinkled on fly agaric mushrooms. Sugar drew the flies in. Once a fly landed and ate, poison in the mushroom killed it.

9Have any conclusions about fungi formed as you read? Did any fact surprise you? One discovery may be that fungi can be fun. Watch for fungi. See what else these special organisms can show you. You can grow mold of your own. Ask a parent or a guardian before you begin. A plastic sandwich bag should hold either one slice of bread or one piece of fruit. The food gives nutrients to mold spores. Add a moist paper towel to the bag. Close it. Moisture stays trapped. Mold needs that moisture if it is to grow. Repeat the steps with several foods. Wait until fuzzy mold appears. About 3 to 5 days is the usual wait. Do different foods grow different molds? Write what you see, or draw it. Some mushrooms are poisonous. The destroying angel, Amanita virosa, grows in woods under trees in eastern North America. The fly agaric, Amanita muscaria, grows in woods under trees through North America. The jack-o'lantern, Omphalotus olearius, grows in clusters on tree stumps. It glows in the dark. Its home is the eastern United States. The false chanterelle, Hygrophoropsis aurantiaca, grows on or near logs and stumps. Its home is northern North America.

10Some mushrooms are not poisonous. The oyster mushroom, Pleurotus ostreatus, grows in clusters on logs and stumps across North America. The shaggy mane, Coprinus comatus, grows on lawns, in fields, and beside roads across North America. The old man of the woods, Strobilomyces floccopus, grows under trees in eastern North America. The king bolette, Boletus edulis, grows under trees that bear cones. It grows in North America outside the central region. Buttons are knots on a mycelium that become a mushroom's fruiting bodies. A cap is the round top of a mushroom. To decompose is to break into simpler chemical substances. Enzymes are chemicals that cause or change reactions while staying unchanged themselves. A fruiting body is the mature part of a fungus, and it produces spores. Fungi are living things that take in nutrients by absorption. Fungicides are chemical solutions that kill fungi or slow their growth. To germinate is to start growing. Gills are mushroom parts whose cells make spores. Hyphae are long threads fungi use to absorb nutrients.

11Infectious describes a disease that can pass from one organism to another. A mycelium is a tangled mass of hyphae. Nutrients are chemicals a living thing needs in order to live. Organisms are living things. To reproduce is to make a new generation. A species is one specific type of living thing. Sporangia are structures on fungal hyphae that make spores. Spores are tiny fungus-made structures that can grow into new fungi. A stalk is the mushroom part that rises from the ground under the cap. Topics in this book include algae and the jack-o'lantern. They include antibiotics and the king bolette. They include athlete's foot and lichen. They include bacteria and the life cycle. They include the bark beetle and Malheur National Forest. They include blight and mildew. They include bread and mold. They include budding and the mushroom. They include the button and the mycelium. They include the cap and the mycologist. They include carbon dioxide and the old man of the woods. They include cheese and the oyster mushroom. They include the destroying angel and the word parasitic. They include Dutch elm disease and penicillin. They include enzymes and photosynthesis. They include the false chanterelle and ringworm. They include Sir Alexander Fleming and the shaggy mane. They include the fly agaric and sporangia. They include the fruiting body and the spore. They include the fungicide and the stalk. They include germinate and yeast. They include gills, the Great Irish Famine, and hyphae.

66. The Mystery of King Tut

1Howard Carter was a British archaeologist. In 1922 he found the tomb of Tutankhamun in Egypt. Egyptian workers helped him. Carter looked at the golden sarcophagus. Tutankhamun is better known as King Tut. He became pharaoh at age nine and died suddenly at about nineteen. He had ruled less than ten years, more than 3,300 years ago. People still ask how and why the young king died.

2Ancient Egypt had about thirty dynasties over three thousand years. The first twenty dynasties make four periods, ending with the New Kingdom. King Tut was in the 18th Dynasty. The New Kingdom began around 1550 BCE, long before Tut was born. Egyptians worshipped many gods. Ra was shown as a falcon, and Amun was a man with a tall crown. Some people then worshipped the Sun itself as Aten, a golden disk with rays. Aten was just one more god until Tut's father, Amenhotep IV, became pharaoh.

3Out of many gods, people picked a few to help with work. Thoth was the god of writing, and Anubis was the god of embalmers. Amenhotep IV began to rule in 1352 BCE and believed only in Aten. He is called the world's first monotheist. He changed his name to Akhenaten, meaning He Who Serves Aten. He tried to destroy temples of the other gods. He moved the capital from Thebes to Akhetaten, the Horizon of Aten. Today that city is Amarna, and the change is the Amarna Revolution.

4King Tut was born about 1345 BCE and named Tutankhaten, the Living Image of Aten. DNA tests showed Akhenaten was most likely his father. His mother was one of Akhenaten's sisters. Nefertiti, Akhenaten's favorite wife, was likely his stepmother after his mother died. Akhenaten died in 1336 BCE after seventeen years. Two short reigns may have followed, perhaps a brother of Tut and perhaps Nefertiti. No one knows for sure who those rulers were. Three or four years later, the nine-year-old became pharaoh.

5Tut wore jewelry, linen clothes, perfumed oils, and makeup. He hunted with a bow from a chariot, and nearly fifty bows were placed in his tomb. He married Ankhesenpaaten, whose name means She Lives Through Aten. They had at least two daughters, but neither lived. A cupbearer kept his drinking cup full. Ay and General Horemheb did the hard work of the court because Tut was so young. Both had served Akhenaten, but they disliked his religious changes. They hoped the boy king would bring back the old gods.

6Ay and Horemheb got Tut to stop the worship of Aten and restore Amun. Tut changed his name to Tutankhamun, and the queen became Ankhesenamun. The court left Amarna and went back to Thebes. In 1327 BCE Tut died at about nineteen, and his death was not recorded. He was mummified and buried in the Valley of the Kings near Thebes. He had no living children. Ay married his widow and ruled until 1323 BCE. Then Horemheb became pharaoh.

7Horemheb smashed Aten temples and statues, and he cut King Tut's name and face from wall art. He died in 1295 BCE after twenty-seven years, and the 18th Dynasty ended. Ramses II later finished destroying Amarna, and sand covered what remained. In the 1800s archaeologists read hieroglyphics on walls. Tombs held goods for the afterlife, but earlier robbers had emptied them. In 1798 a soldier found the Rosetta Stone, a black rock with three writings. Two writings were ancient Egyptian and one was Greek. Scholars learned that the symbols stood for sounds, not whole words.

8By the early 1900s, Tut was the only known pharaoh whose tomb was still missing. Carter searched more than five years, then found it in 1922. The mummy wore a large gold mask and lay in three nested coffins. The inner coffin was about 242 pounds of pure gold. Carter wanted half the treasure for England, but every piece stayed in Egypt. Today Egypt may send artifacts on tour, yet Egypt decides. Ay and Horemheb both became pharaohs after Tut, so some suspected murder. A statue inscription says Horemheb was innocent of foul play.

9The cupbearer and the attendant Tutu could enter the king's bedroom. Either might have hit his head while he slept or pushed him down stairs. For years people thought a blow to the head killed Tut. X-rays in 1968 and 1978 showed damage at the back of the skull. In 2005 Dr. Zahi Hawass led a study in Cairo with a CT scanner. The scanner takes many X-rays, and a computer makes detailed images. Hawass said Tut did not die from a blow to the head. The skull damage came after death, probably while he was embalmed.

10The CT scans showed a cleft palate and a club foot. Tut needed a cane, and many canes were in his tomb. A broken leg had not healed, and Hawass said infection may have killed him days later. Tests from 2007 to 2010 found severe malaria and bone necrosis in his left foot. Some say the leg broke during embalming or when Carter's team took the body out. It is still possible he was poisoned. New DNA evidence makes theories easier to test, but experts may not agree. What is your interpretation?

11These are examples of Egyptian hieroglyphics. They come from the Temple of Amun. The Temple of Amun shows these hieroglyphics.

67. The Nobel Prize

1The Nobel Prize is the world’s top award in arts, science, and culture. Just being nominated is a lifetime honor. The prizes were first given on December 10, 1901, five years after Alfred Nobel died. From 1901 to 2015, 874 people, called Nobel Laureates, and 26 groups won prizes. For many years, five prizes were given each year in physics, chemistry, physiology or medicine, literature, and peace. A sixth prize, in economics, began in 1968. The prizes came from the vision of Alfred Nobel. Every medal shows him on the front, and each prize has a different picture on the back.

2Alfred Bernhard Nobel was born on October 21, 1833, in Stockholm, Sweden. He was one of four sons of Caroline and Immanuel Nobel, an engineer and businessman. By 1842 the family had moved to St. Petersburg, Russia, where Alfred received a first-class education. By age seventeen he spoke Swedish, Russian, French, German, and English, and he liked chemistry. In 1850 he studied chemical engineering in Paris and worked with nitroglycerine, a new liquid too dangerous for practical use. The family returned to Sweden in 1859, and his brother Emil was killed in an explosives accident in 1864. Alfred did not give up, and in 1866 he mixed nitroglycerine with fine sand to make dynamite. Dynamite made him rich and famous, changed building and war, and his 1896 will created the prize foundation after five years of family lawsuits.

3The great honor of the prizes comes from careful research, and the committees change every year. Scholars, scientists, and former winners ask thousands of leaders to nominate people, and the voting is secret. Prizes are given each December 10, the anniversary of Nobel’s death. Three groups in Sweden award economics, literature, and the sciences, while a committee in Norway awards the Peace Prize. Nominations open in September, are due in February, are cut to a short list by May, and are reviewed in summer. Winners are chosen in October, and the ceremony is in December. Marie Curie, born in Poland, was the first woman winner, sharing the 1903 physics prize for radioactivity.
