In 1911 she won chemistry for two new elements, her daughter later won chemistry, and in 2020 three women won science prizes.

4Have you heard of the formula E = mc2? Albert Einstein’s theory of relativity changed how we think about the physical world. German-born Einstein won in 1921 for theoretical physics, and the prize helped his career. In 1945 Sir Alexander Fleming of Scotland won in medicine for penicillin, which saved many lives. Tu Youyou won in physiology or medicine in 2015, the first mainland Chinese scientist to win a science Nobel. She used old Chinese medical texts to find a cure for malaria, tested it on herself, and saved millions of lives. In 2019 Esther Duflo became the second woman and the youngest economics winner for work on global poverty. In 1993 Toni Morrison became the first African American woman, and the first Black woman, to win in literature.

5Gabriel García Márquez, born in Colombia, won the literature prize in 1982 for writing that mixes the fantastic and the real. He and other winners show how the literature prize honors influential writers. Dr. Martin Luther King Jr., Mother Teresa, and Nelson Mandela won Peace Prizes for helping people who faced injustice. The 1964 prize honored King’s fight for civil rights in the United States. Nelson Mandela won in 1993 for his long fight against harsh rule in South Africa. In 2011 Ellen Johnson Sirleaf, Leymah Gbowee, and Tawakkol Karman shared the Peace Prize. Sirleaf had fought for democracy in Liberia, Gbowee led women for peace, and Karman pushed for free expression in Yemen. Malala Yousafzai, the youngest laureate, won in 2014 at seventeen after the Taliban attacked her for defending girls’ education.

6Some prize winners cause controversy. Theodore Roosevelt won the Peace Prize in 1905 for helping end a war between Russia and Japan. Critics said his use of military power in the Philippines, Cuba, and Panama should have kept him from the award. Henry Kissinger won in 1973 for a Vietnam cease-fire, but critics pointed to the secret bombing of Cambodia. His co-winner, Le Duc Tho, turned down the prize to avoid being linked with Kissinger. Barack Obama’s 2009 Peace Prize was also questioned as too soon, yet he gave the $1.4 million to charity. Some people say a committee of Norwegian politicians may be unfair when it chooses Peace Prize winners. Of 954 winners, only 70 have been Asian and only 17 Black, and as of 2022 no Black person had won in the sciences.

7Each winner receives cash, a diploma, and a gold medal with Alfred Nobel’s image. In 2015 one prize was eight million Swedish kronor, or just under one million U.S. dollars. If several people share a prize, that money is divided equally. The prizes are given at the Nobel Banquet in Stockholm City Hall. There are speeches, dancing, a full concert, and a fancy dinner. The Nobel Prize has not been free of controversy. Still, it has had a lasting and positive influence around the world. The fame and cash help great thinkers continue work for freedom, peace, and better lives.

68. The Normandy Landing

1This book begins with the morning itself, then the war behind it, then a plan called Operation Overlord. Five beaches follow in this order. The first is Utah. Then comes Omaha. Then comes Gold. Then comes Juno. Sword is last. A closing look and a word list come last. Hundreds of men crouched in dozens of small boats made for landing. Families far away filled their thoughts. The boats sat just off a coast. Germany occupied that part of France. Most of these men were not career soldiers. Lifelong military training was not their story. Some had been teachers. Some had been store clerks. Some had been housepainters. They were ordinary citizens. One home country was the United States. Another was the United Kingdom. Canada was another. A draft had pulled them into service. The war was World War II. Rough water rocked the landing boats. Seasickness hit many of them. Fear hit all of them. The mission started with a jump into cold water. Then came a run across open sand. German soldiers were shooting. Those German soldiers had been ordinary citizens too, before the war. The world these men knew had changed. Losing was not a choice they could accept. Planners had given the mission a code name. The name was Operation Overlord. For the Allies, this fight had to be won. This is the story of D-Day. What the letter D means. In military language, D stands for day. The code marked the date of any major operation. It was not limited to one battle. Days before that date, and days after it, used plus and minus signs. This particular day was June 6, 1944. D minus 3 meant three days earlier. That earlier date was June 3. D plus 3 meant three days later. That later date was June 9.

2A map of Europe in 1942 names Ireland and Prussia. It names England, the Netherlands, Poland, and Germany. Belgium and Czechoslovakia appear. Luxembourg, France, and Austria appear. Switzerland, Hungary, and Romania appear. Portugal, Yugoslavia, and Bulgaria appear. Spain, Italy, Albania, and Greece appear. Waters on the map are the Atlantic Ocean and the Mediterranean Sea. A key marks Axis control, neutral countries, and Allied control. The background. World War II was the biggest war of modern times. German troops marching into Poland were the start. The month was September 1939. Poland fell quickly. German troops then turned west. France was invaded in June 1940. Four years followed. Germany was not alone. Italy and Japan were the other Axis powers. Together they took most of Europe. They also took many islands in the Pacific. Allied nations tried to stop them. The United Kingdom was an Ally. The United States was an Ally. Canada was an Ally. The Soviet Union was an Ally. Many other nations stood with them. Freeing France would trap German troops. Allied-held France would lie on one side. The Soviet Union would lie on the other. Operation Overlord. For Allied forces, few dates in this war mattered more than June 6, 1944. Boats put men ashore on the beaches of Normandy, in France. That landing opened a long, brutal job. The job was to free Europe. The job was also to end the war. No earlier military operation had been this ambitious. More than 156,000 men had to cross the English Channel. Their equipment had to cross. Their tanks had to cross. The Axis could not be allowed to learn the plan in time. If they learned it, they could mass their forces. They could crush the landing. A map of the landing points names Portland and Southampton. It names Great Britain, Portsmouth, and Shoreham-by-Sea. Dartmouth is marked. The water is the English Channel. Marks show where U.S. forces, British forces, and Canadian forces would go. Utah is one beach on the plan. Omaha is another. Gold is another. Juno is another. Sword is the last. France is the far shore. Airborne troops would jump by parachute. Their zones lay across northern France. The jumps were set for early morning on June 6. Other soldiers would leave naval landing craft. They would storm the five beaches. German troops guarded those beaches. The hope was a foothold on the French coast. From there the advance into France could start.

3A Utah Beach map marks German positions. American spots on the map are shown for midnight. Counts for Utah Beach: 20,000 U.S. troops came ashore. Military vehicles that landed numbered 1,700. U.S. casualties numbered 589. The beaches. Utah Beach. The United States 4th Infantry Division was assigned here. So were the 82nd Airborne Division and the 101st Airborne Division. Landing time for the 4th Infantry Division was 6:30 in the morning. Five hours earlier, paratroopers had already jumped. They came from the 82nd and from the 101st. Drop points sat 2 to 5 miles inland. That span is 3.2 to 8.0 kilometers. One job was to hold the main road. The road ran from Valognes to Carentan. Say Valognes as vah-LOHN-yuh. Say Carentan as kah-rohn-TOHN. German troops could not tell what the paratroopers were. They might be the main attack. They might be a decoy, meant to pull eyes off a bigger assault. The beach landing at 6:30 was supposed to use that confusion. Those troops were to push inland and meet the paratroopers as fast as possible. Bad weather made strong currents. Amphibious boats therefore missed their beach targets. The miss was 2,200 yards, which is 2 kilometers. Men had to wade in from the water. Brigadier General Theodore Roosevelt Jr. was with them. He was the oldest son of Theodore Roosevelt, a former U.S. president. He told his men the war would begin from the spot where they stood. He ordered them forward. Luckily the beachfront was not heavily defended. American losses stayed small next to the losses on the other beaches. Afternoon brought a meeting. The 4th Infantry Division joined the American paratroopers. German defense there collapsed quickly. By night the Americans had moved about 4 miles inland. That distance is 6.4 kilometers. They were still about 1 mile, or 1.6 kilometers, from more paratroopers. Those paratroopers were the 82nd Airborne at Ste. Mere-Eglise. Say that town sahnt-MER-e-GLEEZ. The town sits some 6 miles north of Carentan. That gap is 9.7 kilometers.

4An Omaha map also shows Utah and Gold nearby. The key marks German resistance. American lines on this map are the ones held at midnight. Counts for Omaha: 34,000 U.S. troops came ashore. Military vehicles lost numbered 136. U.S. casualties numbered 3,686. Omaha Beach. The United States 1st Army fought here. This beach stretched 6 miles, or 9.7 kilometers. None of the other four beaches was bigger. Lieutenant General Omar Bradley led the 1st Army. From the start, his men were at a disadvantage. Cliffs 100 feet high looked down on the sand. That height is 30.5 meters. Attacking upward was very hard. The Germans had added dragon's teeth. Those were concrete obstacles shaped like teeth. Each stood 3 to 4 feet tall, or 0.9 to 1.2 meters. Mines packed the obstacles. The aim was to destroy landing craft. German gunners fired from the cliff tops. They also fired from thirteen resistance nests. A nest was a small defensive post that could fight on its own. Advantage did not sit with the American side. That was true before any shot. The attack was timed for 6:30 in the morning. The tide would be low. The dragon's teeth would show. Infantry would land with twenty-nine amphibious tanks. The tanks were Shermans. Armor was supposed to give the Allies a huge edge in firepower. Disaster came when the tanks left their boats too far out. All but two flooded. They sank to the ocean floor. Crews could not be saved. The tanks could not be saved. Men on the sand never got the armored cover they expected. Weather made things worse. Strong wind and a rising tide shoved many landing craft far off course. Confusion spread through the troops. German fire cut the Americans down at a frightening pace. Losses grew so bad that Lieutenant General Bradley thought about calling the whole operation off. The only path left was to keep going.

5A note on the picture says this beach runs 5 miles, or 8 kilometers. Rocky cliffs fence it in. Staying alive meant a sprint toward those cliffs. Heavy German gunfire had to be dodged the whole way. Men who reached the rock then had to climb. The cliffs towered over them. While they climbed, destroyers of the U.S. Navy came as near as they dared. The ships struck German fortifications. That fire was important relief. It pulled German attention off the Americans on the sand. Until then, the defenders had been fixed on beating those men. Night found the Americans gripping Omaha Beach. Storms had already forced the invasion to wait. The first chosen date was June 5. Allies picked it because they expected clear sight. They expected light wind from the northwest. They expected low tides. If that day failed, another two weeks would pass before conditions looked right again. June 5 turned stormy. Strong wind and high waves wrecked any chance of a landing from the sea. Clouds hung low. Pilots could not see targets. They could not drop paratroopers on the mark. They could not hit enemy positions. The next morning was not much kinder. Gold Beach. A map key marks German resistance. It marks British positions as of midnight. Counts for Gold: 24,970 British troops came ashore. Military vehicles that landed numbered 2,100. British casualties numbered 1,023. Britain's 50th Infantry Division fought here. Gold was one of two beaches taken by British troops. It sat in the middle of the five. Landing time was 7:25 in the morning. Weather struck at once as a major problem. Strong wind had let seawater cover German mines. Other beach obstacles were covered too. British engineers could not take the mines apart on the schedule they had set.

6Hidden mines damaged twenty armored cars. The day could have turned disastrous for the British. It did not, because naval guns and attacks from the air had already knocked out many German defenders. Afternoon found the British holding most of Gold Beach. They pushed inland quickly. They also took the fishing village of Arromanches. Say that name ar-roh-MAHNSH. Later, Mulberry harbor was built at this town. Allies used the harbor to unload supplies. Gold Beach counts as a tremendous success in strategy. British troops could then join the Canadians. Those Canadians had landed at Juno Beach. Mulberry harbor ran about 6 miles, or 10 kilometers. Flexible roadways of steel formed it. Steel pontoons held the roads up. Concrete pontoons held them up as well. Juno Beach. A map key marks German resistance. It marks Canadian positions as of midnight. Counts for Juno: 21,400 Canadian troops came ashore. Military vehicles that landed numbered 3,200. Canadian casualties numbered 961. Canada's 3rd Infantry had the job of taking Juno. Going inland was the aim. The inland move would tie together two British beaches. Gold was one. Sword was the other. Bad weather and a rising tide got in the way. German mines sat partly underwater. Canadian engineers who handled demolition could not destroy the mines. Damage from the mines was severe. Landing craft lost or hurt by them came to 30 percent.

7A picture shows a bulldozer tank. With a machine like that, American troops got through the dragon's teeth the Germans had built. Damaged boats left many Canadian soldiers struggling. They had to wade to the sand. Those who arrived met a powerful blast of German fire. In the first wave, survival was about a fifty-fifty chance. The Canadians refused to quit. They fought their way off the sand. They fought farther inland. Once clear of the beach, they took charge quickly. Losses were heavy. Even so, their inland advance beat the advance on each of the other four beaches. A number of towns fell to them. They linked up with the British who were at Gold. Sword Beach. A map key marks German resistance. It marks British positions as of midnight. Counts for Sword: 29,000 British troops came ashore. Military vehicles that landed numbered 2,603. British casualties numbered 630. Britain's 3rd Infantry Division raided this beach. It was the other British beach. The main aim was the town of Caen. Say Caen as KAHN. Caen lay 9.3 miles inland, which is 15 kilometers. Every main road in the region ran through that town. Allied success inland depended on holding those roads. The raid opened at 7:25 in the morning. British troops stormed the sand. Paratroopers landed inland. They came from the 3rd Parachute Brigade and the 5th Parachute Brigade. They joined the soldiers already on the beach. German resistance at Sword was light compared with the other beaches. The British were fortunate in that.

8After the landing, tanks in town streets became a common sight. Tour-La-Ville, in Normandy, was one such town. By 8:00 in the morning, most of the fighting on Sword was finished. By 1:00 in the afternoon, British commandos had met the paratroopers. The plan was working in every detail. Safety did not last. Germany launched a counterattack. By 8:00 at night a German unit was on the sand. It was the 192nd. Its title was Panzer Grenadier Regiment. The German gain was only temporary. Their vehicles sat in the open. Allied tanks were already at Sword. Allied fighter planes were there too. Those weapons could hit the German vehicles. The counterattack failed. It still blocked one planned link. British troops could not meet the Canadians from Juno on that same day. Caen stayed out of British reach for now. Even so, the Allies counted the mission a huge victory. The beach was made stable. Allied forces moved roughly 4 miles inland. That distance is 6.4 kilometers. The next day they met the Canadians. Caen itself fell six weeks later. The date was July 20, 1944. A closing look. Allied lives lost were many. Axis lives lost were many. Operation Overlord was still a tremendous success. The sacrifice bought a foothold. From that foothold, Allied forces could start pushing German forces back. Occupied France could be freed. The day was a huge military victory. Grief belonged to the day as well. So much happened that exact loss counts were hard to write down. Casualty is the word for every armed-forces loss. The count includes people killed. It includes people wounded. It includes people missing in action. It includes prisoners of war. Historians estimate German casualties somewhere from four thousand to nine thousand. Allied casualties that day passed six thousand. Fighting continued for about another year. Allied troops kept taking back lands in Europe that Germany held. May 8, 1945, is when Germany gave up. Japan's surrender came on August 15 of that year. That second surrender officially ended the war. D-Day was a major turning point in World War II. So was the later freeing of France. Allied soldiers gave work and sacrifice on D-Day. Take that away, and the war might have ended another way.

9Words to know. A paratrooper is a soldier trained to leap from an airplane and fight behind enemy lines. Panzer is the name for one German tank of this war. Mourning is grief, especially grief for someone who has died. Mines are bombs hidden under ground or under water. They explode when touched or moved. Liberation is the act of setting people free. Infantry are soldiers trained, armed, and equipped to fight on foot. Fortifications are defensive builds, including forts, meant to make a place harder to attack. A foothold is a first position from which a further advance can start. Drafted means ordered into military service, especially in wartime. A decoy is a person or an object that draws someone, or something, away from the real target. Commandos belong to a unit trained for fast raids inside enemy land. Casualties are people hurt, killed, or missing in a war, an accident, or a disaster. Axis powers were the countries lined up against the Allies during this war. Germany was one. Italy was one. Japan was one. Amphibious describes military moves in which troops enter enemy land from the water. Allies were nations that aided each other. Their enemies in this war were Germany, Italy, and Japan. Great Britain was among them. The United States was among them. France was among them. The Soviet Union was among them.

69. The Panama Canal

1Ships pass through the Gatun locks on the eastern side of the Panama Canal. The cargo ship Ancon made the first trip through the canal in 1914. On August 15, 1914, Ancon sailed through narrow channels and around tall mountains. That was the first ship to travel from the Atlantic to the Pacific through the new canal. Many people had thought this marvel would never be built. A journey that once took months could now take only days. The fifty-mile canal was built with skill, American technology, and the hard work of thousands of laborers.

2The Panama Canal is about 50 miles long. Using it saves a sea route of 5,300 miles, or 8,370 kilometers. Workers spent ten years cutting earth from the isthmus and fighting many obstacles. The canal boosted world trade and helped the United States become a world power. More than one hundred years later, ships from yachts to aircraft carriers still use it. Spanish explorers first dreamed of this water path, but the technology was not ready in the 1700s. In the early 1800s, Britain and France made plans, but they did not build a canal. In 1848, a ranch hand found gold in California, and people rushed west from the East.

3There was no railroad across the young country, and the land west of the Mississippi was wild. The trek over mountains, plains, and deserts was long and dangerous. A sea trip was safer but took much longer. Some travelers crossed Panama by land, then boarded another ship for California. Others sailed around Cape Horn through heavy winds and rough seas. The land crossing took about a week and was not pleasant. By 1855, trains ran on a 47-mile, or 75.6-kilometer, line between Panama's coasts.

4Panama is a hot, rainy ribbon of land between Central and South America. It is the narrowest strip between the Atlantic and Pacific Oceans, with thick jungles and many plants and animals. In 1879, Ferdinand de Lesseps led a French company that began to dig a canal. He had built the 102-mile Suez Canal and hoped to finish this shorter canal in ten years. France signed a treaty with Colombia, which owned the isthmus. De Lesseps and Philippe Bunau-Varilla brought workers and heavy machines. He ignored warnings that the jungle was unlike the sandy land of Egypt. Digging began slowly.

5Damp rainforest air made steel equipment rust quickly. Workers labored in heavy rain and stifling heat. Many died in mudslides, accidents, or diseases spread by mosquitoes. After seven years the French project failed, and de Lesseps left. More than $287 million was spent, and more than twenty thousand lives were lost. For ten years, no one tried to build the canal. President Theodore Roosevelt took office in 1901 and wanted a canal for trade, Asian markets, and warships. On November 18, 1903, the United States signed a treaty with the new Republic of Panama.

6The first American plan was a canal in Nicaragua, but Bunau-Varilla urged work at the French site. Colombia's congress rejected a U.S. treaty as unfair. On November 3, 1903, rebels took control, and the revolution ended by sundown. The United States recognized Panama's independence three days later. Workers and steam shovels dug an almost 50-mile canal from Colón to Panama City. By 1906, more than 85 percent of workers had been treated for mosquito diseases. Dr. William Gorgas had standing water emptied, homes sprayed, and beds covered with netting. Yellow fever cases fell to one by November 1906, and malaria deaths later fell below 1 percent.

7In 1904, John Wallace led the work, and Roosevelt told him to make the dirt fly. The earth was thick, sticky mud that slid back into the trenches. By June 1905, most American workers had left, and Wallace quit. John Stevens took charge of seventeen thousand workers whose morale was low. He stopped digging, built roads, and repaired the old Panama Railway to haul mud away. He also built churches, hospitals, mess halls, and schools. A sea-level canal through the mountains would have added many years. Stevens planned locks longer than three football fields, a dam on the Chagres River, and a huge lake.

8Each lock was 70 feet deep and 110 feet wide, and building them took four years. Stevens resigned in 1907, and Major George Washington Goethals took over. Crews built three sets of locks and Gatun Dam across the Chagres River. In 1914 the locks worked as planned, and the canal became an important route. A ship enters a lock, the steel doors close, and water from above lifts the ship. Ships rise through three locks to Gatun Lake, then the process is reversed on the far side. Gatun, Pedro Miguel, and Miraflores locks link the Caribbean Sea and the Pacific Ocean. Gatun Lake also helped nearby wildlife by reducing the need for new roads.

9The United States controlled the canal until the end of 1999, when Panama took over under a treaty. By the early twenty-first century, the canal was showing its age. More than fourteen thousand ships and three hundred million tons of cargo passed through each year. It needed a makeover for larger ships and traffic jams. In 2006, the people of Panama voted for two new sets of locks and two new lanes. In 2016, forty thousand workers finished the $5.4 billion, ten-year project. The new locks let much larger ships pass and can double the cargo handled each day. A tugboat helps a freighter make its way through the canal.

70. The Plague!

1A map shows how the Black Plague spread from 1347 CE to 1351 CE. It names many cities, the Atlantic Ocean, the Black Sea, and the Mediterranean Sea, and some places were mostly unaffected. In October 1347, a dozen trading ships reached Messina, in Sicily, after a long trip across the Black Sea. Sicily is part of modern-day Italy, and such ships often brought news and goods from the East. Most of the crew were dead or in agony, with pus and blood from black boils. People in Messina had heard rumors of a terrible disease in Asia, and these ships brought the plague. Later called the Black Death, it spread across Europe and North Africa by people and by ships. It killed tens of millions, rich and poor alike, and was one of the worst disease outbreaks in history.

2In the 1300s, no one yet linked germs, or fleas, to illness. In 1894, almost 550 years later, scientists named the likely cause: a bacterium called Yersinia pestis. It gets into the digestive tract of fleas that feed on black rats and other rodents. When rodents died, many historians believe, fleas then bit people and other animals. A flea bites an infected rodent, the bacteria multiply in the flea, and then the flea bites a person. Dirty, crowded cities dumped waste in the streets, so flea bites were common. Some think it spread too fast for this bacterium alone, and they also name cholera, typhus, or an unknown microbe. It remains the leading suspect, DNA is in mid-1300s mass graves, and people spread it by cough, blood, or saliva.

3There was no effective treatment for the plague. Signs took three to five days: headache, fever, chills, diarrhea, vomiting, swelling, and intense pain. Victims might die fast or last three to five more days, but only one in five survived. Bubonic plague attacks lymph nodes in the neck, groin, and underarms, which help the body fight disease. The nodes swell into black boils called buboes, and poisoned blood and pus can kill. Pneumonic plague makes the lungs bleed and can spread in tiny drops from a cough. Septicemic plague grows in the blood, poisons it, and is almost always fatal. By 1348 travelers and ships spread it through filthy towns, and by 1351 almost all Europe was reached.

4When the plague arrived, many people panicked and fled, and their flight often spread it. Families left the sick, shops closed, and doctors, clergy, and officials ran away. No one knew the cause, so people tried a skinned chicken, unicorn-horn potions, incense, or flowers. Flagellants whipped their backs with spiked whips and prayed God would forgive their sins. Others ate, drank, and partied, sure the world was ending. Some Christians wrongly blamed Jews, and more than two hundred Jewish communities were wiped out. Pope Clement VI said Jews were not at fault and should not be harmed. Some ports used a forty-day quarantine, and leaders limited travel and buried the dead in mass graves.

5From 1347 to 1353, many survivors found half their town gone, with shops shut and little food. Records were poor, but about eighty million Europeans lived before the plague. Experts think 40 to 60 percent died, though the rate differed by place. Spain and southern France may have lost 75 to 80 percent, while Germany and England lost closer to 20 percent. Cities suffered most: nearly 60 percent of Londoners died, and half of Paris. After 1351 it eased, maybe from immunity or a milder form, and no later outbreak matched it for five hundred years. Too few artisans, laborers, and peasants brought a work crisis, empty fields, dead livestock, and starvation. Workers could demand better pay, and some peasants moved for better chances.

6Survivors also questioned religion, the church, and the lords and kings who could not protect them. Farming improved, with better methods and tools, so fewer hands could grow more food. The heavy plow let horses, not oxen, till fields faster and helped end food shortages. Communities improved sanitation and found better ways to limit and treat disease. A disaster on the scale of the Black Death is almost impossible now, though plague bacteria still live in some rodent fleas. Antibiotics stop most cases, and modern sanitation helps control disease. Scottish biologist Alexander Fleming discovered penicillin in 1928. He won the Nobel Prize in Medicine in 1945 for that find.

7A chart of Europe’s population, in millions, runs from the year 1000 to 1700. It marks the start of the Black Death and the later recovery. The data come from William Langer, The Black Death, 1964. It took two hundred years for Europe’s population to bounce back. In the mid-1300s, horror made many people think the world was ending. By death rates, it remains the most destructive event in European history. Some historians link that ruin to the Renaissance, from the late 1300s to the 1600s. The name means rebirth in French, and a rebirth in medicine, science, art, and philosophy was what Europe needed.

71. The U.S. Constitution

1In the summer of 1787, delegates met in Philadelphia. They came to write a constitution for the United States. George Washington was a leader there. Benjamin Franklin was a leader there. James Madison was a leader there too. Madison was the main author. Thomas Jefferson was away. John Adams was away. They were ambassadors in Europe. Their document has lasted for centuries. It is the oldest written constitution still in use. It is the highest law in the land. It created a republic. Powers are separate and balanced. Majority rule is protected. Minority rights are protected too. Franklin said they had made a republic, if they could keep it. The new nation might be fragile.

2After the thirteen states won independence from Great Britain in 1783, they needed a new form of government. The Articles of Confederation had been used since 1781. They gave most power to the states. Congress had little authority. Congress could not force the states to provide taxes. It could not force them to provide troops. It could not do much that the states opposed. By 1786 that weakness called for a stronger central government. So the Constitutional Convention met in 1787. The United States is a democracy, but not a pure one. In a pure democracy, citizens themselves vote on proposed laws. This nation is a republic. Citizens elect senators and representatives. Those officials pass bills that become the nation's laws. With Washington leading, they created three branches. Congress makes laws. The presidency carries them out. Courts decide their use. Eleven states approved the Constitution by early 1789. Washington was elected that February. North Carolina and Rhode Island approved by May 1790.

3We the people are the first three words. They stand for the central idea of democracy. The opening lists the reasons for the plan. Those reasons are a stronger union and justice. They include peace at home. They include a common defense. They include the general good. They include liberty for people who come later. The Constitution has seven articles. The first three explain how the three branches work. Article I divides Congress into the House and the Senate. House members are elected every two years. Senators serve six-year terms. Congress may collect taxes. Congress may provide for defense. Congress may declare war. The president is commander in chief. Washington was the first president. Jefferson was the third. A president must be a natural-born citizen. A president must be at least thirty-five years old. Electors chosen by state legislatures elect the president. This system was later called the Electoral College. It is still combined with a popular vote.

4The founders created the Electoral College for a clear reason. They thought most citizens would know little about people running for president. Before planes, trains, and cars, candidates could not travel easily. Newspapers were the main source of news. Some founders also feared direct votes by the people. They thought such votes were the same as rule by an angry crowd. So electors in each state would choose the president and the vice president. Those electors were thought to know more. Article III sets up lower federal courts. It also sets up a Supreme Court of nine justices. That court is the final decider in federal cases. Other articles cover relations among the states. They cover amendments. They cover debts and treaties. They cover how to approve the Constitution. States feared too much federal power. So the Constitution divides power. Checks and balances limit each branch. It requires a fair legal process. It requires a fair jury. It forbids bail that is far too high. It forbids cruel and unusual punishment. It protects free speech.

5The power to amend the Constitution makes it a living document. It can change with society's needs. It was amended before every state had approved it. Some states wanted stronger protection of rights. James Madison wrote proposed amendments. The First Congress approved twelve in late 1789. It sent them to the states. Ten were approved. They became the Bill of Rights. The First Amendment is the most important addition. It protects speech. It protects religion. It protects the press. It protects the right to assemble in peace. Free speech is not absolute. Threats of harm are not protected. Lies under oath in court are not protected. People may follow any religion or none. Government and religion stay separate. The press may criticize officials, even the president. Citizens may meet in peace to discuss concerns. Thousands did so in Washington, D.C., to hear Martin Luther King Jr. in 1963.

6The Fourth Amendment bars unfair searches of a home. The Fifth Amendment protects people accused of crimes. The Sixth Amendment does this too. The Tenth Amendment saves unnamed powers for the states. That is why education laws differ from state to state. State tax laws differ too. Seventeen more amendments were added over the years. They ended slavery. They gave the vote to African Americans and to women. They created a federal income tax. Only one amendment has ever been undone. In 1933 the Twenty-First Amendment undid the Eighteenth. The Eighteenth was adopted in 1919. It banned making, selling, or transporting alcoholic drinks. That ban began Prohibition. The Constitution has served as a model abroad. But it kept slavery until an amendment after the Civil War ended it. Critics say two Senate votes per state give less populous states a voice beyond their number of citizens. Winning takes a majority of electoral votes. A candidate can win the popular vote and lose. This has happened five times, including 2000 and 2016.

7Women marched in the streets of New York in 1915. They marched for the right to vote. The Nineteenth Amendment was approved in 1920. The writers did the best they could at the time. Even with faults, their document has lasted well over two hundred years. The Constitution still defines how the national government is organized. It still defines how that government works. When it falls short of a modern society's needs, amendments offer a way to update it. This living document will hopefully guide the United States for centuries to come.

72. The U.S. Government At Work

1The United States Bullion Depository is at Fort Knox, Kentucky. The founding fathers drafted the Declaration of Independence. A new house needs experts such as carpenters, roofers, plumbers, and electricians. Those workers set up systems that the home can rely on. The new United States was home to about 2.5 million people. The country needed reliable systems that worked well for its citizens. The founders wanted experts to look after the nation’s many systems. Those experts would help the president run the country.

2In 1788, American leaders ratified the Constitution of the United States. Its principles and rules still guide how the country is governed. The Constitution divides the government into three branches. The legislative branch, the Senate and the House of Representatives, makes laws and represents constituents. The judicial branch oversees courts and decides how laws can and should be used. The executive branch, led by the president, carries out laws and manages most operations. The founders did not want a monarchy, and Washington said one man could not do all the great business of the State. The Constitution allowed expert secretaries to form the president’s cabinet and help the country’s systems work.

3By 1788, Congress had created Foreign Affairs, War, and the Treasury. The Office of Attorney General began in 1789, but it was not a department then. Washington chose qualified men from both parties and different regions to unify the nation, and the Senate approved them. The first cabinet included Knox for War, Hamilton for Treasury, Jefferson for State, and Randolph as Attorney General. The first cabinet meeting took place in February 1793. Jefferson believed in states’ rights and often disagreed with Hamilton, who wanted a strong federal government. Jefferson resigned in December 1793, and later presidents mostly chose members who supported them. They answer only to the president, Congress cannot fire them, and they advise but do not share authority.

4As the nation grew, cabinets grew and more experts were appointed. Foreign Affairs became the Department of State, focused on diplomacy by ambassadors and diplomats. Over fifty bureaus deal with passports, terrorism prevention, food security, and cultural affairs. Main offices are in Washington, D.C., with over 300 embassies in more than 190 countries. In 1947, Congress unified the Army, Navy, Marines, and Air Force into Defense at the Pentagon. The president is commander in chief, but this largest department manages the military, with over 1.3 million service personnel and 742,000 civilian employees. Treasury in Washington, D.C., still does most of its 1789 duties, including banks, taxes, and currency. The Postal Service left Treasury in 1829, and business matters later went to the Department of Commerce.

5In 1870, the Attorney General’s office became the Department of Justice. It handles legal matters for the executive branch and enforces federal laws through the FBI, U.S. marshals, and U.S. attorneys. It also investigates civil rights violations and immigration concerns, manages federal prisons, and includes the DEA. Justice enforces the law like a local police force, but it is not part of the judicial branch. Federal courts decide whether laws are constitutional and whether Justice enforces them correctly. The Department of Homeland Security was established in 2002. This third-largest department has almost a quarter of a million workers who safeguard citizens inside U.S. borders. Its Coast Guard, Secret Service, TSA, and ICE guard against cyberattacks, smuggling or invasion by sea, airplane sabotage, and natural disasters. The Coast Guard is an Armed Forces branch that controls domestic and international waters. It was never in Defense, and it moved from Treasury to Transportation in 1967.

6The Department of the Interior was established in 1849 and is often called the nation’s housekeeper. Its U.S. Geological Survey was established in 1879 and has mapped the country’s lands since then. Its main duty is the conservation and management of natural resources. Fish and Wildlife, the Bureau of Indian Affairs, and the National Park Service are part of it, and it manages other public lands. As of 2018, ten other cabinet departments also help run the government, including Energy, Transportation, Agriculture, and Labor. Secretaries consult the president to share what is happening and to get input on policies. People worry about poor awareness, wasted taxpayer money, and officials who assume powers beyond the Constitution. In 2018, the Supreme Court urged reform of the expanded cabinet and told officials not to overstep the Constitution.

7The cabinet also sets who takes office if something happens to the president. As of 2018, the vice president comes first in the immediate order of succession. The order also includes the Speaker of the House and the president pro tempore of the Senate. After the president pro tempore of the Senate, the successors are the department heads. The cabinet grew from the original four departments to over four million workers. That growth helped the United States function as times changed and the country grew. Cabinet departments contain many offices, agencies, or bureaus. The cabinet has changed with the times, but its important role has not changed. Experts in the departments try to do great good as servants of the president and the citizens.

73. Three Cities and the Fires That Remade Them

1Fire can serve people, and fire can harm them. This book tells that double story. One great fire happened in Rome. Another is the Great Fire of London. A third is the Chicago Fire. The close looks at what later cities learned. Safety rules come after that, along with meanings of hard words. People learned to use fire thousands of years ago. Warmth was one use. Cooking food was another. Since those early days, fire has sometimes slipped free of human hands. Homes have been torn apart by it. Cities have been torn apart by it. People who start fires on purpose are called arsonists. Hundreds of deaths each year come from their fires. Property damage from those fires reaches millions of dollars each year. Fire has also been a weapon. Parts of Jerusalem were burned during the Crusades. German cities were hit with firebombs during World War II. Even so, accidents by people start most city fires, whether the fires are large or small. Unsafe conditions start many of them too.

2Ways to fight fire were worked out by the Egyptians. They did that work only after a destructive fire had already struck. Bad luck, sadly, often teaches the clearest lesson. People who lived through city fires saw the need for fire safety only once the flames were gone. Fire prevention and firefighting became clear needs only then. After a great fire in ancient Egypt, a water pump was invented there. Bucket brigades were organized too. Volunteers in a bucket brigade passed water buckets along a line of people toward the fire. Rome, London, and Chicago each suffered a terrible fire. The destruction was huge. New ways to stop fires, and to fight them, came out of those same fires. In the year 64, Rome had the good sides of a large city. It had the not-so-good sides too. Libraries, art galleries, and markets were open to visitors. Chariot races could be watched at the Circus Maximus. Rome's stadium could seat more people than most football stadiums of modern times. Indoor plumbing belonged to the rich. Their homes were comfortable and full of space. Most citizens of Rome did not live like that. The city's people numbered more than a million. Cramped, stuffy apartment buildings held most of them. Some buildings rose as high as six stories. The buildings were poorly made. Wood made them easy to burn. A bathroom on the first floor was shared by everyone in the building. Open flames were used for cooking. Open flames were used for light. The summer of the year 64 brought horrible heat to Rome. The city was dry. Fires were a common event. July 19 was the day a fire began. It began in a shop near the Circus Maximus. Warm breezes helped the flames travel. The marketplace burned. Parts of the stadium burned. Nearby homes burned. Roman firemen fought with hand pumps and leather hoses. Their best effort was not enough. The fire moved too fast. Crowds in a panic blocked them. Unruly mobs blocked them too. Emperor Nero was not in the city when the fire began. Heat had sent him to a home on the coast.

3A picture shows citizens of Rome in a panic while homes and temples burn. Nero returned so he could direct the fight. Bucket brigades got to work. People cleared firebreaks. A firebreak is ground in the fire's path with everything burnable taken away. Across nine days the fire died down and flared up several times. Firebreaks, together with plain good luck, finally ended it. Homes were gone for thousands of people. Ways of earning a living were gone too. The number of dead is not known for sure. Nero set up help for people without homes. Temporary shelters went into his own gardens. Public buildings and parks were used as well. Extra food came in from outside Rome. Merchants were ordered by Nero to drop the price of grain. Many Romans still thought Nero himself had lit the fire. They believed he wanted a new city built to honor himself. To shift the blame, he steered people toward a story that Christians had set the blaze. That story became an excuse to persecute the Christians. To persecute is to mistreat. Rome did take lessons from the fire. A large water supply would always be kept close by. New buildings would be stone, not wood. Burned debris was even used to fill marshy ground. A new city stood where the old one had been. Streets were wider. Homes were safer. Reading and writing in Latin and in Greek were taught in Roman schools. Students learned a Latin word, flamma. Flamma is one of many Latin words that mean fire. English words that grow from flamma include flame and flammable.

4Sixteen hundred years went by. In other parts of the world, daily life had not improved very much. Fire prevention had not improved very much either. London in 1666 dumped its garbage in the streets. Bathing was thought unimportant. Rats that carried bubonic plague filled most buildings. Flea bites from those rats spread the disease. In the years before the fire, the plague had already killed thousands. Wooden buildings with thatched roofs stood packed together. Upper stories jutted over the narrow streets. They nearly met in the middle. Life in London could still feel exciting. A rider in the street might be the king, Charles II. A good play might be seen. A coffeehouse might offer rest among local celebrities. A few pennies could buy a fresh, warm roll. The shop belonged to Thomas Farynor, baker to the king, on Pudding Lane. Evening on September 1, 1666, was when Thomas Farynor finished his work. He believed the cook fires were out. They were not. Around one in the morning, a spark left an oven and landed on a pile of wood. The pile caught fire. Thomas woke to someone crying Fire. His family and his servants tried to get out. Smoke rose up the stairs in clouds. They left through an attic window. They jumped to the roof of the next house. Neighbors took buckets from a building close by. They tried to kill the flames. Control was soon lost. People grabbed what they could carry. They led their families to safer ground. Stopping the fire, or at least slowing it, was still possible. A fire hook is a long pole. It could yank building material out of the fire's path and open a firebreak. Mayor Thomas Bludworth did not see the fire as serious enough for so drastic a step. The flames kept moving. Samuel Pepys was a Londoner. He lived through the plague and through the Great Fire. People remember him among the greatest keepers of diaries in the world. His diaries tell a great deal about England in the 17th century.

5A picture shows people of London rushing away from the fire. Flames roared, and wind roared with them. People fleeing must have been terrified. Warehouses full of rope, paper, and other burnable goods were reached by the fire. A maid warned a government official named Samuel Pepys. She told him the fire was spreading. Alarmed, Pepys hurried to the king. Soldiers and militiamen were sent by King Charles to fight the flames. He set up fire posts stocked with food, water, and equipment. Trained militiamen gathered at the posts to help. Charles placed the Duke of York over the firefighting. The duke's men pulled down houses that stood in the fire's path. That work made firebreaks. Gunpowder was used to blow up large buildings. At last the fire burned itself out and stopped. London lost many buildings from medieval times. Records list only nine deaths in the fire. One of those dead was a maid who worked for Thomas Farynor. The true count is probably much higher. Many people in London had no home left. They had no job left. Eighty percent of the city was destroyed. The loss included 87 churches. It included more than 13,000 homes. Where fleeing people gathered, tent cities rose. Organized charity such as the Red Cross did not exist yet. Help came from family, from neighbors, from churches, and from employers.

6King Charles gave the rebuilding job to two architects. They were Christopher Wren and John Evelyn. Wood was replaced with brick. Streets were made wider. More space was left between houses. Years passed before the work was finished. St. Paul's Cathedral was still unfinished in the years right after the fire. It was completed in 1712. That was 46 years after the fire. New laws told businesses and owners of property to keep garbage off streets and walkways. Improving how 100,000 Londoners lived cost a terrible price. That price was the fire. The Thames River runs through London. It was one of the few places that stayed safe. Before its own great fire, Chicago was huge and booming. In the late 1800s the city really bustled. Livestock was slaughtered at its stockyards and turned into meat. Much of the nation's food came from those yards. Travelers crossing the country often stopped to visit. Fine restaurants and hotels were a point of pride. So were entertainment, big stores, and museums. Families could use a beach along Lake Michigan. They could watch cattle arrive from the West at the stockyards.

7Chicago, like Rome and like London, had slums. Those neighborhoods held poorly built wooden houses set close together. Inspection of housing was lax. Warehouses did not have to follow fire codes. They held all sorts of materials that could burn. Chicago did have a professional fire department. The department was badly overworked. Few people could recall a summer as dry as 1871. From the Fourth of July to early October, the rain that fell measured about 3.8 centimeters, or 1.5 inches. Dying trees dropped dead leaves. Small fires kept springing up, and firefighters grew exhausted putting them out. Evening on October 8, 1871, brought a spark into the hay of a barn. The barn belonged to Catherine O'Leary. It stood on DeKoven Street. Mrs. O'Leary earned a modest living by selling milk. A later story said a cow kicked over a lamp and started the blaze. That story was never proved. A quick end to the fire should have been possible. That night the firefighters went to an address that was wrong. Other buildings were soon burning. Winds that earned Chicago the name Windy City carried the fire onward. No one stopped it in time. The Chicago River itself caught fire. An oily film lay on the water. Then flames took the gasworks. That plant pumped gas used for heat and for light. Hotel guests fled. Parents gathered their children. Many headed for Lake Michigan, hoping to get away. Fleeing crowds packed the bridges. Hot cinders and sparks dropped from the sky. The fire's horrible noise mixed with church bells. Bells warned other parts of town. Smoke grew so thick that even one block ahead was invisible. People in a panic blocked many of the city's bridges.

8Wind joined with fire and made a firestorm. A firestorm is super-heated wind. Wherever that wind blows, flame spreads. Burning chunks were lifted and thrown onto buildings. Those buildings held oil, fabric, and kerosene. Each of those would feed the fire. Brave people stayed to fight the flames and to save the helpless. Some were professional firefighters. Some were ordinary citizens. Even the station that pumped water was destroyed. The fire department's work was blocked by that loss. Hotels of luxury fell into the flames. Plain houses fell too. Three days passed while the fire burned. Buildings not yet burning were blown up so firebreaks could be made. Rain came on October 10. Firefighters used that rain to put the flames out. The fire ended at last. Relief came first. Then the loss became clear. About 300 people were dead. Others were still missing. Homes, possessions, and workplaces were gone. Dust and ash still hung in the air. Some parts of the city were still too hot to enter. Federal troops came to stop looting. General Philip Sheridan was in command. Supplies and volunteers were sent to Chicago. Survivors who still had enough gathered food and clothing for people without homes. Shelters were opened by the Chicago Relief and Aid Society. Later the society gave materials so small temporary houses could be built. Owners of businesses set up tables or carts and went back to work. Some people left the city as soon as they could. Most remained and rebuilt. Rebuilding moved rather slowly. Changes that might prevent another fire met resistance. Bit by bit, people saw the need for stricter rules on buildings. They saw the need for stricter rules on preventing fire. Safer housing went up. Officials made the fire department better. The lesson was hard, and it was learned again. A view from above shows how large the Chicago fire was.

9Pictures of Chicago in ruins reached the front pages of news across the country. Peshtigo, Wisconsin, burned on that same night. Its fire was even more destructive than Chicago's. That summer, Peshtigo and the towns around it had the same drought Chicago had. Forests had been cleared by farmers and by railroad workers. Piles of dry wood and dry leaves were simply left on the ground. On the night of October 8, a huge firestorm swallowed the whole region. The ground shook under the townspeople. Tornado-force winds pushed a wall of flame at the town. Less than one hour later, the whole town was gone. Peshtigo had 1,700 citizens. Eight hundred of them died. Deaths in the forest fire are estimated at 1,200. The fire jumped Green Bay. Parts of Michigan burned as well. Among natural disasters in U.S. history, this fire is one of the deadliest. Survivors carried terrible scars on their bodies. They carried terrible scars in their minds. The Peshtigo fire is almost forgotten. It fell on the same night as the Chicago fire, which is more famous. Chicago's fire killed fewer people. Even a very small town today has a fire department with good equipment. Good fortune puts us in an age that treats fire safety as very important. Strict fire codes cover every area of the country. Professional fire departments train to prevent fires, to fight them, and to save lives. Volunteer departments train in those same skills. Fire alarms and sprinkler systems are built into buildings. Firewalls stand between houses. Schools and the media teach people how to keep fires from starting. They teach how to plan for an emergency. Terrible fires still happen, even with all this work. Modern prevention and modern firefighting give us a hope. We hope never again to see fires like those of Rome, London, and Chicago.

10Reports of fires in the United States reach almost two million every year. A fire can kill, so these safety rules should always be followed. Do not play with matches or lighters. Do not play with materials that burn easily, such as gasoline. Leave cooking and the stove alone until you are allowed, and until an adult stays to watch. Keep blankets, clothes, and every other object off lamps, radiators, and heaters. A covering like that could catch fire fast. Your home should have at least one smoke detector on each floor. Detectors near bedrooms matter most of all. They can wake you if a fire starts. Check smoke alarms every six months. Change the batteries at least once each year. Keep a household fire extinguisher near any heat or any flame in or near your home. Your family needs a plan for getting out of a fire. Walk through the home and learn at least two exits from every room. An exit may be a door. It may be a window on the first floor. It may be a window that has a safe escape ladder. Pick a meeting place that is easy to find. It must sit a safe distance from the building. Name one person to count family members so you know all are safe. Name a different family member to reach a neighbor's house and call 911. Practice the escape plan together. Knowing the steps saves time when an emergency comes. It may save your life.

11If fire starts in your home, these rules help your family get out fast and stay safe. Leave as fast as you can. Go to the meeting place you chose. Leave your belongings behind, even when they are very valuable. Objects can be replaced. You cannot be replaced. As you leave, try to warn the rest of the family. Yell the word Fire. Bang on doors you pass. Poisonous gases are in smoke. Smoke can be hot enough to scorch lungs. Smoke gathers near the ceiling, so keep low. Stay in a crouch, and crawl when you must. A damp towel or cloth held at your mouth can work as a mask. Do not return to a burning building, whatever the reason. If your clothes catch fire, stop at once, drop, and roll. If another person's clothes catch fire, that person should stop, drop, and roll right away. A towel, a blanket, a rug, or a cloth can cover the person and help kill the flames. A bucket brigade is a line of people passing buckets, one person to the next, from water to a fire. Militiamen are volunteers who carry out police duties. To persecute is to mistreat one specific group. Firebreaks are areas cleared of fuel and of anything that can burn. Refugees are people who flee trouble and have nowhere to go. A fire hook is a long, heavy hook that pulls structures down to open a firebreak. Slums are poor, crowded parts of a city. Stockyards are places where livestock is killed and made into meat. A firestorm is super-heated wind that carries fire across a wide area. Thatched roofs are roofs built of straw. A gasworks is a factory that processes fuel and pumps it. Unruly means hard to control, or disobedient. Lax means relaxed, or not strict. Looting means robbing on a wide scale.

74. Tokyo Imperial Palace

1Tokyo is a sea of tall buildings and bright lights. In the center of the city is a green oasis. It has neat gardens and wooden buildings. Stone walls surround the Tokyo Imperial Palace. A fortress on the palace grounds looks very different from Tokyo’s modern skyscrapers. The palace grounds are one of the most valuable pieces of land on Earth. The palace is the home of the emperor and empress of Japan. They belong to the world’s oldest monarchy.

2Tokyo lies on the Pacific coast of Honshu, Japan’s main island. The Imperial Palace is in the heart of Tokyo, near an inlet of Tokyo Bay. It is near the National Theatre, Tokyo Disneyland, and the National Diet Building. Tokyo is home to thirty-five million people. It is the largest metropolitan area in the world. Parliament makes the laws, and the emperor has a symbolic role in this constitutional monarchy. The current palace stands on the site of Edo Castle, once Japan’s largest castle, and is home to the imperial family. For hundreds of years, shoguns held most power while the emperor held very little. A 1657 fire destroyed the main tower, the shogun did not rebuild it, and the base still stands.

3The reign of Tokugawa Ieyasu and his heirs lasted more than 260 years, called the Edo period. In 1868 a revolution gave power back to the emperor and ended the shoguns. Emperor Meiji moved the imperial home from Kyoto, where it had been for almost one thousand years, to Tokyo. Workers faced earthquakes, money problems, and fires, and finished the new palace in 1888. The outside looked traditional, but the inside had Western chairs, curtains, floors, fireplaces, and chandeliers. Japan had stayed fairly closed to the West, so these changes were new. Older rooms used low tables, zaisu chairs, paper screens, and tatami straw mats. In April 1942 the United States firebombed Tokyo but not the palace itself, yet nearby fires badly damaged it.

4After World War II, Japan rebuilt the palace and finished in 1968. Workers used modern methods but kept the old look. Some buildings are only a few decades old, while the Edo tower base, wall sections, guard houses, and bridges are centuries old. The Imperial Household Agency building went up on the grounds in 1935. The royal family lived there for a time after the war while the new palace was built. Edo workers built the stone walls, including the Otemon Gate, by the kirikomihagi method. They shaped the earth like stairs, laid small rocks for drainage, and stacked stones with no mortar. A slight slope made the walls harder for enemies to climb but safer in earthquakes.

5Traditional buildings were wood, like the Suwano-chaya Tea House on the grounds. Emperor Meiji thought about stone, but an earthquake cracked a stone dining hall, so that plan was dropped. He built his palace of wood because wood was safer where earthquakes are common. After the war, workers used steel-reinforced concrete, but the palace still has a sloping roof with long eaves. The grounds cover about 115 hectares, or 284 acres, and moats and defensive stone walls surround them. Some walls date from the Edo period, and several museums stand on the grounds. These include the Science Museum, the Tokyo National Museum of Modern Art, and the Museum of Imperial Collections. The site also has Kitanomaru Park, the East Gardens, the Imperial Palace, the Outer Garden, and Nijubashi Bridge.

6The grounds have four parts: the Outer Garden, the inner grounds, Kitanomaru Park, and the East Gardens. The Outer Garden is a public park with grass, trees, and new scenery each season. Nijubashi Bridge links it to the inner grounds, and tourists love the view over the moat with a guard tower. The bridge opens for special guests, and for the public on January 2 and on the emperor’s birthday. The palace has 24,175 square meters of space, about four and a half soccer fields, on two stories plus a lower level. Rooms for state business, audiences, dining, banquets, and rest surround an open courtyard. The emperor appoints prime ministers, justices, and ambassadors, and he hosts foreign guests at state dinners. In January 2017, Emperor Akihito and Empress Michiko greeted New Year visitors from a palace balcony.

7Near the palace, the Imperial Household Agency plans the family’s events and cares for the grounds. Since 1871, empresses have raised silkworms at the Imperial Cocoonery, and the silk repairs old kimonos. Empress Michiko has attached silkworm eggs to an oak twig there. The emperor and empress live in this area, near private Shinto shrines. Kitanomaru Park, a public park since 1969, is lovely in spring when cherry trees bloom. It holds the Science Museum and the National Museum of Modern Art, beside older Edo sites. The free East Gardens have ponds, lawns, plants, Japan’s last Edo garden, and old guard houses, walls, and gates. A seventeenth-century scroll shows Edo Castle, whose stone base still stands, and the Museum of Imperial Collections shows art from as far back as the fourth century.

8The palace is important in modern Japan, where the emperor receives foreign officials and awards honors. Tourists, residents, and joggers use the grounds, which offer a quiet place in a fast city. Emperor Hirohito, who reigned from 1926 to 1989, loved nature and did not allow pesticides so insects would come. The grounds hold more than 3,600 animal and insect species, 1,366 plant species, and 20 percent of Tokyo’s trees. In spring, over 260 cherry trees bloom over the Chidori-ga-fuchi moat in the northwest. This is Tokyo’s most popular place to see cherry blossoms, and over a million people come from late March to early April. Visitors walk under the flowers or ride in rowboats, while about one thousand agency workers care for the site. In 2016 the agency spent about 5.5 billion yen, or 48 million U.S. dollars, and it spends billions of yen each year.

9The Imperial Palace is in the center of Tokyo. The city grew outward from the palace. In busy Tokyo, the grounds seem untouched by time. Lush grounds hold thick forests, calm gardens, and graceful wooden buildings that comfort weary people. Shoguns and emperors have lived here for centuries. The grounds are a cherished part of Japanese history and culture. They will be a beloved part of Tokyo life for generations to come.

75. Two Ways People Get Their Energy

1This book starts with different styles, then defines the terms. It looks at strengths and challenges for introverts and for extroverts. It clears up myths, tells how to honor a temperament, and ends by valuing diversity. A word list comes last. Angela and Lisa are sisters. A picture shows that they differ in some ways. Two years separate their ages. Angela likes to stay home. She reads, or she does art projects in her room. She also likes writing stories. She likes playing with her pet hamster. Outside, she likes being in nature. She likes watching plants and wildlife with care. Lisa would rather be with friends than do anything else. She likes games with them. Parties for birthdays appeal to her. So do other gatherings with people. Her pet rabbit is a frequent playmate when she is home. Friends receive jewelry from her, including bracelets that stand for friendship. She talks a lot. She keeps asking Angela to join the play. Angela would rather be alone. In some ways the sisters are alike. Both are creative in art. Both love animals. They differ in a basic way. Angela is an introvert. Lisa is an extrovert.

2Introvert and extrovert are terms for different styles. The styles cover how a person feels energized, how a person deals with other people, and how a person handles information. The biggest difference is about social time. That time tends to tire introverts. It tends to energize extroverts. A chart lists more traits. These are broad patterns. They may not fit every person. Most people mix the two types. The types form a continuum. Many people are ambiverts. An ambivert has clear qualities of both types. The situation decides which side of an ambivert's temperament shows up. The line runs from introvert to ambivert to extrovert. Introverts get energy from time alone. Extroverts get energy from time with other people. Introverts tend to notice their inner world more. That world is the mind and the feelings. Extroverts tend to notice the outer world more. That world is people and activities. One-to-one talk is what introverts prefer. Large groups can feel uncomfortable to them. Extroverts prefer talk in groups. Large groups feel comfortable to them. Introverts have fewer friendships, and those friendships are closer. Extroverts have more friends. Casual ties are part of that larger friend group. Introverts often listen more. Extroverts often talk more. Introverts do not like the attention on them. Extroverts like attention. Introverts are comfortable when they focus on several things at once. That skill is called multitasking. One task at a time is the focus extroverts prefer. Introverts prefer to watch a new task before they do it. Extroverts learn best by doing. Introverts take time when they decide. Extroverts often decide quickly. Distractions disrupt introverts. Distractions do not bother extroverts. Introverts share opinions when someone asks. Extroverts offer opinions freely. Brain structure may differ between the two types. Scientists are looking into that possibility. They are also studying differences in brain chemistry. The evidence points to a trait people have when they are born. It is not learned. Temperament is a person's basic nature. Personality is different. Experiences shape personality. Education can influence personality. Upbringing can too. So can many different events. None of those change temperament.

3The words introvert and extrovert come from Carl Jung. Say his name YOONG. He lived from 1875 to 1961. He was a Swiss psychologist. He built a theory of psychological types. People still use that theory widely. The theory has four pairs of contrasting qualities. The introvert-extrovert pair is seen as the most important. Surveys have taken in those qualities. One survey is the Myers-Briggs Type Indicator, or MBTI. It names sixteen different combinations of qualities. Many people find these profiles useful for understanding themselves. Jung's list pairs the qualities this way. Extroverted, marked E, means a stronger focus on the outer world. Introverted, marked I, means a stronger focus on the inner world. That pair is about preferred focus. Intuitive, marked N, means a stronger focus on impressions and meanings. Sensing, marked S, means a stronger focus on physical reality. That pair is about how a person receives information. The letter F stands for Feeling. It means paying more attention to people's cares and to how a decision changes their lives. Thinking, marked T, means a stronger focus on logic and on pros and cons. That pair is about making decisions. Judging, marked J, means a structured and orderly way of living, with a preference for certainty. Perceiving, marked P, means a flexible way of living, with more comfort around uncertainty. That pair is about how a person lives life. One style is not above the other. Both styles are equally healthy. Both are useful in certain circumstances. A culture may still prize one style more than the other. Take the United States as an example. People there place a higher value on extroversion. People often treat it as normal. Some Americans treat introverts as if something is missing, or as if they are lesser. This culture often pushes introverts to copy extrovert behavior. That means more outgoing, more assertive, and more interested in group activities. Japan is one country where introversion gets more acceptance. It is even encouraged. Both types have strengths. Both have challenges. The descriptions that follow are broad. They do not fit every person. Among Americans, introverts are as few as one-quarter of the people. They are as many as one-half. Extroverts are as few as one-half. They are as many as three-quarters. Introverts tend to focus inward. Extroverts tend to focus outward.

4Introverts tend to be independent. They tend to be self-motivated. They work well alone. They solve problems in creative ways. They think things through with care. They are often well prepared. They pay attention to detail. Some situations and settings can overwhelm them. They do not like interruptions. Background noise often distracts them. A sudden demand for an answer is something they dislike. Speaking up in class may be hard. Speaking up at work may be hard too. Solitude is a need that friends and family may miss. A first meeting often goes well for an extrovert. They make friends easily. Links with other people come easily to them. They work well in groups. Working alone may be hard for them. They may get bored easily. Boredom may make them very uncomfortable. Too many words is another risk. They may not notice that other people need space or quiet. Trouble can come when parents, or other relatives, stand on the opposite end of the spectrum. It helps to talk about differences in an open and respectful way. It helps to find a balance that meets everyone's needs. Extroverts have an advantage in many modern classrooms of this country. Desks may sit in pairs or in small groups. Group work is emphasized. Students may not get room or minutes for work they do alone. Public speaking may be required. All of that can challenge introverted students. Some schools have changed classrooms to meet these issues. One solution is a quiet zone. Another is some quiet time each day for students. Another is a quiet choice instead of the usual recess. Teachers can also urge students to learn independent work. They can urge work in pairs as well as work in groups. Certain jobs gain a great deal from either type. Writing, art, and science are careers that often attract introverts. Those jobs often require working alone. They often require deep work on one task at a time. Teaching often attracts extroverts. So do sales and politics. Those jobs move fast. Those jobs call for quick decisions. They call for frequent contact with other people.

5Both types are misunderstood at times. Wrong ideas about them are widespread. A common myth says introverts are shy. Introversion and shyness can overlap. They are still distinct. They do not always go together. A shy person feels nervous, or ill at ease, when people are around. Shy people often want very much to socialize. They avoid it because they fear rejection. Fearing social time is not the same as caring little about it. Little interest is often true of introverts. Shy people cannot turn shyness off whenever they wish. When they choose to, introverts can often behave in an extrovert way. Another wrong idea says introverts must be antisocial or insecure. The idea comes from the fact that they join fewer social events. They are neither. They get overstimulated easily. They seek solitude so they can take care of themselves. A picture shows introversion on top and shyness below. They are very different. A common wrong idea about extroverts says life is easy for them. It says they are happier than introverts. In a social setting, an extrovert often seems more at ease than an introvert. They still have challenges, just as everyone else does. An extrovert may feel as uncomfortable as an introvert in some situations. The extrovert may hide it better. A further myth calls extroverts pushy people who listen poorly. The truth is that connection with other people delights extroverts. They can often befriend people in very effective ways. They draw people into conversation. Introverts can feel flooded by that lively outreach. A third myth says extroverts do not like to be alone. Often they do like it. They just need shorter periods to recharge. The alone time they need may differ in kind from an introvert's. Friendship between the two types is possible. They often enjoy similar things. They do those things in different ways.

6Are you an introvert, an extrovert, or an ambivert? If you are not sure, look back at the chart. Does one column fit you far more than the other column? Maybe your fit is midway. Knowing where you sit on that three-part spectrum is useful. Knowing yourself will help you feel more confident about who you are. It will help you understand your moods and your preferences. Choices that feed you become easier with that knowledge. It can also help you talk with others so they can respect your needs better. An introvert's wish to be alone is less likely to be heard as an insult once others understand. An extrovert's wish for company is easier for others to accept, even when they cannot come along. Tips for introverts follow. Being an introvert is fine, even where the culture does not fully value it. Give yourself regular time alone so you can recharge. Tell friends, family, and teachers why you need time alone. Pick hobbies that leave room to think, to reflect, and to daydream. New people and new activities may require extra time before you feel at ease. Take that time. Do not worry if you do not have a lot of friends. A few good ones matter more. Tips for extroverts follow. Pick hobbies that put you with other people. Plan pleasant solo activities for times when no companion is free. High activity may leave you needing a wind-down. A wind-down need not be silent or motionless. Find what works best for you. If you are an ambivert, call on your introvert qualities and your extrovert qualities as you need them. Enjoy having both.

7Angela and Lisa have both chosen work that honors their temperaments. Many people also succeed in jobs that are not a perfect match. Those jobs may take more energy. They may be more stressful. Introverts and extroverts offer different skills and different perspectives. All of those gifts are valuable. Understanding different temperaments matters for good communication and teamwork. That is true in families, in schools, and in workplaces. It fosters acceptance and mutual respect. When a group splits tasks by temperament, it uses different people's strengths well. Angela and Lisa are adults now. Angela is an artist and a research assistant who works from home. Lisa is an event planner and a receptionist in a busy medical office. Knowing their temperaments lets each sister honor her nature and respect their differences. Word list. Anxiety means worry or nervousness, usually about what is unknown or has not happened yet. An assertive person has a bold or confident style. Circumstances are conditions, facts, or events that affect a situation. An impression is an opinion, a belief, or a notion about a person or a thing. Insecure means lacking self-confidence or certainty. People interact when they work as a team or talk and act with one another. Misconceptions are wrong ideas based on inaccurate or incomplete information. Too much activity or excitement can leave a person overstimulated. That feeling is mental or physical anxiety, or a sense of being overcome. Social means having to do with society, community, or a connection between people. Solitude is the experience of being alone. A spectrum is a range. A trait is a feature or a quality of animals, plants, things, or groups.

76. United Arab Emirates

1A family shops in a mall in Dubai. The United Arab Emirates is a Middle Eastern country. The Middle East sits where Asia, Africa, and Europe meet. Most countries there are in Asia. The UAE is entirely in Asia. It covers about 83,600 square kilometers of the Arabian Peninsula and borders Saudi Arabia and Oman. Most of its 1,318 kilometers of coast runs along the Persian Gulf, which people in the region call the Arabian Gulf. The gulf flows out to the Indian Ocean, and a small eastern coast lies on the Gulf of Oman.

2The UAE is a federation of seven states: Abu Dhabi, Dubai, Sharjah, Ajman, Umm al Quwain, Ras al Khaimah, and Al Fujairah. Each state has a capital of the same name, and Abu Dhabi, the federation capital, lies on an island. Abu Dhabi, the second-largest city, dates back to 3,000 BCE and has white-domed mosques and shining skyscrapers. Dubai is the largest city and a center for trade and tourism. It has the world's tallest building, the world's only seven-star hotel, and one of the largest artificial islands. In 1968 the city had thirteen cars, and today planners build double-decker roads for 1.4 million vehicles. Most people are immigrants from Arab countries or South Asia who came for work, and citizens are called Emirati. Emirati men wear a long white garment and a scarf, and outside women wear a dark cloak and cover their heads.

3Islam is the most common religion, and in 2005 most people were Muslims. Students learn Modern Standard Arabic, the official language, and many people also speak English or nearby dialects. Many jobs in oil and construction are held by men, and many foreign workers are young men without families. Men make up two-thirds of the population. A construction worker works on a skyscraper in Abu Dhabi. The coast is hot and humid, with swamps, marshes, shoals, and islands. The Hajar Mountains rise 2,000 meters in the east, but they are only a small part of the land.
