Yearly rain is just 10 to 15 centimeters, and desert heat can reach 49 degrees Celsius while north winds help make huge sand dunes.

4Most of the desert is hard to live in, but oases are fertile places where plants and animals can thrive. Al Ain, the fourth-largest city, lies about 160 kilometers east of Abu Dhabi and has more than 614,000 people. Its oasis is the largest in the UAE, and people harvest date palms, alfalfa, and mangoes there. Desert wildlife includes the Arabian oryx, Gordon's wildcat, the sand fox, and sand gazelles. Overhunting threatened the Arabian oryx, so people took the animals into captivity and bred them. Humans have lived on the peninsula for thousands of years and adopted Islam around 600 CE. In 1820 Britain signed a truce with coastal sheikhs, and the lands became the Trucial States. Abu Dhabi Marine Areas found oil in 1958, then oil was found in Dubai and Sharjah, and the economy grew fast.

5In 1968, money problems led Britain to announce that it would leave the gulf. The Trucial States, Qatar, and Bahrain talked about forming a federation. Qatar and Bahrain became independent, six states formed the UAE in 1971, and Ras al Khaimah joined in 1972. The UAE is a member of the Gulf Cooperation Council with Kuwait, Bahrain, Qatar, Saudi Arabia, and Oman. Members work on security and the economy, and they have strengthened military cooperation. On December 2 the country celebrates independence, and Emirati girls carry the flag in a parade. The Grand Mosque honors Sheikh Zayed bin Sultan Al-Nahyan, who helped build the economy while preserving Islamic culture. Burj Khalifa is the world's tallest building at 830 meters, and Emirates Palace has 348 rooms and 1.3 kilometers of beach.

6The Burj Khalifa took six years to build and opened in 2010. Palm Jumeirah is one of the world's largest human-made islands and looks like a palm tree from above. It was billed as the eighth wonder of the world, with four thousand luxury villas and apartments, and some people live there permanently. Ferrari World in Abu Dhabi covers 86,000 square meters and is the world's first Ferrari-themed park. Visitors there enjoy thrill rides and drive miniature cars. Ski Dubai has slopes, slides, and jumps in a park of machine-made snow. Oil made the economy flourish, immigrants keep coming, and the culture keeps changing while holding its Arabic past. Mosques hundreds of years old stand beside modern skyscrapers, and the future looks bright.

7About 5,927,482 people live in the UAE. The land covers 83,600 square kilometers, or 32,278 square miles. The capital city is Abu Dhabi. The primary language is Arabic. The primary religion is Islam. The currency is the UAE dirham. These facts come from the CIA World Factbook of 2016.

77. Weave It!

1Weaving makes fabric by lacing two sets of threads or fibers together. This art and useful craft goes back tens of thousands of years. People still weave fibers to make clothing, rugs, and much more. Before a new pattern, a weaver needs two sets of threads. The warp is vertical threads held in place, often on a frame, by tension. The weft is one or more horizontal threads. The weaver laces the weft over and under the warp and packs it down so the threads form fabric. This book has two projects, a paper mat and a friendship bracelet, and each one weaves in a different way.

2You need construction paper that is 9 by 12 inches, plus a pencil, a ruler, scissors, and a glue stick. Use one whole piece and cut a second piece into strips that are 1 by 9 inches. Paper weaving can make placemats, book covers, and colorful designs for a bare wall. Choose one piece as the warp, or foundation, and fold it in half. Draw a straight line 1 inch from the long open edge. Draw ten to twelve lines from the fold to that line. Make them straight, curved, or both, but not too close to the edges or too hard to cut. Cut from the fold and stop at the straight line without cutting it, then unfold the paper so the cuts run up and down.

3Take a 1 by 9 inch strip of a different color for the weft. Insert it across, over one warp and under the next, until you reach the far edge. Start on the right if you are right-handed, or on the left if you are left-handed. Slide this weft all the way to the top edge, stopping at the horizontal line. Start the next strip under one if the row before began over one. Keep changing how each row starts, and slide each strip up next to the one before it. Be very gentle with the last strip so you do not tear the paper. Glue the loose weft edges on both sides, and your first weaving is finished.

4Try different thicknesses of warps and wefts to make new patterns. Try wrapping paper or origami paper, but handle thin paper with care so it does not tear. You can cut curved weft strips, but insert them in the same order you cut them so they lock together. With a different color of paper, cut a heart or butterfly from the center and cut the weft and warp to show that shape. Paint or draw a picture on paper and use that paper as the warp. This finger-weaving bracelet uses colored embroidery floss, round toothpicks, masking tape, and beads if you want them. Cut four to six warp threads, each 1 foot long, and one weft thread 4 feet long. Line the ends up and tie an overhand knot 3 inches from the end.

5Tape the knotted tails to the table a few inches from the edge closest to you. Separate the long weft from the warp threads and choose an order for the warps. Smooth the warps flat and tape them in that order near the edge of the table. Weave a toothpick under the knot, over one warp and under the next, to the other side. Leave that toothpick in place and tape both ends to the table. Use another toothpick to lift every other warp, the opposite threads from the first toothpick. Lift it to open a shed, the space between the raised warps and the ones flat on the table. Feed the weft all the way through, pull it across, and pack it tight against the top without pulling the taped warps loose.

6Keep lifting every other warp, feeding the weft through, and packing it tight. You may need to move the weaving and tape it again as it grows. If the weft covers the warp threads, tighten the warps and add more tape at the end closest to you. If you run low on weft, cut another piece and join it with a square knot. Place the knot in the middle of a row so it stays hidden. Stop when the weaving is as long as you want it to be. Remove the bottom tape, tie an overhand knot to hold the last weft row, and cut the tails to 3 inches. Remove the top tape and the toothpick, then finish the fringe with braiding or a knot on each thread, and add beads if you like.

7Use an overhand knot in the tails, not the woven area, to tie on the bracelet or attach the weaving. Try patterns such as alternating warp colors or a stripe in the middle. With six or more warp threads, you can try other patterns. Two of these patterns go over two and under two. Start them with an even number of warp threads, not counting the one that becomes the weft.

8If weaving paper and thread by hand is fun, you might enjoy other kinds of weaving too. Many kinds use a loom, a tool that holds yarn or thread while a weaver works. Looms come in many shapes and sizes, such as hand looms, frame looms, backstrap looms, and floor looms. Small looms can make friendship bracelets instead of using tape. You can also weave a Peruvian friendship bracelet. For centuries, people all over the world have woven rugs, clothing, accessories, and more. Weaving has deep meaningful roots in many cultures, people still learn about it and enjoy it today, and these projects are just a taste. You learned basic principles, used your imagination, tried materials, patterns, and colors, and you can use these ideas in other weaving and have fun!

9Kira Freed wrote Weaving Around the World. Reading A-Z at www.readinga-z.com published it in 2007. Veronica Burningham wrote Weaving Without a Loom, and Search Press in Kent, Great Britain, published it in 1998. Kathleen Monaghan and Hermon Joyner wrote You Can Weave! That book has projects for young weavers, and Davis Publications in Worcester, Mass., published it in 2000. On the Internet, you can find weaving projects that do not need special equipment. Try typing finger weaving or card weaving into your favorite search engine.

78. Weaving Around the World

1Weaving makes clothes, sheets, blankets, towels, and fine art. Woven fibers are strong. Early nets caught more fish than hooks or spears. Woven ropes helped people move big things. Leather strips could become vests, belts, and bags. The warp is held tight on a loom or by a weight. The weft goes over and under the warp. Then the weaver packs it tight.

2Woven items go back to early human history. Each place has its own materials, patterns, and colors. Weavers used local yarn, wool, silk, cotton, plants, and grass. A vertical loom copies an ancient Viking loom, and an 1879 picture shows a weaver in India. People may have learned from bird nests, matted grass, or braided fingers. They made mats from grasses, reeds, and leaves for floors, roofs, walls, or carrying. Skills passed from one generation to the next. Fine string came 20,000 to 30,000 years ago, and simple looms came between 8,000 and 3,000 BCE.

3An antique wooden loom let weft pass over and under warp. Early looms used poles in the ground or clay and stone weights. People made household cloth this way for thousands of years. In China's Shang Period, from 1766 to 1122 BCE, bars and a lever made a shed. A weaver could pass a spool through groups of warp threads. This Chinese design is the basis of modern looms. Later looms made more cloth and finer patterns. From the late 1700s to the early 1800s, weaving became a machine industry, and a loom from about 1890 still makes fabric at a museum.

4Today most textiles come from machines, but many artisans still weave by hand. They keep old tools, skills, and traditions. The Navajo settled in northern Arizona and New Mexico more than 600 years ago. They raised sheep for food, wool, and clothing in a dry land. Europeans later killed and moved them onto reservations in Arizona, Utah, and New Mexico. Weaving began in the 1600s through contact with Pueblo people to the east. Pueblo men wove, but among the Navajo it became women's work. For two centuries they made ponchos and belts, and the Navajo Nation has more than 330,000 members.

5In the late 1800s, new yarns, dyes, and trade changed Navajo weaving. Weavers made wall hangings and rugs more than clothing. Their weaving is still fine art and a source of income. A Navajo woman weaves a rug. The Maya reached a peak around 300 to 900 CE in Guatemala. Their descendants are close to half the people there, and many live in mountain villages. They have used backstrap looms since before settlers came in the 1520s. One end is tied to a tree or wall and the other to the weaver, who leans to loosen or tighten the warp.

6Complex patterns can take many years to learn. This weaving uses bright colors, animals, nature, and geometric shapes. Mothers and grandmothers teach girls at age seven or eight. The cloth becomes blouses, shawls, belts, bags, and carriers for babies or goods. Harris tweed is high-quality wool from the Outer Hebrides of Scotland. Those remote islands have rough coasts, treeless hills, and many sheep. Islanders keep their Gaelic culture and language. Around 1900, heavier looms turned the work from women to men, and weavers made more cloth.

7Harris tweed was inspected, and a certificate built trust and sales. Weavers became more efficient, but they still weave by hand. A 1980s loom and a tweed handbag show this island tradition. Turkish and Persian rugs are famous for beauty and fine detail. The tradition goes back at least to 7,000 BCE, and each style belongs to a region. This area was once the center of the Ottoman Empire. Many peoples, languages, religions, and cultures mixed there for centuries. Weaving there has generally been women's work, and two women in Turkey make carpets.

8Carpets are sold in Morocco. Kilims are flat weaves, made from warp and weft in geometric patterns. Villagers made them for decoration, wrapping, room dividers, and floors. Carpets are raised weaves made by weaving and knotting, so they are thick and heavy. Many have floral or nature patterns and were used for floors, beds, and prayer. Their motifs show heritage as well as use and beauty. Kente cloth is made in Ghana and Togo, especially by Asante and Ewe men. Bright strips three to eight inches wide are cut and sewn into larger ceremonial cloth.

9Kente designs have names and meanings tied to beliefs, history, and social life. Long ago only royalty wore it, but now everyone can. It is a symbol of cultural pride, and weavers may wear their own work. Different kinds of Kente cloth can be displayed, and a weaver may work on one long strip. Raranga is a Maori weaving method in New Zealand. The plant harakeke is a strong lily, not true flax, and the Maori have used it for at least one thousand years. They make mats, baskets, clothing, and other items. Weaving is mostly women's art, but men use harakeke for ropes, fishing lines, building, and hunting.

10You can also learn about Indonesian ikat, Japanese kasuri, Nepalese inlay, Peruvian tapestry, Samoan fine mats, and silk weaving in India, France, and China. The Maori believe the gods create through artisans, so raranga is both spiritual and useful. Ancestors passed it down, and it shows the culture survived after the British oppressed the Maori and stole their land. This book shows only a few of the world's weaving traditions. Each tradition reflects native materials, everyday needs, and cultural symbols. A handwoven piece in a museum, gallery, or shop tells a story. Many of these cultures still thrive, so notice the woven things you use. Even machine cloth tells a story, and one machine can weave complex patterns quickly.

11Artisans make craft items by hand. Mechanized work brings machines into making cloth. A backstrap loom joins a tree or wall to the weaver's body. Motifs are designs that carry stories or themes. Flat weaves use warp and weft without knots, while raised weaves add knots. A shed is the opening where weft threads pass. Warp threads form the base, and weft threads cross them to make cloth. Jeans, placemats, and bags are cloth goods too.

79. When Many People Solve One Problem

1This book begins with gamers who came to the rescue. It then tells of a prize for finding longitude and a prize for keeping food. It shows people working together, then crowdsourcing in our time, including supercomputers and games. It covers tests of ideas, raising money, times the crowd goes wrong, and a better future. A word list ends it. Scientists spent ten years on one mystery: the way proteins fold. Many microbes that cause disease are built from proteins in an important way. If researchers understood that structure, they might find cures. Years passed with no success. Science then asked an unlikely helper: people who play computer games. Foldit is a computer game. The University of Washington created it in 2008. The task for a player is to design proteins that are new. Within three weeks, the players had figured out a protein's structure. That protein was in a deadly virus. Scientists had been stuck on it for years. Thousands of gamers used their brains together. Even the brightest scientists, working alone, had less power than that group. Asking so many minds to help at one time is called crowdsourcing. One task, or one problem, can be handed to the general public by a person or by an organization. The help may be ideas. It may be services. People often volunteer because each person's share of the project is small. A small effort still adds up when hundreds or thousands of people join in.

2One of the world's first crowdsourcing efforts fixed a problem that had worried people for centuries. Sailors who are far from land need to know exactly where they are. The difference can be life or death. A ship that loses its position can hit shoals or other dangers and sink. Sailors have long found their latitude, which is how far north or south they are. They watch the Sun's position at noon with care. East-west position is called longitude. In earlier ages, sailors could only guess it. In 1714, Britain's government offered cash to anyone who found a reliable way to know longitude at sea. People all over the country worked on the problem for years. A clockmaker named John Harrison found a solution in 1741. He knew longitude could be worked out from the Sun's position if the correct time was known. The best clocks of the 1700s used pendulums. Timekeeping came from a weight that swung one way and then the other. Those clocks failed at sea. Wind and waves make ships toss and turn. Harrison invented a new kind of clock. It could keep time at sea. Sailors could then work out longitude at any hour of the day. Crowdsourcing also helped with problems on land. Napoleon Bonaparte was France's greatest general. He once said that an army's march depends on its stomach. He meant that soldiers cannot fight if they have nothing to eat. In 1795, France's government offered a prize. Someone had to invent a way to keep food safe for a long military campaign. Nicolas Appert made candy for a living. After fourteen years he had a method that worked. He sealed food so no air could get in. He used empty champagne bottles. The prize money paid for a factory. It was the first one that canned food. People everywhere still use the method he perfected. Modern machines make canning fast and easy.

3A contest is not the only way to put many minds on one problem. The public can chase one goal together. They do not have to compete. In 1849, Joseph Henry asked for help. His job as a scientist was at the Smithsonian Institution. About 150 volunteers were asked to gather facts about the weather. The places were all over the United States. People tracked local weather with care. Each month they sent reports by telegraph. The project kept growing for the ten years that followed. Canada sent weather watchers. Mexico joined in. Latin America joined in. The Caribbean joined in. A book in 1861 printed what the volunteers had learned about storms and about weather. The climate record came to depend on that book. Joseph Henry lived from 1797 to 1878. The Smithsonian Institution had him as its earliest leader. Today the Smithsonian includes centers for research and for education. Museums are part of it. Galleries, gardens, and a zoo are part of it too. Crowdsourcing has kept changing as new technologies have appeared. The Internet made it easier for people to work on projects together than it was in the past. Wikipedia is an online encyclopedia built by a crowd. Members of the general public may write its articles. They may check facts. They may edit. Millions of users share more experience and knowledge as a group than any old encyclopedia staff could. Science is one topic. History, sports, and television shows are others. Wikipedia launched in 2001. It now has more than five million articles. On the Internet, no general reference is more popular.

4Supercomputers are very powerful machines. Scientists have used them for a long time. Those machines crunch numbers and do complex calculations. Supercomputers cost a great deal. Getting time on one can be hard. Crowdsourcing on the Internet let scientists build huge networks. Thousands or millions of ordinary computers, in homes, offices, and schools, now do work that once needed a supercomputer. A teenage girl watches a screensaver called SETI@home. It studies radio signals from space, looking for signs of alien life. SETI is a short form of Search for Extraterrestrial Intelligence. For decades, scientists have collected electromagnetic signals from space. The search looks across the galaxy for hints that alien life is there. A screensaver came out in 1999. Its name is SETI@home, which means SETI at home. Millions of resting computers lend their processing power to the program. Each machine studies a small unit of the collected data. The same method is now used for testing software, for math, for biology, and for more. Leave your computer, and it might spend that time helping science. One of the most exciting crowdsourcing methods uses computer games. Foldit's success led scientists to release more games. Ordinary people become lab helpers in those games. In Eyewire, gamers map the brain's neurons. Planet Hunters players located forty planets that resemble Earth. Professional astronomers had missed those planets. Phylo shows patterns of colored squares. Tetris, the game of puzzle blocks, is what they resemble. What they really do is line up sequences of DNA. Their play aids research into diseases that genetic disorders cause. Fraxinus has the look of a game made of puzzles. A set pattern is shown, and players match shapes of color to it. Ash-tree DNA is in the game. So is DNA from a fungus. That fungus is killing the trees. The goal is real protection for the trees, and players hunt for new methods. Quantum Moves asks players to shift an atom. The tool is a beam of light, and the methods are new. Computer games let scientists crowdsource important research of every kind.

5Crowdsourcing is not only for science. In 2008, Lego began asking the crowd for ideas. New sets were the goal, both for the toy bricks and for characters. Anyone age thirteen or older could suggest a new Lego set. People could also vote for ideas they liked. Lego would think about building and selling a set if a year of voting reached at least ten thousand. Fans could invent far more ideas than the company's designers could. Fans could point the company toward sets people would want. A person from the public offered a set idea. The year was 2016. It would celebrate women who worked at the space agency NASA. Her idea shot up in the voting. The next year, Lego released a set of 231 pieces called Women of NASA. It became a best seller at once. The set shows astronomer Nancy Grace Roman. It shows computer scientist Margaret Hamilton. Sally Ride, an astronaut, is in the set. Mae Jemison, also an astronaut, is in it too. Money for a cause can be raised by a group. That is another form of crowdsourcing. An invention can cost more than one person has. So can a movie. So can a political campaign. A few dollars from each of thousands of people, or from millions, grows quickly. Crowdfunding of this kind has paid for new albums of music. It has paid for films and for video games. The sums reach thousands of dollars, and sometimes millions. Star Citizen is an online game set in space. In 2014 it gathered over $150 million so it could be developed. Nobody had raised that much for such a project before then. Crowdfunding has also served many charitable causes. AdoptTogether is a website. It lets people donate money to families who want to adopt a child. AdoptTogether was founded by Hank Fortener. A picture shows him speaking at a fund-raiser. Children who need homes are the reason for the event. After an adoption, donors hear news about the child. People are more likely to give money when they feel more involved.

6YouTube hosted a 2014 fund-raiser. People called it the Ice Bucket Challenge. It went viral. Movie stars and ordinary kids alike made videos of themselves. They dumped ice water on their heads. Each video asked people to give money to help cure ALS. The nervous system is what ALS attacks. ALS is a disease. The challenge brought in $115 million. Doctors used part of it to help identify the gene that causes the disease. Ordinary people helped by the millions, and a cure is now one step nearer. Hainan is a province in China. Young people there joined the challenge with ice buckets. The day was World ALS Day in 2017. Crowdsourcing is a powerful tool. A crowd, once its power is released, is not easy to steer. Britain's government learned that in 2016. They let the public vote on a name for a new ship for polar research. The ship cost $287 million. They expected a name after a famous polar explorer such as Ernest Shackleton. A naturalist was another expected namesake. David Attenborough, who films nature, was the example. Internet voters grabbed a less scientific name instead. The winner was Boaty McBoatface. The organizers would not accept the winning name, silly as the vote was. David Attenborough got the ship's name in spite of the vote. He speaks in a picture taken when the ship's keel was laid. The ship carries his name as an honor. The public was disappointed. To soothe them, the silly name went to a drone that does research for the ship. That drone is a submersible. Antarctic waters are already being explored by Boaty McBoatface. The people who ran the naming campaign learned a lesson. If you hand a choice to a crowd, you may not get the result you hoped for.

7Failure happens, but progress can still come from this powerful tool. Future years will probably see it grow still more popular. Projects already use crowdsourcing to help the environment, to cure disease, and to explore space. A history-changing project could include you. Two physics-based computer games help researchers measure and catalog how humans perform at various tasks. One game, shown at the top, is Potential Penguin. The other, shown at the bottom, is Skim Racer. Glossary. Charitable means generous toward people in need. Pendulums are weights that hang from points that do not move, and they swing from side to side. Collective means several people, or several things, are treated as a single group. To preserve is to stop decay, or to keep something in its first state. A living thing's genes are stored as information in a code. That code is DNA. It is short for deoxyribonucleic acid. Proposals are ideas shared so others may consider them and discuss them. Electromagnetic refers to electrical fields and magnetic fields acting on each other, or to something that comes from that action. A screensaver is a computer program. The computer is on, but nobody is using it. The program puts images on the screen, and those images change or move. Microbes are microscopic living things. A shoal is high ground beneath the water. Boats are in danger there. Neurons are nerve cells. Information moves inside the brain on these cells. It also moves between the brain and the rest of the body. Viral describes something shared quickly and widely over social media.

80. Why Fresh Water Is Precious

1Tadpoles race against time as their rock puddle shrinks. In many places, people treat water as something that will always be on hand. Daily life is full of water. Faucets release it on demand. After rain, sidewalks hold puddles. Lakes look full. Rivers look full. Stores sell water in bottles. Public fountains give people a drink. Lake Baikal lies in Russia. Among freshwater lakes, none on Earth is deeper. None is older either. By volume, Baikal is the largest lake. By surface area, the record belongs to Lake Superior. Picture a glass as wide as the United States. All the water on the planet would go into that glass. The water would stand 145 kilometers high. That height is 90 miles. Water piled that high can seem like more than enough. Even so, drinking water is still out of reach for many people.

2Fresh water is growing more precious than gold. It is growing more precious than oil as well. Usable water on this planet falls short of what people need. Salt water makes up most of the supply. People cannot drink it. Crops cannot be watered with it. Fresh water is only about 3 percent of the total. Polar ice caps lock away most of that fresh share. Glaciers lock away more of it. Aquifers, which are rock layers underground, lock away still more. Salt water in the oceans accounts for 97 percent. The fresh share is the other 3 percent. Of all fresh water, ice caps and glaciers hold 68.7 percent. Groundwater holds 30.1 percent. Surface water holds 0.3 percent. Of fresh water on the surface, lakes hold 87 percent. Rivers hold 2 percent. Swamps hold 11 percent. Drinking water worldwide draws 25 percent to 40 percent from groundwater. Food crops draw an even bigger share from groundwater. Those underground supplies are getting smaller. Water is scarce across Saudi Arabia. People there can still obtain it because the country has a high income. Across the planet, water is not shared out evenly. A few countries hold more than the rest. People's needs can still be met in most countries. The missing piece is often a way to deliver the water. Hard-to-reach sources are easier to tap where income is high. Wells are costly to dig where income is low. Dams that form reservoirs are costly to build there too. Today, reachable usable fresh water is only 1 percent of the fresh total. Stress sits on that 1 percent. One cause is pollution. Another cause is demand, which climbs as population climbs. Climate change is a third cause.

3On many continents, polluted water is a serious trouble. The Amazon rainforest in South America spreads across 7 million square kilometers. In square miles, that spread is 2.7 million. Roughly thirty million people make their homes there. Plant species there number more than thirty thousand. Untreated human waste has entered the Amazon River for decades. Toxic chemicals have entered it as well. These poisons are slowly wrecking the jungle's fragile ecosystem. Brazil's Amazon River receives many kinds of waste. Elsewhere, in low-income places, fresh water is often left untreated. Organisms that carry disease then multiply. Sickness follows in other forms. Running water is missing for many households. Indoor plumbing is missing too. Rivers and streams then receive waste loaded with bacteria. People drink from those toxic waterways. They cook with that water. They bathe in it. A report from the United Nations Development Program states a grim share. Patients with waterborne illnesses fill half of hospital beds worldwide. In 2014, a resident of Dimock, Pennsylvania, raised a jug of fouled water. An official in Washington, D.C., viewed the jug. Residents there, including him, wanted fracking on nearby land halted. Oil and gas can be obtained by fracking. Other pollution troubles face high-income countries, including the United States. Clean drinking water is available to most people there. Farm runoff is the challenge seen most often. Rain carries chemicals off fields and into waterways. Rain also carries animal waste off farms. U.S. waters face an old sewage treatment system. They face oil spills. They face wastewater left by fracking for oil and gas. They face radioactive waste. Several kinds of water pollution are limited by the Clean Water Act. Wider protections have been the goal of some administrations. Narrower protections have been the goal of others. A mobile phone is more common worldwide than a toilet.

4A human milestone arrived in 2011. An infant girl was born on October 30. Her birthplace was the Philippines. She became person number 7 billion. By 2050, experts expect 9.7 billion people to be walking the planet. Each of them will seek food. Each will seek energy. Each will seek land. Each will seek water. Each person then has a smaller water share as population grows. Earth's population in 1950 was 2.5 billion. Fresh water used then was only a third of today's use. Water was widely seen then as a resource without a limit. Research now shows that 1 in 9 people lack safe drinking water. Many of them live on less than 8 liters a day. Two gallons is that same daily amount. By 2050, water-stressed regions are expected to hold over half the world's people. Poverty helps explain polluted water in Bangladesh. Overpopulation helps explain it too. A population of 250 million is possible there by 2050. Compared with 2020, that count would add more than 85 million people. Polluted drinking water is a far smaller threat in high-income countries. They can often afford to purify water. Human waste can often be handled properly there. Shortages can still follow population growth where income is high. Water use per person is higher in those countries. Yearly human water use is charted in trillion cubic meters. A single cubic meter equals 264 gallons. Population, in billions, appears on the same chart. A four-person family in the United States averages 1,514 liters of water in a day. Four hundred gallons is that family total. One person accounts for roughly 379 liters. One hundred gallons is that personal share. A few users could keep using water at this rate without emptying the supply. Limited sources now support ever more people. By 2050, Los Angeles County is expected to pass thirteen million residents. Local water there can support only one million people. Distant rivers supply Los Angeles. The same practice serves many other cities of the western United States. The cost of moving that water is high. Enough river water for every user is gone.

5People around the world already feel climate change. Extreme weather is one result. Storms belong to that pattern. Floods belong to that pattern. The threat to the global water supply may be the worst threat of all. Scientists look ahead to 2050. More than five billion people may then face severe shortages. Climate change would be the cause. Too much carbon dioxide in the air is behind climate change. Other greenhouse gases add to the problem. A large share of those gases comes from burned fossil fuels. Those gases trap the Sun's heat close to the surface. Heat stays inside a greenhouse in much the same way. Earth's temperature rises overall as a result. That warming leads to climate change. The extra heat disrupts the water cycle. Evaporation and precipitation must stay in a delicate balance. That balance is the water cycle. Surface water evaporates faster when temperatures rise. The atmosphere receives that extra vapor. Places do not all feel the same effect. Some areas may dry out. Other areas may receive too much precipitation. A drought is a long spell of precipitation that is abnormally low. Water then runs short. Drought risk is especially high around the Mediterranean. The Middle East shares that high risk. Most scientists in the United States blame climate change for an extreme drought. The Southwest is hit. California is hit. Problems from the Southwest drought date to 1999. Lake Mead's water has plunged. Nevada and Arizona meet at the lake's border. The Colorado River feeds the lake. Fresh water from the lake serves twenty-five million people. Rationing is likely for desert residents if rains stay low. A drought contingency plan produced agreed cuts in 2019. Those cuts were a first. Arizona took part. Nevada took part. Mexico took part. The Colorado River supports nearly forty million people. It fades to a trickle and never reaches the sea.

6Melting has struck glaciers that stand high among the Himalaya mountains. Scientists blame climate change for that melt. Those glaciers help supply 1.5 billion people with fresh water. India is home to many of them. Pakistan is home to many of them. Six other countries in Asia are home to many of them. Melting used to happen slowly. Tributaries of the Indus received the melt. Tributaries of the Ganges received it. Tributaries of the Brahmaputra received it. Rapid melt is now using up the stored water. At the same time, the extra melt has caused severe flooding. All three rivers have flooded. As climate change worsens, energy production has less water. Livestock raising has less water. Crop growing has less water. Wildlife is harmed. Natural ecosystems are harmed. Cities now fight over who has the best claim to water. States join that fight. Countries join that fight. India and Pakistan feel growing tension over water. India and China feel it too. All three are racing to build dams. Himalayan headwaters are the sites. Those headwaters feed important rivers below. The Nile is another river under argument. Some African countries disagree on the strongest claim. Few places on Earth are drier than the Middle East. Some experts say water will be the cause of the next war there. One person's choice to conserve can still have an effect. A state can conserve as well. California's driest recorded year was 2013. The governor requested a voluntary reduction of 20 percent. Residents did reduce use. The dry spell was over in 2017. Use rose once the dry spell ended. Shut off the tap while brushing your teeth. Keep showers short. Take a bath if you prefer to linger. Repair leaks. Repair drips. Meat can be a smaller part of meals. Potatoes of equal amount need far less water than beef. The gap is 125 times, on average. Used items can replace new ones when that choice is open. Recycled products can replace them too. A rain barrel can catch water and hold it for plants. Yards do better without added chemicals. Pollution comes from plastic. Plastic use can be cut when a choice exists. A bottle you can refill can stand in for bottled water. A bicycle ride is one way to help stop climate change. Walking helps that effort. Public transit helps that effort.

7Competing water uses appear on one chart. High-income countries are one group on it. The world as a whole is another. Low- and middle-income countries are the third. UNESCO published the figures in the World Water Development Report. Domestic use in high-income countries is 11 percent. Industry there is 59 percent. Farming there is 30 percent. Domestic use worldwide is 8 percent. Industry worldwide is 22 percent. Farming worldwide is 70 percent. Domestic use in low- and middle-income countries is 8 percent. Industry there is 10 percent. Farming there is 82 percent. National governments also need to hunt for answers. Workers in Saudi Arabia turn salt water into fresh water. Desalination is the name of that process. The cost is high. Desalination plants are beyond the budget of many countries. Singapore, an island nation, purifies wastewater. Dishwashing sends some of that wastewater. Toilets send some of it. Baths send some of it. Showers send some of it. A few other countries purify wastewater as well. This method supplies one-third of Singapore's water. Around 70 percent of water worldwide goes to farming. Some farmers are finding ways to use less. Some ranchers are reaching that goal too. Cities that grow quickly could take the water that is saved. A simple fix for the planet's water trouble does not exist. Conserving this precious resource is still everyone's job. Our generation is not the only one with that need. Generations not yet born share it. People cannot see carbon dioxide, because it is an invisible gas. Burning fossil fuels often creates it. Climate change means lasting shifts in Earth's weather patterns. Conserve means to guard a natural place, or a resource, so it lasts longer. Distributed means spread or scattered across an area. An ecosystem is living things plus their habitat. Fossil fuels are coal, oil, and natural gas taken out of the ground. Fresh water is water that is not salty. Pollution is harmful substances put into air, water, or soil. The harm that follows counts as pollution too. A population is all members of one species in one area. Purify means to cleanse. Dirt and bad things are removed. Rationing limits how goods are portioned when the supply is short. A resource that is not used up completely can be called sustainable.
